\begin{document}
\title{A Dichotomy for Complex Boolean Holant\\[0.15em]
with Binary Disequality}

\author[1]{Chenghua Liu}
\author[2]{Boning Meng}

\affil[1]{Institute of Software, Chinese Academy of Sciences, Beijing, China}
\affil[2]{University of Regensburg, Regensburg, Germany}
\affil[ ]{\texttt{liuch.russell@gmail.com}, \texttt{mengboning2013@gmail.com}}
\date{}

\hypersetup{pageanchor=false}
\begin{titlepage}
\maketitle
\thispagestyle{empty}

\begin{abstract}
\normalsize
We classify Boolean Holant problems with a supplied binary disequality signature and an arbitrary fixed finite set of algebraic complex-valued signatures. Each problem is polynomial-time computable or \(\SharpP\)-hard, and the tractability criterion is decidable. The proof combines arity reduction with the growth of the available binary transfer group and uses stable pairs to recover the structure of higher-arity signatures. We also prove a dichotomy for the disequality-edge model when the signature set contains an endpoint-nondegenerate irreducible eight-vertex signature, resolving the corresponding arity-four cases in the main proof.
\end{abstract}
\end{titlepage}


\clearpage
\pagenumbering{arabic}
\hypersetup{pageanchor=true}

% BEGIN SHORT PAPER
\section{Introduction}
\label{s:sec:p1-introduction}

Many counting problems ask for a global sum determined by local
constraints: each assignment receives the product of its local weights,
and these products are summed over all assignments.  A complexity
dichotomy identifies the choices of local signatures that admit
polynomial-time evaluation and proves \(\SharpP\)-hardness for every
remaining choice.  On the Boolean domain, this program has a long history
in counting constraint satisfaction problems (\(\#\mathrm{CSP}\)).
Creignou and Hermann classified the unweighted case
\cite{CreignouHermann1996}.  Later dichotomies treated nonnegative rational
weights \cite{DyerGoldbergJerrum2009}, rational weights of mixed signs
\cite{BulatovDyerGoldbergJalseniusRicherby2009}, and complex weights
\cite{CaiLuXia2014}.  Complex weights permit cancellations that yield
tractable cases absent from the nonnegative setting
\cite{CaiLuXia2014}, making the algebra of the weights part of the
classification.
Over arbitrary finite domains, counting-CSP dichotomies have likewise
been established for unweighted constraints
\cite{Bulatov2013,DyerRicherby2013}, nonnegative rational weights
\cite{BulatovDyerGoldbergJalseniusJerrumRicherby2012}, nonnegative algebraic
weights \cite{CaiChenLu2016}, and algebraic complex weights
\cite{CaiChen2017}.

The Holant framework brings the availability of local constraints into
this classification problem \cite{CaiLuXia2009}.  A counting-CSP instance
can be represented by a bipartite graph whose variable vertices carry
equality constraints, enforcing agreement among all occurrences of a
variable.  Holant allows the available vertex signatures to be prescribed
without supplying these equalities.  This accommodates problems such as
counting perfect matchings, where each vertex requires exactly one
incident edge to be selected.  It also makes the construction of useful
local signatures a part of the classification proof.  Holographic
transformations, originating in Valiant's algorithms \cite{Valiant2008},
give another source of tractability: a simultaneous change of basis can
expose structure in the local signatures while preserving the value of the
total sum.

Boolean Holant dichotomies have addressed both restrictions on the
signatures and assumptions about auxiliary signatures.  With no prescribed
auxiliary signatures, classifications were obtained for symmetric
real-valued signatures \cite{HuangLu2016}, symmetric complex-valued
signatures \cite{CaiGuoWilliams2016}, arbitrary nonnegative signatures
\cite{LinWang2018}, and arbitrary real-valued signatures
\cite{ShaoCai2020}.  Here symmetry means that a signature depends only on
the number of ones in its input.  Another line of work supplies unary
signatures: \(\Holant^*\) supplies all of them
\cite{CaiLuXia2011HolantStar}, \(\Holant^+\) supplies four distinguished
ones \cite{Backens2017}, and \(\Holant^c\) supplies the two pinnings
\(\DeltaZero=[1,0]^{\mathsf T}\) and
\(\DeltaOne=[0,1]^{\mathsf T}\).
The last model was classified for symmetric complex signatures
\cite{CaiHuangLu2012Holantc}, arbitrary real signatures
\cite{CaiLuXia2018RealHolantc}, and algebraic complex signatures
\cite{Backens2021,Backens2025}.  The real-valued classification introduced
the local-affine tractable family \(\cL\), which also occurs here.

Meng, Wang, Xia, and Zheng proved a tensor-factor extraction theorem and
classified sets containing a nonzero odd-arity signature. Their condition
(PC) lists seven cases \cite[conference version, Theorem~30]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}.
For two cases involving weighted Eulerian orientations
\cite{MengWangXia2025EO}, the original bound was \(\FP^{\mathsf{NP}}\).
Guan, Shao, and Shi gave polynomial-time algorithms for these cases
\cite{GuanShaoShi2026}. We write \(\Tract\) for the resulting seven-case
criterion, the most general tractability condition currently known for
algebraic complex-valued Boolean Holant.
Even-arity signatures present a different resource constraint.  Every
gadget built entirely from them has an even number of external inputs,
because each internal edge consumes two inputs.  Such gadgets cannot
supply a unary signature, so binary signatures are the smallest
positive-arity objects available for further constructions.  We study
the case in which binary disequality, requiring two bits to differ, is
supplied.

There is also a precedent for prescribing disequality in counting CSP.
In \(\#\mathrm{CSP}_d\), each variable occurs a number of times divisible
by \(d\).  Cai, Fu, and Shao classified
\(\#\mathrm{CSP}_d(\Neq_2,\cF)\) for complex signatures
\cite[full version, Section~5.1 and Theorem~5.3]{CaiFuShao2020Entanglement}.
Shao and Cai used its hardness conclusion in their real Holant dichotomy
\cite[full version, Theorem~2.24]{ShaoCai2020}.

Prescribing binary disequality is useful for three further reasons.
First, ordinary edges enforce equality, and the supplied binary
signature enforces disequality. Together they restrict the binary groups
in Xia's framework \cite[Section~4 and Table~2]{Xia2026FrameworkV1} to
the marked forms analyzed here. Second, inserting binary vertices lets
us use disequality edges with equality supplied as a vertex signature.
We can thus choose the edge model suited to a construction while keeping
the original signatures in their original coordinates. Third, the classification applies to any Holant problem for which
a reduction obtains disequality. For the complementary problems, the
inability to obtain it supplies a structural restriction for further
classification.

\paragraph{The problem and main results.}
A Boolean signature of arity \(n\) is a function
\(f:\{0,1\}^n\to\mathbb C\).  In a Holant instance, a graph vertex
of degree \(n\) carries such a signature, with its incident edges ordered.
For a fixed finite set \(\cF\) of signatures, the problem
\(\Holant(\cF)\) evaluates
\[
  \sum_{\sigma:E\to\{0,1\}}
  \prod_{v\in V}f_v\bigl(\sigma|_{E(v)}\bigr),
  \qquad f_v\in\cF.
\]
Here \(E(v)\) is the ordered list of edge incidences at \(v\).
\Cref{subsec:holant-definitions} gives the conventions for loops and
multiple edges.  The graph and its vertex labels form the input. The set
\(\cF\) is fixed.  Write
\[
 X=(\Neq_2)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad I=(\Eq_2)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Our problem is \(\Holant(\cF,X):=\Holant(\cF\cup\{X\})\).
Thus any number of degree-two vertices may enforce opposite values on
their two incident edges.

With \(X\) supplied, three of the seven cases of \(\Tract\) are
excluded (\cref{lem:tract-X-anchor}). Write \(\ReTract\)
for the restriction to the other four. In one case every signature is a
tensor product of signatures of arity at most two. In each of the other
three, a common change of basis puts the edge and all vertex signatures
in the affine class \(\cA\), the product class \(\cP\), or the local-affine
class \(\cL\) (Section~\ref{s:short:tools}). The prefix \emph{Re} records this
restriction and the correspondence with the tractable cases of the
real-valued Holant dichotomy of Shao and Cai~\cite{ShaoCai2020}.

\begin{theorem}[Binary-disequality Holant dichotomy]
\label{s:thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy}
For every finite set of algebraic complex-valued Boolean signatures \(\cF\),
the problem \(\Holant(\cF,X)\) is polynomial-time computable when
\(\ReTract(\cF\cup\{X\})\) holds and is \(\SharpP\)-hard otherwise.
The tractability criterion is decidable from exact algebraic input.
\end{theorem}

The proof also classifies sets containing a particular kind of
arity-four signature, without a supplied equality signature.  Following Meng, Wang, Xia, and Zheng
\cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025}, write
\(\KHolant(\cF):=\Holant(\Neq_2\mid\cF)\) for the bipartite Holant
problem with disequality on the left and signatures from \(\cF\) on the
right.  Equivalently, each connection between signature inputs enforces
disequality.

A quaternary signature \(q\) is
\emph{endpoint-nondegenerate} when
\[
 q({\mathbf{x}})=0\quad\text{for odd }\wt({\mathbf{x}}),\qquad q(0000)q(1111)\ne0.
\]
Here \(\wt(\mathbf{x})\) counts the ones in \(\mathbf{x}\). Only the
eight even-weight inputs can have nonzero values, giving the eight-vertex
form used below.
A nonzero positive-arity signature is \emph{irreducible} if it has no factorization
\(a\otimes b\) on two nonempty blocks of inputs, allowing a permutation of inputs and a nonzero scalar.

\begin{theorem}[Quaternary \(\KHolant\) dichotomy]
\label{s:thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite set of algebraic complex-valued Boolean signatures.
Suppose that \(\cF\) contains an endpoint-nondegenerate irreducible
quaternary signature.  Then \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is polynomial-time computable
if \(\ReTract(\cF)\) holds and is \(\SharpP\)-hard otherwise.  The
tractability criterion is decidable from exact algebraic input.
\end{theorem}


The dichotomy for the eight-vertex model
\cite[arXiv version~2, Theorem~3.1]{CaiFu2023} classifies a single
eight-vertex signature \(q\) with nonzero endpoints. Even if \(q\) alone
is tractable, using it with other signatures can make the problem hard.
Theorem~\ref{s:thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy} settles the complexity
of the whole set whenever a construction yields an endpoint-nondegenerate
irreducible quaternary signature.

\paragraph{From existing tools to stable pairs.}
The odd-arity classification and tensor-factor extraction of
\cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025} provide two starting points. Obtaining a
nonzero odd-arity signature invokes a known dichotomy. In the cases
considered here, their factor-extraction theorem allows us to simulate
irreducible factors and retain them for subsequent constructions. Two existing approaches suggest
how to use these tools. Shao and Cai's real-valued proof uses arity
descent \cite[Sections~3--4]{ShaoCai2020}, but its orthogonality and norm
arguments do not directly control cancellation for arbitrary complex
signatures. Xia's finite-group approach
\cite[Sections~4--5]{Xia2026FrameworkV1} organizes the available binary
constructions. The remaining task is to determine how these constructions
constrain higher-arity signatures. We couple these approaches by tracking a nonbinary
irreducible signature \(f\) of minimum arity together with a group \(G\).
After the preliminary reductions, \(G\) consists of the transfer classes
\([BX]\) of all ordered nonzero binaries \(B\) directly realizable by
gadgets over the current set. Brackets identify matrices up to nonzero scalar.

Close a pair of inputs of \(f\) with an available binary and retain
the irreducible factors of the output by reduction. An unresolved
nonbinary factor has smaller arity. A binary factor whose transfer class
lies outside \(G\) gives a different form of progress. Once retained,
it can be used directly in gadgets over the enlarged set. The complete
group strictly enlarges, so we can test the same \(f\) with new
contractions even when its arity has not decreased.
Figure~\ref{s:fig:complete-group-growth} shows the possible strict
inclusions. Bounded arity descent, a uniform bound on group orders,
and finite processing at each stage ensure termination.

If neither form of progress occurs and the complexity remains unresolved,
every prescribed one-copy contraction, including those obtained by attaching realizable
binaries to selected inputs, gives zero or a product of retained binaries on a perfect
matching. This is the stable condition. We call the outputs cards.
Fix the pair of inputs being closed and compare its nonzero cards. To
recover a common factor, we first seek a matching compatible with the
cards. Even when the pairings agree, the binary factors on an edge from
which we want to extract a common factor must be proportional across
the cards.

Gadgets test these requirements by preserving a proposed disagreement down to
bounded arity, where exhaustive classifications apply. When the
contractions recover the slices of \(f\), a common factor of the cards
lifts to \(f\), contradicting irreducibility. Other outcomes determine
the complexity or enlarge the binary group. In the cyclic case the
surviving arity-eight core, the indicator of the Reed--Muller code
\(RM(1,3)\) up to the equivalences in Section~\ref{s:short:groups}, permits
group enlargement. In the standard Klein-four
and internal tetrahedral cases the whole set can admit a common real
or affine basis, respectively.

The short account below gives the classification argument and selected
proof mechanisms. The full paper follows the shared references. Numbers prefixed by F refer to its statements and proofs. Recognition takes the signature
set as input, whereas the signature set is fixed in each evaluation problem.

\section{Classification results and reductions}
\label{s:short:tools}
In the disequality-edge model, the equality constraint of an ordinary edge is realized
by two \(X\)-edges joined through an \(I\)-vertex, since \(XIX=I\).
Conversely, an \(X\)-edge becomes an \(X\)-vertex in the ordinary model,
and an \(I\)-vertex can be suppressed. Inserting and suppressing these
binary vertices, using \(X^2=I\), gives
\[
 \Holant(\cF,X)\equivT\KHolant(\cF\cup\{I\}).
\]
Thus proving Theorem~\ref{s:thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy} amounts to classifying
disequality-edge problems whose signature sets contain \(I\).
For counting problems \(A,B\), write \(A\leT B\) if \(A\) has a
polynomial-time Turing reduction to \(B\), and \(A\equivT B\) if both
reductions hold. A finite set \(\cS\) is \emph{simulated} by
\(\cF\) if \(\KHolant(\cF\cup\cS)\leT\KHolant(\cF)\).
We use \emph{realized} for a signature obtained by a gadget.

The second model equivalence is a holographic transformation
\cite{Valiant2008}. Set
\[
 K=\frac1{\sqrt2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\i&-i\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad K^{\mathsf T}K=X,
 \qquad K\cF=\{K^{\otimes\arity(f)}f:f\in\cF\}.
\]
Transforming every signature by \(K\) makes contraction along an ordinary
edge equal to the original contraction against \(X\). The partition
function is unchanged, so
\[
 \KHolant(\cF)\equivT\Holant(K\cF).
\]

The tractable classes used here are the affine class \(\cA\)
\cite{CaiLuXia2009,CaiLuXia2014}, the product class \(\cP\)
\cite{DyerGoldbergJerrum2009,CaiLuXia2014}, and the local-affine class
\(\cL\) \cite{CaiLuXia2018RealHolantc}. We use their standard
definitions from these papers. The fourth case is the tensor closure
\(\angles{\cT}\), where \(\cT\) consists of signatures of arity at most
two. Splitting these tensor products reduces evaluation to paths and cycles.

Holographic transformations explain the common-transformability
condition. For edge signature \(E\in\{I,X\}\) and
\(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP,\cL\}\), it requires a single invertible matrix \(M\)
such that
\[
 M^{\mathsf T}EM\in\cC,\qquad M^{-1}\cF\subseteq\cC.
\]
Here \(M^{-1}\) acts on every input of each signature. Transforming all
vertices by \(M^{-1}\) and all edges by \(M^{\mathsf T}EM\) preserves
the partition function. Both conditions put the entire network in the
same tractable class, using one common basis.
Write \(\ReTract_E\) for these three possibilities together with
\(\cF\subseteq\angles{\cT}\). Theorem~\ref{s:thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy}
uses \(E=I\) and Theorem~\ref{s:thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy}
uses \(E=X\). Elsewhere \(\ReTract=\ReTract_I\).
The corresponding criteria in the two models satisfy
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \ReTract_X(\cF)&\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ReTract(K\cF),\\
 \ReTract(\cF\cup\{X\})&\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ReTract(K(\cF\cup\{I\})).
\end{aligned}
\]
The proofs are in \cref{subsec:tract-predicate} and
the discussion following \cref{lem:p1-equality-wire-equivalence}.

Both predicates use the zero and nullary preprocessing of \cref{subsec:tract-predicate}. In Theorem~\ref{s:thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy},
\(X\in\cF\cup\{X\}\) excludes the three additional cases of \(\Tract\).
In the equivalent disequality-edge model the set contains \(I\), so its
image under \(K\) contains \(K^{\otimes2}I=\diag(1,-1)\), which excludes
the same cases (\cref{lem:tract-X-anchor}).

A nonzero odd-arity signature, including a unary, invokes the known
classification and algorithms \cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025,GuanShaoShi2026}.
A nonzero singular binary has unary tensor factors. For a nonsingular binary \(B\), the transfer \(BX\)
has infinite projective order if no positive power is a scalar matrix.
Such a binary yields a rank-one binary by interpolation. For the signature sets containing \(I\), factor
extraction and the unary reductions therefore resolve these cases
(\cref{lem:binary-interpolation} and
\cref{lem:common-unary-exit}).

The factor-extraction theorem of \cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025} lets us
simulate the factors of an available tensor product unless all supports
lie on the same side of the middle Hamming layer (including that layer).
Weighted Eulerian-orientation results classify this exception
\cite{MengWangXia2025EO,GuanShaoShi2026}. Since \(I(00)=I(11)=1\),
a set containing \(I\) has support on both sides. We retain all
previous signatures when passing to the set containing the simulated
factors. The problems remain Turing-equivalent, hardness transfers to
the preceding problem, and the same common basis establishes the
tractable condition for the preceding set.
We call the set factor-closed once all its nonzero positive-arity
irreducible tensor factors have been retained.

Two further reductions will be used in the proof of Theorem~\ref{s:thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy}.
A generalized equality is supported exactly on the all-zero and all-one
strings, with both values nonzero. At arity at least four it permits
the unary reduction. An equality anchor is a realized quaternary for
which one change of basis turns the edge into binary equality and the
quaternary into four-ary equality, up to nonzero scalars. It likewise
permits a nonzero unary to be simulated. These reductions classify the
whole set (\cref{lem:pure-ge} and
\cref{lem:equality-anchor}).

\section{From an eight-vertex signature to a signature-set dichotomy}
\label{s:short:eight}
The dichotomy for the eight-vertex model classifies \(\Holant(X\mid q)\)
for a single endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature \(q\)
\cite[arXiv version~2, Theorem~3.1]{CaiFu2023}. Its hard case gives
hardness for any set realizing \(q\). In the tractable cases we must
determine how \(q\) interacts with the rest of the set. We allow \(q\)
to be reducible here because later gadgets can produce such a signature
and supply its factors.

\begin{proposition}[Eight-vertex reduction]
\label{s:short:eight-reduction}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite factor-closed retained set directly realizing an
endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature \(q\). Then
\(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard, or \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds,
or the original \(q\) factors across two pairs of inputs. In the last
case both binary factors can be adjoined by reduction, and one is a
nonsingular weighted equality \(\diag(p,q')\), with \(pq'\ne0\).
\end{proposition}

To prove the proposition, we examine the three exceptions of the
single-signature theorem. Product transformability
leaves three structural possibilities: a generalized equality, a product
of two weighted equalities, or a dense even-parity form that realizes an
equality anchor. The generalized equality and the anchor yield a simulated unary as
explained in Section~\ref{s:short:tools}, and therefore classify the whole set. The product of two binaries
is the factorization outcome of the proposition. The classification of product forms uses the
support and the ranks of the three pair flattenings, so it identifies
factors of \(q\) itself.

The endpoint conditions reduce local-affine transformability to affine
transformability. In the affine case, a common diagonal change of basis
preserves the disequality edge up to scalar and puts \(q\) into a normal
form with support of size two, four, or eight. The diagonal change
preserves the two nonzero endpoints of \(q\), so factor extraction
remains available throughout this analysis.

Support two gives a generalized equality, so the same unary reduction
applies. For support four, constant assignments on the two input pairs
define logical bits with
coefficient matrix \(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\C&D\end{smallmatrix}\right)\),
where \(AD=\pm BC\). If \(AD=-BC\), a two-copy gadget gives a generalized
equality. If \(AD=BC\), the normal form factors into two weighted
equalities. Undoing the diagonal change gives the required factorization
of the original \(q\).

On full even-parity support, the constructions give an equality anchor,
a nonzero rank-one binary, or a tensor product of nonsingular weighted equalities.
In the last case, extracting a factor and normalizing it to equality
allows a single-input flip, producing a quaternary with full odd-parity
support. A finite analysis of its contractions gives an equality anchor,
a nonzero rank-one binary, a binary of infinite projective transfer order,
or an endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature with support of size six.
The anchor invokes the equality-anchor reduction. A rank-one binary
supplies a unary factor, while an infinite-order binary first yields a
rank-one binary by interpolation. The support-six output lies outside
the three transformable classes and is hard by the dichotomy for the
eight-vertex model \cite[arXiv version~2, Theorem~3.1]{CaiFu2023}.
Thus each full-support case classifies the entire retained set.

The reductions transfer these conclusions back to the original set. The normal forms and finite gadget analysis,
including the exact computations and coverage arguments, are given in
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-normal-form},
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-gadget-exits}, and
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-odd}.

In Theorem~\ref{s:thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy}, irreducibility
excludes the factorization of \(q\). Its two nonzero endpoints give
support on both sides of the middle layer. \Cref{lem:tract-X-anchor} therefore reduces \(\Tract(K\cF)\) to
\(\ReTract(K\cF)\). An irreducible \(q\) of arity four also excludes
the tensor-product case. By the criterion correspondence in
Section~\ref{s:short:tools}, the three common-transformability cases give
the tractable side of the theorem (\cref{cor:p1-eight-vertex-reduced-interface}).

\section{Stabilizing a signature and its binary group}
\label{s:short:deck}
We return to Theorem~\ref{s:thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy}. By
Section~\ref{s:short:tools} we may work with disequality edges and
supplied \(I\). After factor extraction and the binary and odd-arity
reductions, choose a nonbinary irreducible \(f\) of minimum
arity \(n=\nu(\cF)\) in the retained factor-closed set \(\cF\).
Its arity is even. We follow the pair \((f,G(\cF))\), recording
\(\cF\) as part of the state because it determines the available gadgets.

For an ordered binary \(B\), define its transfer by \(T_B=BX\).
Series composition realizes \(BXC\), with \(T_{BXC}=T_BT_C\).
After the binary reductions, every nonzero realizable binary is
nonsingular with finite projective transfer order. We call such a binary
\emph{safe}. Put
\[
 G(\cF)=\{[BX]:B\text{ is a safe ordered binary directly realizable over }\cF\}.
\]
Here \([BX]\) is the transfer class of \(B\), identifying matrices
up to nonzero scalar. This \emph{complete transfer group} includes the
classes of all realizable binary gadgets, of arbitrary size.
It is finite. In a fixed coefficient field \(L\), an eigenvalue ratio
of order \(r\) satisfies \(\varphi(r)\le2[L:\mathbb Q]\), bounding
the possible orders. Powers supply inverses. The faithful adjoint
representation and Burnside's finite-exponent theorem give finiteness
\cite{Burnside1905}. The finite subgroups of \(\PGL_2(\mathbb C)\)
then give a uniform order bound in this field (\cref{lem:bounded-torsion}).

Fix realizable ordered representatives \(B_g\) for \(g\in G\).
A \(G\)-matching signature is
\[
 c\bigotimes_{e\in M}B_{g_e}^{(e)},\qquad c\ne0,
\]
where \(M\) is an oriented perfect matching of the inputs and the
superscript places the binary on that ordered pair. The scalar absorbs
the choice of representatives. To test \(f\), attach a representative,
in either orientation, to any subset of its inputs through \(X\)-edges.
Write \(f^\delta\) for the resulting dressed signature. Close one ordered pair \((i,j)\)
with another representative \(B_h^\omega\), where
\(B_h^{\mathrm{id}}=B_h\) and \(B_h^{\mathrm{rev}}=B_h^{\mathsf T}\).
The \emph{matching deck} is the finite family
\[
 \mathfrak D_G(f)=\{0\}\ \cup\
 \left\{\rho\!\left(\partial_{(i,j)}^{XB_h^\omega X}f^\delta\right)
 :\delta,i\ne j,h,\omega,\rho\right\},
\]
which collects the outputs obtained by applying the allowed binary
dressings to a single copy of \(f\) and then closing one self-loop.
Here \(\partial_{(i,j)}^Q u\) sums the two inputs against the kernel
\(Q=XB_h^\omega X\), with one \(X\)-edge at each end of the closing binary. The permutation \(\rho\) ranges over residual input orders. Exactly one pair
is closed and at least two inputs remain. Every nonzero card is a direct
gadget output.

\begin{definition}[Stable state]
\label{s:short:stable}
Let \(\cF\) be finite, factor-closed, and contain \(I\). Let
\(f\in\cF\) be a minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature, and
let \(G=G(\cF)\) be finite with fixed realizable binary representatives.
The state \((\cF,G,f)\) is \emph{deck-stable} if every nonzero card
of \(\mathfrak D_G(f)\) is a \(G\)-matching signature and all its
factors are retained in \(\cF\).
\end{definition}

Thus stability says that every allowed self-loop, including the specified
input dressings, gives zero or a tensor product of already available
binaries. Including input dressings makes this condition apply to the changes of
binary action used in later comparisons.

\begin{lemma}[Stabilization, \cref{lem:minimum-core-deck}]
\label{s:short:stabilization}
Let \(\cF_0\ni I\) be a retained factor-closed finite set, and let
\(f\in\cF_0\) be an even nonbinary irreducible signature of minimum arity.
The reductions either establish that \(\KHolant(\cF_0)\) is
polynomial-time computable or \(\SharpP\)-hard, or yield a finite retained
factor-closed extension \(\cF'\supseteq\cF_0\) with
\(\KHolant(\cF')\equivT\KHolant(\cF_0)\) such that either its minimum
nonbinary arity is smaller or \((\cF',G(\cF'),f)\) is deck-stable.
The procedure can strictly enlarge the complete binary group before
reaching either extension.
\end{lemma}

Process each card and retain its irreducible factors using factor
extraction. A nonbinary factor has arity at most \(n-2\).
Odd-arity factors and unsafe binaries invoke the established complexity
classifications. Otherwise there are two distinct forms of progress:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\roman*)}]
\item \emph{Lowering the arity.} An even nonbinary factor gives an
extension with smaller minimum nonbinary arity. Choose a minimum-arity
irreducible in this extension as the new core signature and restart
stabilization.
\item \emph{Enlarging the binary group.} Retain the new binary factors.
If a safe binary \(B\) has \([BX]\notin G\), the complete transfer group
strictly enlarges. Keep the same core \(f\) and regenerate its deck
using the larger group, which supplies additional contraction tests.
\end{enumerate}
Each extension retains the previous signatures, including \(I\), and
preserves Turing equivalence. Binary factors whose transfer classes
already lie in \(G\) do not by themselves give strict group growth.

Iterating these steps must eventually reach a stable pair unless the
complexity is determined first. The two forms of progress are finite
for different reasons.
Each replacement of the core strictly decreases its arity. Since arity
is an integer bounded below, only finitely many such replacements occur.
Each group enlargement is strict, and all previous binary resources
remain available even when the core changes. In the original
coordinates, gadget evaluation and normalized factor extraction stay
in one fixed number field. The complete groups therefore form an
increasing chain with the uniform order bound established above, so
only finitely many strict enlargements occur.

For a fixed core and group, the deck and its factorization trees are
finite, with each distinct card of this deck processed once. After the last core replacement
and group enlargement, this finite processing either determines the
complexity or leaves every nonzero card as a tensor product of retained
binaries from the current group. This is the stable condition, relative
to the final retained set and its complete group.

\section{Marked groups and their possible growth}
\label{s:short:marked}
The complete binary group determines the contractions available in the
matching argument. Its abstract type is cyclic, dihedral, or one of
the Platonic groups \(A_4,S_4,A_5\), by the classification of finite
subgroups of \(\PGL_2(\mathbb C)\). The supplied \(I\) puts \(x=[X]\)
in \(G\). Set \(Z=\diag(1,-1)\), \(Y=ZX\), and \(j=[Z]\).
Figure~\ref{s:fig:complete-group-growth} shows the possible strict
inclusions \(G<G^+\). Its superscripts distinguish groups of the
same abstract type that supply different contractions in our coordinates.

\caption{Possible strict growth of the complete transfer group with the
supplied equality fixed. Numbered arrows indicate subgroup inclusions
between marked forms, with their parameter conditions listed below the

\label{s:fig:complete-group-growth}

\clearpage

Reversing the inputs of a binary replaces
its transfer \(T\) by \(XT^{\mathsf T}X\), with
\([XT^{\mathsf T}X]=j[T]^{-1}j\). Since reversal preserves
realizability and \(G\) is closed under inverses, \(jGj=G\). Thus
\[
 \widehat G=\langle G,j\rangle,\qquad [\widehat G:G]\le2,
 \qquad \langle x,j\rangle\cong V_4.
\]
The positions of \(x\) and \(j\) in \(\widehat G\), including whether \(j\in G\),
distinguish the marked forms.

\paragraph{Cyclic and dihedral groups.}
Write \(C_N\) for the abstract cyclic group of order \(N\) and
\(D_{2m}\) for the abstract dihedral group of order \(2m\).
Let \(\mu_m\) be the \(m\)-th roots of unity and put
\(D(t)=\diag(1,t)\), \(C(t)=(1+t)I+i(1-t)Y\), and
\(A(t)=(1+t)I+(1-t)X\). In the original coordinates the cyclic
transfer group is \(\CycX{N}=\{[A(t)]:t\in\mu_N\}\), with \(N\)
even. Its smallest form is \(\CycX{2}=\langle x\rangle\). For \(N>2\),
\(j\notin G\) and \(\widehat G\cong D_{2N}\). The dihedral forms are
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \DihZ{2m}&=\{[D(t)],[XD(t)]:t\in\mu_m\},&
 \DihY{2m}&=\{[C(t)],[XC(t)]:t\in\mu_m\},\\
 \DihXzero{2m}&=\{[A(t)],[ZA(t)]:t\in\mu_m\},&
 \DihXone{2m}&=\{[A(t)],[ZA(\eta t)]:t\in\mu_m\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Here \(m\ge3\), with even \(m\) in the last two forms and
\(\eta\in\mu_{2m}\setminus\mu_m\). The subscript gives the group order.
The superscripts \(Z,Y,X\) specify the rotation axis, with rotation
matrices in \(\Span\{I,Z\}\), \(\Span\{I,Y\}\), or \(\Span\{I,X\}\).
The labels \((X,0)\) and \((X,1)\) distinguish the unshifted and shifted
reflection cosets. In the former \(j\in G\), whereas in the latter
\(j\notin G\). At the Klein-four boundary the two groups are
isomorphic to \(V_4\). In our coordinates the standard Pauli subgroup is
\(\KleinStd=\DihZ{4}=\DihY{4}\) and contains \(j\). The exotic subgroup
is \(\KleinEx=\DihXone{4}\) and does not contain \(j\).

\paragraph{Platonic groups.}
The tetrahedral forms are \(\TetInt\), with \(G=\widehat G\cong A_4\), and
\(\TetExt\), with \(G\cong A_4\) normal in \(\widehat G\cong S_4\).
Their superscripts record whether \(j\) is internal or external to \(G\).
For the four octahedral forms \(\OctDD,\OctTT,\OctDT,\OctTD\),
one has \(G=\widehat G\cong S_4\). Each superscript gives the cycle types of
\((x,j)\), where \(d\) denotes a double transposition and \(t\) a
transposition. Finally \(\IcoZero,\IcoOne\) are two coordinate forms
of the same abstract marked icosahedral type, with \(G=\widehat G\cong A_5\).
Explicit matrices and the complete marked classification appear in
\cref{thm:marked-finite-group-routing}.

If a retained extension gives \(G<G^+\), a path in
Figure~\ref{s:fig:complete-group-growth} leads from the marked type of
\(G\) to that of \(G^+\), with divisibility steps within a family
suppressed (\cref{prop:finite-marked-successor-routing}).
The binaries realizable over the enlarged set determine which inclusions
occur and which additional contractions can be used.

\section{Reconstruction from stable contractions}
\label{s:short:mechanism}
Fix a stable pair with core \(f\) and retained set \(\cF\). Every allowed one-copy self-loop on
\(f\) gives zero or a tensor product of retained binaries. We use these
contractions to recover enough of \(f\) to determine the complexity of \(\KHolant(\cF)\) or
to contradict the irreducibility of \(f\). The difficulty is that each
nonzero card comes with its own pairing of the remaining inputs and its
own binary factors on those pairs.

\paragraph{Comparing contractions at a fixed pair.}
Fix a pair \(p\) of inputs of \(f\). Closing \(p\) with different binary
kernels gives cards on the same remaining inputs. We can therefore
compare their values at each assignment. Write
\(\Phi_p^f(Q)=\partial_p^Q f\) for the card obtained with kernel \(Q\).
For example, suppose three available kernels satisfy \(Q_3=aQ_1+bQ_2\),
and write \(C_i=\Phi_p^f(Q_i)\). Linearity of contraction gives
\[
 C_3(z)=aC_1(z)+bC_2(z)
\]
for every assignment \(z\) to the remaining inputs. Wherever \(C_1\)
and \(C_2\) both vanish, \(C_3\) must vanish as well. A proposed choice
of matchings and factors that violates this identity at even one
assignment is ruled out.

\paragraph{Testing a disagreement at low arity.}
Suppose two cards pair one input with different partners. To test
whether these pairings are compatible, we keep the inputs that exhibit
the difference and close pairs elsewhere. On disjoint pairs these
closures commute with the contraction at \(p\). The two smaller cards
can thus be compared as contractions of the same smaller signature.
Each closure sums coefficients, so a choice of kernel can erase the
very entries that distinguish the pairings. The group-specific argument
must exhibit available kernels that keep the relevant entries nonzero.
If the difference survives down to bounded arity, the low-arity
classification can rule it out or yield a further reduction.

Once the matching is fixed, extracting a common binary on a chosen
edge requires the factors there to be proportional across the nonzero
cards. To test this, fix the inputs outside that edge and list the four
values obtained by varying its two inputs. If two such vectors, from
different cards or different assignments, were not proportional, no
single binary could factor out on that edge. A gadget testing this
possibility must preserve entries that witness the failure of
proportionality.

\paragraph{Lifting a common factor to \(f\).}
Suppose the available kernels span \(\Mat_2(\mathbb C)\). Choose four
that form a basis and close the same ordered pair \(p\) with each.
Write \(f_{ab}(z)=f(a,b,z)\), ordering the inputs with \(p\) first
and letting the remaining assignment \(z\) vary. The coordinate kernel
selecting \(ab\) is a linear combination of the four basis kernels,
so \(f_{ab}\) is the same linear combination of their cards. If the nonzero
cards all share a factor \(P\) on the same nonempty block of remaining
inputs, each slice has the form \(P\otimes r_{ab}\), allowing
\(r_{ab}=0\). Putting the slices back together gives \(f=P\otimes r\)
up to input order, where the four \(r_{ab}\) are the slices of \(r\)
obtained by fixing \(p\) to \(ab\). Since \(r\) contains the inputs of
\(p\), this factorization contradicts irreducibility. If the kernels
recover only combinations of slices, the group analysis must determine
what those combinations imply.

Some comparisons require additional gadgets, including gadgets built
from two copies of \(f\). Stability applies to the prescribed one-copy
cards. For each additional gadget we first extract the factors of its
output and apply the reductions of Section~\ref{s:short:deck}
(\cref{lem:matching-synthesis-measure} and
\cref{lem:token-coverage}).

\section{How the group cases use the common mechanism}
\label{s:short:groups}
\paragraph{Dihedral groups.}
For \(G=\DihZ{2m}\), \(m\ge3\), the allowed kernels at a fixed pair
give two families of cards, up to nonzero scalars,
\[
 E(t)=f_{00}+t f_{11},\qquad O(t)=f_{10}+t f_{01},
 \qquad t\in\mu_m.
\]
Within each family, the support constraints force one matching.
On that matching the coefficient minors have degree at most two in
\(t\). They vanish at three distinct phases, forcing all but at most
one binary factor to be fixed up to scalar (\cref{lem:app-nd-sector-ruling}). For arity at least eight, suppose
two cards have different matchings or differ on two edges of the same
matching. Monomial closures outside the deleted pair preserve this
disagreement in an arity-six contraction of \(f\). Its two inherited
cards are incompatible with a matching product. Yet its first closure
is a stable card, and further monomial closures preserve the matching
product whenever the result is nonzero. This rules out the disagreement (\cref{lem:app-nd-localization} and \cref{lem:app-nd-bounded-consumer}).
If two cards differ on edge \(e\), a third differing from the first
on another edge would differ from the second on both. Thus the
nonzero cards share a product on all but one common edge (any edge
suffices if all cards are proportional).
Two phase evaluations in each family recover the four slices, so this
product factors out of \(f\) by Section~\ref{s:short:mechanism}.

At arity six a direct support and pencil analysis forces factorization
(\cref{lem:nd-six-prime-base}). At arity four invertible
dressings give an eight-vertex signature to which
Section~\ref{s:short:eight} applies. The forms \(\DihY{2m}\) and
\(\DihXzero{2m}\) reduce to the standard analysis by transformations of
the whole network, including its edges. For \(\DihXone{2m}\), once the
support matching is fixed, weighted two-copy gadgets compare the ratios
of coefficients obtained by changing the assignment on one edge.
After the lower-arity outputs have been processed, the resulting
identities force these ratios to be independent of the other edge
assignments. This yields the required factorization. \Cref{sec:normalized-dihedral} gives the transformations and
the low-arity classifications.

\paragraph{Cyclic groups.}
In the original coordinates \(G=\CycX{N}\) and its transfer span is
\(\Span\{I,X\}\). Write \(P=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\).
Since \(P^{-1}A(t)P=2D(t)\), the group in Walsh coordinates is
\(P^{-1}GP=C_N^{\rm std}=\{[D(t)]:t\in\mu_N\}\).
For \(\widetilde f=(P^{-1})^{\otimes n}f\), the closures test the
sum and difference of two slices. The nonzero cards are matching
products whose overlaps constrain both support and coefficients.
After the binary and arity-four reductions, a surviving core forces
\(N=2\), excluding the \(N>2\) cyclic node of
Figure~\ref{s:fig:complete-group-growth}. In the original coordinates
\[
 f\sim\RMcore:=\one[RM(1,3)],\qquad G=\CycX{2}=\{[I],[X]\}.
\]
Its Walsh-coordinate group is \(C_2^{\rm std}=\{[I],[Z]\}\).
The code \(RM(1,3)\) consists of the eight-bit evaluation vectors of
affine linear functions on \(\Ftwo^3\). Here \(\sim\) allows a nonzero
scalar, input permutation, and realizable input \(X\)-dressings.
To identify it, the matching slices reconstruct a doubly-even self-dual
support, with a unique weight-four word through each triple of
coordinates. This forces the Reed--Muller support. Comparisons on its
affine planes then fix the coefficients up to a sign character, removed
by the allowed dressings. Applying the coefficient constraint after
every available single-input dressing also forces the complete group
to be \(\CycX{2}\) in the original coordinates (\cref{lem:rm-support-classification}
and \cref{lem:rm-safe-group-collapse}).

This core supplies a reduction that strictly enlarges the binary group. Once \(\RMcore\) is available and the binary and arity-four
cases have been resolved, the limited-appearance reduction of Shao and
Cai \cite[full version, Section~8.2, Lemmas~8.9--8.10]{ShaoCai2020}
permits adjoining the Bell signatures. In the original coordinates the binaries
\(Z,Y\) supply transfer classes \([ZX]=[Y]\) and \([YX]=[Z]\).
Their matrix representatives lie outside \(\Span\{I,X\}\). The complete group therefore strictly enlarges
and stabilization resumes. This is the shortcut stated in \cref{rem:rm-binary-enlargement-shortcut}, which also describes
the stronger internal structural classification retained in the full proof.

\paragraph{Klein-four groups.}
The standard Pauli subgroup \(\KleinStd\) supplies the four Bell kernels \(I,X,Z,Y\).
For two signatures of arity at least five, suppose their corresponding
cards are linearly dependent for every input pair and each of the four
kernels. The relation may depend on the pair and the kernel, and zero
cards are allowed. Then the signatures themselves are linearly dependent
(\cref{lem:v4-pauli-projective-determination}). Apply
this to a retained signature and its complex conjugate. If the
signature is not proportional to a real signature, some pair card
must have the same obstruction. Descent reaches arity four, where
classification resolves the set or enlarges the group, unless an
earlier reduction already gives progress. If all retained signatures
are proportional to real signatures in the same coordinates, the real
Holant dichotomy \cite{ShaoCai2020} applies.

For \(\KleinEx\), the common matching method
forces a proper factor. The arity-six classification supplies the
base case. At higher arity the four cards at a fixed deleted pair share
one residual matching. If an edge has a fixed binary factor across
these cards, reconstruction extracts it from \(f\). Otherwise the
four labels on each edge vary by a permutation. Crossing two edges
compares these permutations and creates a support incompatible with
a matching product. The arity-eight boundary needs two such contraction
choices. From arity ten one suffices. This reduces the branch to the
arity-four analysis. \Cref{thm:standard-v4-relative}
and \cref{thm:exotic-v4-relative-rigidity} give the two conclusions.

\paragraph{Platonic groups.}
Fix the deleted pair \(p\) and regard each available kernel as a
projective point. For three collinear points, a linear relation among
their kernel representatives gives the same relation among their cards
under \(\Phi_p^f\). This constrains the possible matching products as in
Section~\ref{s:short:mechanism}. A common factor can lift to \(f\).
In the octahedral and icosahedral cases a surviving disagreement can
be localized to arity six. Finite classifications then give a quaternary
signature covered by Section~\ref{s:short:eight} or a binary whose transfer
class enlarges the group (\cref{sec:platonic-decks}).
For \(\TetExt\), the gadgets determine the complexity, strictly enlarge
the group, or expose a proper factor of \(f\), contradicting its
irreducibility in the last case (\cref{thm:external-a4-deck-rigidity}).

For \(\TetInt\) the whole retained set may admit a common affine basis.
Work in the coordinates of the internal tetrahedral form. If a remaining
irreducible is nonaffine, the shortening argument exposes a factor
or produces a smaller signature that is still nonaffine in those coordinates
(\cref{lem:a4rel-physical-code-shortening}). Iteration
would reach the already excluded cases of arity at most eight. Applying this argument
in the same basis to every remaining irreducible gives the common affine
presentation.
\section{Completing the dichotomy}
For a set containing \(I\), stabilization brings each unresolved case
to a stable pair. The group arguments compare its contractions to
reconstruct a proper factor, contradicting irreducibility, or reduce a
disagreement to low arity. Together with the structural exceptions
and the reductions that enlarge the binary group, these arguments
classify stable pairs (\cref{thm:matching-deck-classification}).
Factor extraction and stabilization then give the dichotomy for sets
containing \(I\) (\cref{thm:p1-eq2-kholant-dichotomy}).

The model equivalence and criterion correspondence in
Section~\ref{s:short:tools} transfer this dichotomy to \(\Holant(\cF,X)\),
giving Theorem~\ref{s:thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy}. The recognition procedure
of \cref{thm:predicate-algorithms} tests the four cases
of \(\ReTract(\cF\cup\{X\})\) on the exact algebraic input, so the
criterion is decidable.

\section*{AI methodology statement}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{AI methodology statement}
We used OpenAI's GPT-5.6 Sol to assist with mathematical exploration and proof development for the arguments in this paper. In Sections~\ref{sec:normalized-dihedral}--\ref{sec:platonic-decks}, we used AI to carry out most of the analysis within each group case, including developing proofs and writing code for the computations used in those proofs. All companion verification code was generated by OpenAI Codex and subsequently reviewed by the authors.

We used OpenAI's GPT-6 Astra to improve the writing and presentation, and support the Lean formalization. We formalized the proof in Lean and incorporated two corrections to the original arXiv version (\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00219v1}{arXiv:2609.00219v1}). We corrected the signs in the two mixed-mark identities in Equation~(132) and restored a common nonzero scalar factor in Lemma~9.19 and its application in the proof of Lemma~9.20. The corrected statements were verified in Lean. The authors independently checked the mathematical claims, proofs, and computational results and take full responsibility for the correctness and originality of the paper.
\label{s:end:main}

\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:p1-introduction}

Many counting problems ask for a global sum determined by local
constraints: each assignment receives the product of its local weights,
and these products are summed over all assignments.  A complexity
dichotomy identifies the choices of local signatures that admit
polynomial-time evaluation and proves \(\SharpP\)-hardness for every
remaining choice.  On the Boolean domain, this program has a long history
in counting constraint satisfaction problems (\(\#\mathrm{CSP}\)).
Creignou and Hermann classified the unweighted case
\cite{CreignouHermann1996}.  Later dichotomies treated nonnegative rational
weights \cite{DyerGoldbergJerrum2009}, rational weights of mixed signs
\cite{BulatovDyerGoldbergJalseniusRicherby2009}, and complex weights
\cite{CaiLuXia2014}.  Complex weights permit cancellations that yield
tractable cases absent from the nonnegative setting
\cite{CaiLuXia2014}, making the algebra of the weights part of the
classification.
Over arbitrary finite domains, counting-CSP dichotomies have likewise
been established for unweighted constraints
\cite{Bulatov2013,DyerRicherby2013}, nonnegative rational weights
\cite{BulatovDyerGoldbergJalseniusJerrumRicherby2012}, nonnegative algebraic
weights \cite{CaiChenLu2016}, and algebraic complex weights
\cite{CaiChen2017}.

The Holant framework brings the availability of local constraints into
this classification problem \cite{CaiLuXia2009}.  A counting-CSP instance
can be represented by a bipartite graph whose variable vertices carry
equality constraints, enforcing agreement among all occurrences of a
variable.  Holant allows the available vertex signatures to be prescribed
without supplying these equalities.  This accommodates problems such as
counting perfect matchings, where each vertex requires exactly one
incident edge to be selected.  It also makes the construction of useful
local signatures a part of the classification proof.  Holographic
transformations, originating in Valiant's algorithms \cite{Valiant2008},
give another source of tractability: a simultaneous change of basis can
expose structure in the local signatures while preserving the value of the
total sum.

Boolean Holant dichotomies have addressed both restrictions on the
signatures and assumptions about auxiliary signatures.  With no prescribed
auxiliary signatures, classifications were obtained for symmetric
real-valued signatures \cite{HuangLu2016}, symmetric complex-valued
signatures \cite{CaiGuoWilliams2016}, arbitrary nonnegative signatures
\cite{LinWang2018}, and arbitrary real-valued signatures
\cite{ShaoCai2020}.  Here symmetry means that a signature depends only on
the number of ones in its input.  Another line of work supplies unary
signatures: \(\Holant^*\) supplies all of them
\cite{CaiLuXia2011HolantStar}, \(\Holant^+\) supplies four distinguished
ones \cite{Backens2017}, and \(\Holant^c\) supplies the two pinnings
\(\DeltaZero=[1,0]^{\mathsf T}\) and
\(\DeltaOne=[0,1]^{\mathsf T}\).
The last model was classified for symmetric complex signatures
\cite{CaiHuangLu2012Holantc}, arbitrary real signatures
\cite{CaiLuXia2018RealHolantc}, and algebraic complex signatures
\cite{Backens2021,Backens2025}.  The real-valued classification introduced
the local-affine tractable family \(\cL\), which also occurs here.

Meng, Wang, Xia, and Zheng proved a tensor-factor extraction theorem and
classified complex-valued Boolean Holant when a nonzero odd-arity signature
is present \cite[Theorems~29 and~30]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}. They collected
seven cases in condition~(PC). For two cases defined by support, the
weighted Eulerian-orientation results of \cite{MengWangXia2025EO} gave
an \(\mathrm{FP}^{\mathrm{NP}}\) upper bound. Guan, Shao, and Shi subsequently
gave polynomial-time algorithms for these cases \cite{GuanShaoShi2026}.
Taken together, the seven cases give the most general known sufficient condition
for tractability of algebraic complex-valued Boolean Holant. We denote
their disjunction by \(\Tract\).
Even-arity signatures present a different resource constraint.  Every
gadget built entirely from them has an even number of external inputs,
because each internal edge consumes two inputs.  Such gadgets cannot
supply a unary signature, so binary signatures are the smallest
positive-arity objects available for further constructions.  We study
the case in which binary disequality, requiring two bits to differ, is
supplied.

There is also a precedent for prescribing disequality in counting CSP.
In \(\#\mathrm{CSP}_d\), each variable occurs a number of times divisible
by \(d\).  Cai, Fu, and Shao classified
\(\#\mathrm{CSP}_d(\Neq_2,\cF)\) for complex signatures
\cite[full version, Section~5.1 and Theorem~5.3]{CaiFuShao2020Entanglement}.
Shao and Cai used its hardness conclusion in their real Holant dichotomy
\cite[full version, Theorem~2.24]{ShaoCai2020}.

Prescribing binary disequality is useful for three further reasons.
First, ordinary edges enforce equality and the supplied binary signature
enforces disequality. Equivalently, we may use disequality edges and supply
binary equality as a vertex signature (\cref{lem:p1-equality-wire-equivalence}).
Together these constraints restrict the realizable binaries in Xia's finite-group
framework \cite[Section~4 and Table~2]{Xia2026FrameworkV1} to the cyclic,
dihedral, Klein-four, and Platonic forms in \cref{thm:marked-finite-group-routing}.
Second, switching between the two formulations only requires inserting or
removing binary vertices. Every signature in the original set keeps its
entries, so we can use whichever edge model suits a construction or
tractable case. Third, the resulting
classification can be applied to other Holant problems whenever a
reduction makes binary disequality available.
When disequality cannot be obtained, that failure gives a structural
restriction on the remaining problems.

\paragraph{The problem and main results.}
A Boolean signature of arity \(n\) is a function
\(f:\{0,1\}^n\to\mathbb C\).  In a Holant instance, a graph vertex
of degree \(n\) carries such a signature, with its incident edges ordered.
For a fixed finite set \(\cF\) of signatures, the problem
\(\Holant(\cF)\) evaluates
\[
  \sum_{\sigma:E\to\{0,1\}}
  \prod_{v\in V}f_v\bigl(\sigma|_{E(v)}\bigr),
  \qquad f_v\in\cF.
\]
Here \(E(v)\) is the ordered list of edge incidences at \(v\).
\Cref{subsec:holant-definitions} gives the conventions for loops and
multiple edges.  The graph and its vertex labels form the input. The set
\(\cF\) is fixed.  Write
\[
 X=(\Neq_2)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad I=(\Eq_2)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Our problem is \(\Holant(\cF,X):=\Holant(\cF\cup\{X\})\).
Thus any number of degree-two vertices may enforce opposite values on
their two incident edges.

With \(X\) supplied, \cref{lem:tract-X-anchor} rules out three of the
seven cases of \(\Tract\). We write \(\ReTract\) for the disjunction of the
remaining four. The signature set lies in the tensor closure \(\angles{\cT}\)
of signatures of arity at most two, or it is transformable into the affine
\(\cA\), product \(\cP\), or local-affine \(\cL\) class. In each transformability case,
one change of basis must place the edge signature of the model and
all the vertex signatures in that class.
The classes and common transformability
are defined in \cref{subsec:standard-classes,def:common-transformability},
and the four cases are collected in \cref{subsec:tract-predicate}.
Here ``Re'' refers both
to the restriction of the general tractability criterion \(\Tract\)
to these four tractable cases and to their correspondence with the
tractable cases in the real-valued Holant dichotomy of Shao and
Cai~\cite{ShaoCai2020}.

\begin{theorem}[Binary-disequality Holant dichotomy]
\label{thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy}
For every finite set of algebraic complex-valued Boolean signatures \(\cF\),
the problem \(\Holant(\cF,X)\) is polynomial-time computable when
\(\ReTract(\cF\cup\{X\})\) holds and is \(\SharpP\)-hard otherwise.
The tractability criterion is decidable from exact algebraic input.
\end{theorem}

With disequality edges, the proof also classifies sets containing an
endpoint-nondegenerate irreducible signature of arity four, without
requiring a supplied equality signature. Following Meng, Wang, Xia, and Zheng
\cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025}, write
\(\KHolant(\cF):=\Holant(\Neq_2\mid\cF)\) for the bipartite Holant
problem with disequality on the left and signatures from \(\cF\) on the
right. Each connection between signature inputs thus enforces disequality.
In the following theorem, transformability in \(\ReTract\) uses this edge \(X\).

A quaternary signature \(q\) is
\emph{endpoint-nondegenerate} when
\[
 q({\mathbf{x}})=0\quad\text{for odd }\wt({\mathbf{x}}),\qquad q(0000)q(1111)\ne0.
\]
Here \(\wt({\mathbf{x}})\) counts the ones in \({\mathbf{x}}\). The eight
even-weight inputs are the allowed configurations of an eight-vertex
signature. The two endpoint entries are required to be nonzero.
A nonzero positive-arity signature is \emph{irreducible} if it has no factorization
\(a\otimes b\) on two nonempty blocks of inputs, allowing a permutation of inputs and a nonzero scalar.

\begin{theorem}[Quaternary \(\KHolant\) dichotomy]
\label{thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite set of algebraic complex-valued Boolean signatures.
Suppose that \(\cF\) contains an endpoint-nondegenerate irreducible
quaternary signature.  Then \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is polynomial-time computable
if \(\ReTract(\cF)\) holds and is \(\SharpP\)-hard otherwise.  The
tractability criterion is decidable from exact algebraic input.
\end{theorem}

Cai and Fu's eight-vertex classification determines the complexity for
a single eight-vertex signature \(q\) with nonzero endpoints
\cite[arXiv version~2, Theorem~3.1]{CaiFu2023}. If \(q\) alone is tractable,
its interaction with the remaining signatures can still make the problem hard.
\Cref{sec:p1-eight-vertex} treats these interactions. Consequently, whenever
a later reduction realizes an endpoint-nondegenerate irreducible eight-vertex
signature, \cref{thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy} classifies the entire available set.

\subsection{Proof strategy}
\label{subsec:intro-proof-strategy}

Two approaches to Boolean Holant dichotomies motivate our proof.
Shao and Cai use arity descent in the real-valued setting, reducing a
signature outside the relevant tensor-product class to a smaller such
signature or a low-arity case \cite[Sections~3--4]{ShaoCai2020}.
Their orthogonality and norm arguments rely on real-valuedness, or
the corresponding conjugation symmetry after a holographic
transformation.  Arbitrary complex signatures lack this guarantee,
so the descent needs another way to control cancellations.
Xia's approach organizes the remaining problems by their finite
binary groups \cite[Sections~4--5]{Xia2026FrameworkV1}.
This specifies the binary constructions available in each case, but
leaves open what they force about higher-arity signatures.

We combine the two approaches in the disequality-edge formulation, where
binary equality is available by \cref{lem:p1-equality-wire-equivalence}.
After the preliminary reductions in \cref{sec:p1-shared-terminals}
(see also \cite{CaiFuXia2018SixVertex,CaiFuShao2020ARS}), choose a nonbinary
irreducible signature \(f\) of minimum arity in the current set \(\cF\).
The complete transfer group \(G\) of \(\cF\) consists of the projective
classes \([BX]\) as \(B\) ranges over all nonsingular ordered binary
signatures directly realizable over \(\cF\), with transfer matrices identified
up to nonzero scalar. Close a pair of inputs
of \(f\) using such a binary. By \cref{lem:factor-saturation}, we can retain the resulting signature's
irreducible factors by reduction. An unresolved nonbinary factor has smaller
arity than \(f\) and continues the descent.

A contraction that produces only binary factors can also advance the proof.
If a retained factor has transfer class outside \(G\), the complete group
of the enlarged set strictly contains \(G\). The factor is available by
reduction, so gadgets over the enlarged set can use binaries that were not
directly realizable over \(\cF\). We can now test the same \(f\) with
additional contractions. \Cref{fig:complete-group-growth} shows the
possible strict inclusions. Which one occurs depends on the binaries
realizable over the enlarged set.

If neither the minimum arity nor the group changes and the reductions have
not settled the complexity, processing the prescribed contractions leaves
every nonzero output as a product of retained binaries on a perfect matching
of the remaining inputs. We call the contraction outputs \emph{cards}.
Together with zero, the cards obtained from one copy of \(f\) by attaching
available binaries to selected inputs and closing one pair form its
\emph{matching deck}. When every nonzero card has such a decomposition
with its factors retained, the state is \emph{deck-stable}
(\cref{def:matching-deck,def:deck-stable-state}).
The group-order bound, arity descent, and finite processing within each
stage give the descent measure of \cref{lem:matching-synthesis-measure}.
\Cref{lem:token-coverage} verifies descent in all four group branches.
Since each extension retains the preceding
set, hardness transfers back along the reductions, and a common tractable
basis restricts to that set (\cref{lem:downward-inheritance}).

\subsection{Structural arguments in the group cases}
\label{subsec:intro-group-arguments}

The availability of both \(\Eq_2\) and \(\Neq_2\) restricts the
forms of the finite binary groups in the given basis.
In the disequality-edge formulation, equality supplies a fixed
involution in the group, while reversal of binary inputs imposes
an additional symmetry.  Together these constraints refine the
abstract group classification into the specific forms of
\cref{thm:marked-finite-group-routing}.  These forms give explicit
restrictions on the supports and coefficients of the cards.

Fix a pair of inputs of \(f\). Each nonzero card obtained by closing this
pair has a matching decomposition, but two cards may pair the same remaining
input with different partners. Even when their matchings agree, extracting
a common binary factor on an edge requires the factors there to be proportional
across the cards being compared. The proportionality scalar can be absorbed
into the rest of each product. The group arguments use further contractions
to test a disagreement at lower arity or use compatible factors to recover
a factor of \(f\), as explained in \cref{subsec:shared-matching-methods}.

\paragraph{Dihedral groups.}
The group binaries constrain the supports and coefficient ratios
of the cards, but these constraints must agree across contractions.
Superposing two matchings gives alternating cycles. \Cref{sec:normalized-dihedral}
uses these cycles to localize matching disagreements to six inputs. In the standard case, excluding these
disagreements forces a common factor, which two phase evaluations
recover in the original signature. This contradicts irreducibility.
The weighted dihedral case requires additional support and
coefficient reconstruction, including Laurent interpolation, to obtain
the same reduction to arity four.

\paragraph{Cyclic groups.}
Fewer independent contractions are available in this case.
After the arity-four cases are resolved or yield group enlargement,
\cref{sec:proper-deck} identifies the surviving minimum-arity signature,
up to input permutations, bit flips and a nonzero scalar, with the
indicator of the length-eight Reed--Muller code \(RM(1,3)\). The proof
reconstructs its support and coefficients by comparing contractions across
input pairs. This signature then realizes sums over affine fibres
(\cref{lem:rm-radon-accessible}).  These sums test the supports and
coefficients of the rest of the set.  When the tests produce no
further reduction, they place the whole set and the edge in the
affine class in one basis.

\paragraph{Klein-four groups.}
In the standard Pauli form, compare two signatures of the same arity
above four by contracting each input pair with each of the four binary
Bell signatures. If the corresponding cards are linearly dependent in
every comparison, the signatures are linearly dependent. \Cref{sec:v4-deck}
applies this test to each signature and its complex conjugate to detect
whether the signature can be made real by rescaling. A failure descends to a quaternary signature with the
same obstruction, whose analysis resolves the complexity or produces
further progress.  If every signature can be made real, the real
Holant dichotomy applies to the whole set.  The other Klein-four
form requires a direct factorization argument.  Its six-input
classification forces agreement among residual factors, which the
crossed contractions then use to obtain factors at higher arities.
These factors exclude a surviving nonbinary irreducible signature.

\paragraph{Platonic groups.}
Fix a pair of inputs and regard the available contraction kernels as
projective points. Linear relations among their representatives pass to
the corresponding cards and constrain their matchings. \Cref{sec:platonic-decks}
uses these relations to obtain a common factor or localize differing
matchings to six inputs. A common factor contradicts irreducibility,
while localized disagreements give small signatures to which the
results for arity-four signatures or binary-group analysis can be applied.  The
octahedral and icosahedral analyses
then produce quaternary signatures or new binaries. The internal
tetrahedral case requires testing whether the whole set is affine
in one basis.  Stabilizer-code constructions shorten a non-affine
signature while preserving its failure of affine membership, unless
a complexity conclusion, factor, or larger group is obtained first.
Once the descent reaches at most eight inputs, the low-arity
classifications rule out a surviving non-affine obstruction.  This
completes the induction to a common affine basis for the remaining
set and its edge.

The low-arity classifications that require exact finite enumeration,
together with their completeness arguments, are stated in
Appendix~\ref{sec:exact-finite-certificates}. \Cref{proofsec:matching-deck-synthesis}
assembles the four branches, and \cref{sec:p1-synthesis} transfers their
conclusions to the original problem. The recognition procedure tests the criterion
on the input set itself (\cref{lem:tract-X-anchor,thm:predicate-algorithms}).
\section{Preliminaries}
\label{sec:preliminaries}

We fix the common algebraic, tensor, and reduction conventions, define the
signature classes, and state the imported dichotomies used across the proof.
The constructive recognition and retained-reduction arguments are in
\cref{sec:p1-shared-terminals}.  Specialized tools are introduced where
their applications begin.

\subsection{Foundational algebraic conventions}
\label{subsec:foundational-algebra}

\subsubsection{Sets, maps, and exact fields}
\label{subsubsec:set-field-exact}

We use \([n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}\), \([0]=\varnothing\), the indicator
\(\one[\mathsf P]\), cardinality \(|S|\), complement \(S^c\) in the
stated ambient set, disjoint union \(\sqcup\), restriction
\(f|_S\), and fibres \(\pi^{-1}(y)=\{x:\pi(x)=y\}\).
Tuples and binary strings are ordered lexicographically.

For a field \(L\), write \(L^\times=L\setminus\{0\}\),
\(\Mat_{m\times n}(L)\) for the \(m\)-by-\(n\) matrices over \(L\),
and \(\Mat_n(L)=\Mat_{n\times n}(L)\).
We use \(\mathbb Z_m=\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z\) and the binary field
\(\Ftwo=\mathbb Z_2\).  The function \(\varphi(m)\) counts the residue
classes modulo \(m\) coprime to \(m\).

We write \(\AlgNums\subseteq\mathbb C\) for the field of algebraic numbers.
We write \(\overline L\) for an algebraic closure, \([L':L]\) for a
finite extension degree, and \(L(\alpha)\) for the generated field.
An \(L\)-conjugate of \(\alpha\) is its image under an embedding
\(L(\alpha)\hookrightarrow\overline L\) fixing \(L\). \(\bar z\) denotes
complex conjugation.  Exact algebraic values are represented by a minimal
polynomial and a rational isolating region selecting the intended complex
root \cite{Cohen1993}.

\subsubsection{Linear, polynomial, and tensor conventions}
\label{subsubsec:linear-polynomial-tensor}

All vector spaces are finite-dimensional unless stated otherwise.  We use
\(\ker T\), \(\operatorname{im}T\), \(\rank T\), \(\Span_L S\),
\(U\oplus V\), \(W^\vee=\operatorname{Hom}_L(W,L)\), and \(W/U\)
with their usual meanings.
An affine subspace is a coset \({\mathbf{a}}+U\), with translation space \(U\).
For matrices, \(A^{\mathsf T}\) denotes transpose, \(\operatorname{tr}A\)
trace, \(I_n\) the identity, and \(\GL_n(L)\) the invertible matrices.
\(\GL(V)\) denotes the invertible linear maps on \(V\).
A pivot is a selected nonzero entry used in elimination or normalization.
An \(L\)-matrix algebra is an \(L\)-subspace of \(\Mat_n(L)\) closed
under multiplication. It is unital if it contains \(I_n\) and
transpose-stable if it is closed under transpose.  Matrix units \(E_{ij}\)
use the stated index order. Boolean indices give \(E_{00},E_{01},E_{10},E_{11}\).
We use the polynomial ring \(L[x_1,\ldots,x_n]\) and monomials
\({\mathbf{x}}^{\mathbf{u}}=\prod_jx_j^{u_j}\), \({\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^n\).

With fixed bases, tensor products have coordinates
\[
 ({\mathbf{v}}_1\otimes\cdots\otimes {\mathbf{v}}_r)_{i_1,\ldots,i_r}
   =({\mathbf{v}}_1)_{i_1}\cdots({\mathbf{v}}_r)_{i_r}.
\]
Such a product is a simple tensor.  The map \(A^{\otimes r}\) applies
\(A\) to every factor.  A bipartite flattening groups selected tensor
coordinates into a row index and the complementary coordinates into a
column index. Its flattening rank is the resulting matrix rank.

\subsubsection{Groups and projective spaces}
\label{subsubsec:group-projective}

We use \(H\le G\) for subgroups, \(H\lhd G\) for normal subgroups,
\([G:H]\) for index, \(G/N\) for a quotient, and \(\langle S\rangle\)
for the subgroup generated by \(S\).  A group presentation is written
\(\langle S:\mathcal R\rangle\).  The order of an element is its least
positive identity power, or infinity. An involution has order two.
The exponent of a finite group is the least common multiple of its element
orders.  Bounded torsion means that one integer \(m\ge1\) satisfies
\(g^m=1\) for every element.  We use the center \(Z(G)\) and automorphism group
\(\operatorname{Aut}(G)\).
For a group action, \(Gx\) and \(G_x\) denote the orbit and stabilizer.
A linear representation is faithful when its homomorphism to \(\GL(V)\)
is injective.  An action of \(H\) by automorphisms of \(N\) gives
\(N\rtimes H\), with product
\((n,h)(n',h')=(n(h\cdot n'),hh')\).  The normalizer and centralizer are
\[
 N_G(H)=\{g:gHg^{-1}=H\},\qquad
 C_G(H)=\{g:gh=hg\text{ for every }h\in H\}.
\]
Write \(S_m,A_m,C_m,D_{2m}\), and \(V_4\) for the symmetric,
alternating, cyclic, dihedral, and Klein four groups.  Our convention is
\(D_{2m}=\langle r,s:r^m=s^2=1,\ srs=r^{-1}\rangle\), of order \(2m\):
\(\langle r\rangle\) consists of rotations and its other coset of
reflections.  For a finite set \(V\), \(\operatorname{Sym}(V)\) is its
permutation group. A double transposition is a product of two disjoint
transpositions.

For a finite-dimensional \(L\)-space \(V\), its projective space is
\(\mathbb P_L(V):=(V\setminus\{0\})/L^\times\). When \(L=\mathbb C\) or is
clear we write \(\mathbb P(V)\).  The class of \({\mathbf{v}}\ne0\) is \([{\mathbf{v}}]\), and
the line through distinct \([{\mathbf{u}}],[{\mathbf{v}}]\) is
\(\mathbb P(\Span_L\{{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\})\).  A projective subspace \(\mathbb P(U)\) has
dimension \(\dim U-1\).  An invertible linear map induces a projective
automorphism, and scalar multiples induce the same one.  We put
\[
 \PGL(V):=\GL(V)/(L^\times I_V),\qquad
 \PGL_n(L):=\GL_n(L)/(L^\times I_n).
\]
Here \(I_V\) is the identity linear map on \(V\).
The diagonal torus in \(\PGL_n(L)\) is the subgroup of projective classes of
nonsingular diagonal matrices.
For a nonsingular \(2\)-by-\(2\) matrix \(T\), the \emph{projective order}
of \([T]\in\PGL_2(\AlgNums)\) is the least \(m\ge1\) for which
\(T^m=\lambda I\) for some \(\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\), and is infinite
if no such \(m\) exists.

\subsection{Complexity conventions}
\label{subsec:complexity-conventions}

We use polynomial-time Turing reductions \(\leT\), allowing adaptive exact
oracle queries. \(\equivT\) means reduction in both directions.  The
classes \(\FP\), \(\SharpP\), and \(\FP^{\mathsf{NP}}\) have their usual
meanings.  For algebraic-valued problems, \(\SharpP\)-hardness means that
every integer-valued \(\SharpP\) function reduces to the exact algebraic
oracle, including polynomial-time postprocessing of its answers.

Signature sets, their coefficient fields, and fixed gadgets are fixed
independently of the input network.  Thus polynomial time measures the
network's encoding length. Exact answers use the chosen effective number
field.  Recognition of a tractable signature set is a separate task whose
input consists of finite exact signature tables.  We prove decidability
for that task without an encoding-size complexity bound.

\subsection{Signatures, tensors, and Holant instances}
\label{subsec:holant-definitions}

We use the fields \(\AlgNums\) and \(\Ftwo\) fixed above.  For \(r\ge0\), an
arity-\(r\) Boolean signature is a function
\(f:\{0,1\}^r\to\AlgNums\), identified with its lexicographically ordered
column tensor.  Its \(r\) input positions are ordered, and this order is
part of the data.  A \emph{relation} is a \(\{0,1\}\)-valued
signature, identified with its support.  Arity zero, one, two, three, and four are called
nullary, unary, binary, ternary, and quaternary.  We write \(\arity(f)=r\) and
\(\supp(f)=\{{\mathbf{x}}:f({\mathbf{x}})\ne0\}\).  For a Boolean
string \({\mathbf{x}}\in\{0,1\}^r\), we also write
\(\supp({\mathbf{x}}):=\{j\in[r]:x_j=1\}\). The argument type distinguishes string
support from signature support.
Whole vectors are written in bold, while their scalar coordinates and
input-position labels are not. All binary-vector arithmetic is over \(\Ftwo\), with \(\oplus\) used when
bitwise addition should be explicit.  The symbols \(\zero^r\) and
\(\one^r\) denote the all-zero and all-one strings, respectively. At \(r=0\),
both denote the unique empty string.

For \(T\subseteq[r]\), \(\one_T\in\{0,1\}^r\) denotes the incidence
vector of \(T\). In particular \(\one_\varnothing=\zero^r\).  We write
\({\mathbf{e}}_j=\one_{\{j\}}\) for the \(j\)-th standard basis vector whenever the
ambient arity is clear.

We write \(\diag(a,b)\) for the diagonal matrix with entries \(a,b\).
For \(s\in\mathbb C\), put \(D(s):=\diag(1,s)\). This matrix is
invertible exactly when \(s\ne0\), and has algebraic entries when \(s\) is algebraic.

Following \cite[Section~2.3.1]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}, the signature
matrix \(M_f=M_{x_1\cdots x_\ell,x_{\ell+1}\cdots x_r}(f)\) has
rows indexed by the first \(\ell\) bits and columns by the remaining
bits, both in lexicographic order.  Its entries are the corresponding
values of \(f\). The subscripts may also specify any other ordered partition of the input positions. For a binary signature,
\(M_f=(f(a,b))_{a,b\in\{0,1\}}\). We also use \(f\) for this matrix
when the input order is fixed.

For \(\tau\in S_r\), define the reordering of inputs
\((\tau f)({\mathbf{x}})=f(x_{\tau^{-1}(1)},\ldots,x_{\tau^{-1}(r)})\).
With this convention, \(\tau(\sigma f)=(\sigma\circ\tau)f\).
On disjoint ordered inputs, \((f\otimes g)({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=f({\mathbf{x}})g({\mathbf{y}})\).
A nonzero positive-arity signature is \emph{irreducible} if, up to permutation of inputs and nonzero scalar, it cannot
be written as \(a\otimes b\) with both \(a\) and \(b\) of positive arity.
A nonzero positive-arity signature that is not irreducible is
\emph{reducible}. We use \emph{irreducible} as in
\cite[Section~2.3.4]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}. The positive-arity convention
excludes scalar factors.  Prime factorization into irreducible signatures is
unique up to these operations and factor order
\cite[Lemma~16]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}.  For any class \(\cC\) of
signatures, its tensor closure \(\angles{\cC}\) consists of tensor products
of members of \(\cC\), up to permutation of inputs and nonzero scalar.  By
convention, it also contains zero and the arity-zero empty product.
Thus \(\angles{\cC}\) denotes tensor closure, whereas \(\langle S\rangle\)
denotes the subgroup generated by \(S\).
A \emph{tensor factorization} partitions the ordered inputs into blocks and
writes the tensor, up to a nonzero scalar and permutation of inputs, as
a tensor product on those blocks.  For example, a \(2+2\) factorization of a quaternary has two binary
factors.

For \({\mathbf{a}}\in\{0,1\}^r\), define \(\delta_{\mathbf{a}}:\{0,1\}^r\to\AlgNums\) by
\[
  \delta_{\mathbf{a}}({\mathbf{x}})=\one[{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{a}}].
\]
We use the unary pinnings
\[
  \DeltaZero=[1,0]^{\mathsf T},
  \qquad
  \DeltaOne=[0,1]^{\mathsf T},
\]
which are point masses forcing the incident bit to \(0\) and \(1\),
respectively.  

For \(r\ge1\), a \emph{complementary-pair signature} (CP) has the form
\begin{equation*}
  c_0\delta_{\mathbf{a}}+c_1\delta_{\bar {\mathbf{a}}},
  \qquad c_0,c_1\in\AlgNums,\quad c_0c_1\ne0,
\end{equation*}
where \(\bar {\mathbf{a}}\) is the bitwise complement.  It is a
\emph{generalized equality} (GE) when \({\mathbf{a}}=\zero^r\): its support is
\(\{\zero^r,\one^r\}\), with the two nonzero weights still arbitrary.
Replacing each argument bit \(x_j\) by \(x_j\oplus a_j\)
puts a CP signature in GE form as a tensor identity.

Define the nullary equality/unit by \((\Eq_0)(\varnothing):=1\).  More generally,
for \(r\ge1\), define the arity-\(r\) equality signature by
\[
  (\Eq_r)(x_1,\ldots,x_r)=\one[x_1=\cdots=x_r].
\]

The binary equality and disequality tensors are
\[
  I=(\Eq_2)=
  \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix},
  \qquad
  X=(\Neq_2)=
  \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]

A \emph{weighted equality} is a binary signature with matrix \(\diag(p,q)\),
where \(p,q\in\AlgNums\). It is nonsingular when \(pq\ne0\).
Binary equality is the case \(p=q=1\).

Graphs are finite multigraphs with ordered incidences at each vertex.
Loops and parallel edges are allowed, and a loop contributes two incidences
to the degree. A matching is a family of disjoint two-element subsets,
and is perfect when it covers its ground set.

A closed signature network \(\Omega\) has ordinary tensor vertices
\(v\), each assigned a signature \(f_v\) and a linearly ordered set
\(H_v\) of \(\arity(f_v)\) half-edges.  The sets \(H_v\) are
disjoint.  Edges pair these half-edges in a perfect matching.  Loops join
two half-edges of one vertex, and parallel edges are allowed.  Each half-edge
has its own Boolean variable.  An edge \(e=\{h,h'\}\) contributes
\(W_e(\tau_h,\tau_{h'})\), and a vertex contributes \(f_v(\tau|_{H_v})\).
The partition function sums their product over all half-edge assignments.
The ordinary model uses \(W_e=I\). The disequality-edge model uses \(W_e=X\).


Write \(Z_{\mathbf W}(\Omega)\) for this partition function with edge kernels
\(\mathbf W=(W_e)_e\), and abbreviate it to \(Z_W(\Omega)\) when every
edge has kernel \(W\).
An instance uses either the ordinary model \(Z_I\) or the
disequality-edge model \(Z_X\),
unless a displayed \(I\) or \(X\) is supplied as an explicit degree-two
signature/gadget.  The problems \(\Holant(\cF)\) and \(\KHolant(\cF)\) ask for
\(Z_I(\Omega)\) and \(Z_X(\Omega)\), respectively, on closed networks
over \(\cF\). When all edge kernels are \(I\), summing the
half-edge variables forces the two endpoints of each edge to agree and
recovers the usual one-bit-per-edge multigraph definition (including loops).
For signature sets \(\cG\) and \(\cF\), the bipartite problem
\(\Holant(\cG\mid\cF)\) is the ordinary (\(I\)-edge) half-edge problem on a
bipartite network: vertices in the left part are assigned signatures from
\(\cG\), and vertices in the right part are assigned signatures from
\(\cF\).  For \(X\)-edges no one-bit compression is made.  Subdivision inserts degree-two vertices on an edge.
Subdividing every \(X\)-edge by a degree-two \(X\)-vertex, with ordinary equality edges on its
two sides, gives the equivalent bipartite formulation
\begin{equation*}
  \KHolant(\cF)\equivT\Holant(X\mid\cF).
\end{equation*}
Throughout, comma-separated signature or signature-set arguments mean
adding their union. For example, \(\Holant(\cF,a,b)\) abbreviates
\(\Holant(\cF\cup\{a,b\})\).

We write
\[
  \Holant(\cF,X)=\Holant(\cF\cup\{X\})
\]
for the same ordinary equality-edge problem with the additional binary
signature \(X\) available as a vertex.
