Subdividing every ordinary edge by an \(\Eq_2\)-vertex gives
\[
  \Holant(\cF)=\Holant(\Eq_2\mid\cF).
\]


An \emph{open gadget} is a finite signature network with ordered, labelled
dangling inputs. Fixing their bits and summing over the internal variables
gives its signature \(T_\Omega\). Let \(\partial\Omega\) be the ordered list
of unpaired vertex half-edges and \(E_{\rm int}(\Omega)\) the internal edges.
For this boundary convention,
\[
 T_\Omega({\mathbf z})=
 \sum_{\substack{\tau:\bigsqcup_v H_v\to\{0,1\}\\
                 \tau|_{\partial\Omega}={\mathbf z}}}
 \prod_v f_v(\tau|_{H_v})
 \prod_{\{h,h'\}\in E_{\rm int}(\Omega)}W_{\{h,h'\}}(\tau_h,\tau_{h'}).
\]
We also allow an isolated edge \(\mathsf E_W\) as a binary gadget with
signature \(W\). A supplied \(I\)- or \(X\)-signature instead labels a
tensor vertex. A closed component contributes its partition function,
and the empty network contributes the nullary unit \(1\).
Joining inputs contracts their bits against the edge kernel, as specified
in \cref{subsec:reduction-conventions}.
Appendix~\ref{app:general-boundaries} gives the equivalent general
representation with free edge endpoints and its boundary-coordinate
correction. Both representations use the same replacement signature
\(T_\Omega\).

\subsection{Zero and nullary preprocessing}
\label{subsec:zero-nullary}

An instance containing an identically zero signature has value zero.
A nullary signature contributes a scalar: delete its vertex and multiply
that scalar back into the final value. The empty residual instance has
value \(1\). After this preprocessing, we continue to write \(\cF\) for
the input signature set and assume it contains neither zero nor nullary
signatures. The tractable predicates are evaluated after this preprocessing.
Intermediate constructions may still produce zero or nullary signatures,
which are handled as specified in each argument.

\subsection{Holographic transformations and the
  \texorpdfstring{\(K\)}{K}-basis}
\label{subsec:holographic}

Let \(i\in\mathbb C\) satisfy \(i^2=-1\), and fix the \(K\)-coordinate matrix
\hypertarget{eq:K-matrix}{}
\begin{equation*}
  K=\frac1{\sqrt2}
  \begin{pmatrix}1&1\\ i&-i\end{pmatrix},
  \qquad
  K^{-1}=\frac1{\sqrt2}
  \begin{pmatrix}1&-i\\1&i\end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation*}

The transformations below rewrite every tensor and its incident edge
consistently, giving exact identities between partition functions.  Their
fixed algebraic coefficients lie in a finite extension of the input
coefficient field.  We follow the exact evaluation model of
\cref{subsec:complexity-conventions}.

For tensors or matrices, write \(A\doteq B\) when
\(A=\lambda B\) for some \(\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\).
Throughout the complexity arguments, we ignore fixed nonzero scalar
factors of signatures.  Rescaling a signature multiplies an instance value
by a computable nonzero factor, so it does not change the complexity.
This convention does not discard relative coefficients in sums or
assignment-dependent weights.  

In \(\Holant(\cG\mid\cF)\), write each left-side signature \(g\)
of arity \(r\) as a row vector and each right-side signature \(f\) of
arity \(s\) as a column vector, both in lexicographic order. For
\(M\in\GL_2(\AlgNums)\), replace them by
\[
  gM^{\otimes r},\qquad (M^{-1})^{\otimes s}f,
\]
respectively. On each edge the two matrices cancel by \(MM^{-1}=I\),
so the transformed instance has the same value. Write
\(gM:=gM^{\otimes r}\), \(Mf:=M^{\otimes s}f\), and
\[
  \cG M:=\{gM:g\in\cG\},\qquad M\cF:=\{Mf:f\in\cF\}.
\]
Applying the transformation in both directions gives
\[
  \Holant(\cG\mid\cF)
  \equivT\Holant(\cG M\mid M^{-1}\cF).
\]
For binary \(f\), the right tensor action has signature matrix
\(M_{Mf}=M M_f M^{\mathsf T}\). For \cref{thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy},
\cref{lem:p1-equality-wire-equivalence} gives an equivalent disequality-edge
problem with \(I\) supplied. For a signature in ordinary
coordinates, write \(\widehat f=K^{-1}f\) and
\(\widehat{\cS}=K^{-1}\cS\).

For ordinary Holant, the left signature \((\Eq_2)\) has row vector
\((1,0,0,1)\). After right multiplication by \(M^{\otimes2}\), its
\((a,b)\)-entry is \(\sum_{x\in\Ftwo}M_{xa}M_{xb}=(M^{\mathsf T}M)_{ab}\).
Consequently,
\begin{equation*}
  \Holant(\Eq_2\mid\cF)
  =\Holant((\Eq_2)M\mid M^{-1}\cF),
  \qquad (\Eq_2)M=M^{\mathsf T}M.
\end{equation*}

Write
\begin{equation*}
  GO(X)
  =
  \{D\in\GL_2(\AlgNums):D^{\mathsf T}XD=\lambda X
    \text{ for some }\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\}
\end{equation*}
for legal global transformations preserving the disequality edge
\(X\) projectively.  For a closed network, if \(D\Omega\) applies \(D\) at
every ordinary vertex and
\(D^{\mathsf T}XD=\lambda X\), then
\begin{equation}
  Z_X(D\Omega)=\lambda^{|E_{\rm int}(\Omega)|}Z_X(\Omega).
  \label{eq:GOX-global-scalar}
\end{equation}
Thus the two problems differ only by the known edge-count scalar. For \(m\ge1\), define
the group of \(m\)-th roots of unity by
\[
  \mu_m=\{z\in\AlgNums:z^m=1\}.
\]
An element \(\omega\in\mu_m\) is a \emph{primitive} \(m\)-th root of
unity when \(m\) is the least positive integer satisfying \(\omega^m=1\).
Since \(K^{\mathsf T}K=X\), putting
\(\widehat{\cF}=K^{-1}\cF\) gives
\begin{equation}
  \Holant(\cF)
  \equivT
  \Holant(X\mid\widehat{\cF})
  \equivT
  \KHolant(\widehat{\cF}).
  \label{eq:K-equivalence}
\end{equation}
For original-coordinate signatures and signature sets, the hats in
\cref{eq:K-equivalence} record passage to \(K\)-coordinates.  Once we work
with a signature set already in \(K\)-coordinates, we suppress
these hats and denote its member signatures simply by \(f\).  Unless stated
otherwise, signatures and signature sets appearing in a \(\KHolant\) problem
are understood to be in \(K\)-coordinates, with edge signature \(X\).

\subsection{Gadgets, interpolation, and factor reductions}
\label{subsec:reduction-conventions}

A \emph{direct gadget} over \(\cF\) is a finite \(X\)-edge network of
\(\cF\)-vertices, possibly using the edge \(\mathsf E_X\), with an
ordered exposed boundary. If its signature, defined in
\cref{subsec:holant-definitions}, is \(T_\Omega=\rho h\), where \(\rho\in\AlgNums^\times\) is a nonzero scalar, then
\begin{equation}
  \KHolant(\cF,h)
  \leT
  \KHolant(\cF).
  \label{eq:direct-gadget-reduction}
\end{equation}
A fixed gadget has constant size.  A gadget realizing \(\rho h\),
\(\rho\ne0\), implements the same Turing reduction after an instance with
\(N\) occurrences is rescaled by \(\rho^{-N}\).
We call such an \(h\) \emph{realizable} over \(\cF\).  More
generally, a finite set \(S\) is \emph{available by reduction} over
\(\cF\) if
\[
  \KHolant(\cF,S)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
\]
Joining two boundary inputs contracts their signature coordinates against
the default edge kernel \(W\), in the inherited input order. On an ordinary
boundary, this inserts a \(W\)-edge. An isolated \(\mathsf E_W\) splices
into one edge, and closing its two ends contributes
\(\sum_{a,b}W(a,b)^2=2\). Vertex replacement gives
\cref{eq:direct-gadget-reduction}.
The same rules for general free endpoints, and their compatibility with
\(GO(X)\)-transport, are proved in Appendix~\ref{app:general-boundaries}.
Every realizable signature is available by reduction.  Tensor factorization
and interpolation may also give reductions without realizing the resulting
signature by a gadget.  A gadget using such a signature therefore gives a
reduction to the preceding set, but need not be realizable over that set.
Algebraic operations on tensors may establish identities or select
contractions. Making their outputs available still requires a gadget or
a reduction. A \emph{slice} fixes selected argument values. Implementing
it by pinnings requires the corresponding signatures. In particular,
bit flips at individual inputs are used only when available or inside a
stated global transform. Interpolation recovers finitely many unknown
coefficients from oracle evaluations of a parameterized gadget family by
solving an invertible linear system. It supplies an oracle reduction.

Let \(f\) have arity \(r\).  For an ordered pair of distinct inputs
\(p=(i,j)\), a binary contraction kernel
\(C:\Ftwo^2\to\AlgNums\), and an assignment to the remaining inputs \({\mathbf{z}}\) indexed by
\([r]\setminus\{i,j\}\) in increasing input order, let
\({\mathbf{z}}^{i\leftarrow a,j\leftarrow b}\) be the length-\(r\) string obtained by
inserting \(a,b\) at inputs \(i,j\).  Define the pair contraction by
\begin{equation*}
  (\partial_p^C f)({\mathbf{z}})
  =
  \sum_{a,b\in\Ftwo}C(a,b)
  f\bigl({\mathbf{z}}^{i\leftarrow a,j\leftarrow b}\bigr).
\end{equation*}
In the notation of Shao--Cai
\cite[full version, Section~2.4]{ShaoCai2020}, their
\(\widehat\partial_{ij}\widehat f\) and \(\partial_{ij}f\) are,
respectively, our \(\partial_{(i,j)}^X\widehat f\) and
\(\partial_{(i,j)}^I f\).  Input order fixes the ordered pair \((i,j)\).
A \emph{pair card} of \(f\) is one such contraction
\(\partial_p^C f\). We shorten this to \emph{card} when the contraction
is specified.  A card is \emph{proper} if its arity is strictly smaller
than its parent's.  Iterated Pauli and exotic cards are defined where
contractions on lists of disjoint pairs are used.

For binary matrices
\(U,V\), the Frobenius pairing is bilinear:
\begin{equation*}
  \langle U,V\rangle_{\mathrm{Frob}}
  =\operatorname{tr}(U^{\mathsf T}V)
  =\sum_{a,b\in\Ftwo}U(a,b)V(a,b),
\end{equation*}
without conjugation. A \emph{binary chain} is a serial
composition of ordered binary gadgets joined by \(X\)-edges.  A
\emph{dressed pair card} \(\partial_p^Cf\) uses a closing binary
chain over the given signature set, attached through the two edge kernels.  Suppose
the closing subnetwork has ordered boundary tensor \(B\).  Its two
attachment \(X\)-edges complement both boundary bits, so
\begin{equation*}
  C(a,b)
  =\sum_{u,v\in\Ftwo}X(a,u)B(u,v)X(v,b)
  =(XBX)(a,b)
  =B(1-a,1-b).
\end{equation*}
Exchanging the two ordered boundary inputs replaces \(B\) by
\(B^{\mathsf T}\), and the same attachment therefore gives
\(XB^{\mathsf T}X\).  These formulas fix the input order used
below. Every dressed pair card so constructed is a gadget. Linear
combinations of cards are only analytic comparisons.

For every ordered binary signature \(B\), its transfer matrix is
\(T_B:=BX\).  If \(B\) is nonsingular, its transfer class is
\([T_B]=[BX]\in\PGL_2(\AlgNums)\).  A nonzero singular matrix has a
class only in the projective matrix space, and the zero matrix has no
projective class.

\subsection{Charge and Hamming layers}
\label{subsec:charge}

All charge terminology in this subsection is in \(K\)-coordinates.
For \({\mathbf{x}}\in\{0,1\}^r\), define its Hamming weight by
\(\wt({\mathbf{x}}):=|\{j:x_j=1\}|\) and its doubled charge by
\[
  q_r({\mathbf{x}})=2\wt({\mathbf{x}})-r.
\]
A \emph{Hamming layer} is the set of strings of one fixed Hamming weight.  A
string has even or odd parity according as its weight is even or odd, and a
signature has \emph{even-parity support} when it vanishes on every odd-parity string.
We distinguish \(\supp f\subseteq M\) from \(\supp f=M\). Coefficients
in a prescribed support set may vanish unless equality is required.
Define the Hamming half-spaces
\[
  HW_r^{\ge}=\{{\mathbf{x}}:q_r({\mathbf{x}})\ge0\},
  \quad
  HW_r^{\le}=\{{\mathbf{x}}:q_r({\mathbf{x}})\le0\},
  \quad
  HW_r^{=}=\{{\mathbf{x}}:q_r({\mathbf{x}})=0\},
\]
and define \(HW_r^{>},HW_r^{<}\) analogously.  A signature is
\(HW^{\ge}\) (respectively, \(HW^{\le}\)) when its support lies in
\(HW_r^{\ge}\) (respectively, \(HW_r^{\le}\)).  A signature set is \emph{common
one-sided} when all members are \(HW^{\ge}\) or all are \(HW^{\le}\).
Otherwise it is \emph{two-sided}, meaning that both charge signs occur
across the supports of its members. A signature is \emph{two-sided} when its own
support contains strings of both charge signs. Common one-sided support
is the exception to tensor-factor extraction in \cref{thm:external-factor}.
An arity-\(2d\) \emph{EO signature} is supported in \(HW_{2d}^=\).

In a closed \(X\)-edge instance, each contributing assignment has one
\(0\)-bit and one \(1\)-bit on every structural edge. Hence
\(\sum_v q_{\arity(f_v)}(\tau|_{H_v})=0\).
For an open gadget, the same cancellation equates the total vertex charge
with the boundary charge. The general-boundary calculation, including
the free-endpoint correction, is given in
Appendix~\ref{app:boundary-charge}.

\begin{remark}[Notation relative to prior work]
\label{rem:mwxz-notation}
We follow \cite[Sections~2.1--2.3]{MengWangXiaZheng2025} in writing
\(\widehat{\cF}=K^{-1}\cF\), \(HW^{\ge}\), \(HW^{\le}\), \(HW^=\),
and \emph{EO signature}.  We also use their equality and disequality notation \(\Eq_r\) and
\(\Neq_2\), with matrix aliases \(I=(\Eq_2)\) and \(X=(\Neq_2)\).
Subscripts in \(HW_r^\bullet\) record arity.

\end{remark}

\subsection{Binary codes and affine geometry}
\label{subsec:binary-affine-conventions}

The support reconstructions in
\cref{sec:normalized-dihedral,sec:proper-deck,sec:v4-deck} use binary
affine spaces and coefficient ratios to describe supports and phases.
We fix their shared Boolean and affine conventions here. The
\hyperref[subsubsec:symmetric-binary-spaces]{symmetric-space tools}
are introduced with the flat-Lagrangian networks.

\label{subsubsec:boolean-affine-tools}

For a finite coordinate set \(S\), put
\(\Ftwo^S:=\{{\mathbf{x}}:S\to\Ftwo\}\).  A \emph{binary code} of length \(n\) is a
subset of \(\Ftwo^n\). Its elements are \emph{codewords}.  It is linear when
it is a subspace and affine when it is a coset of a linear code.  The truth
table of a Boolean function lists its values in the fixed lexicographic input
order.  For a commutative ring \(R\), a Boolean polynomial over \(R\) is an
element of the quotient ring of \(R[x_1,\ldots,x_n]\) by the ideal generated
by the polynomials \(x_j^2-x_j\), \(j\in[n]\), and hence has a unique
multilinear representative.  Its degree is the largest number of
distinct variables in a nonzero monomial, and a mixed term contains at least
two distinct variables.  An affine-linear form is a constant plus a linear
form.  A \emph{quadratic phase} is \(i^{Q({\mathbf{x}})}\), where
\(Q:\Ftwo^n\to\mathbb Z_4\) has multilinear degree at most two and even
coefficients on mixed terms. We call \(Q\) its quadratic phase polynomial. A character of an additive group is a group
homomorphism into the multiplicative group of the coefficient field.  These
Boolean normal forms are instances of Boolean-lattice Möbius inversion
\cite[Chapters~1--2]{Carlet2021}.

For an affine subspace \({\mathbf{a}}+U\), \(U\) is its \emph{translation space}.
An affine map \({\mathbf{a}}+U\to {\mathbf{b}}+V\) has the form
\({\mathbf{a}}+{\mathbf{u}}\mapsto {\mathbf{b}}+T({\mathbf{u}})\) for a linear map \(T:U\to V\).  If an affine surjection
\(\pi:{\mathbf{a}}+U\twoheadrightarrow D\) onto an affine space \(D\) is fixed, an
\emph{affine section} is an
affine map \(\sigma:D\to {\mathbf{a}}+U\) with
\(\pi\circ\sigma=\mathrm{id}_D\).  For \(S\subseteq[n]\), in increasing
coordinate order, define coordinate projection and zero extension by
\[
 \pi_S:\Ftwo^n\to\Ftwo^S,\quad \pi_S({\mathbf{x}})=(x_i)_{i\in S},
 \qquad
 \iota_S:\Ftwo^S\to\Ftwo^n,\quad
 (\iota_S{\mathbf{y}})_i=\begin{cases}y_i,&i\in S,\\0,&i\notin S.\end{cases}
\]


For \(Q:\Ftwo^d\to R\), with \(R\) a commutative ring, its
\emph{Boolean Möbius expansion} is the unique multilinear expression
\[
 Q({\mathbf{x}})=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat Q_S\prod_{j\in S}x_j,
 \qquad
 \widehat Q_S
 =\sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S|-|T|}Q(\one_T).
\]
The coefficient table \((\widehat Q_S)_S\) is the Boolean Möbius transform.
The formula is interpreted in the stated coefficient ring, including
\(\Ftwo\) and \(\mathbb Z_4\).  Direct substitution is the Möbius-inversion
proof of uniqueness.  This additive transform is distinct from the
multiplicative Möbius inversion used later.

\subsection{Bell signatures}
\label{subsec:pauli-notation}

The Bell tensors form a common basis for binary contractions.
We fix their ordered signs.

We also fix the sign and skew matrices
\[
  Z=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix},
  \qquad
  Y=ZX=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]

For \(d,\ell,u,v\in\Ftwo\), define the ordered Bell signature
\begin{equation*}
  B_{d,\ell}(u,v)
  =
  \one[u\oplus v=d](-1)^{\ell u}.
\end{equation*}
Write \(B_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=B_{d,\ell}\) for \({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=(d,\ell)^{\mathsf T}\).  Input order matters
for \(B_{1,1}\), which is negated under reversal.

With truth-table order \(00,01,10,11\), these four signatures are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
  B_{0,0}&=(1,0,0,1)=I,&
  B_{0,1}&=(1,0,0,-1)=Z,\\
  B_{1,0}&=(0,1,1,0)=X,&
  B_{1,1}&=(0,1,-1,0)=Y.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:bell-matrix-dictionary}
\end{equation}

A \emph{Pauli binary} is a nonzero scalar multiple of one of the four
ordered Bell tensors \(B_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\), retaining its input order.
It is \emph{realizable} over a signature set when a direct gadget over that
set realizes it up to a nonzero scalar, as in
\cref{subsec:reduction-conventions}.

\begin{remark}[The Bell-signature dictionary of Shao--Cai]
The tuple in \cref{eq:bell-matrix-dictionary} is Shao--Cai's
\((=^+_2,=^-_2,\neq^+_2,\neq^-_2)\) \cite[Section~2]{ShaoCai2020}.
\(Y^{\mathsf T}=-Y\).  Their holographic matrix named \(Z\) is our \(K\),
whereas our \(Z\) is only the diagonal sign matrix.
\end{remark}

The Bell tables satisfy the
exact bilinear orthogonality identity
\[
 \langle B_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},B_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}'}\rangle_{\mathrm{Frob}}
 =2\,\one[{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}'].
\]



For an ordered binary tensor \(A\) and an ordered pair of inputs \((p,q)\),
the notation \(A^{(p,q)}\) places the first and second inputs of \(A\) on
inputs \(p\) and \(q\), respectively. For an ordered pair \(e=(p,q)\), we also write \(A^{(e)}\). For an even finite input set \(R\), a
\emph{Bell product} on \(R\) is a nonzero scalar multiple of
\[
 \bigotimes_{e=(p_e,q_e)\in M}B_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_e}^{(p_e,q_e)},
 \qquad {\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_e\in\Ftwo^2,
\]
where \(M\) is a perfect matching of \(R\) and each pair of \(M\) has been
given the displayed orientation.  Reversing a pair carrying \(B_{1,1}\)
only changes the overall scalar sign, so this class is independent of the
chosen orientations.  For \(R=\varnothing\), a Bell product is a nonzero
nullary signature.


\subsection{Tractable signature sets and imported dichotomies}
\label{sec:predicate}

\subsubsection{The standard signature classes}
\label{subsec:standard-classes}

We include the zero signature in every class below.  The affine class
\(\cA\) consists of signatures
\begin{equation}
  f({\mathbf{x}})=\lambda\,\one[A{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{b}}]\,i^{Q({\mathbf{x}})},
  \label{eq:affine-class}
\end{equation}
where \(\lambda\in\AlgNums\setminus\{0\}\) and, for
\(r=\arity(f)\), there is an \(m\ge0\) with
\(A\in\Ftwo^{m\times r}\) and \({\mathbf{b}}\in\Ftwo^m\).  The function
\(Q:\{0,1\}^r\to\mathbb Z_4\), where
\(\mathbb Z_4=\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z\), has a multilinear representative
\[
 Q({\mathbf{x}})=q_0+\sum_{j=1}^r q_jx_j
      +\sum_{1\le j<\ell\le r}q_{j\ell}x_jx_\ell\pmod4,
 \qquad q_{j\ell}\in\{0,2\},
\]
so it has degree at most two and even mixed coefficients.
Equivalently, the phase is a product of fourth-root phases of affine linear
forms interpreted as \(\{0,1\}\)-valued integers.
Membership in \(\cA\) always refers to the displayed computational
coordinates.

The product class \(\cP\) consists of zero and all signatures of the form
\(\lambda\prod_jh_j\), where \(0\ne\lambda\in\AlgNums\) and every factor is
either a nonzero unary signature \(u(x_i)\), a binary equality
\(I(x_i,x_j)\), or a binary disequality \(X(x_i,x_j)\) on designated
signature variables.  Empty products and repeated variables are allowed.
An inconsistent product denotes the zero signature.

Define the primitive eighth root and the phase matrix by
\[
 \zeta_8:=e^{\pi i/4}=\frac{1+i}{\sqrt2},
 \qquad T_2:=D(\zeta_8).
\]
The local-affine class \(\cL\) consists of the signatures \(f\) such that
\begin{equation*}
 \left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{\arity(f)}T_2^{\alpha_j}\right)f\in\cA
 \qquad\text{for every }{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\supp(f).
\end{equation*}

Let \(\cT\) be the set of all unary and binary signatures, and define the
signature class \(\cM\) by
\begin{equation*}
  \cM=\{f:f({\mathbf{x}})=0\text{ whenever }\wt({\mathbf{x}})>1\}.
\end{equation*}

The Reed--Muller arguments also use the following subclass of \(\cA\),
whose supports are affine translates of self-dual binary codes.
Let \(L\) be a linear subspace of \(\Ftwo^{2k}\).  Its orthogonal complement
under the standard dot product
\({\mathbf{x}}\mathbin{\cdot}{\mathbf{y}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{2k}x_jy_j\in\Ftwo\) is
\[
 L^\perp
 =
 \{{\mathbf{y}}\in\Ftwo^{2k}:{\mathbf{x}}\mathbin{\cdot}{\mathbf{y}}=0
     \text{ for every }{\mathbf{x}}\in L\}.
\]
Call \(L\) \emph{self-dual for the dot product} when \(L=L^\perp\), and an arity-\(2k\)
nonzero signature \emph{flat-Lagrangian} when it has the form
\begin{equation*}
  f({\mathbf{x}})=c\,\one[{\mathbf{x}}\in {\mathbf{a}}+L],\qquad
  0\ne c\in\AlgNums,\quad {\mathbf{a}}\in\Ftwo^{2k},\quad L=L^\perp.
\end{equation*}
Let \(\FLag\) denote the zero signature together with all flat-Lagrangian
signatures of every even arity.
\begin{definition}[Common transformability and \(K\)-presentation]
\label{def:common-transformability}
\label{def:common-K-presentation}
Let \(\cC\) be a signature class closed under nonzero scalars and permutations of inputs.  A
finite signature set \(\cF\) is \(\cC\)-transformable if one
\(M\in\GL_2(\AlgNums)\) satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 M^{\mathsf T}M\in\cC,\qquad M^{-1}\cF\subseteq\cC.
\end{equation*}
Equivalently, for \(\widehat{\cF}=K^{-1}\cF\), one matrix
\(U=K^{-1}M\) gives the common \(K\)-presentation
\begin{equation*}
 U^{\mathsf T}XU\in\cC,\qquad U^{-1}\widehat{\cF}\subseteq\cC.
\end{equation*}
We abbreviate this condition by
\begin{equation}
 \PresK(\cF;\cC)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists U\in\GL_2(\AlgNums):
 \quad U^{\mathsf T}XU\in\cC,
 \qquad U^{-1}(K^{-1}\cF)\subseteq\cC.
 \label{eq:common-K-presentation-predicate}
\end{equation}
If \(\cF\) is already in \(K\)-coordinates, its common
\(\cC\) \(K\)-presentation is precisely
\[
 \PresK(K\cF;\cC)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists U\in\GL_2(\AlgNums):\quad
 U^{\mathsf T}XU\in\cC,\qquad U^{-1}\cF\subseteq\cC.
\]
These conditions include the edge and require one basis
for the entire signature set, not one basis per signature.  We use this definition
for \(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP,\cL\}\) and for the flat-Lagrangian subclass.
The character-flat subclass is introduced where it is used in
\cref{proofsubsec:rm-pauli-tools}.
\end{definition}

Since \(\FLag\subseteq\cA\), a common flat-Lagrangian
\(K\)-presentation is a common affine \(K\)-presentation.

\subsubsection{The Eulerian-orientation predicate}
\label{subsec:eo-predicate}

Two cases of \(\Tract\) use the same Eulerian-orientation criterion.
In the common one-sided case, we restrict each signature to its central
Hamming layer. In the single-layer case with both charge signs represented
across the set, we append pins to each signature to obtain central support. For an arity-\(r\) signature \(g\), its central
restriction is
\begin{equation*}
  g|_{\EO}({\mathbf{x}})=g({\mathbf{x}})\one[q_r({\mathbf{x}})=0].
\end{equation*}
It is zero for odd \(r\). We omit zero restrictions from the signature
set and define
\begin{equation*}
  \cF|_{\EO}=\{g|_{\EO}:g\in\cF,\ g|_{\EO}\not\equiv0\}.
\end{equation*}
This omission is a convention for the set. In a closed instance over a
common-one-sided set, an occurrence whose central restriction is zero
makes the instance value zero.

Let \(g\ne0\) be a signature of arity \(k\) supported on the single Hamming
layer of weight \(d\).  Its \emph{EO completion} \cite[Section~6.2]{MengWangXia2025EO} is the ordered tensor
\begin{equation}
  g_{\to\EO}
  =
  \begin{cases}
    g\otimes\DeltaZero^{\otimes(2d-k)},
      & 2d\ge k,\\
    g\otimes\DeltaOne^{\otimes(k-2d)},
      & 2d<k.
  \end{cases}
  \label{eq:eo-completion}
\end{equation}
The original inputs precede the added unaries. A zeroth power is the nullary
unit.  The completed signature has even arity and central support, hence is
EO\@.  For a signature set of nonzero single-layer signatures, define its
EO-completion signature set by
\begin{equation*}
  \cF_{\to\EO}=\{g_{\to\EO}:g\in\cF\}.
\end{equation*}

For an arity-\(2d\) EO signature \(g\), an oriented perfect pairing
\(\pi=((a_1,b_1),\ldots,(a_d,b_d))\) has unordered pairs partitioning
\([2d]\).  For \({\mathbf{z}}\in\Ftwo^d\), let \({\mathbf{y}}\in\Ftwo^{2d}\) be the unique string
with \(y_{a_j}=z_j\) and \(y_{b_j}=1-z_j\). The pairing induces
\begin{equation*}
  g^\pi(z_1,\ldots,z_d)=g({\mathbf{y}}).
\end{equation*}

\begin{definition}[The predicate \(\EOTract\)]
\label{def:eo-tract}
For a finite EO signature set \(\cF\), \(\EOTract(\cF)\) holds if there exist a
single sign \(\varepsilon\in\{+1,-1\}\) and a single class
\(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP\}\), both shared by the entire signature set, such that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item for every \(g\in\cF\) and all
      \({\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\supp(g)\), if
      \({\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\), then
      \({\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in\supp(g)\) or
      \(\varepsilon q_{\arity(g)}({\boldsymbol{\delta}})>0\);
\item for every \(g\in\cF\) and every oriented perfect pairing \(\pi\)
      of its inputs, \(g^\pi\in\cC\).
\end{enumerate}
In particular, \(\EOTract(\varnothing)\) holds.
\end{definition}

The common-sign condition follows \cite[Theorem~26]{MengWangXia2025EO}.
The opposite-pair restrictions defining the affine and product EO classes
appear in \cite[Definition~22]{MengWangXia2024TypicalEO}.
Our compressed form substitutes \(y_{b_j}=1-y_{a_j}\).
After ordering the inputs as \((a_1,\ldots,a_d,b_1,\ldots,b_d)\), the
full zero-extended restriction is
\[
 R_\pi g({\mathbf z},{\mathbf w})
 =g({\mathbf z},{\mathbf w})\prod_{j=1}^dX(z_j,w_j)
 =g^\pi({\mathbf z})\prod_{j=1}^dX(z_j,w_j).
\]
For \(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP\}\), adjoining these disequality factors preserves
\(\cC\), so \(g^\pi\in\cC\) implies \(R_\pi g\in\cC\).
Conversely, each \(z_j\) determines exactly one \(w_j\), and
\[
 \sum_{{\mathbf w}\in\Ftwo^d}R_\pi g({\mathbf z},{\mathbf w})=g^\pi({\mathbf z}).
\]
This variable elimination preserves affine and product normal forms:
in the affine case it is affine-variable summation, and in the product
case substituting \(w_j=1-z_j\) preserves unary and equality/disequality
factors. Hence \(R_\pi g\in\cC\) if and only if \(g^\pi\in\cC\).
These are identities of normal forms. Gadget availability follows the
conventions of \cref{subsec:reduction-conventions}.

The source papers use unoriented pairs.  Reversing one pair applies the
bit permutation \(z_j\mapsto1-z_j\), and reordering pairs is a permutation of inputs. Both preserve \(\cA\) and \(\cP\).  Hence the oriented
predicate and the source predicate are equivalent.  For a fixed signature
set, the number of pairings and orientations is a constant independent of
the network input.  If the signature set itself is input, these are finite
exact checks within the decidability procedure of
\cref{subsec:complexity-conventions}. No polynomial bound in that input's
encoding length is asserted.

\subsubsection{The tractability predicates}
\label{subsec:tract-predicate}

The reductions use all seven cases of \(\Tract\) for intermediate
signature sets. \Cref{lem:tract-X-anchor} identifies when these reduce
to the four cases of \(\ReTract\).

When either predicate below is applied to an arbitrary finite input set, its
argument is first reduced to the nonzero positive-arity part according to
\cref{subsec:zero-nullary}.  The definitions may therefore be stated for
preprocessed signature sets without carrying a separate superscript in the
public criteria.

For a preprocessed signature set \(\cF\), define its \(K\)-coordinate image
\(\widehat{\cF}=K^{-1}\cF\).  Define \(\Tract(\cF)\) to be the disjunction
of the following seven cases.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\arabic*)}]
\item \label{case:one-sided}
      The signature set \(\widehat{\cF}\) is common one-sided, in the explicit
      sense that
      \[
       [\,\forall\widehat f\in\widehat{\cF},\
       \supp(\widehat f)\subseteq HW_{\arity(\widehat f)}^{\ge}\,]
       \quad\text{or}\quad
       [\,\forall\widehat f\in\widehat{\cF},\
       \supp(\widehat f)\subseteq HW_{\arity(\widehat f)}^{\le}\,],
      \]
      and \(\EOTract(\widehat{\cF}|_{\EO})\) holds.

\item \label{case:single-weight}
      Every \(\widehat f\in\widehat{\cF}\) is supported on one Hamming
      weight \(d_f\).  Across the signature set, some string in a signature support has negative
      charge and some (possibly other) string in a signature support has positive charge, and
      \(\EOTract(\widehat{\cF}_{\to\EO})\) holds, with the completion
      operation defined in \cref{eq:eo-completion}.

\item \label{case:arity-two}
      \(\cF\subseteq\angles{\cT}\).

\item \label{case:matching}
      \(\cF\subseteq\angles{K\cM}\), or
      \(\cF\subseteq\angles{KX\cM}\).

\item \label{case:affine}
      \(\cF\) is \(\cA\)-transformable.

\item \label{case:product}
      \(\cF\) is \(\cP\)-transformable.

\item \label{case:local-affine}
      \(\cF\) is \(\cL\)-transformable.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{remark}
The matrix symbols in \cref{case:matching} act tensorwise at the arity of
each signature.  In particular,
\[
\begin{aligned}
 f\in\angles{K\cM}
 &\Longleftrightarrow
 \text{ every irreducible factor of }K^{-1}f\text{ lies in }\cM,\\
 f\in\angles{KX\cM}
 &\Longleftrightarrow
 \text{ every irreducible factor of }XK^{-1}f\text{ lies in }\cM.
\end{aligned}
\]
\end{remark}

The four cases indexed by \(\cT,\cA,\cP,\cL\) form the reduced
tractability predicate used in the main theorems.

\begin{definition}[The reduced tractability predicate \(\ReTract\)]
\label{def:tract-X}
For a preprocessed signature set \(\cF\), define
\hypertarget{eq:tract-X}{}
\begin{equation*}
\begin{split}
 \ReTract(\cF)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 &\cF\subseteq\angles{\cT},
 \\[-0.2em]
 &\text{or \(\cF\) is \(\cA\)-transformable,}
 \\[-0.2em]
 &\text{or \(\cF\) is \(\cP\)-transformable,}
 \\[-0.2em]
 &\text{or \(\cF\) is \(\cL\)-transformable.}
\end{split}
\end{equation*}
Thus \(\ReTract\) is exactly the disjunction of
\cref{case:arity-two,case:affine,case:product,case:local-affine}.
\end{definition}

To make the edge condition explicit, for \(E\in\{I,X\}\) write
\(\ReTract_E(\cF)\) for the same four cases, with transformability
requiring \(M^{\mathsf T}EM\in\cC\) and
\(M^{-1}\cF\subseteq\cC\) for one common \(M\).
The introduction suppresses \(E\), using \(I\) in
\cref{thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy} and \(X\) in
\cref{thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy}. Elsewhere the unadorned
\(\ReTract\) is \(\ReTract_I\), as in \cref{def:tract-X}.
Since \(K^{\mathsf T}K=X\),
\hypertarget{eq:retract-K-coordinates}{}
\[
 \ReTract_X(\cF)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\ReTract(K\cF).
\]
Indeed, substituting \(M=KU\) identifies the edge condition
\(M^{\mathsf T}M\in\cC\) with \(U^{\mathsf T}XU\in\cC\) and gives
\(M^{-1}K\cF=U^{-1}\cF\). Invertible single-input maps also preserve
\(\angles{\cT}\). Thus the \(K\)-change expresses the same four
tractable cases in ordinary-Holant coordinates.

\begin{lemma}[Reduction to \(\ReTract\)]
\label{lem:tract-X-anchor}
Let \(\cF\) be a preprocessed signature set. If \(K^{-1}\cF\) contains a
two-sided irreducible signature of arity at least two, then
\[
 \Tract(\cF)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\ReTract(\cF).
\]
In particular, this equivalence holds if \(X\in\cF\) or
\(Z=\diag(1,-1)\in\cF\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(g\in K^{-1}\cF\) be two-sided and irreducible, with
\(n:=\arity(g)\ge2\). Its support contains strings of both charge signs
and hence of different Hamming weights. Thus
\cref{case:one-sided,case:single-weight} are impossible.

By irreducibility and the characterization preceding \cref{def:tract-X},
\cref{case:matching} would require \(g\in\cM\) or
\(X^{\otimes n}g\in\cM\). A signature in \(\cM\) is supported at
weights at most one, whose doubled charges are at most \(2-n\le0\).
Complementing all inputs reverses the charge sign. Either possibility
therefore makes \(g\) one-sided, a contradiction. Only the four cases
of \(\ReTract\) remain. The reverse implication holds by definition.

For the binary special cases, \(K^{-1}X=-iZ\) and \(K^{-1}Z=I\).
Both images have matrix rank two and nonzero values at \(00\) and \(11\),
so they are irreducible and two-sided.
\end{proof}

In \cref{thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy}, \(\cF\cup\{X\}\) contains \(X\), so the
first binary special case applies directly. In \cref{thm:p1-eq2-kholant-dichotomy},
\(I\in\cF\) gives \(Z=K^{\otimes2}I\in K\cF\), so the second applies.
For \cref{thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy}, the nonzero values at
\(0000\) and \(1111\) make the irreducible quaternary two-sided.
Its arity also excludes \cref{case:arity-two}, since invertible maps at
individual inputs preserve tensor irreducibility. Thus only the three
transformability cases of \(\ReTract\) remain under that hypothesis.

\subsubsection{Imported dichotomies and decomposition}
\label{subsec:imported-results}

Unless an ordinary \(\Holant\) symbol or an explicit source edge model
\(\Holant(X\mid\cdot)\) is displayed, the imported \(\KHolant\) statements
below use \(K\)-coordinates (so the edge tensor is \(X\)). Applying
the fixed \(K\)-change gives the corresponding ordinary statement.  All
signature sets in these statements are fixed and preprocessed.
In particular, every later use of \cref{thm:external-odd} on a signature set
\(\cF\) means the holographic equivalence
\[
 \KHolant(\cF)\equivT\Holant(K\cF),
 \qquad
 K\cF=\{K^{\otimes\arity(g)}g:g\in\cF\},
\]
followed by the ordinary theorem on \(K\cF\) and the inverse holographic equivalence.
The known nonzero edge and gadget scalars are restored by the record.

\begin{theorem}[Weighted Eulerian orientations
{\cite[Theorem~26]{MengWangXia2025EO}; \cite[Theorem~1.1]{GuanShaoShi2026}}]
\label{thm:external-eo}
For each fixed finite preprocessed algebraic EO signature set \(\cF\), if \(\EOTract(\cF)\) holds,
then \(\KHolant(\cF)\in\FP\). Otherwise
\(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Odd-arity Holant
{\cite[full version, Theorem~33; conference version, Theorem~30]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}}]
\label{thm:external-odd}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite preprocessed algebraic signature set containing a
nonzero odd-arity signature.  Then \(\Holant(\cF)\in\FP\) if
\(\Tract(\cF)\) holds, and is \(\SharpP\)-hard otherwise.
\end{theorem}

Its first two cases were originally in \(\FP^{\mathsf{NP}}\). The
stated \(\FP\) conclusion follows from
\cite[Theorem~1.1 and Corollary~1.2]{GuanShaoShi2026}.
Thus a set containing a nonzero odd-arity signature is covered by this
classification. The remaining structural analysis concerns even-arity
signatures. The next theorem allows us to separate their tensor factors.

\begin{theorem}[Tensor-factor extraction
{\cite[full version, Theorem~32 and Remark~39; conference version,
Theorem~29]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}}]
\label{thm:external-factor}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite preprocessed algebraic signature set in \(K\)-coordinates, and let \(h=a\otimes b\) be a nonzero tensor product with
algebraic factors \(a,b\) of positive arity (equivalently, apply the source theorem
to the ordinary set \(K(\cF\cup\{h\})\)).  Any nullary scalar factor is
absorbed during preprocessing.  Unless
\(\cF\cup\{h\}\) is common one-sided,
\[
  \KHolant(\cF,a,b)\equivT\KHolant(\cF,h).
\]
\end{theorem}

In the common-one-sided exception, \(\KHolant(\cF,h)\) is in \(\FP\)
when \(\EOTract((\cF\cup\{h\})|_{\EO})\) holds and is
\(\SharpP\)-hard otherwise, by the one-sided clause of the cited theorem
and \cref{thm:external-eo}.

In the remaining case, the displayed equivalence permits factor extraction.
We next justify
iterating this reduction and handle any odd-arity factors that it produces.

\section{Shared Reductions and Effective Recognition}
\label{sec:p1-shared-terminals}

The structural classification needs both algorithms for its tractable cases
and reductions that apply across several branches. We first establish
recognition and evaluation, then show how adding factors and changing basis
preserve the connection to the original problem. The remaining tools use
binary interpolation, single-occurrence replication, and higher equalities
to obtain nonzero unaries. The odd-arity dichotomy then classifies the whole
signature set. Direct realization and Turing access follow
\cref{subsec:reduction-conventions}.

\subsection{Recognition and evaluation of tractable cases}
\label{subsec:shared-recognition}

Recognition of the predicates in \cref{sec:predicate} combines finite
support and factor tests with common-basis tests for the transformable
cases. Polynomial constraints decide the latter and produce an exact
witness for the edge and all signatures. For each fixed signature set,
the resulting representations give polynomial-time instance evaluation.

\subsubsection{Effective algebraic geometry}
\label{subsubsec:effective-algebraic-geometry}

The transformability test in \cref{lem:algebraic-transform-witness} asks
for one matrix satisfying polynomial conditions for the edge and every
signature. Work over the exact coefficient field \(L\). For an ideal
\(I\subseteq L[x_1,\ldots,x_n]\), write \(V_{\overline L}(I)\) for its
common zero set. Appendix~\ref{app:exact-algebraic-tools} records the
algebraic tools used to decide whether this set is empty and to recover
an exact witness when it is nonempty.

\begin{lemma}[Effective algebraic transformability witnesses]
\label{lem:algebraic-transform-witness}
Let \(E\in\Mat_2(\AlgNums)\), let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic
signature set, and let \(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP,\cL\}\).  Suppose there is a matrix
\(U\in\GL_2(\mathbb C)\) such that
\[
  U^{\mathsf T}EU\in\cC,
  \qquad
  U^{-1}\cF\subseteq\cC,
\]
where membership in \(\cC\) allows complex values for the free scalar and
unary parameters in its normal forms.  Then such a matrix exists in
\(\GL_2(\AlgNums)\).  Moreover, for exact algebraic input, the existence of a
matrix is decidable and an algebraic matrix can be produced.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(L\) be a number field containing the entries of \(E\) and \(\cF\),
as well as \(i\) and \(\zeta_8=(1+i)/\sqrt2\) (equivalently \(i\) and
\(\sqrt2\)).  There are only finitely many combinatorial normal-form
types compatible with the arities: affine supports and phase coefficients,
product partitions with equality or disequality signs and unary weights,
and, for \(\cL\), the finitely many prescribed
\(T_2\)-twisted affine conditions.  Enumerate one shared type choice for the transformed edge and
all transformed signatures.

For \(\cP\), repeated factors cause no infinitude: at fixed arity we
canonically enumerate the support set, tensor partition, factor
multiplicities, equality/disequality signs, unary phase type, and permutation of inputs.  On a fixed pair of variables, repeated copies of the same
\(I\)- or \(X\)-factor are idempotent and are retained as a single factor.
The simultaneous presence of an \(I\)- and an \(X\)-factor makes the whole
product zero.  A loop \(I(x,x)\) is the scalar one and a loop \(X(x,x)\)
is zero.  Unary factors on one variable are multiplied and combined into
one unary (or zero). These simplifications give a finite canonical enumeration.
For \(\cL\), enumerate the prospective transformed support set and affine
phase data. Requiring entries to be zero outside this support and nonzero
on it, together with the twist conditions, gives finitely many polynomial
equalities and inequations.  Thus every candidate, including one whose
transformed support is not known in advance, is one of finitely many
constructible systems.

Fix one such branch, and let \(\mathbf x\) be the tuple of all its scalar
variables: the four entries of \(U\), the scalar and unary parameters in the
chosen normal forms, and the auxiliary variables introduced below.  Introduce
an auxiliary scalar variable \(z\) and impose
\[
 z\det U=1.
\]
Here \(\operatorname{adj}(U)\) denotes the classical adjugate, characterized
by \(U\operatorname{adj}(U)=(\det U)I\). Hence on this solution set
\(U^{-1}=z\operatorname{adj}(U)\).  After this substitution, equality of
every transformed tensor entry with its proposed normal form is a polynomial
equation over \(L\).  If the branch also has the nonvanishing polynomial
constraints \(p_1,\ldots,p_s\ne0\), introduce one more auxiliary scalar
variable \(t\) and replace all these inequations by the single equation
\[
 t\prod_{\ell=1}^s p_\ell=1.
\]
Thus the branch is exactly the zero set of a finitely generated ideal
\(I\subseteq L[\mathbf x]\).

Compute a Gr\"obner basis of \(I\) over the exact number field \(L\).
For the algebraic closure \(\overline L\subseteq\mathbb C\) of this
embedded field, ideal membership and the weak Nullstellensatz give
\[
 V_{\overline L}(I)=\varnothing
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 1\in I,
\]
and the latter is equivalent to the reduced Gr\"obner basis being
\(\{1\}\).  Hence emptiness of each branch is decidable.  Moreover, a
complex solution makes \(I\) proper, so the weak Nullstellensatz supplies
a point over \(\overline L=\AlgNums\). Conversely every such point is a
complex solution.  In a zero-dimensional branch, standard elimination
followed by triangular decomposition produces exact minimal polynomials and
isolating data for one point.  A nonempty positive-dimensional branch is
also effective: enumerate algebraic tuples fairly, first by total exact
encoding height and then lexicographically, and test every displayed
equation exactly.  The weak Nullstellensatz guarantees that this search
eventually reaches a point of the branch. Reading the \(U\)-coordinates
gives the required algebraic witness. These effective procedures follow
from Gr\"obner-basis theory \cite{Cohen1993,CoxLittleOShea2015}, without
an encoding-size or running-time bound.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Evaluation for signatures in \texorpdfstring{\(\cM\)}{M}}
\label{subsubsec:one-hot-evaluation}

For signatures in \(\cM\), each vertex accepts at most one incident
bit equal to one. This reduces evaluation to trees and unicyclic components,
as the following lemma shows. Here a factor graph records signature vertices
and their incidences with edge kernels. A unicyclic component has exactly
one cycle, including a loop or a two-cycle formed by parallel edges.
Cycles are identified under reversal and cyclic changes of starting point.
Suppression removes subdivision vertices.


\begin{lemma}[Evaluation for \(\cM\)]
\label{lem:one-hot-matching-evaluation}
Fix a finite algebraic signature set, and let \(\Gamma\) be a closed
factor-expanded finite \(X\)-edge network over it (each
occurrence has first been replaced by its fixed irreducible factors, with
the induced input connections and scalar).  If a vertex signature is
zero, return zero. Otherwise, for every vertex \(w\), let \(h_w\) denote its
nonzero signature, define its arity by \(d_w:=\arity(h_w)\),
and suppose
\(\supp(h_w)\subseteq\{\zero^{d_w},{\mathbf{e}}_1,\ldots,{\mathbf{e}}_{d_w}\}\).  Define its
weight at the all-zero string \(a_w:=h_w(\zero^{d_w})\) and weight-one input coefficients
\(b_{w,p}:=h_w({\mathbf{e}}_p)\).  Then \(Z_X(\Gamma)\) is computable using
\(O(|V(\Gamma)|+|E_{\rm int}(\Gamma)|)\) arithmetic operations in the fixed
coefficient field, and hence in polynomial bit time. \(V(\Gamma)\) is the
vertex set.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For an \(X\)-edge exactly one of its two half-edges receives bit \(1\).
Consequently assignments are in bijection with choices of one endpoint of
each edge, subject to at most one chosen endpoint at each vertex.  For such a
feasible endpoint-choice assignment \(\omega\), write
\(\operatorname{occ}_\omega(w)\in\{0,1\}\) for the number of selected
endpoints at \(w\), and let \(s_w(\omega)\) be the selected input when it
exists.  The weight of \(\omega\) is
\[
  \prod_{\operatorname{occ}_\omega(w)=0}a_w
  \prod_{\operatorname{occ}_\omega(w)=1}b_{w,s_w(\omega)}.
\]
The product of the fixed nonzero scalar factors recorded during the
factor expansion is multiplied into the final component product (and a
zero expansion scalar was already handled by returning zero), so it does
not change the recurrence below.
For a connected component, let \(e\) and \(v\) denote its edge and vertex
counts.  A component with \(e>v\) has value zero.  Every surviving component
has \(e=v-1\) (a tree) or \(e=v\) (unicyclic).
On a rooted tree, let \(p_w\) be the parent-edge input at \(w\),
and let \(p_{wc}\) be the input at \(w\) of the child edge \(wc\).  If
\(F_w(1)\) (resp. \(F_w(0)\)) denotes the subtree sum when the parent edge
selects \(w\) (resp.\ its parent), then
\[
\begin{aligned}
 F_w(1)&=b_{w,p_w}\prod_cF_c(1),\\
 F_w(0)&=a_w\prod_cF_c(1)+
 \sum_c b_{w,p_{wc}}F_c(0)\prod_{d\ne c}F_d(1).
\end{aligned}
\]
At the root use the second line without a parent term.  Order the children as
\(c_1,\ldots,c_m\).  Products and sums are evaluated without division by
the prefix products
\(P_j:=\prod_{\ell<j}F_{c_\ell}(1)\) and suffix products
\(S_j:=\prod_{\ell>j}F_{c_\ell}(1)\), for which
\(P_jS_j=\prod_{\ell\ne j}F_{c_\ell}(1)\) even
when some factors vanish.  For a unicyclic component delete one cycle
edge \(e\), with endpoint inputs \(p_u\) at \(u\) and \(p_v\) at \(v\).
Enumerate the two legal choices \(p\in\{p_u,p_v\}\), and let \(v(p)\)
be the vertex incident to the chosen input.  The inputs are distinct even
when \(e\) is a loop.  For each choice, root the resulting tree at
\(v(p)\) and impose that \(p\) is already occupied through \(e\):
at that root use
\[
 b_{v(p),p}\prod_cF_c(1).
\]
This forces every remaining incident edge at that root to select its child.
Use the ordinary
tree recurrence on all proper subtrees and perform this same forced-root
calculation separately for each \(p\). Sum the two values.
The prefix/suffix evaluations use linearly many field operations.  Every
intermediate entry is a sum of at most exponentially many products of
\(O(|V|+|E_{\rm int}|)\) fixed coefficients.  In a fixed rational basis of
their number field, a common denominator and the numerator sizes therefore
have \(O(|V|+|E_{\rm int}|)\) bits, giving polynomial bit time.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Decidability and polynomial-time evaluation}
\label{subsec:predicate-algorithms}

\begin{theorem}[Recognition and evaluation]
\label{thm:predicate-algorithms}
For every finite algebraic signature set \(\cF\), the predicate \(\Tract(\cF)\),
with the preprocessing convention of \cref{subsec:zero-nullary}, is
decidable.  If it holds, then \(\Holant(\cF)\in\FP\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The two EO cases are decided by finitely many exact tests of support,
layers, affine subspaces, and phases. Enumerate all oriented pairings and the
four shared \((\varepsilon,\cC)\)-choices.  A signature's tensor
decomposition can be found by enumerating all nontrivial bipartitions
\(S\mid S^c\), where
\(\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq[\arity(f)]\) and
\(S^c=[\arity(f)]\setminus S\), and testing whether the corresponding
matrix flattening has rank one. Recursively applying this test to the
factors decides \cref{case:arity-two,case:matching}.  Meanwhile,
\cref{lem:algebraic-transform-witness} with \(E=I\) decides
\cref{case:affine,case:product,case:local-affine} and returns the common
witness.

For the two EO cases, evaluation uses value-preserving transformations
of the input instance. Charge conservation reduces \cref{case:one-sided} to
\cref{thm:external-eo}: in a closed contributing assignment all local
charges have the common sign and sum to zero, so every local charge is zero
and only the central restrictions contribute. Deleting the off-central
entries therefore preserves the value. In \cref{case:single-weight}, an
occurrence of arity \(k_f\) and weight \(d_f\) has charge
\(c_f=2d_f-k_f\). Each connected component \(C\) must satisfy
\begin{equation*}
  \sum_{v\in V(C)}c_{f_v}=0.
\end{equation*}
Failure gives zero. Otherwise construct an auxiliary EO instance:
replace each occurrence \(f_v\) by its fixed completion
\((f_v)_{\to\EO}\), and in each component pair the newly appended
\(\DeltaZero\)-inputs (from \(c_{f_v}>0\)) with the appended
\(\DeltaOne\)-inputs (from \(c_{f_v}<0\)) by \(X\)-edges.  The charge
balance makes the two numbers equal, and every original assignment has a
unique extension to this auxiliary instance (and conversely), so its
partition function is unchanged
\cite[Section~6.2, proof of Corollary~68]{MengWangXia2025EO}.
The finite completed signature set is EO, so \cref{thm:external-eo}
supplies the polynomial-time evaluator.
After expansion into irreducible factors, the arity-two branch is a graph of maximum
degree two and is evaluated by transfer matrices on paths and cycles.
For the matching cases, first apply the common \(K\)-coordinate
equivalence, the possible global \(X\)-toggle, and expansion into irreducible factors.
The toggle is a change preserving the disequality edge because \(X^{\mathsf T}XX=X\).
After this change, every counted irreducible factor \(h\) satisfies
\(h({\mathbf{x}})=0\) for \(\wt({\mathbf{x}})>1\).  Only structural equality kernels, or degree-two equality
representations introduced solely to state the source theorem, are
suppressed before the factor graph is built. An explicit arity-two signature
occurrence remains a factor vertex unless it has first been eliminated by the
path/cycle preprocessing of \cref{lem:p1-equality-wire-equivalence}, with its
scalar recorded.  Thus
\cref{lem:one-hot-matching-evaluation} gives a polynomial-time evaluator
using linearly many field operations. The
expansion has constant size per occurrence because the signature set is
fixed.  Finally, a common change-of-basis matrix \(M\) gives
\[
  \Holant((\Eq_2)M\mid M^{-1}\cF),
  \qquad(\Eq_2)M=M^{\mathsf T}M,
\]
with both sides in the same class
\(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP,\cL\}\).  Forgetting the
bipartition yields an ordinary \(\Holant(\cC)\) instance, computable in
\(\FP\): affine instances are quadratic Gauss sums, product instances split
into unary/equality/disequality components, and local-affine instances are a
restriction of the corrected complex-valued \(\Holant^c\) evaluator of
\cite{Backens2021,Backens2025} (the real-valued presentation
\cite[Corollary~3.1]{CaiLuXia2018RealHolantc} gives the same local routine).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Retained reductions and normalization}
\label{sec:retained-normalization}

Fix the preprocessed signature set \(\cF_0\) at the beginning of a
structural branch.  We form finite signature sets from \(\cF_0\) by
adding realizable signatures, adding factors through the decomposition
theorem, or, within the eight-vertex reduction, applying a global \(GO(X)\)
change of basis.  We call a set obtained in this way a \emph{retained set}.
Each addition is justified by its gadget or factor reduction, and each
change of basis acts on the whole set. Interpolation is used only to
resolve the current case.

We record the current coordinates and signature set as \((A,\cF)\), with
\[
 A\in GO(X),\qquad A\cF_0\subseteq\cF,
 \qquad \KHolant(\cF)\equivT\KHolant(\cF_0).
\]
Adding signatures in the current basis leaves \(A\) unchanged.
The normalization data in \cref{lem:normalization-ledger} records the
coefficient field and pulled-back group. The eight-vertex interface
returns its outputs in the original coordinates, as specified in
\cref{cor:p1-eight-vertex-reduced-interface,sec:p1-binary-phase}.

For each signature, specify its gadget or reduction, input order, and
changes of basis.  Inverse transformations express it in any earlier basis.
Nonzero scalar factors follow the convention in \cref{subsec:holographic}.

\subsubsection{Finite induction and termination}
\label{subsec:worklist-termination}

The reductions in \cref{subsec:safe-matching-cards} retain tensor factors
and sometimes enlarge the finite group of binary transfers.  The orders
below distinguish finite processing of a fixed family from a restart with
a smaller arity or larger group.

\label{subsubsec:worklist-termination}

A strict order is irreflexive and transitive.  It is \emph{well founded}
when it has no infinite descending chain.  A finite multiset over a set
\(E\) records elements of \(E\) with multiplicity. The multiset sum \(\uplus\) adds multiplicities.  Given a strict order \(<\) on \(E\) and finite multisets \(A,B\) over
\(E\), its strict multiset extension is defined by \(A<_{\rm mul}B\) when
\[
 B=Z\uplus X,\qquad
 A=Z\uplus Y,\qquad X\ne\varnothing,
\]
for finite multisets \(X,Y,Z\) over \(E\), and every \(y\in Y\) satisfies
\(y<x\) for some \(x\in X\).  The multiset
extension of a well-founded strict order is well founded
\cite{DershowitzManna1979}.  Lexicographic order compares the first
coordinate at which two tuples differ. Finite lexicographic products of
well-founded orders are well founded by induction on the number of
coordinates.  Well-founded induction proves a property at \(x\) assuming it
at every strict predecessor of \(x\).

We index unfinished signature occurrences by a finite multiset.  Each
occurrence retains its realizing gadget or reduction and its ordered inputs.
To identify duplicate signatures, divide by the first nonzero coefficient
in the fixed input order.  Repeated
occurrences are still counted with multiplicity in the induction measures.
The reductions test their listed cases in order. The first applicable case
determines the next step.

\subsubsection{Factor closure}
\label{subsec:factor-saturation}

Record each recursive tensor factorization as a tree, retaining the ordered
blocks of inputs, permutation of inputs, and nonzero scalar at every split.  The leaves
are the irreducible factors.

For a preprocessed finite signature set \(\cF\), choose an exact
irreducible tensor factorization of every member and let \(\Pi(\cF)\) be the
joint signature set of all irreducible factors, with ordered blocks of inputs retained.
We call \(\cF\) \emph{factor-closed} when
\(\Pi(\cF)\subseteq\cF\).

\begin{lemma}[Factor closure]
\label{lem:factor-saturation}
If \(\cF\) is not common one-sided, then
\begin{equation}
  \KHolant(\cF)
  \equivT
  \KHolant(\cF,\Pi(\cF)).
  \label{eq:factor-saturation}
\end{equation}
Moreover, every invocation of factor closure is finite. The factorization
procedure and its strictly decreasing potential are given in the proof.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Start with one root occurrence for each member of the current
finite retained set and add a factor only through the decomposition theorem.  Let \(h_u\) denote the signature carried by a nonzero node \(u\).
Such a node, carrying its ordered block of inputs, is either
proved irreducible, or is replaced by one split
\[
 h_u\doteq a_u\otimes b_u,
 \qquad 1\leq\arity(a_u),\arity(b_u)<\arity(h_u),
\]
with the nonzero scalar and the permutation of inputs recorded with the factorization.
Record and discard nullary scalars, and remove irreducible unary nodes
before forming the unresolved queue. Normalize each node by its first
nonzero entry and retain its ordered input block. Identical normalized
signatures may share one retained signature, while their occurrences remain
distinct work items. Let \(\mathcal W\) be the multiset of unresolved
occurrences, all of arity at least two. The potential
\begin{equation}
  \Phi(\mathcal W)
  =
  \sum_{u\in\mathcal W}(\arity(h_u)-1)
  \label{eq:factor-potential}
\end{equation}
is a nonnegative integer. Splitting an item of arity \(r+s\) removes
\(r+s-1\) and adds at most \((r-1)+(s-1)=r+s-2\). Already pending or
processed normalized children only lower this amount further, and identifying
an irreducible signature removes its summand. Thus every step strictly
decreases \(\Phi\), and \(\Pi(\cF)\) is the finite set of canonical
irreducible leaves. Its independence of processing order follows from
uniqueness of irreducible tensor factorization in \cref{subsec:holant-definitions}.

Apply the decomposition theorem at each queued split
\(h_u=a_u\otimes b_u\) to the current signature set
\cite[full version, Theorem~32 and Remark~39]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}.
The original non-one-sided signature set remains a subset, so the
exceptional case never reappears.  Iteration adds every factor, while
set inclusion gives the reverse reduction. These two directions give
\cref{eq:factor-saturation}. All reduction scalars and ordered blocks
are available for later gadgets.
\end{proof}

For the initial reduction, each original signature can also be reconstructed
as the tensor product of its irreducible factors, with its input permutation
and nonzero scalar restored. Consequently, outside the common-one-sided
case,
\[
 \KHolant(\cF)\equivT\KHolant(\Pi(\cF)).
\]
We may therefore take the initial signature set to consist of irreducible
signatures and continue to denote it by \(\cF\). If a nonzero odd-arity
factor occurs, \cref{thm:external-odd} applies after the fixed
\(K\)-change. If all factors have arity at most two, the evaluation in
\cref{thm:predicate-algorithms} applies. The remaining analysis starts with
even-arity irreducible signatures and at least one of arity at least four.
Later constructions can have reducible outputs. At those steps we use the
extension \(\cF\cup\Pi(\cF)\) in \cref{eq:factor-saturation}, retaining
the outputs as well as their factors for the subsequent group comparison.

For a factor-closed signature set \(\cF\) containing an irreducible
signature of arity at least three, define its minimum arity of a nonbinary irreducible signature by
\begin{equation*}
 \nu(\cF)
 =\min\{\arity(h):h\in\cF
          \text{ is irreducible and }\arity(h)\ge3\}.
\end{equation*}
This parameter is used only after the branch in which every irreducible factor has
arity at most two has been removed.

\subsubsection{Preservation under taking subsets and global normalization}

To recover the tractability criterion for the original set, we must both
restrict to a subset and undo any global normalization. The next two
lemmas justify these steps.

\begin{lemma}[Preservation under taking subsets]
\label{lem:downward-inheritance}
If \(\cF\subseteq\cF'\) are preprocessed signature sets and
\(\Tract(K\cF')\) holds, then \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
All cases except the single-weight one are hereditary by definition.
In the latter case a signature subset either retains both charge signs and the
same EO-completion witness, or becomes one-sided.  In the latter event, its
central members are unchanged by both restriction and EO completion, whereas
all off-central members restrict to zero.  Hence its nonzero central
restrictions form a subset of the original completion signature set, and the
same shared EO witness applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\(GO(X)\)-invariance of transported tractability]
\label{lem:GOX-tract-invariance}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite preprocessed signature set and let
\(D\in GO(X)\).  Then
\begin{equation*}
  \Tract(K\cF)
  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  \Tract\bigl(K(D\cF)\bigr).
\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write the matrix \(D=\begin{psmallmatrix}a&b\\ c&d\end{psmallmatrix}\), and
let \(\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\) be its conformal multiplier, defined by
\(D^{\mathsf T}XD=\lambda X\).  Comparing entries gives
\(ac=bd=0\) and \(ad+bc=\lambda\ne0\).  Thus \(D\) is diagonal or
anti-diagonal.  The former preserves every string in the support. The latter
complements it. On a fixed layer its multiplier is constant.  Hence the two
EO cases are preserved (with the support side and shared sign
possibly exchanged), irreducible factor arities are unchanged, and
\(\angles{\cM}\) and \(\angles{X\cM}\) are preserved or exchanged.

Define the conjugate matrix \(A=KDK^{-1}\), so
\(A^{\mathsf T}A=\lambda I\).  If the matrix \(W\) is a common
\(\cC\)-transformability witness, define the matrix
\(W':=AW\).  It satisfies
\[
  (W')^{-1}A(K\cF)=W^{-1}(K\cF)\subseteq\cC,
  \qquad
  (W')^{\mathsf T}W'
  =\lambda W^{\mathsf T}W\in\cC
\]
for \(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP,\cL\}\).  Applying the same argument to \(D^{-1}\)
proves the converse.
\end{proof}

The following lemma records the data needed to transport reductions,
group enlargements, and tractable presentations back to the original
signature set. Its continuing-state use is confined to the eight-vertex
reduction.

\begin{lemma}[Normalization data]
\label{lem:normalization-ledger}
Let \(\cF_0\) be the signature set at the beginning of a structural
branch in which coordinate transport is permitted.  Let \(L_0\) be a fixed
number field containing all entries of
\(\cF_0\), their complex conjugates, and all algebraic constants already fixed by
the coordinate conventions.  Thus \(L_0\) is the fixed initial coefficient field, not a
field enlarged after each normalization.  Every continuing normalization
inside that branch is an explicitly recorded \(GO(X)\) change of coordinates, and tensor
splits are performed after pulling back by the current \(A\), using a nonzero
pivot and row--column ratios.  Thus the pulled-back data (rather than the
displayed current coordinates) stay over \(L_0\), and the pulled-back group
remains inside \(\PGL_2(L_0)\).  The
structural reductions maintain a current signature set \(\cF\),
a matrix \(A\in GO(X)\), and its nonzero conformal factor
\(\mu_A\in\AlgNums^\times\), satisfying
\begin{equation}
  A\cF_0\subseteq\cF,\qquad
  A^{\mathsf T}XA=\mu_A X,\qquad
  \KHolant(\cF)\equivT\KHolant(\cF_0).
  \label{eq:normalization-ledger}
\end{equation}
Every positive-arity \(h\in\cF\), up to a nonzero scalar, also has the
field-compatible form
\begin{equation*}
 h=A^{\otimes\arity(h)}h_0
 \quad\text{for a tensor }h_0\text{ over }L_0.
\end{equation*}
At a residual node where every directly realizable nonsingular transfer has
finite projective order, these transfers form a group. Denote by
\(G(\cF)\) the group of their classes \([BX]\), where \(B\) ranges over
directly realizable nonsingular ordered binaries over \(\cF\).  Then the
pulled-back group
\begin{equation*}
 \overline G(A,\cF)
 :=[A]^{-1}G(\cF)[A]
 \le \PGL_2(L_0)
\end{equation*}
is unchanged by a global normalization and is monotone when signatures are added.
Adding realizable signatures and factor closure preserve \(A\).
If \(D\in GO(X)\) is applied globally to the current signature set, the record
updates by
\hypertarget{eq:normalization-ledger-update}{}
\begin{equation*}
  (A,\cF)\longmapsto(DA,D\cF).
\end{equation*}
The new reduction state again satisfies \cref{eq:normalization-ledger}, up to the
known edge scalar from \cref{eq:GOX-global-scalar}.  Before a continuing
branch leaves \cref{sec:p1-eight-vertex}, apply the inverse of the accumulated
matrix \(A\).  The returned reduction state therefore has \(A=I\) in the fixed original coordinates and retains any supplied equality signature.
Generic interpolation is used only as a terminal transition and is not a
record-preserving update.  If an interpolation output were ever allowed to
re-enter a continuing finite-group state, the effective coefficient field
and the pulled-back group would first have to be enlarged explicitly.

If \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds, then
\(\Tract(K\cF_0)\) holds.  Separately, if
\(V\) is a common \(\cC\) \(K\)-presentation of \(\cF\), where
\(\cC\) is closed under nonzero scalar multiplication, then the pullback
matrix
\begin{equation*}
  W:=A^{-1}V
\end{equation*}
is a common \(\cC\) \(K\)-presentation of \(\cF_0\).  In particular, this
applies to \(\cA,\cP,\cL\) and to the flat-Lagrangian subclass.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The assertions hold initially for \((I,\cF_0)\).  Adding realizable signatures and
factor closure preserve them by
\cref{eq:direct-gadget-reduction,eq:factor-saturation}.  A global
\(D\in GO(X)\), with nonzero conformal factor
\(\mu_D\in\AlgNums^\times\) defined by \(D^{\mathsf T}XD=\mu_DX\), replaces \(A\) by
\(DA\). Then
\((DA)^{\mathsf T}X(DA)=\mu_D\mu_AX\), and
\cref{eq:GOX-global-scalar} supplies the stated equivalence.  Conversely,
the transported inclusion \(A\cF_0\subseteq\cF\), followed by the
inverse global transformation, reduces the initial problem to the current
one. 

Inductively pull every construction through \(A\).  Gadget contraction
stays over \(L_0\). At a tensor split, normalizing one nonzero entry and
taking row--column ratios chooses the factors over \(L_0\).  Moreover
\(A^{\mathsf T}X\doteq XA^{-1}\).  Thus, if a current binary \(B\) has the
record form \(B=A^{\otimes2}B_0\) for a binary tensor \(B_0\) over \(L_0\),
its projective transfer pulls back by
conjugation:
\[
 [BX]=[A(B_0X)A^{-1}].
\]
The identity transfer is realized by the disequality edge, and chaining realizes
products.  At such a finite-projective-order residual node, the inverse of each transfer is a
positive power of that transfer, so the realizable classes form a group.
The displayed conjugation shows that global normalization leaves
\(\overline G(A,\cF)\) unchanged, while adding signatures can only add realizable transfers.  This proves the field and group assertions.
Finally, if \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds, preservation under taking subsets first gives
\(\Tract(K(A\cF_0))\), and \cref{lem:GOX-tract-invariance} then gives
\(\Tract(K\cF_0)\).  If \(V\) is an explicit common \(K\)-presentation, then
\(W=A^{-1}V\) satisfies
\[
 W^{-1}\cF_0\subseteq V^{-1}\cF\subseteq\cC,
 \qquad
 W^{\mathsf T}XW=\mu_A^{-1}V^{\mathsf T}XV\in\cC,
\]
which proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}
\subsection{Binary Interpolation}
\label{proofsubsec:central-descent}

A singular nonzero binary gives unary factors. For an infinite-order
transfer, interpolation first gives a rank-one binary. The following lemma
establishes both reductions, including the size and query bounds needed
for polynomial-time interpolation.

\begin{lemma}[Binary interpolation]
\label{lem:binary-interpolation}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic, retained, two-sided signature set,
let \(B\ne0\) be an ordered binary directly realizable over \(\cF\),
and define its transfer matrix \(A=BX\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item If \(B\) has the rank-one factorization \(B=u\otimes v\), where
      \(u\) and \(v\) are nonzero algebraic unary signatures, then
      \[
        \KHolant(\cF,u,v)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
      \]
\item If \(A\) is nonsingular and has infinite projective order, there is
      a nonzero rank-one binary \(H\) such that
      \[
        \KHolant(\cF,H)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
      \]
      For \(m\ge1\) designated occurrences of \(H\), the interpolation uses
      \(m+1\) oracle queries, \(O(m^2)\) added vertices per query, and hence
      \(O(m^3)\) added vertices over all queries.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For the singular case, choose a nonzero algebraic pivot of the rank-one matrix
and take the corresponding algebraic row and column factors (absorbing the
pivot into one factor). Then \(B=u\otimes v\) with \(u,v\) algebraic, and
\cref{thm:external-factor} applies.  Two-sidedness excludes its one-sided
exception.  For the other case, a length-\(t\) chain realizes the ordered
binary signature \(B_t:=A^tX\).  If \(A\) is diagonalizable, choose an eigenbasis matrix
\(V\in\GL_2(\AlgNums)\) and nonzero eigenvalues
\(\lambda_0,\lambda_1\in\AlgNums^\times\) such that
\(A=V\diag(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)V^{-1}\).  For \(j\in\{0,1\}\), let
\(E_{jj}\) be the \(2\times2\) matrix with \((a,b)\)-entry
\(\one[a=b=j]\), for \(a,b\in\{0,1\}\), and define the rank-one binary
matrix \(H_j=VE_{jj}V^{-1}X\).  Then
\[
 B_t=\lambda_0^tH_0+\lambda_1^tH_1,
\]
with \(H_0,H_1\) rank one.  Replacing the \(m\) designated \(H_0\)-vertices by \(B_t\) and
dividing by \(\lambda_1^{tm}\) yields evaluations of a polynomial \(P(z)\)
of degree at most \(m\) at the distinct points
\(z=(\lambda_0/\lambda_1)^t\), \(1\le t\le m+1\). The coefficient of
\(z^m\) is the all-\(H_0\) value.  These points are distinct because
\([A]\) has infinite projective order, so \(\lambda_0/\lambda_1\) is not a
root of unity.

In the remaining infinite-order Jordan case, write \(A=\lambda I+N\), where
\(\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\) is the eigenvalue and \(N\) is a nonzero
nilpotent \(2\times2\) matrix satisfying \(N^2=0\).  Then
\(B_t=\lambda^t(X+t\lambda^{-1}NX)\).  After normalization the oracle value
is a polynomial in \(t\) of degree at most \(m\), whose coefficient of
\(t^m\) is the value with the rank-one binary \(\lambda^{-1}NX\) at every
designated vertex.
Indeed, \(N\ne0\) and \(N^2=0\) in dimension two imply
\(\rank(N)=1\), and right multiplication by \(X\) preserves
rank.
Both cases use \(m+1\) queries. \(t\le m+1\) gives \(O(m^2)\) replacement
size for each query and \(O(m^3)\) in total.  The eigenvalues and basis
matrices are fixed algebraic numbers.  Their powers and the interpolation
systems have polynomial encoding length, so exact interpolation is
polynomial-time postprocessing.
\end{proof}

Both reductions give access to a nonzero unary after factor extraction.
The dichotomy in \cref{lem:common-unary-exit} below then applies to the
whole current set. Thus the later unresolved binary analysis concerns
nonsingular transfers of finite projective order.

\subsection{Single-occurrence reductions and nonzero unaries}
\label{subsec:single-occurrence-carriers}

The next reductions start with one occurrence of an auxiliary signature.
A fixed gadget passes that signature to the next copy and supplies one
additional output. Repeating the construction supplies as many outputs
as needed while using the auxiliary signature only once.
The single-occurrence replication result in
\cref{lem:single-occurrence-replication} originates in an unpublished
expanded version of \cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025}, which is not publicly
available. We include the statement and proof here, with the ordered
connections explicit.

Define the ordered carrier signature
\[
  \Delta=\DeltaZero\otimes\DeltaOne,
\]
with the displayed order of its two inputs.  For a signature \(g\), the
superscripts \(g^{=1}\) and \(g^{\leq1}\) mean that an oracle instance contains,
respectively, exactly one or at most one \(g\)-vertex.  

\begin{lemma}[Universal one-pair reduction]
\label{lem:universal-directed-pair}
For every fixed finite algebraic signature set \(\cF\),
\[
  \KHolant(\cF,\Delta^{\leq1})
  \leT
  \KHolant(\cF).
\]

\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The zero-occurrence case is already an instance over \(\cF\).  Consider an
instance with one \(\Delta\)-vertex.  Delete that vertex, retaining its two
incident \(X\)-attachments as edges to ordered free boundary inputs.
With the boundary bits read at these free endpoints, the raw signature is
\[
  H=\begin{pmatrix}h_{00}&h_{01}\\h_{10}&h_{11}\end{pmatrix},
  \qquad\text{the desired one-\(\Delta\) value is }h_{01}.
\]
Each free endpoint carries an \(X\)-edge, so the signature for vertex
replacement is \(XHX\) by \cref{eq:half-edge-boundary-tensor}.
Closing the two exposed inputs by an \(X\)-edge gives an oracle value
\[
  S=h_{01}+h_{10}.
\]
Let \(\mathcal B_\cF\) be the set of ordered binary tensors realized by
finite \(\cF\)-networks.  If every \(B\in\mathcal B_\cF\) satisfies
  \(B_{01}=B_{10}\), then \(XHX\in\mathcal B_\cF\) is symmetric, so
  \(H\) is symmetric and the desired value is
  \(S/2\).

Otherwise fix once and for all a realized ordered binary
\[
  B=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix},
  \qquad b\ne c.
\]
Insert its fixed network at the deleted location in the two boundary
orientations.  The two oracle values are
\[
  T_+=ah_{00}+bh_{01}+ch_{10}+dh_{11},
  \qquad
  T_-=ah_{00}+ch_{01}+bh_{10}+dh_{11}.
\]
Thus
\[
  h_{01}
  =\frac12\left(S+\frac{T_+-T_-}{b-c}\right).
\]
The ordered inputs are essential in the subtraction.  The witness network for
\(B\) is fixed with \(\cF\), hence has constant size, and the reduction
uses at most three oracle queries.
\end{proof}

The preceding reduction allows one carrier occurrence. To obtain
unrestricted access to a new signature, we use a gadget that preserves
the carrier while producing another output. The next lemma states the
required replication and nonzero-closure conditions for ordinary Holant
and \(\KHolant\), with equality and disequality edges, respectively.

\begin{lemma}[Single-occurrence replication]
\label{lem:single-occurrence-replication}
Let \(\cF\) be a fixed signature set and let \(g,h\) be nonzero signatures.  For ordinary Holant, all connections use \(I\). Suppose that
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(\Holant(\cF,g^{=1})\leT\Holant(\cF)\);
\item a fixed gadget \(C\) over \(\cF\) has three disjoint ordered
      boundary blocks \(A,P,H\), where
      \(|A|=|P|=\arity(g)\) and \(|H|=\arity(h)\).
      Connecting one \(g\)-vertex coordinatewise to \(A\) realizes
      \(\rho\,g(P)\otimes h(H)\), for a nonzero scalar
      \(\rho\in\AlgNums^\times\). Between consecutive copies
      \(C_\ell,C_{\ell+1}\), connect \(P_\ell\) coordinatewise to
      \(A_{\ell+1}\), with no other input identifications.
      For every \(k\ge1\), this chain begins with exactly one
      \(g\)-vertex and realizes
      \(\rho^k g(P_k)\otimes\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^k h(H_\ell)\), with
      pairwise disjoint output blocks \(P_k,H_1,\ldots,H_k\); and
\item some fixed \(\Holant(\cF,g^{=1})\)-instance \(J\) has
      \(Z_I(J)\ne0\).
\end{enumerate}
Then
\[
  \Holant(\cF,h)\leT\Holant(\cF).
\]
For K-Holant, all connections instead use \(X\), assumption~1 is
\(\KHolant(\cF,g^{=1})\leT\KHolant(\cF)\), and assumption~3
requires a \(\KHolant(\cF,g^{=1})\)-instance \(J\) with \(Z_X(J)\ne0\).
With item~2 imposed using these \(X\)-edges, the conclusion is
\[
  \KHolant(\cF,h)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By the ordered chain composition in item~2, iterating the gadget
produces \(g\otimes h^{\otimes k}\) with one \(g\)-vertex, size \(O(k)\),
and a nonzero scalar.  Hence an instance containing one \(g\) and \(k\) designated
\(h\)-vertices reduces to an instance containing exactly one \(g\). Divide
out the scalar.  For an instance \(\Omega\) over \(\cF\cup\{h\}\), take the
disjoint union \(\Omega\sqcup J\).  Its value is
\(Z_I(\Omega)Z_I(J)\), so division by
the fixed nonzero number \(Z_I(J)\) inserts the one carrier required by the
first reduction.  The context and \(J\) are fixed, so the construction has
size linear in the number of \(h\)-occurrences.  Composing with item~1
proves the ordinary-Holant claim. For K-Holant, the same construction
uses \(X\)-edges and the disjoint-union value is
\(Z_X(\Omega)Z_X(J)\). Division by the stipulated nonzero \(Z_X(J)\)
and the K-Holant version of item~1 give its stated reduction.
\end{proof}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\normalsize,box/.style={draw,minimum width=9mm,minimum height=7mm}]
\node[draw,circle] (g) at (0,0) {\(g\)};
\node[box] (c1) at (1.6,0) {\(C_1\)};
\node (dots) at (3.1,0) {\(\cdots\)};
\node[box] (ck) at (4.6,0) {\(C_k\)};
\draw (g)--node[above]{\(A_1\)}(c1);
\draw (c1)--node[above]{\(P_1\)}(dots);
\draw (dots)--node[above]{\(A_k\)}(ck);
\draw (ck)--node[above]{\(P_k\)}(6,0);
\draw (c1.south)--node[right]{\(H_1\)}(1.6,-.8);
\draw (ck.south)--node[right]{\(H_k\)}(4.6,-.8);
\end{tikzpicture}
\par\normalsize The chain in item~2 of \cref{lem:single-occurrence-replication}.
Each connection represents the corresponding ordered block of edges.
\end{center}


Once these reductions provide a nonzero unary, the imported odd-arity
dichotomy applies to the extended set. Its conclusion transfers to the
original set as follows.

\begin{lemma}[Dichotomy with a nonzero unary]
\label{lem:common-unary-exit}
Let \(\cF\) be a signature set and let \(u\ne0\) be unary.
In ordinary Holant,
if \(\Holant(\cF,u)\leT\Holant(\cF)\), then
\(\Holant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard unless \(\Tract(\cF)\) holds. In the disequality-edge model,
if \(\KHolant(\cF,u)\leT\KHolant(\cF)\), then
\(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard unless \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For the first assertion, apply \cref{thm:external-odd} to
\(\cF\cup\{u\}\).  A tractable presentation of that set restricts to one for
\(\cF\).  For the second, transform to ordinary coordinates:
\[
  \KHolant(\cF,u)
  \equivT
  \Holant(K\cF,Ku).
\]
The signature \(Ku\) is a nonzero unary, so the same argument applies.
\end{proof}

For unary columns \(g,u\), their contraction is the bilinear scalar
\[
  g^{\mathsf T}u:=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}}g(x)u(x),
\]
with no complex conjugation.
For the higher-equality construction below, the carrier must have a
nonzero closure. The next lemma supplies a companion unary with this
property and a reduction allowing one occurrence of the pair.

\begin{lemma}[Unary companion]
\label{lem:unary-companion}
Let \(\cF\) be a fixed finite algebraic signature set and let \(g\ne0\) be
unary.  There is an algebraic unary \(u\) such that
\[
  g^{\mathsf T}u\ne0,
  \qquad
  \Holant(\cF,(g\otimes u)^{=1})\leT\Holant(\cF).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We first prove the claim for \(g=\DeltaZero\).  Let \(\mathcal B_\cF\) be
the set of ordered binary tensors realized by finite \(\cF\)-networks with
ordinary equality edges.  Deleting one occurrence of
\(\DeltaZero\otimes u\), where \(u=[s,t]^{\mathsf T}\), leaves a binary
context \(H=(h_{ij})\), and the desired value is
\(sh_{00}+th_{01}\).  Equality-closing the exposed inputs gives the oracle value
\[
  S:=h_{00}+h_{11}.
\]
We choose \(u\) by the following exhaustive alternatives.

If every \(H\in\mathcal B_\cF\) satisfies \(h_{00}=h_{11}\), take
\(u=[2,0]^{\mathsf T}\). Then the desired value is \(S\).  Otherwise fix
\[
  G_1=\begin{pmatrix}a_1&b_1\\c_1&d_1\end{pmatrix}
  \in\mathcal B_\cF,
  \qquad a_1\ne d_1.
\]
If every realized binary has equal off-diagonal entries, then
\(b_1=c_1=b\).  For \(b=0\), take \(u=[1,0]^{\mathsf T}\).  Inserting
\(G_1\) gives the oracle value \(T:=a_1h_{00}+d_1h_{11}\), whence
\[
  h_{00}=\frac{T-d_1S}{a_1-d_1}.
\]
For \(b\ne0\), take \(u=[a_1-d_1,2b]^{\mathsf T}\).  The same insertion,
now using \(h_{01}=h_{10}\), gives
\[
  T-d_1S=(a_1-d_1)h_{00}+2bh_{01}.
\]

It remains that some
\[
  G_2=\begin{pmatrix}a_2&b_2\\c_2&d_2\end{pmatrix}
  \in\mathcal B_\cF
  \quad\text{satisfies}\quad b_2\ne c_2.
\]
Take \(u=[a_1-d_1,b_1+c_1]^{\mathsf T}\).  Let \(T_1\) be the value obtained
by inserting \(G_1\), and let \(T_2,T_3\) use the two orientations of
\(G_2\).  Then
\[
  \frac{T_2-T_3}{b_2-c_2}=h_{01}-h_{10},
\]
and therefore
\[
  T_1-d_1S+
  \frac{c_1}{b_2-c_2}(T_2-T_3)
  =(a_1-d_1)h_{00}+(b_1+c_1)h_{01}.
\]
In every case the first entry of \(u\) is nonzero, so
\(\DeltaZero^{\mathsf T}u\ne0\).

Now write the unary column vector \(g=[a,b]^{\mathsf T}\).  If
\(a^2+b^2=0\), then projectively
\(g\doteq[1,i]^{\mathsf T}\) or \(g\doteq[1,-i]^{\mathsf T}\).  In the first
case take \(u=[1,-i]^{\mathsf T}\), and in the second take
\(u=[1,i]^{\mathsf T}\).  Under the ordinary-to-disequality-edge
\(K^{-1}\)-transformation the pair is, up to
a nonzero scalar and input order,
\(\DeltaZero\otimes\DeltaOne\).  The reduction follows from
\cref{lem:universal-directed-pair}, and \(g^{\mathsf T}u\ne0\).

If \(a^2+b^2\ne0\), choose the scalar \(r\in\AlgNums\) with
\(r^2=a^2+b^2\) and define the matrix
\[
  Q=\frac1r\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&-a\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Then \(Q^{\mathsf T}Q=I\) and \(Qg=r\DeltaZero\).  Apply the point-mass
case to \(Q\cF\), obtaining \(u'=[s,t]^{\mathsf T}\) with \(s\ne0\), and
define the unary vector \(u=Q^{-1}u'\).  Because \(Q^{\mathsf T}Q=I\), the
holographic identity leaves every ordinary equality edge unchanged and pulls
the reduction back to \(\cF\), while
\[
  g^{\mathsf T}u=(Qg)^{\mathsf T}u'=rs\ne0.\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}
