\begin{corollary}[Higher equality exposes unaries]
\label{cor:higher-equality-unary}
Let \(d\ge4\) and let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set.
In ordinary Holant, the reduction
\(\Holant(\cF\cup\{\Eq_d\})\leT\Holant(\cF)\) implies
\[
 \Holant(\cF\cup\{\DeltaZero,\DeltaOne\})\leT\Holant(\cF).
\]
In the disequality-edge model, the reduction
\(\KHolant(\cF\cup\{\Eq_d\})\leT\KHolant(\cF)\) implies
\[
 \KHolant(\cF\cup\{\DeltaZero,\DeltaOne\})\leT\KHolant(\cF).
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
First work with ordinary equality edges and add the accessible \(\Eq_d\).
For \(j\in\{0,1\}\), apply \cref{lem:unary-companion} to
\({\mathbf{e}}_j=\delta_j\), obtaining \(u_j\) with \(u_j(j)\ne0\) and the
one-occurrence carrier \(v_j={\mathbf{e}}_j\otimes u_j\).  Attach the \({\mathbf{e}}_j\)-input of
that carrier to one input of \(\Eq_d\).  Up to input order the output is
\[
  v_j\otimes {\mathbf{e}}_j^{\otimes(d-2)}.
\]
Closing the carrier through equality has value \(u_j(j)\ne0\), so
\cref{lem:single-occurrence-replication} gives access to
\({\mathbf{e}}_j^{\otimes(d-2)}\).  Passing temporarily to \(K\)-coordinates, the unary
\(K^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_j\) has two nonzero entries. Hence this tensor power is not in the
common-one-sided exception of \cref{thm:external-factor}.  Factor extraction
therefore exposes \({\mathbf{e}}_j\) after transforming back.

Now work in \(\KHolant\) and use the carrier \(\Delta\).  Attach its two factors,
through \(X\)-edges and in the two orientations, to two copies of
\(\Eq_d\).  The output is
\[
  \Delta^{\otimes(d-1)}
  =\Delta\otimes\Delta^{\otimes(d-2)}
\]
up to input order.  The \(X\)-contraction of \(\Delta\) has value one, so
\cref{lem:universal-directed-pair,lem:single-occurrence-replication} give
access to \(\Delta^{\otimes(d-2)}\).  The available \(\Eq_d\) contains both
endpoint strings \(\zero^d,\one^d\), of doubled charges \(-d,+d\), and hence
excludes the common-one-sided exception. Applying
\cref{thm:external-factor} exposes \(\DeltaZero\) and \(\DeltaOne\).  Finally
compose with the assumed reduction that removes \(\Eq_d\).
\end{proof}

Either point mass is a nonzero unary signature, so
\cref{lem:common-unary-exit} determines the complexity of the original
problem. Thus a reduction obtaining \(\Eq_d\), for \(d\ge4\), ends the
current structural case.

\subsection{Equality and generalized-equality anchors}
\label{proofsubsec:equality-anchor}

We now apply the higher-equality reduction after a common change of
basis, and then adapt the carrier construction to arbitrary nonzero
endpoint weights. The first application requires the same basis change to turn
the edge into equality and the quaternary signature into a multiple of
four-ary equality. We call this configuration an equality anchor.

\begin{definition}[Equality anchor]
\label{def:equality-anchor}
A signature set \(\cF\) has an \emph{equality anchor} if it directly
realizes an ordered quaternary signature \(g\) for which there exist a matrix
\(U\in\GL_2(\AlgNums)\) and nonzero algebraic scalars
\(\kappa,\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\) satisfying
\[
  (U^{-1})^{\mathsf T}XU^{-1}=\kappa\,(\Eq_2),
  \qquad
  U^{\otimes4}g=\lambda\,(\Eq_4).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Dichotomy from a transformed quaternary equality]
\label{lem:equality-anchor}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set with an equality anchor
\(g\), and fix witnesses \(U,\kappa,\lambda\) as in
\cref{def:equality-anchor}.  Then either \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is
\(\SharpP\)-hard or \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Apply the disequality-edge holographic identity with \(M=U^{-1}\) to the whole
problem.  It sends
each right signature \(f\) to \(Uf\) and the disequality edge to
\((U^{-1})^{\mathsf T}XU^{-1}=\kappa(\Eq_2)\). For a queried instance
\(\Omega\), the edge factor \(\kappa^{|E_{\rm int}(\Omega)|}\) is known.
Thus the direct anchor becomes
\(Ug=\lambda(\Eq_4)\), so \(\Eq_4\) is accessible in the ordinary-Holant
problem over \(U\cF\).  By
\cref{cor:higher-equality-unary}, a nonzero unary \(u\) is accessible there.
Pulling the reduction back exposes the nonzero unary \(U^{-1}u\) over
\(\cF\).  The conclusion is now \cref{lem:common-unary-exit}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Dichotomy from a generalized equality]
\label{lem:pure-ge}
Suppose a retained finite algebraic signature set \(\cF\) directly realizes
the arity-\(q\) generalized-equality signature
\[
  G_q:=c_0\delta_{\zero^q}+c_1\delta_{\one^q},
  \qquad c_0,c_1\in\AlgNums^\times,\qquad q\ge4.
\]
Then either \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard or
\(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Direct realizability gives
\(\KHolant(\cF\cup\{G_q\})\leT\KHolant(\cF)\).
We use this extension in the construction below.
Use the ordered carrier \(\Delta\) from
\cref{subsec:single-occurrence-carriers}.  Attach its
two factors through \(X\)-edges, in opposite orientations, to one input
of each of two copies of \(G_q\).  The two copies are forced onto opposite
endpoint strings, and the external tensor is
\[
  c_0c_1\,\Delta^{\otimes(q-1)}
  =
  c_0c_1\,\Delta\otimes\Delta^{\otimes(q-2)}
\]
up to input order.  The \(X\)-contraction of the carrier has value one.
Thus \cref{lem:universal-directed-pair,lem:single-occurrence-replication}
give access to \(\Delta^{\otimes(q-2)}\).  Since \(G_q\) contains
both endpoint strings, of doubled charges \(-q,+q\),
\cref{thm:external-factor} has no common-one-sided
exception and exposes \(\DeltaZero\) and \(\DeltaOne\).  Apply
\cref{lem:common-unary-exit} over \(\cF\cup\{G_q\}\).  A hard conclusion composes
back to \(\cF\), while
\(\Tract(K(\cF\cup\{G_q\}))\) implies \(\Tract(K\cF)\) by
\cref{lem:downward-inheritance}.
\end{proof}
\section{Eight-Vertex Signatures with Nonzero Endpoints}
\label{sec:p1-eight-vertex}

The eight-vertex dichotomy classifies the problem with a single
quaternary signature. When that signature is realized over a larger set,
its hard case transfers immediately, but its tractable cases do not
classify the other signatures. We analyze those product, affine, and
local-affine cases using the unary and equality reductions of
\cref{sec:p1-shared-terminals}. They either classify the whole retained
set or factor the original quaternary into weighted equalities. Its
irreducibility in \cref{thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy} rules out
this last outcome. The group analysis also calls this reduction on
possibly reducible quaternary signatures and can retain their factors.

\begin{theorem}[Quaternary eight-vertex boundary
{\cite[arXiv version~2, Theorem~3.1]{CaiFu2023}}]
\label{thm:external-eight-vertex}
Let \(M\) be an algebraic quaternary signature satisfying
\[
 \supp(M)\subseteq\{{\mathbf{x}}\in\{0,1\}^4:\wt({\mathbf{x}})=2\}\cup\{0000,1111\},\qquad
 M(0000)M(1111)\ne0.
\]
Then \(\Holant(X\mid M)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard unless there exist
\(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP,\cL\}\) and \(U\in\GL_2(\AlgNums)\) such that
\[
  U^{\mathsf T}XU\in\cC,\qquad U^{-1}M\in\cC.
\]
Every exception is in \(\FP\).  The source permits \(U\) over \(\mathbb C\).
\Cref{lem:algebraic-transform-witness} makes it algebraic.
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}[Endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature]
\label{def:p1-endpoint-eight-vertex}
An \emph{endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature} is a quaternary
signature \(q\) satisfying
\[
 q({\mathbf{x}})=0\quad\text{when \(\wt({\mathbf{x}})\) is odd},
 \qquad q(0000)q(1111)\ne0.
\]
Thus its support is contained in the eight even-parity strings, while both
endpoint values are nonzero.
\end{definition}
We use the two entries \(q(0000),q(1111)\), the six weight-two entries, and
the three flattenings with row--column splits
\(12\mid34\), \(13\mid24\), and \(14\mid23\), respectively. For example,
\(12\mid34\) uses \((x_1,x_2)\) as row index and \((x_3,x_4)\) as column
index.  We list their ranks in this order.
A complementary pair of two-element input blocks is \(J,J^c\subseteq[4]\) with
\(|J|=2\).

We first handle product transformability and reduce local-affine
transformability to affine transformability. The affine case then uses
the finite interfaces in
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-normal-form,thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-gadget-exits,thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-odd}.
For an irreducible quaternary, the continuing factorization is impossible,
giving \cref{thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy}.

Throughout this section, weighted equality and nonsingularity have the
meaning fixed in \cref{subsec:holant-definitions}.  For a signature class
\(\cC\), the statement that one signature \(M\) is \(\cC\)-transformable
uses the singleton convention: the set \(\{M\}\) and the transformed disequality edge have one common transformability witness, as in
\cref{def:common-transformability}.

\subsection{Product and local-affine exceptions}

For a product-transformable signature, we distinguish the forms that
yield an equality reduction from the form that splits into two binaries.

\begin{lemma}[Product-transformable eight-vertex signatures]
\label{lem:p1-eight-vertex-product-transform}\leavevmode\newline
Let \(M\) be an endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature.  If \(M\) is
\(\cP\)-transformable in the sense of
\cref{thm:external-eight-vertex}, then exactly one of the following
structural outcomes applies (up to a permutation of inputs):
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(M\) is a generalized equality;
\item \(M\) is a tensor product of two nonsingular weighted equalities;
\item \(M\) has full even-parity support, has the dense form
      \cref{eq:p1-eight-vertex-product-dense-form}, and every retained set
      directly realizing \(M\) also directly realizes an equality anchor.
\end{enumerate}
In particular, every
\(\cP\)-transformable endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature has support size \(2\), \(4\), or
\(8\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose an algebraic witness matrix
\(T=(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{smallmatrix})\) by
\cref{lem:algebraic-transform-witness}, and define the transformed signature
\(H=(T^{-1})^{\otimes4}M\in\cP\).  The transformed edge is
\[
 T^{\mathsf T}XT=
 \begin{pmatrix}2ac&ad+bc\\ad+bc&2bd\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Because this transformed edge is nonsingular and belongs to \(\cP\), its
support is either equality or disequality.  Disequality support makes
\(T\) monomial.  Equality support gives \(ad+bc=0\). Define the nonzero
scalar ratio \(\lambda:=b/a\).  Then
\[
 T=\diag(a,c)\begin{pmatrix}1&\lambda\\1&-\lambda\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad ac\lambda\ne0.
\]

If \(H\) is irreducible, product type makes it a CP signature
for some \({\mathbf{s}}\in\Ftwo^4\), with nonzero coefficients
\(A,B\in\AlgNums^\times\):
\[
 H=A\delta_{\mathbf{s}}+B\delta_{\bar {\mathbf{s}}},\qquad AB\ne0.
\]
In the monomial case the two nonzero endpoint values force
\(\{{\mathbf{s}},\bar {\mathbf{s}}\}=\{\zero^4,\one^4\}\), giving outcome~1.  Otherwise, for
\(k=\wt({\mathbf{s}})\) and \(w=\wt({\mathbf{x}})\),
\[
 M({\mathbf{x}})=a^{4-w}c^w(-1)^{{\mathbf{s}}\cdot {\mathbf{x}}}
 \bigl(A\lambda^k+B\lambda^{4-k}(-1)^w\bigr).
\]
The odd-weight zeros are equivalent to \(B/A=\lambda^{2k-4}\).  Hence all
even entries are nonzero.  Define the nonzero overall scale
\(\rho:=2A\lambda^k a^4\) and weight-ratio scalar \(r:=c/a\). The entries
then have the form
\begin{equation}
 M({\mathbf{x}})=\rho r^{\wt({\mathbf{x}})}(-1)^{{\mathbf{s}}\cdot {\mathbf{x}}}
 \one[\wt({\mathbf{x}})\text{ is even}],\qquad \rho r\ne0.
 \label{eq:p1-eight-vertex-product-dense-form}
\end{equation}

For this dense case choose nonzero algebraic scalars
\(q_0,q_1\in\AlgNums^\times\) satisfying the first two equations below,
define the diagonal matrix \(Q:=\diag(q_0,q_1)\), and define the
normalization matrix \(R:=QT^{-1}\):
\[
 q_0^2=2ac,\qquad q_1/q_0=i\lambda,\qquad R=QT^{-1}.
\]
Then, since
\(\lambda^{2k-4}(i\lambda)^{4-2k}=(-1)^k\), there is a nonzero scalar
\(\eta\in\AlgNums^\times\) such that
\begin{equation}
 (R^{-1})^{\mathsf T}XR^{-1}=I,\qquad
 R^{\otimes4}M=\eta\bigl(\delta_{\mathbf{s}}+(-1)^k\delta_{\bar {\mathbf{s}}}\bigr).
 \label{eq:p1-eight-vertex-product-anchor-normal-form}
\end{equation}
Define the signature
\(F_{\mathbf{s}}=\delta_{\mathbf{s}}+(-1)^k\delta_{\bar {\mathbf{s}}}\).  In these coordinates the
disequality edge is equality by \cref{eq:p1-eight-vertex-product-anchor-normal-form}.  A nonzero
multiple of \(\Eq_4\) uses at most two copies of \(F_{\mathbf{s}}\): one for
\(k=0,4\). For \(k=2\), expose the two
\(s=0\) inputs of the first and the two \(s=1\) inputs of the second, pairing
the remaining inputs. For \(k=1\), expose the three majority-zero inputs of
the first and the minority-one input of the second, join the first's
minority input to one majority input of the second, and equality-close the
second's last two majority inputs.  The construction for \(k=3\) complements all bits.
In each two-copy construction,
an all-equal external string has exactly one compatible internal assignment,
while every other external string has zero contribution.  The resulting
signature is \(\Eq_4\) for \(k=2\) and \(-(\Eq_4)\) for \(k=1,3\).
Pulling it back through \(R\) gives a
direct gadget \(g\) over \(M\) with \(R^{\otimes4}g=\lambda'(\Eq_4)\),
\(\lambda'\ne0\). Together with \cref{eq:p1-eight-vertex-product-anchor-normal-form}
this is outcome~3.

If \(H\) is not irreducible, neither is \(M\).  Its nonzero endpoints make
every factor nonzero at both endpoints.  Even parity excludes an odd-sized
block, so the only proper tensor-factor partition is
\([4]=S\sqcup S^c\) with \(|S|=|S^c|=2\), where
\(S^c=[4]\setminus S\). The odd-weight zeros then force both binary factors
to be diagonal.  Thus they are nonsingular weighted equalities and
\[
 M(\zero^4)M(\one^4)=M(\one_S)M(\one_{S^c}).
\]
This is outcome~2 and completes the classification.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Reductions from product-transformable eight-vertex signatures]
\label{cor:p1-eight-vertex-product-operational}
Let \(\cF\) be a retained, factor-closed finite algebraic signature
set that directly realizes a product-transformable endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature
\(M\).  Then at least one of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\roman*)}]
\item \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard;
\item \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds;
\item both factors in the two-binary factorization of \(M\), including
      a nonsingular weighted-equality factor, can be added to \(\cF\)
      by the factor-closure reduction. The extension is finite, retained,
      and factor-closed, and its \(\KHolant\) problem reduces to
      \(\KHolant(\cF)\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{lem:p1-eight-vertex-product-transform}.  Its generalized-equality
form is closed by \cref{lem:pure-ge}, and its dense form is closed by
\cref{lem:equality-anchor}.  These give alternatives~(i)--(ii), with
hardness composing to \(\cF\) and tractability descending to
\(\cF\) by \cref{lem:downward-inheritance}.

In the remaining form, add the directly realized \(M\) and retain the
complete factor batch from its decomposition across two pairs of inputs.  The
endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature is nonzero on both endpoint
strings \(\zero^4\) and \(\one^4\), whose doubled charges are \(-4\) and
\(+4\), respectively, so the common-one-sided exception in
\cref{lem:factor-saturation} is impossible.  The resulting finite
factor-closed signature set reduces to \(\cF\) and
contains both nonsingular weighted-equality factors.  This is
alternative~(iii).
\end{proof}

It remains to treat the affine and local-affine cases. The next lemma
shows that even parity lets us replace a local-affine witness by an
affine witness, so one affine analysis will cover both.

\begin{lemma}[Local-affine reduction to affine]
\label{lem:p1-eight-vertex-local-affine-collapse}
Let \(M\) be a nonzero quaternary even-parity signature.  If one matrix
\(T\in\GL_2(\mathbb C)\) satisfies
\[
  T^{\mathsf T}XT\in\cL,
  \qquad (T^{-1})^{\otimes4}M\in\cL,
\]
then there is a matrix \(T_0\in\GL_2(\mathbb C)\) with
\[
  T_0^{\mathsf T}XT_0\in\cA,
  \qquad (T_0^{-1})^{\otimes4}M\in\cA.
\]
If \(T\in\GL_2(\AlgNums)\), then \(T_0\) is algebraic.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For any nonempty affine set \(S\subseteq\Ftwo^n\), choose a base point
\({\mathbf{s}}\in S\) and write \(S={\mathbf{s}}+C\), where \(C\leq\Ftwo^n\) is a linear subspace.  The subspace

\[
  C=\{{\mathbf{x}}\oplus {\mathbf{y}}:{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in S\},
\]

is independent of \({\mathbf{s}}\).  It is self-orthogonal if
\({\mathbf{u}}\mathbin{\cdot}{\mathbf{v}}=0\) over \(\Ftwo\) for all \({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\in C\).

Use the phase scalar \(\zeta_8\) from \cref{subsec:standard-classes}.  For an arity-\(n\) signature
\(F\) and \({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}}\in\Ftwo^n\), define the local twist selected by \({\mathbf{z}}\) as
\[
  \Theta_{\mathbf{z}}:=\bigotimes_{j=1}^nT_2^{z_j},
  \qquad
  (\Theta_{\mathbf{z}}F)({\mathbf{y}})
  =\zeta_8^{\langle {\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}}\rangle_{\mathbb Z}}F({\mathbf{y}}),
  \qquad
  \langle {\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{y}}\rangle_{\mathbb Z}:=\sum_{j=1}^n z_jy_j.
\]
By the local-affine criterion
\cite[Definition~3.1 and Theorem~3.2]{CaiLuXia2018RealHolantc}, the
translation space of every support is self-orthogonal, and
\(\Theta_{\mathbf{z}}F\in\cA\) for every \({\mathbf{z}}\in\supp(F)\) when \(F\in\cL\).  Apply
this first to
the symmetric nonsingular binary \(E=T^{\mathsf T}XT\).  Self-orthogonality
rules
out full support, and nonsingularity rules out singleton support. Hence
\(\supp(E)\) is equality or disequality.  Disequality support would give
\[
  E=pX\notin\cL,
\]
since \(01\in\supp(E)\) and the twist required by this string in the support has
relative phase \(\zeta_8\notin\mu_4\), so it is not affine.  Thus
\(E=\diag(p,q)\), where \(p,q\in\AlgNums^\times\) are its nonzero diagonal
entries (their algebraicity follows from \(E\in\cL\)). The \(00\)-twist is
the identity, so
\(q/p\in\mu_4\).  Writing
\(T=(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{smallmatrix})\), the off-diagonal
equation yields \(q/p=-(b/a)^2\).  Hence, with the scalar parameters
\(\rho:=b/a\in\mu_8\) and \(t:=c/a\in\mathbb C^\times\), using the diagonal family defined in
\cref{subsec:holant-definitions},
\[
  T=aD(t)PD(\rho),
  \quad at\ne0,\quad \rho^8=1.
\]
Remove the right factor \(D(\rho)\) by defining \(T_0:=aD(t)P\).
To prove that this is an affine witness, we examine the resulting
signature through the sum
\[
  W_t({\mathbf{y}})=
  \sum_{\substack{{\mathbf{x}}\in\Ftwo^4\\\wt({\mathbf{x}})\equiv0\pmod 2}}
  t^{-\wt({\mathbf{x}})}M({\mathbf{x}})(-1)^{{\mathbf{x}}\cdot {\mathbf{y}}}.
\]
Let \(N:=(T^{-1})^{\otimes4}M\) denote the transformed local-affine
signature.  Since \(P^{-1}=P/2\), define
\[
  \kappa_0:=(2a)^{-4}\ne0,
  \qquad
  W'_t:=\kappa_0W_t.
\]
\begin{equation}
  (T^{-1})^{\otimes4}M({\mathbf{y}})=\rho^{-\wt({\mathbf{y}})}W'_t({\mathbf{y}}),
  \qquad
  (T_0^{-1})^{\otimes4}M({\mathbf{y}})=W'_t({\mathbf{y}}),
  \label{eq:p1-eight-vertex-local-affine-delete}
\end{equation}
Since \(N\in\cL\) and \(\rho\in\mu_8\),
\(W'_t({\mathbf{y}})=\rho^{\wt({\mathbf{y}})}N({\mathbf{y}})\) is algebraic for every \({\mathbf{y}}\).
Moreover,
\[
  T_0^{\mathsf T}XT_0
  =2a^2t\diag(1,-1)
  =p\diag(1,-1)\in\cA,
\]
and parity gives \(W'_t(\bar {\mathbf{y}})=W'_t({\mathbf{y}})\).

The support of \(N\), which is also the support of
\(W'_t\), is an affine coset \({\mathbf{s}}+C\).  Self-orthogonality and the
identity \(W'_t(\bar {\mathbf{y}})=W'_t({\mathbf{y}})\), which makes this support
closed under bitwise complement, give
\(\one^4\in C\) and \(1\le\dim C\le2\).  For dimension one, the support is
a complementary pair on which \(W'_t\) has equal values, so
\(W'_t\in\cA\).
For dimension two, after permuting inputs,
\[
  C=\Span_{\Ftwo}\{\one^4,{\mathbf{w}}\},
  \qquad \wt({\mathbf{w}})=2.
\]
For \(u,v\in\Ftwo\), index the four strings in the support by
\({\mathbf{x}}_{uv}:={\mathbf{s}}\oplus u\one^4\oplus v{\mathbf{w}}\).  For a signature \(F\) that is nonzero on
these four strings, define its cross-ratio functional by
\(\operatorname{cr}(F):=F({\mathbf{x}}_{00})F({\mathbf{x}}_{11})/
(F({\mathbf{x}}_{10})F({\mathbf{x}}_{01}))\).  Complement symmetry gives
\(\operatorname{cr}(W'_t)=1\), while the nowhere-zero restriction of
any member of \(\cA\) with this support has cross ratio \(\pm1\).  The
quotient \(\Ftwo^4/C\) has exactly three nonzero classes.  Up to a permutation of inputs and \({\mathbf{w}}\leftrightarrow\bar {\mathbf{w}}\), each of the two odd cosets has
representative \(({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{w}})=(0001,0011)\), and the unique even coset has
representative \(({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{w}})=(0101,0011)\).  Thus the displayed cases exhaust
all three nonzero quotient classes.  In both representatives
the \(\rho^{-\wt({\mathbf{y}})}\) factor has cross ratio one and the direct four-point
check gives
\[
 \langle {\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{x}}_{00}\rangle_{\mathbb Z}
 +\langle {\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{x}}_{11}\rangle_{\mathbb Z}
 -\langle {\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{x}}_{10}\rangle_{\mathbb Z}
 -\langle {\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{x}}_{01}\rangle_{\mathbb Z}=2.
\]
The local twist selected by \({\mathbf{s}}\) is \(N_{\mathbf{s}}:=\Theta_{\mathbf{s}}N\), so
\(N_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{y}})=\zeta_8^{\langle {\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{y}}\rangle_{\mathbb Z}}N({\mathbf{y}})\).  It would therefore
have cross ratio \(\zeta_8^2=i\), contradicting \(N_{\mathbf{s}}\in\cA\).  Hence
\({\mathbf{s}}+C=C\).

If the complement-symmetric values of \(W'_t\) are \(A\) on the endpoints
and \(B\) on the central pair, then \(N\) has values
\((A,\rho^{-4}A,\rho^{-2}B,\rho^{-2}B)\).  Since \(0\in\supp(N)\), its
selected local twist is the identity, so \(N\in\cA\) and
\(\rho^{-2}B/A\in\mu_4\).  As \(\rho^2\in\mu_4\), also \(B/A\in\mu_4\),
whence \(W'_t\in\cA\).  Now
\cref{eq:p1-eight-vertex-local-affine-delete} proves the
claim.  If the original witness is algebraic, so are \(t\) and \(T_0\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{The finite affine quaternary certificate}
\label{proofsubsec:affine-certificate}

The affine witness may initially use an arbitrary invertible matrix.
We first reduce it to a diagonal change of basis, which preserves the
disequality edge up to scalar. The proof derives a finite list of matrix
forms and applies the classification in
Appendix~\ref{appsec:cert-eight-vertex}, checked by
\path{certificates/verify_affine_q4.py}. The corollary then uses the
resulting signatures in reductions for the whole retained set.

\begin{proposition}[Affine quaternary certificate]
\label{prop:p1-affine-eight-vertex-certificate}
Let \(M\) be an algebraic affine-transformable
endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature.  Then \(M\) admits an
algebraic invertible diagonal matrix
\(D_{\mathrm{aff}}=\diag(d_0,d_1)\), with
\(d_0,d_1\in\AlgNums^\times\), such that
\[
  (D_{\mathrm{aff}}^{-1})^{\otimes4}M\in\cA,
  \qquad D_{\mathrm{aff}}^{\mathsf T}XD_{\mathrm{aff}}\doteq X.
\]
For some \(J\subseteq[4]\) with \(|J|=2\), write
\(J^c:=[4]\setminus J\).  In these coordinates its support is exactly one
of
\[
  \{\zero^4,\one^4\},
  \qquad
  \{\zero^4,\one^4,\one_J,\one_{J^c}\},
  \qquad
  \{{\mathbf{x}}:\wt({\mathbf{x}})\text{ is even}\}.
\]
The first support is a generalized equality.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{lem:algebraic-transform-witness}, choose an algebraic affine change-of-basis matrix
\(U=(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{smallmatrix})\), and define the
transformed binary edge \(E:=U^{\mathsf T}XU\).  A nonsingular symmetric affine binary has
disequality support, equality support, or full support.  In the first
case,
\[
  U=D_0K_0,\qquad K_0\in\{I,X\},
\]
for an invertible diagonal \(D_0\).
In the second, \(ad+bc=0\), so
\[
 U=D_0\begin{pmatrix}1&r\\1&-r\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad r\in\mu_8,
\]
for an invertible diagonal \(D_0\).  In the full-support case, after
normalizing its \(00\)-entry, \(E\) has the affine form
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}1&u\\u&-u^2\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad u\in\mu_4.
\]
Writing \(U=D_0(\begin{smallmatrix}1&r\\1&s\end{smallmatrix})\) gives
\(r+s=2u\) and \(rs=-u^2\). Hence
\(\{r,s\}=\{u(1+\sqrt2),u(1-\sqrt2)\}\).
Denote the resulting eighteen-element set of representative matrices by
\(\mathscr K_{\mathrm{aff}}\). Its explicit list appears in
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-normal-form}.  These three calculations give precisely
\[
 U=D_0K_0,\qquad K_0\in\mathscr K_{\mathrm{aff}},
\]
which proves completeness of the list.

Now define the affine signature \(f=(U^{-1})^{\otimes4}M\in\cA\).  Since
\(M\) is an endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature and \(D_0\)
is diagonal, \(K_0^{\otimes4}f\) satisfies the hypotheses of
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-normal-form}.  The certificate is projective, so the
normalization factor for \(f\) can be absorbed into a global scalar.
The same classification theorem therefore yields
\[
  \gamma,t\in\AlgNums^\times
\]
and a normalized affine signature \(h\in\cA\), with
\(h(\zero^4)=1\), such that
\[
 K_0^{\otimes4}f
 =\gamma D(t)^{\otimes4}h.
\]
Moreover, \(\supp(h)\) is one of the three supports displayed above.
Consequently the
single algebraic diagonal matrix \(D_\star=D_0D(t)\) satisfies
\((D_\star^{-1})^{\otimes4}M\doteq h\), and
\(D_\star^{\mathsf T}XD_\star\doteq X\).  Taking
\(D_{\mathrm{aff}}=D_\star\) proves the asserted diagonal
normalization.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Reductions from affine quaternaries]
\label{cor:p1-affine-eight-vertex-operational}
Let \(\cF\) be a retained, factor-closed finite algebraic signature
set that directly realizes an affine-transformable endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature
\(M\).
Then at least one of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\roman*)}]
\item \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard;
\item \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds;
\item the complete factor batch from the gadget construction can be
      added to \(\cF\) by reduction, giving a finite retained factor-closed
      extension whose \(\KHolant\) problem reduces to \(\KHolant(\cF)\). The original
      signature \(M\) has, up to a permutation of inputs and a nonzero
      scalar, a genuine factorization across two pairs of inputs.
      One of its proper binary factors is a nonsingular weighted equality
      \(E:=\diag(p,q)\), where \(p,q\in\AlgNums^\times\), retained in
      this extension through the factor-closure reduction.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Choose \(D_\star=D_{\mathrm{aff}}\) from
\cref{prop:p1-affine-eight-vertex-certificate}.  Apply the global change
\(D_\star^{-1}\), and normalize the resulting quaternary to an affine
signature \(h\) with \(h(\zero^4)=1\).  Thus
\[
 M\doteq D_\star^{\otimes4}h.
\]
Keep every direct output over the current signature set, and add proper
tensor factors only through factor closure.  Record these
constructions and the chosen normal form in the normalization data of
\cref{lem:normalization-ledger}. The normalized signature \(h\) has
nonzero entries at both endpoints, so every resulting set is two-sided and
\cref{lem:factor-saturation} applies.

The normal form has three possible supports. The support-two form is a
generalized equality. For support four, the distinction between an
equality construction and a factorization is determined by the following
coefficient matrix. Let \(J\sqcup J^c=[4]\) be the two pairs of inputs.  For \(u,v\in\Ftwo\), put
\({\mathbf x}_{uv}:=u\one_J\oplus v\one_{J^c}\), and define the logical coefficient matrix by
\[
 F:=\bigl(h({\mathbf x}_{uv})\bigr)_{u,v\in\Ftwo}
   =\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\ C&D\end{pmatrix},\qquad ABCD\ne0.
\]
Here \(u\) is the row bit and \(v\) is the column bit.
For an ordered binary tensor \(E\), write \(E^{(J)}\) for its placement on
the two inputs of \(J\) in their inherited order, and define
\(E^{(J^c)}\) analogously.
If \(AD=-BC\), the two-copy \(X\)-edge gadget in
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-gadget-exits}\textnormal{(ii)} directly realizes a nonzero pure
generalized equality.  If \(AD=BC\), then, on these two blocks,
\[
  h=E_1^{(J)}\otimes E_2^{(J^c)}
\]
for nonsingular weighted equalities \(E_1,E_2\).  Since \(D_\star\) is
diagonal,
\[
  M\doteq
  (D_\star E_1D_\star^{\mathsf T})^{(J)}\otimes
  (D_\star E_2D_\star^{\mathsf T})^{(J^c)}.
\]
Thus the original endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature itself has a
genuine factorization across two pairs of inputs into nonsingular weighted
equalities.  Add \(M\) and apply factor closure to
obtain both factors by reduction.

On full even-parity support,
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-gadget-exits}\textnormal{(iii)}, together with
\cref{lem:p1-eight-vertex-product-transform}, gives, over the current signature set,
either a directly realized equality anchor, a directly realized nonzero
rank-one binary, or a directly realized product
\[
  cD(r)\otimes D(r),
  \qquad c\in\AlgNums^\times,
  \qquad r\in\mu_4,
\]
where \(D(r)\) is a nonsingular weighted-equality matrix.
In the last case, \cref{lem:factor-saturation} makes \(D(r)\) available
by reduction. We use it to construct an odd-parity signature and continue
the full-support analysis. Choose
the scalar \(s\in\mu_8\) with \(s^2=r^{-1}\), and use \(D(s)\) for the equality normalization.  Then
\[
  (D(s))^{\mathsf T}XD(s)=sX,
  \qquad
  D(s)D(r)(D(s))^{\mathsf T}=I.
\]
Apply this normalization and define
the transformed signature \(h_r=(D(s))^{\otimes4}h\). Attach one input of
\(h_r\) by a \(X\)-edge
to one input of the retained equality \(I\), leaving the other equality input
external.  This is a gadget over the current signature set,
and it complements that input of \(h_r\), producing, up to a nonzero scalar, an odd-parity quaternary \(p\) that is nonzero on all eight
odd-weight strings and is used in
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-odd}.  Because \(s\in\mu_8\), its normalized
nonzero entries are fourth roots of unity.

The twelve disequality-loop and equality-mediated binary gadgets of
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-odd} are therefore realizable at this node.  If a test gives a nonzero rank-one binary or a nonsingular binary of
infinite projective order, the unary reductions below apply. Otherwise
the same theorem classifies the remaining signatures, including the
simultaneous-zero cases of its loop tests. Its exceptional family directly
realizes the certified endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature with
support of cardinality six.  For every other remaining signature, choose a certified
\(\delta\in\mathbb Z_4\), choose \(\tau_\delta\in\mu_8\) with
\(\tau_\delta^2=i^\delta\), and apply the further legal global-normalization
matrix \(D(\tau_\delta)\).  Let \(p_\delta\) denote the certified
phase-shifted quaternary signature with odd-parity support from
\cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-odd}.  Since \(p\) is supported only at
Hamming weights one and three,
\[
  D(\tau_\delta)^{\otimes4}p\doteq p_\delta.
\]
\Cref{thm:cert-interface-affine-q4-odd} now gives, through its disequality self-loop
and two-copy constructions, either a nonzero rank-one binary or a
product-transformable endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex quaternary in the
dense form \cref{eq:p1-eight-vertex-product-dense-form}. The latter directly
yields an equality anchor by
\cref{lem:p1-eight-vertex-product-transform}.

For the eight-vertex signature with support of size six,
\cref{lem:p1-eight-vertex-product-transform} rules out
\(\cP\)-transformability. The diagonal normalization in
\cref{prop:p1-affine-eight-vertex-certificate} preserves support and allows
only sizes \(2,4,8\), ruling out \(\cA\)-transformability.
\Cref{lem:p1-eight-vertex-local-affine-collapse} then rules out
\(\cL\)-transformability, so \cref{thm:external-eight-vertex} gives hardness.

The support-two case and the support-four case \(AD=-BC\) are resolved by
\cref{lem:pure-ge}. Equality anchors are resolved by
\cref{lem:equality-anchor}.  A nonzero rank-one binary exposes a unary by
\cref{lem:binary-interpolation}, and an infinite-order binary first
interpolates such a binary.  In both cases
\cref{lem:common-unary-exit} applies.  An exposed odd-arity signature is
resolved by \cref{thm:external-odd} after the holographic \(K\)-coordinate equivalence.
Every case with full even-parity support above therefore gives hardness or tractability.

The sole continuing case, the support-four form \(AD=BC\), gives the
displayed factorization of the original \(M\) into two nonsingular
weighted-equality factors after undoing the diagonal normalization.
By \cref{lem:normalization-ledger}, hardness reductions compose back
to \(\cF\), tractable conclusions descend to \(K\cF\), and this
continuing state returns to the original coordinates.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Consequences for the Full Signature Set}

We now combine the three transformability cases. The conclusion either
classifies the entire signature set or supplies proper binary factors of
the original quaternary for the later group analysis.

\begin{corollary}[Reductions from eight-vertex signatures]
\label{cor:p1-eight-vertex-reduced-interface}
Let \(\cF\) be a retained, factor-closed finite algebraic
signature set containing a directly realizable endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature \(M\).
Then at least one of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\roman*)}]
\item \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard;
\item \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds;
\item the complete factor batch from a genuine factorization of the
      original \(M\) across two pairs of inputs can be added to
      \(\cF\) by reduction, giving a finite retained factor-closed
      extension whose \(\KHolant\) problem reduces to \(\KHolant(\cF)\). This extension
      contains a proper tensor factor of \(M\) of the form
      \[
        E:=\diag(p,q),\qquad p,q\in\AlgNums^\times.
      \]
\end{enumerate}
In item~(iii), the factor \(E\) is available by reduction from the original \(M\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If all six weight-two entries vanish, the signature is a generalized
equality and is closed by
\cref{lem:pure-ge}.  Otherwise apply the Cai--Fu quaternary boundary
\cref{thm:external-eight-vertex}.  Its hard branch is item~(i).  Its
product-transformable branch is handled by
\cref{cor:p1-eight-vertex-product-operational}.  A local-affine branch first
collapses to affine by \cref{lem:p1-eight-vertex-local-affine-collapse} and then
uses \cref{cor:p1-affine-eight-vertex-operational}. An affine branch uses that corollary directly.

These two corollaries give the three stated outcomes.
Composing the reductions gives hardness for \(\cF\), and restricting the
tractability criterion gives the same conclusion for \(K\cF\).  Apply
\cref{lem:normalization-ledger} to return to the original basis.
The sole continuing output is the retained nonsingular weighted-equality
factor in item~(iii).
\end{proof}

The continuing outcome factors the original quaternary signature, so the
irreducibility hypothesis in \cref{thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy} rules it out.

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy}]
Choose an endpoint-nondegenerate irreducible quaternary
\(q\in\cF\).  Apply \cref{subsec:zero-nullary}, retaining the name \(\cF\).
The signature \(q\) survives, and the tractability criterion is unchanged.
By the \hyperlink{eq:retract-K-coordinates}{coordinate identity in
Section~\ref{subsec:tract-predicate}}, the criterion in the theorem is
\(\ReTract(K\cF)\) in ordinary-Holant coordinates.
Since \(q\) is two-sided, \cref{lem:tract-X-anchor} gives
\[
 \Tract(K\cF)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\ReTract(K\cF).
\]
If \(\ReTract(K\cF)\) holds, then
\cref{thm:predicate-algorithms,eq:K-equivalence} gives
\(\KHolant(\cF)\in\FP\), and the root evaluator restores the deleted
nullary scalars. Now suppose that \(\ReTract(K\cF)\) fails, so
\(\Tract(K\cF)\) fails as well. The two-sided signature \(q\)
excludes the common-one-sided exception. By
\cref{lem:factor-saturation}, adding \(\Pi(\cF)\) gives a finite
factor-closed extension equivalent to the original problem. By
\cref{lem:downward-inheritance}, its tractability criterion also fails.
Continue to denote this extension by \(\cF\). It still contains the
original irreducible signature \(q\).

Apply \cref{cor:p1-eight-vertex-reduced-interface} to \(q\) over
\(\cF\).  Its tractable case is excluded by the preceding paragraph.
Its continuing outcome gives a genuine \(2+2\) factorization of the original
\(q\), contradicting irreducibility. Hence \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is
\(\SharpP\)-hard.
The factor-closure equivalence and preprocessing transfer this hardness
to the original input problem. Finally, the same predicate equivalence and
\cref{thm:predicate-algorithms} give decidability.
\end{proof}
\section{Equality-Accessible Matching Decks}
\label{sec:weighted-equality}
\label{sec:p1-binary-phase}

This section organizes the structural proof of the binary-disequality
dichotomy. By the equivalence below, we work with disequality edges and
the supplied binary equality \(I=(\Eq_2)\). The central task is to combine
the classification of binary signatures with reductions of higher-arity
irreducible signatures. Binary transfers determine the available
contractions, while factors of their outputs can supply new transfers
and change the group under analysis.

After the unary reductions have settled the singular and infinite-order
binary cases, the remaining transfers form a finite group. The supplied
\(I\) gives the transfer class \([X]\), and reversal makes \([Z]\)
normalize the group. These two distinguished classes restrict its form.
We then choose a minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature and
contract it using the available binaries. Retaining the outputs and their
factors can settle the complexity, lower the minimum arity, or
enlarge the complete transfer group. When the remaining process stabilizes, the
contraction outputs are products of binaries on perfect matchings.
This is the structure analyzed in the four group branches.

The progress mechanism gives those branches a common task: determine the
complexity of the current set or produce a reduction that advances the
structural analysis. A fixed number field bounds strict group enlargement,
and finite processing controls the steps that leave both the group and
minimum arity unchanged. A further induction treats the unresolved
irreducible occurrences in the whole set. These ingredients connect the
local constructions in \cref{sec:normalized-dihedral,sec:proper-deck,sec:v4-deck,sec:platonic-decks}
to the final dichotomy proof in \cref{sec:p1-synthesis}.

Throughout, the supplied \(I\) stays in the original coordinates.
The eight-vertex reductions return to these coordinates, and any additional
weighted equality is retained as a binary signature. Continuing changes
of basis preserve both \(X\) and \(I\) projectively. Other basis changes
are used within reductions that settle the complexity. A \emph{resolved
complexity leaf}, or \emph{resolved leaf}, means hardness,
\(\Tract\) for the entire retained set,
or a common tractable \(K\)-presentation as in
\cref{def:common-K-presentation}.

\begin{lemma}[Equality-edge-model equivalence]
\label{lem:p1-equality-wire-equivalence}
For every signature set \(\cF\),
\begin{equation}
  \KHolant(\cF,I)
  \equivT
  \Holant(\cF,X),
  \label{eq:intro-wire-bridge}
\end{equation}
where the right side uses ordinary equality edges and has \(X\) as an
available binary signature.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Forward, subdivide each \(X\)-edge by an \(X\)-vertex and suppress
degree-two \(I\)-vertices as equality edges.  Conversely, splice the two
incident inputs of the ordinary \(X\)-vertices after first preprocessing the
subgraph induced by those vertices.  Every such component is a path or a
cycle, because \(X\) has arity two.  A path with two incidences at
non-\(X\) vertices collapses to \(I\) or \(X\) according to the parity of
the number of \(X\)-vertices. A cycle with no external incidence contributes
zero for odd length and the scalar \(2\) for even length.  (A self-loop is
the length-one case.)  These are exact identities obtained by summing the
internal equality-edge bits, and they also cover adjacent \(X\)-vertices and
parallel edges.  For each resulting \(X\) kernel use one structural
\(\mathsf E_X\) edge (no additional tensor vertex). For each resulting
\(I\) kernel, and for each ordinary equality edge whose endpoints are not
in an \(X\)-component, use
\[
  \text{input}-X-I-X-\text{input}.
\]
Indeed, \(\sum_{u,v}X(a,u)I(u,v)X(v,b)=I(a,b)\).  Multiply the final
answer by the known product of the even-cycle scalars (or return zero for
an odd cycle).  The preprocessing and gadget replacements are linear-size and
preserve ordered incidences.
\end{proof}

The signatures in \(\cF\) keep their entries under this gadget replacement.
The four tractable cases correspond as well:
\hypertarget{eq:retract-edge-bridge}{}
\[
 \ReTract(\cF\cup\{X\})
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \ReTract_X(\cF\cup\{I\})
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \ReTract(K(\cF\cup\{I\})).
\]
The last equivalence is the \hyperlink{eq:retract-K-coordinates}{coordinate
identity in Section~\ref{subsec:tract-predicate}}. The arity-two case is
unchanged by adjoining either binary. For
\(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP,\cL\}\), nonsingular symmetric binaries in
\(\cC\) are closed under inversion and under \(EBE\).
The latter is \cref{lem:symmetric-binary-pullback}. Inversion preserves
the binary affine and product normal forms, and the local-affine forms
\(a\diag(1,i^k)\) identified in its proof.
For an ordinary-Holant witness \(M\), put
\(E=M^{\mathsf T}M\) and \(B=M^{-1}XM^{-\mathsf T}\).
Then the disequality edge becomes \(M^{\mathsf T}XM=EBE\), and the
supplied \(I\) becomes \(E^{-1}\), both in \(\cC\).
Conversely, for a disequality-edge witness \(U\), put
\(E=U^{\mathsf T}XU\) and \(B=U^{-1}IU^{-\mathsf T}\).
The ordinary edge becomes \(U^{\mathsf T}U=EBE\), and the supplied
\(X\) becomes \(U^{-1}XU^{-\mathsf T}=BEB\), both in \(\cC\).
The same witness transforms every member of \(\cF\) in each direction.

Consequently, the following statement is equivalent to
\cref{thm:p1-deq2-dichotomy} and is the target of the structural analysis
beginning here.  Its proof is completed in \cref{sec:p1-synthesis}.

\begin{theorem}[Equality-accessible \(\KHolant\) dichotomy]
\label{thm:p1-eq2-kholant-dichotomy}
For every finite set \(\cF\) of algebraic complex-valued Boolean signatures
with \(I=(\Eq_2)\in\cF\), the problem \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is polynomial-time
computable when \(\ReTract(K\cF)\) holds and is \(\SharpP\)-hard otherwise.
The tractability criterion is decidable from exact algebraic input.
\end{theorem}

For a set containing \(I\), the transformed set contains
\(Z=K^{\otimes2}I=\diag(1,-1)\).  Thus \cref{lem:tract-X-anchor}
reduces the full criterion \(\Tract\) to the four cases of
\(\ReTract\).

Since \(I(00)=I(11)=1\), the supplied \(I\) excludes the common one-sided support exception, so
\cref{lem:factor-saturation} allows the initial reduction to irreducible
signatures while retaining \(I\). Odd-arity factors are covered by
\cref{thm:external-odd}, and a set of binary factors is covered by the
arity-two case. We therefore begin the remaining analysis with a finite
set \(\cF\) of nonzero even-arity irreducible signatures, containing
\(I\) and at least one signature of arity at least four. Such a set is
factor-closed. Later outputs and their factors are retained as they are
added. The final proof in \cref{sec:p1-synthesis} transfers the resulting
classification to the original set. We first control the binary transfers.

\subsection{Binary transfers and complete groups}
\label{subsec:binary-transfer-control}
Binary chains turn composition into matrix multiplication.  Bounding the
group they generate gives a finite set of contraction kernels for the
matching deck.

For an ordered binary signature \(B\), recall its transfer
\[
  T_B=BX.
\]
Joining \(B\) to another ordered binary signature \(C\) in series through
one \(X\)-edge realizes \(BXC\),
and therefore
\[
  T_{BXC}=T_BT_C.
\]
For a transfer matrix \(T\), define its reversal by the anti-involution
\begin{equation}
  T^\#:=XT^{\mathsf T}X;
  \qquad
  T^\#=(\det T)ZT^{-1}Z\quad\text{if }T\text{ is nonsingular}.
  \label{eq:transfer-reversal}
\end{equation}
Here ``anti-involution'' means explicitly that
\[
  (ST)^\#=T^\#S^\#,
  \qquad
  (T^\#)^\#=T
\]
for all transfer matrices \(S,T\).

\begin{lemma}[Bounded torsion]
\label{lem:bounded-torsion}
Fix a number field \(L\), and let \(G\) be the set of projective
transfers of all directly realizable nonzero nonsingular ordered
binary gadgets over a fixed signature set with entries in \(L\).
If \(G\ne\varnothing\) and every element of \(G\) has finite
projective order, then \(G\) is a finite subgroup of
\(\PGL_2(L)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Define the field degree \(D=[L:\mathbb Q]\).  Let \([T]\in G\) have
projective order
\(r\).  By definition,
\[
  T^r=\eta I
\]
for some \(\eta\in L^\times\), and \(r\) is the least positive
integer with this property.

The minimal polynomial \(m_T\) divides \(x^r-\eta\), which is square-free
because \(\gcd(x^r-\eta,rx^{r-1})=1\) in characteristic zero.
Thus \(T\) is diagonalizable over the algebraic closure.  Choose
an eigenbasis matrix \(V\in\GL_2(\AlgNums)\) and eigenvalues
\(\lambda_1,\lambda_2\in\AlgNums^\times\) such that
\[
  V^{-1}TV=\diag(\lambda_1,\lambda_2).
\]
Define the eigenvalue ratio
\[
  \zeta:=\frac{\lambda_1}{\lambda_2}.
\]
The equality \(T^r=\eta I\) gives \(\zeta^r=1\), and the minimality of
\(r\) shows that \(\zeta\) is a primitive \(r\)-th root of unity.
Moreover,
\[
  \zeta+\zeta^{-1}
  =
  \frac{(\operatorname{tr}T)^2}{\det T}-2
  \in L.
\]
For \(r>2\),
\[
  [\mathbb Q(\zeta+\zeta^{-1}):\mathbb Q]
  =\frac{\varphi(r)}2,
\]
so \(\varphi(r)\le2D\), also valid for \(r=1,2\).  The integers with this
bound form an effectively finite set: each prime power \(p^a\mid r\)
satisfies \(p^{a-1}(p-1)\le2D\), bounding both \(p\) and \(a\).
Consequently all projective orders divide the computable exponent
\[
  e_D=\operatorname{lcm}\{r:\varphi(r)\le2D\}.
\]

Series composition gives product closure.  A positive power of every
element is the identity, so the identity and inverses also belong to
\(G\).  Hence \(G\) is a subgroup.  Put
\[
 \mathfrak{sl}_2(L)
 :=\{A\in\Mat_2(L):\operatorname{tr}A=0\}.
\]
The adjoint representation is
\[
  \operatorname{Ad}:\PGL_2(L)\hookrightarrow
  \GL(\mathfrak{sl}_2(L))\cong\GL_3(L),
  \qquad
  \operatorname{Ad}([T])(A)=TAT^{-1}.
\]
The conjugation homomorphism has trivial kernel: a matrix commuting with all trace-zero matrices
is scalar.  Burnside's finite-exponent theorem states that a subgroup
\(H\le\GL_n(\mathbb C)\) satisfying \(h^e=I\) for one fixed \(e\) and
every \(h\in H\) is finite \cite{Burnside1905}.  Apply it to
\(\operatorname{Ad}(G)\le\GL_3(\mathbb C)\) with \(e=e_D\).
Thus all binary gadgets, despite their unbounded sizes, have only finitely
many projective transfer classes.
\end{proof}

For a finite algebraic retained, factor-closed, two-sided signature set
\(\cF\), call a nonzero directly realizable ordered binary \emph{safe} if it
is nonsingular and its transfer has finite projective order.  Call it
\emph{unsafe} if it is singular or its transfer has infinite projective
order.  Call the set \emph{binary-residual} when every nonzero realizable ordered
binary is safe.  We work in this case. Otherwise
\cref{lem:binary-interpolation,lem:common-unary-exit} resolves the branch.  Series composition and
\cref{lem:bounded-torsion} then give a finite group: take \(L\) to be the
number field generated by the entries of \(\cF\), which also contains the
entries of every direct gadget transfer.
The group is
\hypertarget{eq:complete-safe-group}{}
\begin{equation*}
  G(\cF)
  =
  \{[BX]:B\text{ is a safe ordered binary directly realizable over }\cF\}.
\end{equation*}
The definition uses all directly realizable binaries.  Below we distinguish
cases according to this group for each signature set.  The decision algorithm for the resulting tractability
cases is given by \cref{thm:predicate-algorithms}. It does not
enumerate gadgets of unbounded size.  We choose realizable representatives as follows.

\paragraph{Realizable binary representatives.}
\label{par:binary-representatives}


For each class \(g\) of a finite realized projective group
\(G\le\PGL_2(\AlgNums)\), choose an ordered binary gadget \(B_g\)
with transfer \(T_{B_g}=B_gX\) and \([T_{B_g}]=g\). For
\(1=[I]\), take \(B_1:=\mathsf E_X=X\), so \(T_{B_1}=I\).
The empty network is the nullary unit. Every realizable ordered binary
with transfer class \(g\) has the form \(B=\rho B_g\) for a unique
\(\rho\in\AlgNums^\times\). Retain the multiplication and reversal
scalars for these representatives, with the input orders and reduction
data of \cref{sec:retained-normalization}. This fixes the scalar factors
when projective identities are used in gadgets.

Whenever the proof says that an external input is X-translated, it means
attaching the binary chain in the current set whose transfer class is \([X]\) and
recording its nonzero scalar in the input data.  The displayed chain realizes this action.

\begin{lemma}[Relative completeness test for realizable binaries]
\label{lem:actual-binary-completeness}
Let \(\cF_0\subseteq\cF\) be finite algebraic retained,
factor-closed signature sets with \(I\in\cF_0\), and suppose that
every nonzero binary directly realizable over \(\cF_0\) is safe.  Put
\(H=G(\cF_0)\).  The following exhaustive ordered case distinction applies:
\begin{enumerate}
\item if \(\cF\) directly realizes a nonzero singular ordered binary,
      its nonzero unary factors are available by the factor reduction;
\item otherwise, if \(\cF\) directly realizes a nonsingular ordered
      binary of infinite projective transfer order, a nonzero rank-one
      binary is available by interpolation;
\item otherwise every nonzero binary directly realizable over \(\cF\) is safe, \(H\le G(\cF)\), and
      every nonsingular realizable binary \(B\) over \(\cF\) with
      \([BX]\notin H\) witnesses the strict inclusion \(H<G(\cF)\).
\end{enumerate}
In the third case, if \(G(\cF)=H\), every nonzero realizable binary over
\(\cF\) is projectively one of the fixed representatives \(B_h\),
\(h\in H\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The supplied \(I\) has both charge signs, so both signature sets are two-sided.
A singular binary is handled by
\cref{lem:binary-interpolation,lem:common-unary-exit}, and an infinite-order
binary by \cref{lem:binary-interpolation}.  If neither exists, \(\cF\)
is binary-residual, so \cref{lem:bounded-torsion} defines \(G(\cF)\).
Every gadget over \(\cF_0\) remains a gadget over \(\cF\), hence
\(H\le G(\cF)\).  The remaining assertions follow directly from the
definitions and the fixed representative choice.
\end{proof}

In the first two cases, \cref{lem:common-unary-exit} determines the
complexity after factor extraction. In the third, a new transfer class
gives strict group enlargement, while equality of the groups permits
reuse of the fixed binary representatives.

\begin{remark}[Connection with binary-signature classifications]
The singular, nonsingular infinite-projective-order, and nonsingular
finite-projective-order split is the \(\KHolant\) form of the binary split
used by Cai--Lu--Xia \cite{CaiLuXia2009,CaiLuXia2018RealHolantc}.  The four
ordered Bell signatures
\(\mathcal B=\{B_{0,0},B_{0,1},B_{1,0},B_{1,1}\}\) represent the projective
Bell classes of Shao--Cai \cite{ShaoCai2020}. Beyond the binary classification, we need the
complete transfer group and uniform control of its higher-arity cards.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Finite projective groups and marked matrices.}
The classification of finite projective groups gives the uniform order
bound needed for stabilization. We record its concrete models here and
analyze the distinguished classes \([X]\) and \([Z]\) in
\cref{sec:matching-decks}.

We use the quaternion
matrix basis
\begin{equation}
  Q_0=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad
  Q_1=\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\i&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
  Q_2=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
  Q_3=\begin{pmatrix}i&0\\0&-i\end{pmatrix}.
  \label{eq:quaternion-basis}
\end{equation}
It is a basis of \(\Mat_2(\mathbb C)\). Moreover, \(Q_0=I\),
\(Q_j^2=-I\) for \(1\le j\le3\), and
\(Q_jQ_k=-Q_kQ_j\) for distinct \(j,k\in\{1,2,3\}\).  These identities are
what the adjective \emph{quaternion} records.

For the concrete Platonic models below, \(\mathrm{int}\) records that
\([Z]\) belongs to the tetrahedral group, \(dd\) records that \([X],[Z]\)
are double transpositions in the octahedral group, and \(0\) labels the
base icosahedral display.

\begin{proposition}[Finite subgroups of \(\PGL_2(\mathbb C)\)]
\label{prop:finite-pgl2-classification}
Every finite subgroup of \(\PGL_2(\mathbb C)\) has exactly one of the following
isomorphism types, and subgroups of the same type are projectively conjugate:
\[
  C_m,\qquad D_{2m},\qquad A_4,\qquad S_4,\qquad A_5,
\]
where \(m\ge1\) for \(C_m\), \(m\ge2\) for \(D_{2m}\),
\(|C_m|=m\), and \(|D_{2m}|=2m\).  If \(\omega\) is a primitive
\(m\)-th root of unity, explicit cyclic and dihedral models are
\[
 C_m^{\rm std}
 =
 \left\{
   \left[\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&\omega^k\end{pmatrix}\right]:
   0\le k<m
 \right\},
\]
and
\[
 D_{2m}^{\rm std}
 =
 C_m^{\rm std}
 \cup
 \left\{
   \left[
     X\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&\omega^k\end{pmatrix}
   \right]:
   0\le k<m
 \right\}.
\]
The diagonal classes are the rotations, and the anti-diagonal coset
consists of the reflections.

In the basis \cref{eq:quaternion-basis}, take the tetrahedral model
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \TetInt
 ={}&
 \{[Q_0],[Q_1],[Q_2],[Q_3]\}\\
 &{}\cup
 \{[Q_0+\epsilon_1Q_1+\epsilon_2Q_2+\epsilon_3Q_3]:
       \epsilon_j\in\{1,-1\}\},
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:tetrahedral-domain}
\end{equation}
and the octahedral model
\begin{equation}
 \OctDD
 =
 \TetInt
 \cup
 \{[Q_\mu+\epsilon Q_\nu]:
       0\le\mu<\nu\le3,\ \epsilon\in\{1,-1\}\}.
 \label{eq:octahedral-domain}
\end{equation}
Finally, define the golden-ratio scalar and its reciprocal by
\[
  \phi:=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2},
  \qquad
  \tau:=\phi^{-1},
\]
and define the icosahedral model by
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \IcoZero
 =\TetInt\ \cup\
 \bigl\{[v_0Q_0+v_1Q_1+v_2Q_2+v_3Q_3]:{}&
 (v_0,v_1,v_2,v_3)\\
 &{}=\pi(0,\epsilon_1,\epsilon_2\phi,\epsilon_3\tau),\
 \ \pi\in A_4,\ \epsilon_j\in\{1,-1\}\bigr\},
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:icosahedral-domain}
\end{equation}
where \(A_4\) acts by even coordinate permutations and projective
duplicates are removed.  The additional part of \(\IcoZero\) has
\(48\) projective classes.  Thus
\[
  \TetInt\cong A_4,\qquad
  \OctDD\cong S_4,\qquad
  \IcoZero\cong A_5,
\]
of orders \(12,24,60\), respectively.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The conjugacy classification is classical
\cite[Section~5.1]{Beardon1983}.  The first two displays are the standard
rotation and reflection models.  Direct matrix multiplication verifies
closure, cardinalities, and element-order distributions of the three
remaining displayed sets, identifying them respectively with
\(A_4,S_4,A_5\).
\end{proof}

For the concrete models in \cref{prop:finite-pgl2-classification}, the
finite matrix checks in its proof consist of reducing products modulo
nonzero scalar, testing closure of the displayed projective sets, and
counting their distinct classes and element orders.  The scalar factors
remain separate from the realizable-gadget records above.

We now choose a minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature and retain the factors
of all its dressed contractions.

\subsection{Stable matching decks and restart control}
\label{subsec:safe-matching-cards}

Fix a retained, factor-closed set \(\cF\ni I\) with complete transfer
group \(G=G(\cF)\). Attach available binaries to a minimum-arity
nonbinary irreducible signature and close one pair of inputs. A nonbinary
factor of the output has smaller arity. If only binary factors remain,
they either settle the complexity, enlarge the group, or form a product
on a perfect matching. The \emph{matching deck} collects these contractions,
using the ordered representatives from \cref{par:binary-representatives}.

We first stabilize this deck for the selected signature. We then give
the descent conditions for subsequent constructions in the group branches.
The final proof also uses an outer induction over unresolved irreducible
occurrences to pass from the selected signature to the whole set.

For an even finite input set \(R\), a \emph{perfect matching} is a partition
of \(R\) into two-element subsets.  An \emph{oriented perfect matching}
also chooses an order within each pair, and a matching product places one
ordered binary factor on every oriented pair.

\begin{definition}[Matching deck]
\label{def:matching-deck}
Let \(\cF\ni I\) be factor-closed and let \(G=G(\cF)\).  On a fixed
ordered input set, a \emph{\(G\)-matching signature} of arity \(2m\) is
\begin{equation*}
  c\bigotimes_{e\in M}B_{g_e}^{(e)},
  \qquad
  c\ne0,\quad g_e\in G,
\end{equation*}
where \(M\) is an oriented perfect matching in the preceding sense and
\(B_{g_e}^{(e)}\) places the chosen ordered representative \(B_{g_e}\) on
the oriented edge \(e\).  We allow a simultaneous permutation of inputs. The
scalar \(c\) absorbs all representative scalars, so the notion depends only
on \(G\).

For \(\omega\in\{\mathrm{id},\mathrm{rev}\}\), put
\(B_g^{\mathrm{id}}=B_g\) and
\(B_g^{\mathrm{rev}}=B_g^{\mathsf T}\).  Let \(\delta\) specify a subset of the inputs and, at each selected input, a representative
\(B_g\) and one of these two orientations.  Attach its recorded binary through
one \(X\)-edge and retain the other binary input in the same position.
Write \(f^\delta\) for the resulting realizable dressed signature.
Here the dressing acts on the specified inputs of \(f\), before the pair
contraction defining a deck card below.

For each \(\delta\), choose distinct ordered inputs \((i,j)\), a closing
representative \(B_h\) with orientation \(\omega\), and a residual input order \(\rho\), leaving at least two inputs.  The resulting card is
\[
 \rho\!\left(\partial_{(i,j)}^{XB_h^\omega X}f^\delta\right).
\]
The \emph{matching deck} \(\mathfrak D_G(f)\) contains zero and all such
one-copy proper pair cards.  Thus one copy of \(f\) is used and exactly one
ordered input pair is closed. The binary dressings and closing chain use the
fixed representatives.  Every nonzero card is a direct gadget output.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Deck-stable state]
\label{def:deck-stable-state}
A retained reduction state \((\cF,G,f)\) is \emph{deck-stable} if \(\cF\) is
finite, factor-closed, and contains \(I\); \(f\in\cF\) is a
minimum-arity irreducible nonbinary signature; \(G=G(\cF)\) is the
complete transfer group, which is finite, with fixed safe binary representatives;
and the deck for the resulting set, with all card factors retained, consists of zero
and \(G\)-matching signatures.  Realizability is always relative to \(\cF\).
Added factors are only available by reduction over their predecessor.
\end{definition}

Adding signatures available by reduction gives a Turing-equivalent problem:
the defining reduction removes the added signatures, and inclusion gives
the reverse reduction.  Direct outputs use
\cref{eq:direct-gadget-reduction}. Their factors use
\cref{lem:factor-saturation}.

To prove stabilization, fix the finite family of contractions that can
be processed while the group and minimum arity stay unchanged. We record
this family and its factorization trees as follows. Fix \((A,\cF,f)\) and
\(H:=\overline G(A,\cF)\), the complete transfer group in the original
coordinates. Throughout this stabilization and descent argument, \(H\)
has this meaning. While \(H\) and \(\nu(\cF)\) remain
unchanged, fix one representative of each group class. A one-copy proper
card is specified by the input dressings, the ordered deleted pair,
the closing representative \(B_h\) and its orientation \(\omega\)
(giving the kernel \(XB_h^\omega X\)), and the residual input order.
Let \(\mathscr U(A,f,H)\) consist of zero and the signatures obtained by
pulling these cards back by \(A\) and dividing each nonzero result by its
first nonzero entry, with the residual inputs kept in order.

Each nonzero member of \(\mathscr U(A,f,H)\) indexes one \emph{token} in
the local queue, even if several constructions yield that member. Store
one such construction, its discarded scalar, and the factorization tree of
the selected card from \cref{lem:factor-saturation}. Equal factors at
different positions in the tree remain distinct occurrences.
Write \(\mathscr U^{\mathrm{fac}}(A,f,H)\) for the token set with this
attached factor work, and write
\[
 Q_{\rm done}\subseteq\mathscr U^{\mathrm{fac}}(A,f,H),
 \qquad
 Q=\mathscr U^{\mathrm{fac}}(A,f,H)\setminus Q_{\rm done}
\]
for the completed and unfinished tokens, respectively. Processing a factor
occurrence takes place within its token's tree, so the queue stays within
this finite set while \(H\) and \(\nu\) are fixed. A lower \(\nu\), a
strict enlargement of the pulled-back group, or a complexity conclusion
stops the local construction. The binary equality \(I\) and the original
coordinates remain fixed throughout.

The next lemma either produces a deck-stable state, resolves the complexity
of the current set, or exposes a smaller nonbinary irreducible signature.

\begin{lemma}[Deck stabilization under factor access]
\label{lem:minimum-core-deck}
Let \(\cF_0\) be a retained, factor-closed finite algebraic
signature set containing the supplied equality \(I\), and let
\(f\in\cF_0\) be an even-arity irreducible nonbinary signature of
arity \(n=\nu(\cF_0)\).

Then an established reduction determines the complexity, or
\(\Tract(K\cF_0)\) holds, or one obtains one of the following
retained extensions:
\begin{enumerate}
\item a finite retained factor-closed set
      \(\cF_{<}\supseteq\cF_0\) such that
      \[
        \KHolant(\cF_{<})\equivT\KHolant(\cF_0),
        \qquad \nu(\cF_{<})<n;
      \]
      every nonbinary irreducible signature retained in this successor has even arity;
\item a finite retained factor-closed set
      \(\cF_\star\supseteq\cF_0\) such that
\begin{equation*}
  \KHolant(\cF_\star)
  \equivT
  \KHolant(\cF_0),
\end{equation*}
and, for \(G_\star=G(\cF_\star)\), every member of
\(\mathfrak D_{G_\star}(f)\) is zero or a
\(G_\star\)-matching signature.
\end{enumerate}

The stable successor retains the chosen representatives, the regenerated
complete deck, and every card factor.  Such factors are available by reduction over,
but need not be directly realizable from, their predecessor.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Construct states \((A_i,\cF_i,H_i,Q_i)\), where
\(H_i=\overline G(A_i,\cF_i)\le\PGL_2(L_0)\) is in the fixed
pulled-back coordinates and \(Q_i\) is the unfinished queue defined above.
At stage \(i\), apply the reductions for unsafe binaries,
consider the complete group of the resulting set \(G_i=G(\cF_i)\), retain one chosen representative of every class, and generate each normalized card in
\(\mathscr U(A_i,f,H_i)\) at most once.  Preprocess and jointly
close the resulting finite batch under irreducible factors to obtain \(\cF_{i+1}\).
The supplied \(I\) excludes the one-sided exception.  Inclusion supplies the
reverse reduction at every exposure step, so every \(\cF_i\) is
Turing-equivalent to \(\cF_0\).

Every nonbinary card factor has arity at most \(n-2\).  Odd factors are
handled by \cref{thm:external-odd}. An even one gives successor~1.
Otherwise only binaries remain.
  No global normalization is made in this fixed-\(I\) procedure.  Completeness
  therefore gives \(H_i\le H_{i+1}\). Only a strict inclusion
  \(H_i\subsetneq H_{i+1}\) is a successor with a strictly larger complete transfer group, and it alone triggers
  regeneration of the complete deck.

Use the fixed pulled-back field \(L_0\) from
\cref{lem:normalization-ledger}.  Gadget evaluation and pivot-normalized
rank-one splitting are performed in pulled-back coordinates, so
\(H_i\le\PGL_2(L_0)\).  Let \(d_{L_0}:=[L_0:\mathbb Q]\) be the field
degree, and define the torsion exponent and group-order bound by
\begin{equation}
  e_{L_0}=\operatorname{lcm}\{r\ge1:\varphi(r)\le2d_{L_0}\},
  \qquad
  M_{L_0}=\max\{2e_{L_0},60\},
  \label{eq:deck-group-bound}
\end{equation}
where \(\varphi\) denotes Euler's totient function.
\Cref{prop:finite-pgl2-classification,lem:bounded-torsion} give
\(|H_i|\le M_{L_0}\).  Hence the strict subgroup chain has at most
\(M_{L_0}-1\) changes. Between changes, stabilization processes only the
tokens indexed by the nonzero cards in \(\mathscr U(A_i,f,H_i)\). Each
token is removed once, and its factorization tree is finite by the decreasing
potential \(\Phi\) in \cref{eq:factor-potential}. A lower-arity factor,
new group class, or complexity conclusion stops the current stage.
Consequently there is a first stage at which both \(H_{i+1}=H_i\) and
the queue is empty. Call this the stabilized stage.
At that stage define the stable retained set and its complete group by
\[
  \cF_\star=\cF_{i+1},
  \qquad G_\star=G_{i+1}.
\]
Every \(0\ne h\in\mathfrak D_{G_\star}(f)\) then has retained irreducible binary
factors \(B_1,\ldots,B_m\) satisfying
\[
  [B_jX]\in G(\cF_\star)=G_\star.
\]
Writing \(B_j=\beta_jB_{g_j}\), their blocks form a perfect matching, so
\[
  h=c\bigotimes_{j=1}^m B_{g_j}
\]
for \(c\ne0\), as required.
\end{proof}

\smallskip
\noindent\textit{Remark (The role of stabilization).}
The contraction and factor arguments interact through the complete
transfer group. Direct gadgets use the current set, whereas a factor
may become available only after a reduction. Once retained, that factor
can participate in further gadgets and enlarge the group, changing both
the available contractions and the applicable group case. The procedure
therefore retains every factor, compares the complete groups, and
regenerates the deck after strict enlargement. This comparison includes
multi-copy binary gadgets beyond the finite batch that produced the factor.

The fixed number field bounds the number of strict enlargements. Between
them, the finite card indices and their factorization trees control the
remaining work. Thus stabilization supplies a common starting point for
the group-specific arguments: each can establish rigidity of the selected
signature or obtain progress that is handled by the same descent proof.
The conditions below make this requirement precise for constructions
beyond the one-copy deck.

Later constructions can lower \(\nu\) or enlarge the complete group.
We call either change an \emph{outer deck successor}. Processing a fixed
family while both remain unchanged is a \emph{within-node closure}.

\begin{definition}[Processing a finite family of signatures]
\label{def:relative-stratum-protocol}
For deck-stable \((\cF,G,f)\), take a finite family of signatures
retained in or directly realized over \(\cF\), with their input orders
and realization scalars.  Add them, preprocess, jointly close the enlarged set under irreducible factors,
apply the reductions for odd and unsafe factors, and consider the group of the resulting set.
After a strict enlargement, regenerate the complete deck. We refer to
these steps as processing the family. Each added signature has its
realization or reduction, as stipulated above. In the fixed partition
\(\mathscr U^{\mathrm{fac}}=Q_{\rm done}\sqcup Q\), each token moves from
\(Q\) to \(Q_{\rm done}\) once its card and all factor occurrences in its
tree have been processed. A completed token stays in \(Q_{\rm done}\).
For families indexed by constructions, completion is recorded at the
specified index even when two constructions give the same signature.
The descent conditions for continuations with the same group and
minimum arity are stated in \cref{def:frozen-token-coverage} and verified
in the group-specific arguments.

Applications of the equality-accessible arity-four boundary also satisfy
the requirements stated after \cref{thm:equality-q4-boundary}.

The current case is complete once finitely many such families resolve every
branch and \(Q=\varnothing\).  Under the conditions of \cref{def:frozen-token-coverage}, a transition leaving the pulled-back
group and \(\nu\) unchanged completes a pending index, decreases its stored
factor potential, or certifies that none remains, and hence is a within-node
closure. Otherwise, \(\nu\) decreases or the pulled-back group enlarges.
\end{definition}

\paragraph{Standing predicates for relative cases.}
To keep the group-specific statements short, write
\hypertarget{eq:stable-predicate}{}
\hypertarget{eq:even-prime-predicate}{}
\begin{align*}
 \Stable(\cF,G,f)
 &\Longleftrightarrow
   (\cF,G,f)\text{ is deck-stable in the sense of
   \cref{def:deck-stable-state}},
   \\
 \Even(\cF)
 &\Longleftrightarrow
   \forall g\in\Pi(\cF),\quad
   \arity(g)\ge3\Longrightarrow \arity(g)\in2\mathbb Z.
\end{align*}

Each later group branch must show that its constructions satisfy the
following conditions. In particular, a step that preserves both \(H\)
and \(\nu\) must consume work in the already fixed finite family.

\begin{definition}[Conditions for descent]
\label{def:frozen-token-coverage}
Fix a deck-stable tuple \((\cF,G,f)\), a coordinate matrix \(A\), the
pulled-back group \(H=\overline G(A,\cF)\), and a fixed partition
\[
 \mathscr U^{\mathrm{fac}}(A,f,H)=Q_{\rm done}\sqcup Q,
\]
where \(Q\) is the unfinished queue.  Let \(\mathfrak B\) be a collection of admissible families.  Processing any
member must give, on each branch, one of:
\begin{enumerate}
\item a resolved complexity leaf;
\item a successor with smaller \(\nu\);
\item a strict enlargement of the pulled-back complete group;
\item unchanged \((H,\nu)\), in which case every continuing card or factor occurrence retained for processing is indexed by a token of \(Q\), and its processing is
      confined to that token's stored card, reduction, and attached factorization tree.  The processing procedure either moves at least one such token to
      \(Q_{\rm done}\), or, while processing one fixed unfinished token,
      strictly decreases the factor potential of its stored worklist.
\end{enumerate}
 A token is marked done only after its full factorization tree is exhausted.
\end{definition}

For a family \(\mathfrak C\) of tractable signature classes,
\begin{equation}
 \Close_{\mathfrak C}(\cF,G,f)
 \label{eq:relative-close-predicate}
\end{equation}
means that finitely many applications of \cref{def:relative-stratum-protocol}
resolve all branches for \((\cF,G,f)\), with \(Q=\varnothing\), and every surviving
tractable case with a common presentation satisfies
\(\PresK(K\cF;\cC)\) for some \(\cC\in\mathfrak C\).
Established complexity conclusions and outer deck successors are also
part of completion. The class restriction applies to cases certified by
a common presentation.
\(\mathfrak C=\varnothing\) means that no tractable case with a common presentation
remains.  Thus \(\Close\) is a completion predicate for this reduction procedure,
whereas tensor closure refers to the class \(\angles{\cC}\).  The predicates
retain all procedure hypotheses and transition requirements.

The verification is necessarily branch-by-branch and uses the reductions
proved in
\cref{sec:normalized-dihedral,sec:proper-deck,sec:v4-deck,sec:platonic-decks}.
It is therefore deferred to \cref{sec:p1-synthesis}, after those four
arguments are available.

The following lexicographic measure strictly decreases on every continuing
step satisfying these conditions.

\begin{lemma}[Deck-transition dichotomy and termination]
\label{lem:matching-synthesis-measure}
Fix the initial number field \(L_0\).  At a continuing all-even,
binary-residual deck-stable node, carry the reduction data \((A,\cF,f)\), a
finite token queue \(Q\), and the supplied equality \(I\).  Let
\(\mathfrak B\) satisfy \cref{def:frozen-token-coverage}, let
\(\cF^+\) be the result of processing one batch in \(\mathfrak B\) over
\(\cF\), and define
the pulled-back group by
\[
  \overline G(A,\cF)=[A]^{-1}G(\cF)[A].
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
  \KHolant(\cF^+)\equivT\KHolant(\cF).
  \label{eq:deck-transition-equivalence}
\end{equation}
Batch outputs are direct over \(\cF\), while their factors need only be
available by reduction there.

After considering odd-arity signatures, unsafe binaries, and unary factors over the whole set, the only
continuing transitions are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(\overline G(A,\cF^+)=\overline G(A,\cF)\) and
      \(\nu(\cF^+)=\nu(\cF)\): process a previously unfinished
      token of \(Q\), complete it or strictly decrease its factor potential,
      or certify that \(Q\) is empty.
\item \(\overline G(A,\cF^+)=\overline G(A,\cF)\) and
      \(\nu(\cF^+)<\nu(\cF)\): choose a new minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature and reapply
      \cref{lem:minimum-core-deck}.
\item \(\overline G(A,\cF)\subsetneq\overline G(A,\cF^+)\): choose
      a minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature in the resulting set, regenerate its deck, and restart marked
      routing.
\end{enumerate}

At every continuing node define the pair by
\begin{equation*}
 \Xi(A,\cF)
 =\bigl(M_{L_0}-|\overline G(A,\cF)|,\nu(\cF)\bigr).
\end{equation*}
Refine it to the tuple
\begin{equation}
  \Xi_{\mathrm{full}}(A,\cF,f,Q)
  =\bigl(M_{L_0}-|\overline G(A,\cF)|,\nu(\cF),
        |Q|,\,
        \Phi_Q\bigr).
  \label{eq:deck-closure-measure}
\end{equation}
For each \(u\in Q\), let \(\mathcal W_u\) be the multiset of still-open
occurrences in its attached factor worklist, and define
\[
 \Phi_Q:=\sum_{u\in Q}\Phi(\mathcal W_u),
\]
using the potential in \cref{eq:factor-potential}.
The displayed tuple is ordered lexicographically.  Items~2--3 strictly
decrease its first two coordinates.  In item~1, completing a token strictly
decreases the third coordinate until the queue is empty.  If a token remains
unfinished during a genuine factor split, the fourth coordinate \(\Phi_Q\)
strictly decreases.  If an exposed factor changes the pulled-back group, the
operation is instead item~3 and is charged to the strict subgroup chain.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Gadget replacement and factor closure prove
\cref{eq:deck-transition-equivalence}. \(I\) excludes the one-sided
exception, and inclusion reverses the reduction.  Adding signatures cannot increase \(\nu\).  The continuing state remains in fixed-\(I\)
coordinates.  New classes can arise only from an exposed factor. Such a
class is precisely a strict successor in the pulled-back group.  By
  \cref{lem:normalization-ledger,eq:deck-group-bound}, every continuing group
lies in \(\PGL_2(L_0)\) and has order at most \(M_{L_0}\).  Therefore a lower
  \(\nu\) decreases the second coordinate of \(\Xi\), strict subgroup
  inclusion decreases the first.  A fixed-\((H,\nu)\) step either decreases the
  finite queue counter in \(\Xi_{\mathrm{full}}\) or, within its fixed token, decreases
\(\Phi\). By \cref{def:frozen-token-coverage,def:relative-stratum-protocol},
item~1 processes only a previously fixed token of \(Q\), so these
decreases exhaust every fixed-\((H,\nu)\) continuation.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Reading the transition measure.}
The table matches each outcome of \cref{lem:matching-synthesis-measure}
with the coordinate that decreases. The field \(L_0\), original
coordinates and \(I\) remain fixed.  Write
\(H^+=\overline G(A,\cF^+)\) and \(\nu^+=\nu(\cF^+)\).
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}L{.23\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}XL{.20\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Outcome & Next operation & Descent or exit\\
\midrule
Resolved complexity leaf & Return the established whole-set conclusion. & Terminal\\[3pt]
\(H\subsetneq H^+\) & Choose a new minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature, regenerate its deck,
and restart marked routing. & First coordinate of \(\Xi_{\mathrm{full}}\)\\[3pt]
\(H^+=H,\ \nu^+<\nu\) & Choose a new minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature and reapply
\cref{lem:minimum-core-deck}. & Second coordinate\\[3pt]
Same \((H,\nu)\), completed token & Move that token from \(Q\) to
\(Q_{\rm done}\) after its full factor tree is processed. & Third coordinate\\[3pt]
Same \((H,\nu)\), unfinished token & Continue inside its fixed factor
tree. A genuine factor split decreases \(\Phi_Q\). & Fourth coordinate\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
When \(Q\) is empty, the local family has been processed.

\begin{definition}[The conclusion required of each construction]
\label{def:protocol-discharged}
Write
\[
 \Sigma=(A,\cF,H,f,Q),
 \qquad H=\overline G(A,\cF),
\]
with the record, deck-stability, fixed queue, and fixed-\(I\) data of
\cref{def:relative-stratum-protocol,def:frozen-token-coverage}.  Write
\[
 \Xi_{\mathrm{full}}(\Sigma)
 :=\Xi_{\mathrm{full}}(A,\cF,f,Q)
 =\bigl(M_{L_0}-|H|,\nu(\cF),|Q|,\Phi_Q\bigr)
\]
for the lexicographic measure in \cref{eq:deck-closure-measure}.

For each concrete construction at \(\Sigma\), we prove that processing its
outputs by \cref{def:relative-stratum-protocol} gives either
\begin{enumerate}
\item a resolved complexity leaf: hardness, a tractable case satisfying \(\Tract\), or
      a tractable case with a common \(K\)-presentation; or
\item a deck-stable tuple \(\Sigma'\), with the same required realization and
      induction data, satisfying
      \[
        \Xi_{\mathrm{full}}(\Sigma')<_{\rm lex}\Xi_{\mathrm{full}}(\Sigma).
      \]
\end{enumerate}
We call the tuple in the second outcome a \emph{strict stable successor}.
This outcome includes a decrease of \(\nu\), a strict enlargement of
the pulled-back complete group, completion of a fixed token, or a strict
decrease of that token's factor potential.  Every
same-\((H,\nu)\) continuation must be indexed by the already fixed queue
and processes only its stored cards, reductions, and factorization trees. A
continuing successor includes this queue data and the strict descent
asserted in item~2.

Item~1 resolves the complexity question. Item~2 establishes strict
progress and continues the induction at \(\Sigma'\). A \emph{resolved
construction}, in the terminology of the relative branch statements,
is one for which either item has been proved, including realizability,
factor access, and descent. A \emph{resolved complexity leaf} always
means item~1.
The assertion \(\Close_{\mathfrak C}(\cF,G,f)\) additionally covers
every case for the current \((H,\nu)\) and restricts the surviving common
presentations to \(\mathfrak C\), as in \cref{eq:relative-close-predicate}.
\end{definition}

For a selected stable tuple \(\Sigma\), the continuing outcome of
\cref{def:protocol-discharged} decreases \(\Xi_{\mathrm{full}}\). To classify the whole
set, the final proof in \cref{sec:p1-synthesis} also uses an outer worklist
induction over unresolved irreducible occurrences. Completing the current
occurrence and choosing another of the same arity advances that outer
induction. This separates progress for the selected signature from
completion of the entire set.

\subsection{The main stable-state theorem and four-way routing}
\label{sec:matching-decks}

The preceding reductions leave a deck-stable tuple
\((\cF,G,f)\).  The next theorem is proved in
\cref{sec:p1-synthesis} from the four branches routed below.  Together with
stabilization, it gives the equality-accessible main theorem
\cref{thm:p1-eq2-kholant-dichotomy}.

\begin{theorem}[Relative matching-deck classification]
\label{thm:matching-deck-classification}
Assume
\[
 \Stable(\cF,G,f),\qquad \arity(f)=\nu(\cF).
\]
In particular, the deck-stable state contains the supplied
equality \(I\).  If \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds, then
\(\KHolant(\cF)\in\FP\). Otherwise \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is
\(\SharpP\)-hard.  Equivalently, by \cref{lem:tract-X-anchor}, the
tractable condition is \(\ReTract(K\cF)\).
\end{theorem}

In each tractable case reached after the hardness reductions, the proof
supplies one common product, affine, or flat-Lagrangian
\(K\)-presentation. By \cref{eq:common-K-presentation-predicate}, the same
