basis also places the transformed edge in that class.

The supplied equality gives \(x=[X]\in G\). Reversal then makes
\(j=[Z]\) normalize \(G\). Let \(\widehat G:=\langle G,j\rangle\) be the
extension obtained by adjoining the mark. The reversal identity gives
\(G\triangleleft \widehat G\) and \([\widehat G:G]\le2\), while the distinct commuting
involutions \(x,j\) generate a Klein-four subgroup of \(\widehat G\).
Their positions refine the abstract finite-group classification into the
four branches used below. We record the group facts and matrix notation
needed to make this refinement explicit.
\label{sec:marked-finite-groups}

\begin{lemma}[Finite projective-group facts]
\label{lem:finite-pgl2-facts}
The following properties will be used below.
\begin{enumerate}
\item A cyclic group has at most one involution, and it has one exactly when
      its order is even.
\item For
      \[
        D_{2m}=\langle r,s:r^m=s^2=1,\ srs=r^{-1}\rangle,
      \]
      the rotations \(\langle r\rangle\cong C_m\) form a normal index-two
      subgroup, and every element outside it is a reflection.  If \(m\) is
      odd, the rotation group is the unique index-two subgroup and the
      center is trivial.  If \(m\) is even and \(m\ge4\), the center is
      \(\{1,r^{m/2}\}\). For \(m=2\), \(D_4\cong V_4\) and all three
      nonidentity elements are central.  Every subgroup of a cyclic or
      dihedral group is cyclic or dihedral.
\item The three involutions of \(A_4\), together with the identity, form
      its unique normal \(V_4\).  The group has no index-two subgroup, and
      the centralizer of an involution is \(V_4\).
\item The unique index-two subgroup of \(S_4\) is \(A_4\).  Its
      involutions are six transpositions and three double transpositions.
      The centralizer of a transposition has order \(4\), whereas the
      centralizer of a double transposition is \(D_8\).  The three double
      transpositions are the nonidentity elements of the unique normal
      \(V_4\).  Conjugation is transitive both on ordered pairs of distinct
      double transpositions and on ordered pairs of disjoint transpositions.
\item The group \(A_5\) is simple and has no index-two subgroup.  Its
      fifteen involutions form one conjugacy class, and the centralizer of
      each is \(V_4\).
\item The element-order distributions are
      \[
      \begin{array}{c|rrrr}
          &1&2&3&\text{order }4\text{ or }5\\ \hline
        A_4&1&3&8&0\\
        S_4&1&9&8&6\text{ of order }4\\
        A_5&1&15&20&24\text{ of order }5.
      \end{array}
      \]
\item For the displayed models,
      \[
        N_{\PGL_2(\mathbb C)}(\TetInt)=\OctDD,
        \qquad
        N_{\PGL_2(\mathbb C)}(\OctDD)=\OctDD,
        \qquad
        N_{\PGL_2(\mathbb C)}(\IcoZero)=\IcoZero.
      \]
\item The projective centralizers of the distinguished involutions are
      \[
      C_{\PGL_2(\mathbb C)}([Z])
      =
      \left\{
      \left[\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\0&b\end{pmatrix}\right]:ab\ne0
      \right\}
      \sqcup
      \left\{
      \left[\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\b&0\end{pmatrix}\right]:ab\ne0
      \right\}.
      \]
      Thus \([Z]\) is the only nonidentity diagonal involution, while
      every nonsingular anti-diagonal class is an involution.  Moreover,
      \[
        C_{\PGL_2(\mathbb C)}\bigl(\langle[X],[Z]\rangle\bigr)
        =\langle[X],[Z]\rangle\cong V_4.
      \]
      Every finite subgroup of the displayed diagonal torus is cyclic.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Items~1--6 follow directly from the cyclic and dihedral presentations and
the usual permutation models of \(A_4,S_4,A_5\)
\cite{DixonMortimer1996}.  For item~7, conjugation
embeds each normalizer modulo its projective centralizer into the
corresponding automorphism group.  The projective centralizers are trivial.
\(\operatorname{Aut}(A_4)\cong S_4\), the displayed \(\OctDD\)
normalizes \(\TetInt\), and every automorphism of \(S_4\) is inner.
Since \(\operatorname{Aut}(A_5)\cong S_5\), an additional outer normalizer
of \(\IcoZero\) would give a finite projective group \(H\) of order \(120\)
containing \(A_5\).  The classification forces \(H\cong C_{120}\) or
\(D_{120}\). Every subgroup of a cyclic or dihedral group is cyclic or
dihedral, so \(H\) cannot contain \(A_5\).  Item~8 follows by solving
\(UZ\doteq ZU\), and then imposing the same identity with \(X\).  The ratio
map \([\diag(a,b)]\mapsto a/b\) embeds the diagonal torus into
\(\mathbb C^\times\). Every finite subgroup of \(\mathbb C^\times\) is a cyclic group
of roots of unity.
\end{proof}

The abstract classification is up to projective conjugacy. Our group
cases also retain the ordered marks \([X],[Z]\) in the original
coordinates. A global change of basis or a binary gadget is used only
with its corresponding holographic identity or realization.

\paragraph{Matrix and marked-pair dictionary.}
Combining \cref{eq:quaternion-basis,eq:bell-matrix-dictionary} gives the
matrix identities
\hypertarget{eq:global-matrix-aliases}{}
\begin{equation*}
 I=Q_0=B_{0,0},\qquad
 iX=Q_1=iB_{1,0},\qquad
 Y=Q_2=B_{1,1},\qquad
 iZ=Q_3=iB_{0,1}.
\end{equation*}
Consequently the marked projective notation used throughout is
\hypertarget{eq:global-mark-aliases}{}
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
  1&=[I]=[Q_0]=[B_{0,0}],&
  x&=[X]=[Q_1]=[B_{1,0}],\\
  j&=[Z]=[Q_3]=[B_{0,1}],&
  xj&=[Q_2]=[B_{1,1}].
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Here the last identity is projective: \(XZ=-Y\), whereas \(ZX=Y\).  For
the transfer convention \(T_B=BX\), the four Bell binaries give
\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
 B&\text{Bell name}&[B]&[T_B]=[BX]\\ \hline
 I&B_{0,0}&1&x\\
 X&B_{1,0}&x&1\\
 Z&B_{0,1}&j&xj\\
 Y&B_{1,1}&xj&j
\end{array}
\end{equation*}
The subscripted \(B_{d,\ell}\) is a fixed ordered Bell signature, whereas
\(B_g\) denotes a chosen realizable binary representative of a general
transfer class \(g\).  Reversal fixes \(I,X,Z\) and sends
\(Y=B_{1,1}\) to \(-Y\), without changing its projective class.

Use the diagonal family \(D(t)\) defined in
\cref{subsec:holant-definitions}.
For the dihedral forms, the subscript gives the group order and the
superscript records the direction of the rotation subgroup: \(Z\), \(Y\),
or \(X\). The labels \((X,0)\) and \((X,1)\) distinguish the unshifted
and shifted reflection cosets, respectively.
For \(m\ge2\), define the matrix family \(C(t)\) and the first two marked
dihedral families
\begin{align*}
 \DihZ{2m}
 &=D_{2m}^{\rm std}=\{[D(t)],[XD(t)]:t\in\mu_m\},
 \\
 C(t)&=(1+t)I+i(1-t)Y,\\
 \DihY{2m}
 &=\{[C(t)],[XC(t)]:t\in\mu_m\}.
\end{align*}
For even \(N\ge2\), define the cyclic group
\[
 A(t)=(1+t)I+(1-t)X,\qquad \CycX{N}:=\{[A(t)]:t\in\mu_N\}.
\]
Its unique involution is \(x=[X]=[A(-1)]\).
For even \(m\), fix \(\eta\in\mu_{2m}\setminus\mu_m\) and define
\begin{equation*}
 \DihXone{2m}
 =
 \CycX{m}
 \cup
 \{[ZA(\eta t)]:t\in\mu_m\}.
\end{equation*}
In the same even parameter range, define the unshifted marked form
\begin{equation*}
 \DihXzero{2m}
 =
 \{[A(t)],[ZA(t)]:t\in\mu_m\}.
\end{equation*}
These are projective displays.  Any suppressed factor of two belongs to
the coordinate model and does not normalize a chosen representative.

The symbols \(\TetInt,\TetExt\),
\(\OctDD,\OctTT,\OctDT,\OctTD\), and
\(\IcoZero,\IcoOne\) denote the marked tetrahedral, octahedral,
and icosahedral forms specified in the theorem. Here \(\mathrm{int}\)
and \(\mathrm{ext}\) record whether \(j\in G\) or \(j\notin G\).
For octahedral forms, \(d\) denotes a double transposition and \(t\) a
transposition, in the order \((x,j)\). The labels \(0,1\) distinguish
the two icosahedral coordinate displays of the same abstract marked type.
\Cref{sec:platonic-decks} gives the explicit fixed-equality representatives
and coefficient correspondences.

\begin{theorem}[Marked finite-group routing]
\label{thm:marked-finite-group-routing}
Let \(\cF\) be a retained factor-closed set containing the
supplied equality \(I\), and let \(G=G(\cF)\) be its complete transfer group (finite, with safe binary representatives).  Recall the ordered marked pair and its generated
group:
\[
 (x,j):=([X],[Z]),\qquad x\in G,\qquad \widehat G:=\langle G,j\rangle.
\]
In the fixed coordinates containing the supplied \(I\), exactly
one of the following alternatives holds:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(G\cong D_{2m}\), \(m\ge3\), and the marked configuration
      \((G;x,j)\) has one of the forms denoted by
      \(\DihZ{2m},\DihY{2m},\DihXone{2m},\DihXzero{2m}\).  The last
      two families occur only for even \(m\).
\item \(G=\CycX{N}\cong C_N\), where \(N\) is even and \(j\notin G\).  This is
      the cyclic branch.
\item \(G\cong V_4\), and \(G\) is either the standard Pauli group
      \(\KleinStd=\DihZ{4}=\DihY{4}\) or the exotic group
      \(\KleinEx=\DihXone{4}\).
\item \(G\) is one of the two marked tetrahedral forms
      \(\TetInt,\TetExt\), the four fixed-equality marked
      octahedral forms
      \(\OctDD,\OctTT,\OctDT,\OctTD\), or one of the two icosahedral displays
      \(\IcoZero,\IcoOne\).
\end{enumerate}

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Positions of the marks.}
All conjugacy statements in this paragraph are taken in \(\widehat G\).  In every
form, \(x\) and \(j\) are distinct commuting involutions and
\(\langle x,j\rangle=\{1,x,j,xj\}\cong V_4\).  In the four dihedral forms the
marked positions are
\begin{align*}
 \DihZ{2m}:&\quad
 x=[XD(1)],\quad j=[D(-1)],\quad xj=[XD(-1)],\qquad
 \widehat G=\begin{cases}\DihZ{2m},&2\mid m,\\ \DihZ{4m},&2\nmid m;\end{cases}\\
 \DihY{2m}:&\quad
 x=[XC(1)],\quad j=[XC(-1)],\quad xj=[C(-1)],\qquad
 \widehat G=\begin{cases}\DihY{2m},&2\mid m,\\ \DihY{4m},&2\nmid m;\end{cases}\\
 \DihXone{2m}:&\quad
 x=[A(-1)]\in G,\quad j=[ZA(1)]\in \widehat G\setminus G,\quad
 xj=[ZA(-1)]\in \widehat G\setminus G;\\[-2pt]
 &\quad
 \widehat G=\{[A(t)]:t\in\mu_{2m}\}\cup
   \{[ZA(t)]:t\in\mu_{2m}\}\cong D_{4m};\\
 \DihXzero{2m}:&\quad
 x=[A(-1)],\quad j=[ZA(1)],\quad xj=[ZA(-1)],\qquad
 \widehat G=G=\DihXzero{2m}.
\end{align*}
For \(G=\DihZ{2m}\), the central half-turn of \(\widehat G\) is \(j\) and the other two marks are reflections.
For \(G=\DihY{2m}\), it is \(xj\) and the reflections are \(x,j\).
In both cases \(j\in G\) exactly when \(m\) is even. The two marked reflections are conjugate in \(\widehat G\) exactly when \(4\mid m\). In
\(\DihXone{2m}\), where \(m\) is even, \(x\) is the central half-turn and
\(j,xj\) are conjugate reflections in \(\widehat G\setminus G\).
In \(\DihXzero{2m}\), \(x\) is again the central half-turn, while
\(j,xj\in G\) are reflections. They are conjugate in \(G\) exactly when
\(4\mid m\).
When \(m\) is odd in either \(\DihZ{2m}\) or \(\DihY{2m}\), one has
\(j,xj\in \widehat G\setminus G\). When \(m\) is even, all three marks lie in \(G\).

In the cyclic branch \(G=\CycX{N}\), the order \(N\) is even,
\(j,xj\in \widehat G\setminus G\), \(\widehat G\cong D_{2N}\), \(x\) is the central half-turn, and
\(j,xj\) are reflections, conjugate exactly when \(4\mid N\).

At \(m=2\), the standard Pauli form has \(\widehat G=G\), with \(x,j,xj\) the three
nonidentity elements (three singleton conjugacy classes) of \(\KleinStd\). The
exotic form has \(j,xj\in \widehat G\setminus G\), \(\widehat G\cong D_8\), central half-turn
\(x\), and conjugate outside reflections \(j,xj\).  In \(\TetInt\),
\(\widehat G=G\cong A_4\) and \(x=[Q_1],j=[Q_3],xj=[Q_2]\) are the three double
transpositions, hence one conjugacy class.  In \(\TetExt\),
\(G\cong A_4\), \(G\triangleleft \widehat G\), and \(\widehat G\cong S_4\).
\(x=[Q_1]\in G\) is a double transposition,
and \(j=[Q_3],xj=[Q_2]\in \widehat G\setminus G\) are disjoint transpositions in one
  class.  In \(\OctDD\), \(\widehat G=G\cong S_4\) and \(x,j,xj\) are the three
  double transpositions.  In \(\OctTT\), \(x,j\) are disjoint
  transpositions and \(xj\) is a double transposition.  In
  \(\OctDT\), \(x\) is a double transposition and \(j,xj\) are
  transpositions. In \(\OctTD\), \(j\) is a double transposition
  and \(x,xj\) are transpositions.  In all four cases \(\widehat G=G\cong S_4\).
  Finally, in
each icosahedral coordinate display \(\widehat G=G\cong A_5\), and
\(x=[Q_1],j=[Q_3],xj=[Q_2]\) lie in its unique involution class.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The reversal identity
\([T]\mapsto j[T]^{-1}j=[ZT^{-1}Z]\) (with \(j=[Z]\)) and completeness give
\[
 jGj=G,\qquad G\triangleleft \widehat G,\qquad [\widehat G:G]\le2.
\]
The two distinct projective involutions \(x,j\) commute, so
\(\KleinStd=\langle x,j\rangle\le\widehat G\).  By
\cref{prop:finite-pgl2-classification}, this leaves an even dihedral group
or one of \(A_4,S_4,A_5\).

Suppose first that \(G\cong D_{2m}\) with \(m\ge3\).  Then \(\widehat G\) is also
dihedral.  Indeed, if \(\widehat G\) were Platonic and \([\widehat G:G]=2\), then \(G\)
would be an index-two normal subgroup.  By
\cref{lem:finite-pgl2-facts}, the only such subgroup among the Platonic
groups is \(A_4\triangleleft S_4\), which is not dihedral.  The case
\(\widehat G=G\) is immediate.  Choose a rotation generator \(r\) and a reflection
generator \(s\) so that
\(\widehat G=\langle r,s:r^n=s^2=1,\ srs=r^{-1}\rangle\).
Since \(\widehat G\) contains \(\langle x,j\rangle\cong V_4\), \(n\) is even.
Exactly one of \(x,j,xj\) is the central half-turn \(r^{n/2}\), and
the other two are reflections.  If the central mark is \(j\) or \(xj\),
we obtain \(\DihZ{2m}\) or \(\DihY{2m}\), respectively.
If it is \(x\), membership \(j\in G\) gives \(\DihXzero{2m}\),
while \(j\notin G\) gives \(\DihXone{2m}\).
For \(j\in G\), one has \(\widehat G=G\), so \(n=m\) is even.
For \(j\notin G\), one has \(n=2m\) and
\(G=\langle r^2,r^ks\rangle\) for some \(k\in\{0,1\}\).
The central half-turn \(r^m\) of \(\widehat G\) belongs to \(G\) exactly when \(m\) is even.
Thus \(m\) is odd in the first two outside-mark cases and even in
\(\DihXone{2m}\), where \(x\in G\).  This exhausts the four forms
and their parameter ranges.

In \(\DihXzero{2m}\), the mark \(j=[Z]\) belongs to the complete group.
Its fixed chosen representative \(B_j\) satisfies
\([B_jX]=j\), hence \(B_j\doteq Y\).  This realizable binary and the already
available \(I\) give the signature-preserving edge switch used in
\cref{thm:marked-A0-reduction}. The distinguished equality is never
replaced or re-anchored.

The marked eigenline equations give the displayed matrices, with
\[
 ZC(t)Z=tC(t^{-1}),\qquad
 x=[XC(1)],
\]
while \(x=[A(-1)]\) is central in both \(\DihXone{2m}\) and
\(\DihXzero{2m}\). Hence the four marked configurations are legally distinct.

Next suppose that \(G\) is cyclic.  By
\cref{lem:finite-pgl2-facts}, its unique involution is \(x\).  A class
commuting with \([X]\) lies in \(\Span\{I,X\}\) or
\(\Span\{Z,Y\}\), and every nonsingular member of the latter is another
involution.  Thus all transfer classes have representatives in
\(\Span\{I,X\}\). Their eigenvalue ratios form \(\mu_N\), where \(N=|G|\).
Thus \(G=\CycX{N}\), \(N\) is even, and \(j\notin G\). Conjugation by \(j\) inverts the eigenvalue ratio of every
class in this algebra, so
\[
 \widehat G=G\rtimes\langle j\rangle\cong D_{2N}.
\]
Consequently, \(x\) is the central half-turn and \(j,xj\) are the two
marked reflections.

It remains to consider \(G\cong V_4\), corresponding to \(m=2\) in the
dihedral notation.  Here
\[
 \KleinStd=\DihZ{4}=\DihY{4}=\{[I],[X],[Z],[Y]\}.
\]
For \(\eta=i\),
\[
 \frac{ZA(i)}{1-i}=Y+iZ,\qquad
 \frac{ZA(-i)}{1+i}=Y-iZ,
\]
so \(\KleinEx=\{[I],x,[Y+iZ],[Y-iZ]\}\). Conversely, every subgroup \(G\cong V_4\) containing \(x\) has the form
\[
 \{[I],x,[aZ+bY],[bZ+aY]\},\qquad a^2\ne b^2,
\]
and \(jGj=G\) gives either \(ab=0\) (the Pauli form) or
\(a^2=-b^2\) (the exotic form \(\KleinEx\)).

Thus the Pauli form has \(\widehat G=G\), with \(x,j,xj\) its three nonidentity
elements.  In the exotic form, \(j,xj\notin G\) and
\(\widehat G=\langle G,j\rangle\cong D_8\). Relative to this \(\widehat G\), \(x\) is the central
half-turn and \(j,xj\) are conjugate reflections in the outside coset.

It remains to route the Platonic groups while retaining the ordered marked
pair.  Let \(G\cong A_4\).  If \(j\in G\), then \(\widehat G=G\), and the three
nonidentity elements \(x,j,xj\) of \(\KleinStd\) are precisely the
double transpositions.  This is \(\TetInt\).  If \(j\notin G\), then
\(|\widehat G|=24\).  By
\cref{prop:finite-pgl2-classification,lem:finite-pgl2-facts}, the
finite-\(\PGL_2\) classification and the subgroup structure of cyclic and
dihedral groups force
\(\widehat G\cong S_4\) with \(G\triangleleft\widehat G\) and \(G\cong A_4\).  Thus \(x\in G\) is a
double transposition, whereas the odd involutions \(j,xj\in \widehat G\setminus G\)
are transpositions.  Since they are distinct and commute, they are disjoint.
This is \(\TetExt\).

Now let \(G\cong S_4\). If \(j\notin G\), then
\(\widehat G=\langle G,j\rangle\) has order \(48\) and is cyclic or dihedral by
\cref{prop:finite-pgl2-classification}, contrary to containing \(S_4\)
by \cref{lem:finite-pgl2-facts}. Hence \(j\in G\).

  The two commuting involutions may independently lie in the transposition
  or double-transposition class, subject to the class of their product.  The
  four resulting ordered class patterns are
  \[
    (dd;d),\qquad (tt;d),\qquad (dt;t),\qquad (td;t),
  \]
  where the entries record the classes of \((x,j;xj)\).  Conjugation in
  \(S_4\) is transitive on ordered pairs in each pattern by
  \cref{lem:finite-pgl2-facts}.  They are therefore exactly the four
  fixed-equality marked types
  \[
    \OctDD,\qquad \OctTT,\qquad
    \OctDT,\qquad \OctTD.
  \]
  Their ordered marks distinguish the four types in the fixed coordinates.
  Concrete representatives and direct finite exits are recorded in
  \cref{sec:platonic-decks} and
  Appendix~\ref{appsec:cert-platonic}.

For \(G\cong A_5\), if \(j\notin G\), then \(\widehat G\) has order \(120\)
and is cyclic or dihedral by \cref{prop:finite-pgl2-classification},
contrary to containing \(A_5\) by \cref{lem:finite-pgl2-facts}.
Thus \(\widehat G=G\). Finally, \cref{lem:finite-pgl2-facts} shows that \(A_5\) has one
involution class, with \(x,j,xj\) in that class.  Once an ordered pair
\((x,j)\) is fixed, two conjugators differ by
\[
 C_{\PGL_2(\mathbb C)}(\langle x,j\rangle)=\langle x,j\rangle.
\]
Each of these four projective classes has a representative in \(GO(X)\).
  Hence the abstract marked type is unique.  Fixed-equality coordinates give
  the two displays \(\IcoZero,\IcoOne\). \Cref{sec:platonic-decks} verifies their
  explicit representatives and a direct coefficient-and-card correspondence
  that leaves the supplied \(I\) untouched.  This deferred coordinate calculation is not used
to classify the abstract group, so the four-way list is exhaustive.
\end{proof}

Each of the four group-specific sections,
\cref{sec:normalized-dihedral,sec:proper-deck,sec:v4-deck,sec:platonic-decks},
proves a relative theorem for its corresponding stable tuple.  It either
determines the complexity at that tuple, or
produces a reduction step whose processing yields a strictly smaller
active tuple under \(\Xi_{\mathrm{full}}\).  In the second case, the deck is stabilized
again and the new tuple is routed anew.  Movement between distinct
same-arity irreducible signatures in a proof for the whole signature set remains governed by the
separate outer worklist induction of \cref{def:protocol-discharged}.

Coefficient and open-network correspondences in the branch proofs
leave the retained set and supplied \(I\) fixed. If factor access
enlarges \(G\), reapply \cref{lem:minimum-core-deck} and the marked
classification to the new complete group.

\begin{remark}[Connection with Xia's binary-group taxonomy]
\label{rem:xia-binary-taxonomy}
Version~1 of Xia's framework
\cite[Sections~1 and~4]{Xia2026FrameworkV1} organizes the finite projective
binary residue into nine \emph{unmarked} rotation-group types.  Here the
group consists of transfer classes \([T_B]=[BX]\), rather than projective
classes \([B]\) of the underlying binary matrices, and the supplied binary equality fixes the
marks
\[
  x=[X]\in G,
  \qquad
  j=[Z],
  \qquad
  jGj=G.
\]
Consequently, our routing refines Xia's taxonomy by the ordered marked pair
\((x,j)\).  The table compares the unmarked types with the cases compatible with
the supplied \(I\) in this section.
\begin{center}
\normalsize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{0.92}
\begin{tabularx}{0.96\linewidth}{@{}L{0.21\linewidth}X@{}}
\toprule
Xia's unmarked type & Corresponding case in this proof\\
\midrule
\(C_1\) &
absent at a continuing node, where a nonidentity transfer is already retained\\
\(C_2\) &
the order-two cyclic case \(\CycX{2}\), including the Reed--Muller case of \cref{sec:proper-deck}\\
\(C_n\), \(n\ge3\) &
  the fixed-\(I\) cyclic case \(\CycX{N}\ni[X]\), with even \(N\ge4\)\\
\(D_{2m}\), \(m\) odd &
\(\DihZ{2m},\DihY{2m}\), with odd \(m\ge3\)\\
\(V_4=D_4\) &
\(\KleinStd,\KleinEx\)\\
\(D_{2m}\), even \(m\ge4\) &
\(\DihZ{2m},\DihY{2m},\DihXzero{2m},\DihXone{2m}\)\\
\(A_4\) & \(\TetInt,\TetExt\)\\
\(S_4\) & \(\OctDD,\OctTT,\OctDT,\OctTD\)\\
\(A_5\) & the two fixed-equality coordinate displays\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
The marked classification and the corresponding signature-set
reductions are proved here.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Strict growth of the complete transfer group}
\label{subsec:complete-group-growth}

The preceding classification identifies the branch for a fixed complete
group. A later contraction or factor reduction may enlarge that group and
return the proof to a different branch. \Cref{fig:complete-group-growth}
shows how these branches fit together, retaining the marked distinctions
that determine which later argument applies.

Work in the fixed coordinates containing the supplied \(I\).  Let
\(\cF\subseteq\cF^+\) be a retained factor-closed set,
put
\[
 G=G(\cF),\qquad G^+=G(\cF^+),
\]
and suppose that the unsafe-binary case distinction has not already applied.  If a
newly exposed ordered binary \(B\) is safe and \([BX]\notin G\), then
\cref{lem:actual-binary-completeness} gives the strict inclusion
\(G<G^+\).  If \([BX]\in G\), its transfer class is already present. We still
consider the complete group of the resulting set, which may realize
additional multi-copy binaries beyond the batch that exposed \(B\).
Accordingly, \(G^+\) is the complete group of realizable transfer classes, and need not equal
the subgroup generated by \(G\) and the first new class.  Whenever
\(G<G^+\), regenerate the deck and reroute before invoking a
group-specific theorem.

\caption{Possible strict growth of the complete transfer group with the
supplied equality fixed. Numbered arrows indicate subgroup inclusions
between marked forms, with their parameter conditions listed below the
graph. Divisibility steps within a family are suppressed. Grouped nodes
collect distinct marked forms at the same level of the diagram.}
\label{fig:complete-group-growth}
\end{figure}

Recall the marked extension \(\widehat G=\langle G,j\rangle\) from \cref{thm:marked-finite-group-routing}.
The diagram tracks inclusions of \(G\). An outside mark \(j\) contributes
to growth only when it enters the complete group of the enlarged set.
For example, adjoining \(j\) to \(\DihXone{2m}\) gives
\(\DihXzero{4m}\). The standard Klein-four node is
\(\KleinStd=\DihZ{4}=\DihY{4}\), and the exotic node is
\(\KleinEx=\DihXone{4}\).

\begin{proposition}[Finite marked-successor routing]
\label{prop:finite-marked-successor-routing}
Let \(\cF\subseteq\cF^+\) be retained factor-closed sets containing
the supplied equality \(I\), both in the binary-residual regime, and suppose
\[
 G=G(\cF)<G^+=G(\cF^+).
\]
After recomputing the complete group and applying the marked classification
\cref{thm:marked-finite-group-routing}, the marked type of \(G^+\) is
reachable from the marked type of \(G\) by a directed
path in \cref{fig:complete-group-growth}, allowing the suppressed
same-family divisibility steps.  The paths describe the group inclusions compatible with the fixed marks.
Their occurrence for a particular signature set depends on its realizable
binaries.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The fixed marks \(x=[X]\) and \(j=[Z]\), together with reversal closure, give
\[
 x\in G<G^+,
 \qquad jGj=G,
 \qquad jG^+j=G^+.
\]
Hence \(\widehat G\le\langle G^+,j\rangle\), and if \(j\in G^+\) then
\(\widehat G\le G^+\).  Apply
\cref{prop:finite-pgl2-classification,lem:finite-pgl2-facts} and retain this
marked-pair constraint throughout.

Suppose first that \(G=\CycX{N}\), where \(N\) is even.  For \(N>2\), every
embedding in a dihedral overgroup places \(\CycX{N}\) in the rotation subgroup;
therefore its unique involution \(x\) is the central half-turn.  If
  \(j\notin G^+\), the routed form is \(\DihXone{2m}\); if \(j\in G^+\),
  the routed form is \(\DihXzero{2m}\).  Enlarging first inside the cyclic family accounts for
the displayed condition \(N\mid m\).  The group \(\CycX{2}=\langle x\rangle\)
is exceptional because \(x\) may instead be a reflection, giving the
odd-parameter entrances to \(\DihZ{2m}\) and \(\DihY{2m}\); the
even-parameter entrances factor through one of the displayed Klein forms.

For the Klein boundary, \(\KleinStd\) already contains \(j\), so a
dihedral overgroup has all three marked involutions and routes to
\(\DihZ{2m}\), \(\DihY{2m}\), or \(\DihXzero{2m}\) with even \(m\).
These three cases are distinguished by which of \(j,xj,x\), respectively,
is the central half-turn of the dihedral overgroup.  In the exotic
form \(\KleinEx=\DihXone{4}\), the mark \(j\) lies outside.
The reflection parameters in \(\DihXone{2m}\) form \(\mu_{2m}\setminus\mu_m\).
Since \(\mu_4\setminus\mu_2=\{i,-i\}\), for even \(m>2\) we have
\[
 \DihXone{4}<\DihXone{2m}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 i^m=-1
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 m\equiv2\pmod4.
\]
When \(4\mid m\), the inclusion \(\KleinEx<\DihXzero{2m}\) factors
through the subgroup \(\langle\KleinEx,j\rangle=\DihXzero{8}\).
More generally,
\[
  \langle\DihXone{2m},j\rangle=\DihXzero{4m}.
\]
The remaining dihedral-over-dihedral possibilities are precisely the
routine divisibility inclusions suppressed from the drawing.  In the
  \((X,1)\)-family, an odd index preserves the shifted reflection coset;
  an even index enters an unshifted \(\DihXzero{2n}\) form.  Thus all cyclic, Klein, and dihedral
successors follow the displayed paths.

It remains to attach the Platonic groups.  The unique normal Klein four in
\(A_4\), together with whether \(j\) belongs to the current group, gives
\[
 \KleinStd<\TetInt,
 \qquad
 \KleinEx<\TetExt.
\]
The displayed models in
\cref{eq:tetrahedral-domain,eq:octahedral-domain,eq:icosahedral-domain}
give
\[
 \TetInt<\OctDD,
 \qquad
 \TetInt<\IcoZero\cap\IcoOne.
\]
For the external tetrahedral form, adjoining \(j\) gives the mixed fixed-\(I\)
form \(\OctDT\).

The only marked dihedral groups of parameter at least three that can enter
a Platonic successor have parameter four, with fixed-mark inclusions
\[
 \DihZ{8}<\OctDD\text{ or }\OctTD,
\qquad
 \DihY{8}<\OctDD\text{ or }\OctTT,
\qquad
 \DihXzero{8}<\OctDD\text{ or }\OctDT.
\]
Indeed, an \(S_4\) dihedral subgroup of parameter at least three has
parameter \(3\) or \(4\), by the element orders in
\cref{lem:finite-pgl2-facts}.  At parameter \(3\), the marked extension
\(\widehat G\) has a rotation of order \(6\), which \(S_4\) does not.
At parameter \(4\), the \(\DihZ{8}\), \(\DihY{8}\), and
\(\DihXzero{8}\) marked positions give the displayed inclusions, while
\(\widehat{\DihXone{8}}\cong D_{16}\) cannot occur. The domain and
second-form matrices in \cref{eq:octahedral-domain,eq:octahedral-second-form},
together with the conjugations and mark checks in
\cref{eq:octahedral-mixed-conjugators,eq:octahedral-mixed-forms,eq:octahedral-mixed-mark-check},
verify the three inclusions. Here the second-form similarity identifies
subgroup classes. The same argument for \(A_5\)
leaves only parameters \(3\) and \(5\), but their marked extensions contain
rotations of order \(6\) and \(10\), absent from \(A_5\).  No
parameter-at-least-three dihedral group lies in \(A_4\): the only possible
parameter is \(3\), and an order-six subgroup would have index two,
contrary to \cref{lem:finite-pgl2-facts}.

Finally, \(\TetExt\) cannot grow to a group isomorphic to \(A_5\), because the
target contains \(j\) and would therefore contain
\(\langle\TetExt,j\rangle\cong S_4\), whereas
\(24\nmid60\).  The groups \(S_4\) and \(A_5\) have no strict finite
overgroup in \(\PGL_2(\mathbb C)\): the finite-group classification and
orders exclude every remaining type.  This proves exhaustiveness.
\end{proof}

Every strict inclusion increases \(|G|\), and therefore decreases the
group coordinate of \(\Xi_{\mathrm{full}}\) in \cref{lem:matching-synthesis-measure}.
The bounded-field argument bounds the possible orders along a run,
including the same-family divisibility steps suppressed in the figure.
Thus the diagram complements the arity descent: when a construction
produces new binary transfers instead of a smaller irreducible signature,
the proof continues in a strictly larger group.

\subsection{Shared methods for the four branch proofs}
\label{subsec:shared-matching-methods}
\label{sec:equality-q4-boundary}
\label{proofsec:finite-certificates}

The group branches use two common tools. Binary contractions determine
either a full two-input slice or only part of it, which separates the
cyclic analysis from the other cases. At arity four, a shared gadget
construction connects the stable deck to the eight-vertex reductions.

\subsubsection{Two-input slices and contractions}
\label{subsubsec:two-port-fibres}

For two binary chains \(L,R\), order each matrix with the free input
as its row and the joined input as its column. Joining their second
inputs through \(X\) gives the external tensor \(LXR^{\mathsf T}\).
This fixes the transpose convention for the contraction kernels below.

For an arity-\(r\) tensor \(f\), ordered inputs \(p=(i,j)\), and an assignment to the remaining inputs \({\mathbf z}\), the \emph{two-input slice} is the binary
matrix
\[
 F^f_{p,{\mathbf z}}(a,b):=
 f\bigl({\mathbf z}^{i\leftarrow a,j\leftarrow b}\bigr),
 \qquad a,b\in\Ftwo.
\]
If \(\mathscr K\le\Mat_2(\mathbb C)\) is a space of contraction matrices, its
Frobenius-orthogonal space is
\[
 \mathscr K^{\perp_{\mathrm{Frob}}}:=
 \{F\in\Mat_2(\mathbb C):
   \langle C,F\rangle_{\mathrm{Frob}}=0\text{ for every }C\in\mathscr K\}.
\]
Thus contractions against \(\mathscr K\) determine the slice modulo
\(\mathscr K^{\perp_{\mathrm{Frob}}}\).

We determine the transfer span and use its fixed identification with
contraction kernels to recover two-input slices.

\begin{definition}[The transfer span \(\mathscr K_G\)]
\label{def:effective-kernel-algebra}
For a retained set in the fixed-\(I,X\) coordinates, with
complete transfer group \(G\), let
\(\mathscr K_G\) be the complex span of the transfer matrices
\(T_B=BX\) of all finite ordered two-input chains \(B\) made from the fixed representatives \(B_g\), \(g\in G\), the explicit \(I\)- or
\(X\)-edge links, and their input reversals.  This is equivalently the span
of independently dressed \(I/X\) kernels after the fixed right
multiplication by \(X\), which is invertible and therefore only relabels
the corresponding slice coordinates. For a closing binary \(B\), its
contraction kernel is \(C=XBX=XT_B\). Thus
\(\langle C,F\rangle_{\mathrm{Frob}}=\langle T_B,XF\rangle_{\mathrm{Frob}}\).
We use this fixed identification when reconstructing slices from
\(\mathscr K_G\).
\end{definition}

The information determined by binary contractions depends on
\(\mathscr K_G\).  The following theorem gives its two possible values.

Define the unnormalized Walsh matrix \(P\) and, for a signature \(f\)
already in \(K\)-coordinates, its Walsh-coordinate tensor \(\widetilde f\) by
\begin{equation}
  P:=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\end{pmatrix},
  \qquad
  \widetilde f:=(P^{-1})^{\otimes\arity(f)}f.
  \label{eq:Walsh-coordinates}
\end{equation}

The normalized Walsh matrix used in the proof of
\cref{thm:dressed-kernel-observability} is
\(S=P/\sqrt2\). Thus \(SXS=Z\), whereas \(P^{\mathsf T}XP=2Z\).
Since \(P^{-1}A(t)P=2D(t)\), the cyclic group in Walsh coordinates is
\(P^{-1}\CycX{N}P=C_N^{\rm std}\).


\begin{theorem}[The span of realizable binary transfers]
\label{thm:dressed-kernel-observability}
Let \(\cF\) be a retained set with normalized \(I,X\), and let
\(G=G(\cF)\).  With \(\mathscr K_G\) as in
\cref{def:effective-kernel-algebra},
\[
  \mathscr K_G\in
  \bigl\{\Span\{I,X\},\Mat_2(\mathbb C)\bigr\}.
\]
Moreover,
\[
 \Span\{I,X\}^{\perp_{\mathrm{Frob}}}
 =
 \left\{
  \begin{pmatrix}u&v\\-v&-u\end{pmatrix}:u,v\in\mathbb C
 \right\}.
\]
\end{theorem}

If \(\mathscr K_G=\Mat_2(\mathbb C)\), four realizable kernel evaluations
determine each two-input slice.  If \(\mathscr K_G=\Span\{I,X\}\), they
determine it modulo the displayed orthogonal complement.

\begin{proof}
Because \(B_1=X\), the span contains \(I\), and the explicit \(I\)-link
gives \(X\).  Series composition, with the second chain taken in its
inward (reversed) input order, has tensor in the original basis \(BXC\) and satisfies
\(T_{BXC}=T_BT_C\), so the span is an algebra.  Reversal gives
\(T^\#=XT^{\mathsf T}X\). Since \(X\) is in the span,
\(T^{\mathsf T}=XT^\#X\) is also in it.  Thus \(\mathscr K_G\) is unital
and transpose-stable.

Conjugate by \(S=P/\sqrt2\), so \(SXS=Z\).  For \(A\) in the conjugated algebra, define its diagonal
and off-diagonal parts by \(D:=(A+ZAZ)/2\) and \(O:=(A-ZAZ)/2\).  If some
\(O\ne0\), write
\(O=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&b\\c&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\).  Since \(Z\)
and \(O\) lie in the algebra, so does
\(ZO=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&b\\-c&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\).  If
\(bc\ne0\), \(O+ZO=2bE_{01}\) and \(O-ZO=2cE_{10}\), where \(E_{01}\)
and \(E_{10}\) use Boolean row and column indices. If one entry is zero,
transpose-stability supplies the missing unit.  Products supply the diagonal
units, hence the algebra is \(\Mat_2(\mathbb C)\).  If every \(O=0\), it is diagonal and contains \(I,Z\), so it is
\(\Span\{I,Z\}\), which conjugates back to \(\Span\{I,X\}\).

In the full case, four realizable transfer matrices can be greedily chosen as a
basis. Their corresponding kernel evaluations reconstruct every two-input slice
under the fixed identification above.  In the proper case, orthogonality to
\(I,X\) is \(a+d=b+c=0\), giving exactly
\(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u&v\\-v&-u\end{smallmatrix}\right)\).
\end{proof}

For the finite groups routed above, observability identifies the routing split
intrinsically:
\[
 G\text{ is cyclic}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathscr K_G=\Span\{I,X\},
\]
\[
 G\text{ is dihedral, Klein four, or Platonic}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathscr K_G=\Mat_2(\mathbb C).
\]
Indeed, the cyclic transfer classes lie in \(\Span\{I,X\}\), whereas a
finite projective subgroup of the units of this proper algebra is cyclic.
Thus \cref{sec:proper-deck} treats \(\mathscr K_G=\Span\{I,X\}\).
The other three branches have \(\mathscr K_G=\Mat_2(\mathbb C)\).

To see what the kernel span tells us about a stable \(f\), fix an ordered
pair \(p\) of its inputs and vary the closing kernel, keeping the other
inputs in the same order. If three available kernels satisfy
\(C_3=\alpha C_1+\beta C_2\), their cards satisfy
\[
  \partial_p^{C_3}f=\alpha\,\partial_p^{C_1}f+\beta\,\partial_p^{C_2}f .
\]
At an assignment where the first two cards vanish, the third must vanish
as well. The proposed matchings and binary factors must give values
consistent with this identity at every assignment.

When the kernels span \(\Mat_2(\mathbb C)\), choose four that form a basis.
Fixing \(p\) to \(a,b\in\{0,1\}\) gives a residual signature \(f_{ab}\)
with \(f_{ab}({\mathbf z})=F^f_{p,{\mathbf z}}(a,b)\).
Thus each \(f_{ab}\) is a linear combination of the four cards. Suppose the nonzero cards among them all have
a common factor \(h\) on the same nonempty block of remaining inputs. Then
\(f_{ab}=h\otimes r_{ab}\) for each \(a,b\), allowing \(r_{ab}=0\).
Define \(r\) by \(r(a,b,\mathord\cdot)=r_{ab}\) in the input order
with \(p\) first. Then \(f=h\otimes r\) up to input order. Since \(r\)
contains the two inputs in \(p\), this contradicts irreducibility.
In the cyclic case the contractions recover only combinations of the four residual signatures.
\Cref{sec:proper-deck} compares these combinations across different input pairs.

\subsubsection{The arity-four reductions}

We first process the binary contractions of a quaternary signature. This
local step checks which complete group applies before the common gadget
construction is used.

For a quaternary tensor \(q\) with ordered inputs \(1,2,3,4\), let
\(M_{x_ix_j,x_kx_\ell}(q)\) be its \(4\times4\) signature matrix, with row index
\((x_i,x_j)\) and column index \((x_k,x_\ell)\).  For a finite
complete transfer group \(G\) and its fixed representatives
\(B_g\), \(g\in G\), define
\[
 \mathcal M_4(G)=
 \left\{
 c\,B_g(x_i,x_j)B_h(x_k,x_\ell):
 \begin{array}{l}
 c\in\AlgNums^\times,\ ((i,j),(k,\ell))\text{ is an oriented}\\[-2pt]
 \text{perfect matching of }[4],\quad g,h\in G
 \end{array}
 \right\}.
\]
Thus \(\mathcal M_4(G)\) is the arity-four \(G\)-matching notion of \cref{def:matching-deck}.  Define the
complete oriented one-copy binary batch as follows.  For an ordered binary
card \(C\), set \(C^{\mathrm{id}}=C\) and
\(C^{\mathrm{rev}}=C^{\mathsf T}\), and put
\[
 \mathscr D_G^{(1)}(q)
 =
 \{0\}\cup
 \left\{
   \bigl(\partial_{(i,j)}^{XB_gX}q\bigr)^\epsilon:
   i,j\in[4],\ i\ne j,\ g\in G,\
   \epsilon\in\{\mathrm{id},\mathrm{rev}\}
 \right\}.
\]
The order \((i,j)\) records the orientation of the deleted pair, and the
choice of \(\epsilon\) records either orientation of the residual pair.
Every displayed member is a direct gadget: \(XB_gX\) is the realizable kernel
obtained by attaching
\(B_g\) through its two \(X\)-edges, and residual reversal is a permutation of inputs.  In this local case analysis, a
\emph{nonbinary irreducible signature} means an irreducible signature of arity at least
three.

\begin{lemma}[Binary contractions of a quaternary signature]
\label{lem:finite-q4-dispatch}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic retained, factor-closed,
  binary-residual signature set containing the supplied equality
\(I\), suppose every retained nonbinary irreducible signature has even arity, and put
\(G=G(\cF)\).  Let \(q\) be a realizable signature over \(\cF\), with
its ordered inputs, realization, and nonzero scalar recorded.  Assume that
\(q\) is endpoint-nondegenerate, irreducible, and
\[
 q\notin\mathcal M_4(G).
\]
The three flattenings satisfy
\[
 \rank M_{x_1x_2,x_3x_4}(q)\ge2,\qquad
 \rank M_{x_1x_3,x_2x_4}(q)\ge2,\qquad
 \rank M_{x_1x_4,x_2x_3}(q)\ge2.
\]
These inequalities follow from irreducibility and give the rank
conditions used in the later arity-four arguments.

Let \(\cF_q\) be the preprocessed signature set obtained by
adding \(q\) and all nonzero tensor cards in
\(\mathscr D_G^{(1)}(q)\), followed by factor closure of this
finite batch.  Then
\[
 \KHolant(\cF_q)\equivT\KHolant(\cF),
\]
and one of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\roman*)}]
\item an established complexity conclusion applies, including one obtained from an
      odd-arity or unary signature, a nonzero singular binary, or
      interpolation from a realizable infinite-order binary. Alternatively,
      a retained lower-arity nonbinary irreducible signature gives an
      outer successor with smaller minimum arity;
\item \(\cF_q\) is binary-residual and
      \[
        G<G_q:=G(\cF_q),
      \]
      with the strict inclusion witnessed by an ordered binary \(B\) realizable
      over \(\cF_q\) such that \([BX]\in G_q\setminus G\);
\item \(\cF_q\) is binary-residual, \(G_q=G\), \(q\) is a
      minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature in \(\cF_q\), and every member
      of \(\mathscr D_G^{(1)}(q)\) is zero or projectively equal to one of the
      fixed \(B_g\), \(g\in G\).  All nonzero card factors are retained. On
      this continuing branch every nonzero card has a trivial factorization tree.
\end{enumerate}
Moreover, on branch~(iii), let \(r\) be an auxiliary realizable quaternary
signature over \(\cF_q\), assume \(r\notin\mathcal M_4(G)\), and suppose
that \(r\) has a genuine \(2+2\) factorization.  Let
\(\cF_{q,r}\) be the preprocessed retained, factor-closed signature set
obtained by adding \(r\) and its nonzero factor batch.  Then either
an established reduction determines the complexity or
\[
  G<G(\cF_{q,r}).
\]
Thus there is no same-group continuing branch.  The case distinction is finite.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The batch \(\mathscr D_G^{(1)}(q)\) is finite: there are six unordered
deleted pairs, two orders for the closing kernel, two residual orders, and
finitely many closing-representative choices \(B_g\).  Its tensor cards are
realizable over \(\cF\), since \(q\) and every \(B_g\) have direct realizations.  Adding them therefore gives one Turing reduction by
\cref{eq:direct-gadget-reduction}, while inclusion gives the reverse one.
Because every resulting set contains \(I\) and is two-sided,
\cref{lem:factor-saturation} may retain the finite factorization tree without
changing the problem up to Turing equivalence.

Dispatch every odd-arity or unary factor and every singular binary factor.
If \(\cF_q\) directly realizes any nonsingular binary of infinite
projective transfer order, apply \cref{lem:binary-interpolation}. This is
item~(i).  Otherwise \(\cF_q\) is binary-residual, so
\cref{lem:bounded-torsion} defines the finite group
\(G_q=G(\cF_q)\).  Retained inclusion gives \(G\le G_q\).  If the
inclusion is strict, the definition of \(G_q\) supplies a realizable binary
representative of a class in \(G_q\setminus G\), proving item~(ii).
The group-enlargement witness is a realizable binary gadget over \(\cF_q\), possibly
using several copies of \(q\).

It remains that \(G_q=G\).  Every nonzero card in
\(\mathscr D_G^{(1)}(q)\) is then nonsingular and has transfer class in
\(G_q=G\), hence is projectively one of the fixed \(B_g\).  Such a binary
has no proper positive-arity tensor factorization.  The tensor \(q\) remains
irreducible under tensor factorization.  Any nonbinary irreducible signature of arity below four would have
arity three and would already have triggered the odd-arity reduction, so
\(q\) has minimum arity of a nonbinary irreducible signature.  This proves item~(iii).

Finally, write \(r\doteq C_1\otimes C_2\) for the auxiliary factorization.
Factor closure makes \(C_1,C_2\) signatures of
\(\cF_{q,r}\). They are not asserted to have been realizable over
\(\cF_q\).  If either is singular, or if the resulting set realizes an infinite-order binary, an established reduction determines the complexity.  Otherwise
\(\cF_{q,r}\) is binary-residual and
\(G\le G(\cF_{q,r})\).  If equality held, both factors would be
nonsingular and would represent classes in \(G\), whence
\(r\in\mathcal M_4(G)\), contradicting the stated hypothesis.  The inclusion
is therefore strict.
\end{proof}

Only item~(iii) of \cref{lem:finite-q4-dispatch} is passed to the
following arity-four construction. The preceding proof uses the finite
local batch and the complete-group comparison, before any appeal to
the global descent measure. The complete stabilized deck permits the
following gadget construction.  Its output is a realizable nonmatching eight-vertex
signature to which \cref{cor:p1-eight-vertex-reduced-interface} applies.

\begin{theorem}[Quaternary signatures with stable matching decks]
\label{thm:equality-q4-boundary}
Assume
\[
 \Stable(\cF,G,q),\qquad \arity(q)=\nu(\cF)=4,
\]
and that \(G\) is one of
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(G=\{1,[X]\}\cong C_2\);
\item \(G=\{[D(t)],[XD(t)]:t\in\mu_m\}\), \(m\ge3\);
\item the standard Pauli \(\KleinStd\);
\item the exotic Klein group
  \begin{equation*}
   \KleinEx
   =
   \left\{[I],[X],
   \left[\begin{pmatrix}i&1\\-1&-i\end{pmatrix}\right],
   \left[\begin{pmatrix}-i&1\\-1&i\end{pmatrix}\right]\right\}.
  \end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
Then a gadget using at most two copies of \(q\) produces an
endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature that is not \(G\)-matching.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The gadget in \cref{thm:cert-interface-equality-q4} uses at most two
copies of the selected signature and gives the asserted nonmatching
endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex output. Its directly realized signature
\(r\) satisfies
\[
  \KHolant(\cF,r)
  \leT\KHolant(\cF).\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

To apply \cref{thm:equality-q4-boundary}, first regenerate the current
quaternary card and its complete oriented one-copy deck, close under
factors, and process them as in \cref{lem:finite-q4-dispatch}.  Auxiliary cards used inside the finite
certificate are local to that proof and are not appended to \(Q\).  Only an added factor batch returned by the eight-vertex reduction can be passed to an
outer closure.

The quantifiers include zero cards, both input orientations for all six
contracted pairs, every current-group representative assignment on the
remaining matched edges, and the fixed representative scalars. Apply
\cref{cor:p1-eight-vertex-reduced-interface} to the output over the whole
retained set. In items~2--4 and the one-copy case of item~1, invertible
dressings preserve irreducibility, so only a hard or tractable case remains.

In the exceptional \(\CycX{2}\) subcase, write
\(a_S=\widetilde q(\one_S)\).  Call it \emph{mixed-sign} when
\(a_\varnothing=a_{[4]}=0\) and two nonzero complementary weight-two
blocks \(\{S,\bar S\}\) have different ratios
\(a_{\bar S}/a_S\in\{1,-1\}\), as in
\cref{thm:cert-interface-equality-q4}\textnormal{(i)}. Only this mixed-sign
\(\CycX{2}\) subcase can reach the continuing factor outcome after the two-copy
construction. Retain its complete added factor batch. The last assertion of
\cref{lem:finite-q4-dispatch} resolves the complexity or gives a strictly
larger complete transfer group, since the output is nonmatching. Every added
factor remains available by reduction over the predecessor, and only this
outer successor is passed back to stabilization.


Every bounded enumeration appears as an exact proposition in
Appendix~\ref{sec:exact-finite-certificates}. The main text supplies the reduction for the whole signature set and its direction.  The coverage map
\path{certificates/theorem-to-script-map.md} records the manuscript anchors
and finite claims checked by each entry point, and
\path{certificates/README.md} gives the replay instructions and the
package-wide computational scope.  The next four sections use the available contraction kernels to constrain
higher-arity signatures in their respective group cases. Each supplies
a complexity conclusion or proves the strict progress required by
\cref{def:protocol-discharged}. \Cref{sec:p1-synthesis} combines these
arguments with stabilization and the outer induction over irreducible
occurrences to classify the whole signature set. We begin with the
marked dihedral case.
\section{Marked Dihedral Matching Decks}
\label{sec:normalized-dihedral}

This section proves the marked-dihedral case of the deck-stable
classification. A deck-stable signature has only zero or \(G\)-matching
proper cards. The difficulty is that different cards may use different
matchings or different binary factors. We show how these local choices
force a proper factor of a higher-arity signature, contradicting its
irreducibility. At arity four, realizable eight-vertex signatures give
the complexity conclusion for the entire retained set.

For the standard group \(\DihZ{2m}\), monomial kernels let us shorten
alternating cycles and preserve selected differences between two cards.
This reduces a disagreement to six inputs, where the pencil
classification and a finite support analysis apply. When the cards
agree outside one binary factor, two phase evaluations recover the four
residual signatures obtained by fixing the deleted pair, giving a common
tensor factor of the original signature.

The other marked groups require control of the edge matrix as well as
the binary transfers. Whole-network equivalences carry
\(\DihY{2m}\) and \(\DihXzero{2m}\) to the standard case.
For \(\DihXone{2m}\), explicit weighted kernels preserve the local
comparisons. The support and coefficient reconstruction developed below
supplies the weighted-kernel argument, including its six-input case.
Each branch gives the conclusion required in
\cref{def:protocol-discharged}; their combination proves the following theorem.

\begin{theorem}[Marked-dihedral deck rigidity]
\label{thm:marked-dihedral}
Assume
\[
 \Stable(\cF,G,f),\qquad \Even(\cF),
\]
and suppose that the complete transfer group has one of the following marked forms:
\[
 G\in
 \{\DihZ{2m}:m\ge3\}
 \cup
 \{\DihY{2m}:m\ge3\}
 \cup
 \{\DihXzero{2m}:m\ge4,\ 2\mid m\}
 \cup
 \{\DihXone{2m}:m\ge4,\ 2\mid m\}.
\]
Then \(\Close_{\{\cP\}}(\cF,G,f)\).
\end{theorem}

The four cases are proved in
\cref{thm:normalized-dihedral,thm:marked-B-reduction,%
thm:marked-A0-reduction,thm:marked-A-rigidity}.

\paragraph{Contractions in the standard case.}
Until \cref{sec:marked-dihedral-sectors}, fix \(G=\DihZ{2m}\),
\(m\ge3\). Let \(n:=\arity(f)\).  We use the conventions of
\cref{subsec:reduction-conventions,def:relative-stratum-protocol,%
def:protocol-discharged} throughout.

If \(n=4\), first regenerate the complete deck of \(f\) and close it under
irreducible factors, apply \cref{thm:equality-q4-boundary}, and apply the eight-vertex reduction to its realizable
endpoint-nondegenerate output using
\cref{cor:p1-eight-vertex-reduced-interface}.  The added factor batch is
closed by \cref{lem:actual-binary-completeness,lem:factor-saturation}. It
gives a resolved complexity leaf or a strict stable successor.  Thus the arity-four
base is resolved, and throughout the remaining standard \(\DihZ{2m}\)
proof we may assume
\[
 n=2k+2\ge6.
\]

For every \(t\in\mu_m\), the representative convention of
\cref{subsec:reduction-conventions} fixes realizable ordered binary outputs
\begin{equation*}
  B_{[D(t)]}=\sigma_t^{\mathrm{diag}}D(t)X,
  \qquad
  B_{[XD(t)]}=\sigma_t^{\mathrm{anti}}XD(t)X,
  \qquad
  \sigma_t^{\mathrm{diag}},\sigma_t^{\mathrm{anti}}\ne0.
\end{equation*}
For a deleted set \(p=\{i,j\}\) equipped with the input order \((i,j)\),
write the four residual signatures obtained by fixing that pair as
\begin{equation*}
  A:=f(00,\mathord\cdot),\quad
  B:=f(01,\mathord\cdot),\quad
  C:=f(10,\mathord\cdot),\quad
  D_{11}:=f(11,\mathord\cdot),
\end{equation*}
and define the associated \((n-2)\)-ary \emph{sector pencils} by
\hypertarget{eq:nd-sector-pencils}{}
\begin{equation*}
 E(t):=A+tD_{11},
 \qquad
 O(t):=C+tB.
\end{equation*}
Here \(E(t)\) is the equal/even sector associated with the deleted-pair
values \(00,11\), while \(O(t)\) is the unequal/odd sector associated with
\(10,01\).  A pencil means a one-parameter linear family \(U+tV\).
The corresponding contraction-kernel spaces
\[
 \Span\{E_{00},E_{11}\}
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 \Span\{E_{01},E_{10}\}
\]
are the even and odd \emph{parity planes}, with the displayed matrix
units as coordinate axes.
For \(t\in\mu_m\), the \(X\)-edges give the exact realizable outputs
\(\sigma_t^{\mathrm{diag}}O(t)\) and \(\sigma_t^{\mathrm{anti}}E(t)\), since
\[
  XB_{[D(t)]}X
    =\sigma_t^{\mathrm{diag}}XD(t),
  \qquad
  XB_{[XD(t)]}X
    =\sigma_t^{\mathrm{anti}}D(t).
\]
For \(t\in\mu_m\), these outputs are deck cards with the displayed
nonzero scalars. The four slices fix the inputs of the deleted pair and
leave all other inputs free. They are the coefficient tensors of the two
sector pencils and will be called their \emph{pencil endpoints}.
Two phase evaluations recover these tensors by linear inversion.
Later \(K_E(t),K_O(t)\in\Mat_2(\mathbb C)\) denote the varying
binary factors of the two sector pencils.

We use \emph{deck card} for a member of the matching deck in
\cref{def:matching-deck}. A sequence of contractions gives a
\emph{contraction output}; constructions using two copies give
\emph{two-copy outputs}.

Write a nonsingular monomial binary, up to scalar, as
\begin{equation*}
  M_\alpha(a)(x,y)
  =\one[x\oplus y=\alpha]a^x,
  \qquad
  \alpha\in\Ftwo,\quad a\ne0.
\end{equation*}
Here \(\alpha\) is its support parity and \(M_\alpha(a)=D(a)X^\alpha\). In particular, \(M_0(a)=D(a)\), while \(XD(t)=tM_1(t^{-1})\) for \(t\ne0\). Reversal changes only the weight and a nonzero scalar.

\subsection{Trace covers and alternating-cycle localization}
\label{proofsubsec:nd-trace-cover}

Consider two nonzero sampled cards of the same signature.  Their matchings and
binary factor classes can disagree.  We will contract common parts while
preserving a disagreement, leaving only four or six inputs to analyze.

\paragraph{Projective tensor geometry.}
\phantomsection\label{subsec:projective-incidence-conventions}

A matching product is a simple tensor after its inputs are grouped into
pairs. Segre geometry expresses this factorization by rank equations.

\label{subsubsec:projective-tensor-geometry}

We use the projective-space conventions of
\cref{subsubsec:group-projective}.  Whenever a projective object occurs in a
tensor equation, a displayed or previously fixed nonzero
representative is understood. We set \(\mathbb P(0):=\varnothing\).

A nonzero tensor \({\mathbf{v}}_1\otimes\cdots\otimes {\mathbf{v}}_s\) is \emph{simple}.  For finite-dimensional spaces \(V_1,\ldots,V_s\), the
Segre map is
\[
 \mathbb P(V_1)\times\cdots\times\mathbb P(V_s)
 \longrightarrow \mathbb P(V_1\otimes\cdots\otimes V_s),
 \qquad ([{\mathbf{v}}_1],\ldots,[{\mathbf{v}}_s])\longmapsto[{\mathbf{v}}_1\otimes\cdots\otimes {\mathbf{v}}_s].
\]
Its image is the \emph{Segre variety}. Its affine cone consists of zero and
the simple tensors.  The Segre ideal is
generated by the \(2\)-by-\(2\) minors of the bipartite flattenings.  A
\emph{one-factor Segre ruling} is a projective linear family in which all
factors but one are fixed, and every projective line contained in a Segre
variety belongs to such a ruling \cite[Chapter~2]{Landsberg2012}.


For two perfect matchings \(M_0,M_1\), an \emph{alternating
\(M_0/M_1\)-cycle} is an even cycle in \(M_0\mathbin\triangle M_1\) whose
edges alternate between the two matchings.  We call a sequence of the
contractions below a \emph{trace cover}. It closes common edges and
shortens alternating cycles while retaining a chosen discrepancy.  Its ordered contraction graph records the connections, edge orientations,
and external-input order.

\begin{definition}[Disagreements between matching products]
\label{def:nd-live-card-disagreement}
The \emph{factor class} of an ordered nonzero binary \(B\) is its
projective class \([B]\). Normalize the sampled deck cards by their
nonzero realization scalars. Two nonzero cards
\emph{disagree} if their residual matchings differ, or if their ordered
binary factor classes differ on at least two edges of the same
matching.  A parity change changes the factor class.  Variation on one designated edge, including its parity, is allowed.

A one-factor Segre ruling, as defined in
\hyperref[subsubsec:projective-tensor-geometry]{the projective tensor conventions}, allows one binary factor to
vary and fixes the other factors on the matching. Thus products on one
such ruling differ in at most that factor class.
\end{definition}

Consider two realizable nonzero sector cards
\begin{equation}
  P_0=\bigotimes_{e\in M_0}B_{0,e},
  \qquad
  P_1=\bigotimes_{e\in M_1}B_{1,e},
  \label{eq:app-nd-two-products}
\end{equation}
where every \(B_{\epsilon,e}\) is a nonsingular monomial binary from the
normalized group.

We close common edges whose ordered binary factors agree projectively.
Connecting one input on each of two residual edges by a realizable
binary fuses those edges in each product. The following identities show
how to choose these contractions while preserving selected parity
differences or nonzero determinants.

\begin{definition}[Parity and coefficient discrepancies]
\label{def:app-nd-tc-tag}
After aligning the common edges of two sector products, a residual matched
factor is a \emph{parity discrepancy} if its support parity changes between the two products.  It is a \emph{coefficient discrepancy} if the parities agree but the two
ordered nonzero-entry vectors are not projectively proportional. In that
case the associated nonzero \(2\times2\) determinant records the coefficient discrepancy.

\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Compatible contractions and cycle shortening]
\label{lem:app-nd-compatible-closure}
Common factors with the same projective class in \cref{eq:app-nd-two-products} can be closed by
realizable group binaries while keeping both products nonzero.  An alternating
\(M_0/M_1\)-cycle can be shortened by two inputs without cancellation, and
a selected coefficient determinant is only multiplied by a nonzero scalar.
Two edges with discrepancies can be fused by a realizable connection while preserving
any distinguished remaining discrepancy.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For ordered monomial factors one has, up to the fixed orientation scalar,
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{x,y}M_\alpha(a)(x,y)M_\alpha(t)(x,y)&=1+at,\\
 \sum_zM_\alpha(a)(u,z)M_\beta(b)(z,v)
  &=b^\alpha M_{\alpha+\beta}
       \bigl(ab^{1-2\alpha}\bigr)(u,v).
\end{align*}
Thus on a common edge the two product traces are nonzero affine functions
of \(t\in\mu_m\). Together they exclude at most two phases.  Since
\(m\ge3\), one allowed phase keeps both nonzero.  Closing an edge then
splices the two incident edges in the other matching.  The displayed fusion has a unique internal lift: each supported
external assignment extends to exactly one supported internal assignment.
Thus there is no cancellation, and every selected minor is multiplied
by nonzero trace and fusion factors.

For an ordered edge with a discrepancy \(q=(u_q,v_q)\), let
\(\alpha_{\xi,q}\in\Ftwo\) denote its support parity in product
\(\xi\in\Ftwo\).  Put
\[
 \delta_q:=\alpha_{0,q}+\alpha_{1,q}\in\Ftwo
 \qquad(q\in\{r,s\}),\qquad
 \alpha_{\xi,q}=\alpha_{0,q}+\xi\delta_q.
\]
One ordered connection \(M_\gamma(t)\) fuses the two factors in each
product.  Applying the displayed fusion identity twice gives
\[
 M_\alpha(a)M_\gamma(t)M_\beta(b)
 =t^\alpha b^{\alpha\oplus\gamma}
 M_{\alpha\oplus\gamma\oplus\beta}
 \!\left(a\,t^{1-2\alpha}b^{1-2(\alpha\oplus\gamma)}\right).
\]
For each supported exposed string the internal lift is unique, and the
fusion scalar is nonzero.  The fused parity difference is
\(\delta_r+\delta_s\).  A mixed pair therefore leaves a parity
discrepancy. Equal types leave at most one coefficient discrepancy, which
may also cancel.  A distinguished discrepancy outside the two spliced
edges is preserved: its determinant is multiplied only by the nonzero
orientation and fusion scalars of the two products.  Each connection
removes two inputs, whether or not the two spliced discrepancies cancel.
All operations use only the \(X\)-edge, the explicit normalized
\(I\)-link,
and fixed realizable group representatives.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Binary factor classes and a quaternary contraction]
\label{lem:app-nd-coefficient-line-factor}
For distinct \(t_0,t_1\in\mu_m\), suppose the four nonzero realizable cards
represented by \(E(t_0),E(t_1),O(t_0),O(t_1)\) at a deleted pair \(p\)
have a common residual matching edge \(e\), with the same support parity.
If all four ordered factors on \(e\) are proportional to one binary \(B_e\), the signature \(f\) has the exact tensor factorization
\[
  f=B_e\otimes h
\]
with a nonsingular monomial binary \(B_e\).  Otherwise choose two cards
whose ordered factors on \(e\) are nonproportional.  If their other
matching edges and support parities agree, a contraction sequence retaining
\(p\cup e\) has a nonzero \(2\times2\) flattening minor.  Without this
parity compatibility no simultaneous trace is asserted.  The factor
identity is analytic. Its factors are exposed only by factor closure.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For the nonproportional pair with the stated compatibility, close every
other common matching edge successively.  At each step
the two traces exclude at most two roots, so
\cref{lem:app-nd-compatible-closure} keeps both products nonzero.  The two
surviving \(e\)-vectors remain nonproportional.  They are contractions of
the resulting realizable quaternary by two kernels on \(p\). Hence that quaternary has a
nonzero flattening minor.  The two kernels are linearly independent: two
distinct phases are independent inside one parity plane, while an even and
an odd kernel have different support parities.  Thus nonproportional
retained \(e\)-vectors really give flattening rank at least two.

For the factor alternative all four ordered \(e\)-factors are proportional. Choose their common representative
\[
 B_e(x_u,x_v)=\one[x_u\oplus x_v=\alpha]b_{x_u},
 \qquad b_0b_1\ne0,
\]
and absorb each card's nonzero scalar into its complementary tensor.  The
two-card inversions
\[
 A=\frac{t_1E(t_0)-t_0E(t_1)}{t_1-t_0},\quad
 D_{11}=\frac{E(t_1)-E(t_0)}{t_1-t_0},\qquad
 C=\frac{t_1O(t_0)-t_0O(t_1)}{t_1-t_0},\quad
 B=\frac{O(t_1)-O(t_0)}{t_1-t_0}
\]
put every slice in \(\mathbb C B_e\otimes V\), where \(V\) is the tensor
space on the residual inputs outside \(e\).  Reassembling the four
slices gives the displayed tensor identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Two-card localization to six inputs]
\label{lem:app-nd-localization}
Fix the deleted pair \(p\), and let two nonzero realizable sampled
\(p\)-cards of the same signature disagree.  There is a realizable
one-copy contraction \(h\), retaining \(p\) and four further inputs, whose
two inherited \(p\)-cards remain nonzero and either have different
residual matchings or differ on both edges of their common residual
matching.  The contraction closes only inputs outside \(p\) and uses
\(O(\arity(f))\) fixed realizable group representatives.  In particular,
\(h\) is not a \(G\)-matching signature.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For each selected pair of cards, choose contractions that keep both
outputs nonzero and preserve their disagreement.

First suppose the two residual matchings differ.  Choose one alternating
cycle as distinguished.  On every other alternating cycle, close an edge
of the first matching by a kernel of the same support parity.  The trace
in the first product excludes at most one phase, whereas the other
product has a unique monomial lift.  Thus both products remain nonzero.
Repeat until that nondistinguished cycle becomes one common edge.

Every common edge outside the distinguished cycle can now be absorbed
into the cycle without any parity compatibility assumption.  Write that
edge as \(\{u,w\}\), select a cycle vertex \(v\), and connect
\(u,v\) by any realizable monomial group kernel.  If the two cycle neighbours
of \(v\) are \(v_0,v_1\), the new matching edges are respectively
\(\{w,v_0\}\) and \(\{w,v_1\}\).  Thus the alternating cycle is
unchanged except that \(w\) replaces \(v\).  Both internal lifts are
unique and have nonzero weights, including when the old common edge had
different support parities in the two products.  Absorb every remaining
common edge in this way.  Finally shorten the distinguished cycle, using
the same trace-and-fusion operation, until it is a four-cycle.  Stop
before closing an edge of that four-cycle.  The two residual four-input
matchings are therefore still different.

Now suppose the residual matching agrees and at least two factor classes
differ.  Select two such edges whose discrepancies will be retained.  Every other edge whose two support
parities agree can be traced directly: the two traces exclude at most
two phases, so \(m\ge3\) leaves an allowed phase keeping both products nonzero.  The two selected edges are untouched by these traces.

An edge outside the selected pair with different support parities is instead
fused with one selected edge by a realizable binary connection.  The other selected edge remains
untouched.  If the selected edge used in the fusion has equal parities but different
factor classes, the fused edge has different support parities and
retains a parity discrepancy.  If both fused edges have different support parities, the
new support parities agree, but the ratio of their normalized fused
weights is
\[
  \kappa t^{\pm2},\qquad \kappa\ne0.
\]
This follows directly from the three-factor fusion identity in
\cref{lem:app-nd-compatible-closure}.  At most two phases make this ratio
equal to one.  Choose another allowed phase. The fused edge then carries a
coefficient discrepancy.  In both cases the operation reduces the number of edges
by one and leaves two edges whose factor classes differ between the products.  Continue until
exactly these two edges remain.

Perform the selected contractions on the original signature, leaving the
inputs of \(p\) open.  Tensor contraction commutes with either of the two
original \(p\)-kernels, so its inherited cards are precisely the two
nonzero products just constructed, with their nonzero scalars.
Each operation removes two inputs, giving the stated size bound.

If \(h\) were a product on three matching edges, deleting \(p\) would
either trace out one of its matching edges, leaving the other two factors
fixed, or fuse its two incident matching edges, leaving the third factor
fixed.  Whenever the two resulting cards are nonzero, their residual
matching is therefore the same and at most the fused factor can vary.
This contradicts either of the two constructed configurations.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Reduction to four inputs or a tensor factor]
\label{lem:app-nd-bounded-consumer}
Every arity-six signature produced in \cref{lem:app-nd-localization} has a complete
reduction through its proper factor and deck batches.  It cannot
close as a \(G\)-matching signature.  A quaternary child is processed
by its own complete factor and deck batch. A crossed factorization into safe binaries
completes that child only.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(h\) be the realizable six-input output, with its inherited nonzero cards,
input order, realization scalar, and originating fixed token retained.
By \cref{lem:app-nd-localization}, \(h\) is not a three-factor matching
product.  Add \(h\), jointly close the enlarged set under irreducible factors, and close every proper
factor and every proper oriented deck card.  Odd or unary factors and
unsafe binaries or safe binaries whose transfer classes lie outside the current group follow their
established exits.  Any proper nonbinary irreducible signature has arity at most four and
is processed by the lower-arity induction, including the arity-four
quaternary deck classification stated in the setup.  Thus this processing does
not use the six-input case being proved.

If no resolved leaf or strict successor occurs, all surviving proper
factors are \(G\)-matching signatures.  A proper factorization of
\(h\) would therefore make \(h\) itself a matching product, a
contradiction.  Otherwise \(h\) is irreducible and every nonzero proper
pair card is a \(G\)-matching signature.  \Cref{lem:nd-six-prime-base} excludes this case by the sector-ruling and
binary-pencil lemmas together with the Boolean support classification.

For a realizable quaternary child, first close its complete factor batch.
If it factors into safe current-group binaries, this completes the child
card index.  Otherwise use the established binary exits or retain its irreducible
quaternary occurrence, regenerate the complete oriented deck, and apply
\cref{thm:equality-q4-boundary,cor:p1-eight-vertex-reduced-interface}.
Every returned factor batch is closed by
\cref{lem:actual-binary-completeness,lem:factor-saturation}.
A factorization completes the corresponding child output. The selected
signature remains under analysis.

In particular, for an active deck-stable signature of arity at least eight there
is an immediate contradiction: the first proper pair contraction outside
\(p\) is zero or a matching product, and further monomial contractions
preserve that property.  Yet localization keeps the two disagreeing
inherited cards nonzero.  The same argument applies after the proper
lower-arity batches of a later occurrence have been closed.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Common-factor consequence.}
\phantomsection\label{par:nd-common-factor-consequence}
% Lean: ClosureStableFactor.global_varying_coordinate; current_ambient_split.
Let \(\mathscr S\) be a nonempty family of nonzero matching products on
one fixed, nonempty oriented matching \(M\).  Suppose that, for every
\(S,T\in\mathscr S\), their binary factor classes differ on at most one
edge of \(M\).  Then there are an edge \(e\in M\) and a nonzero product
\(P\) on \(M\setminus\{e\}\) such that
\[
  \mathscr S\subseteq\mathbb C P\otimes V_e,
  \qquad V_e:=(\mathbb C^2)^{\otimes e}.
\]
Here \((\mathbb C^2)^{\otimes e}:=\bigotimes_{j\in e}\mathbb C^2\),
with factors in the fixed input order, and proportional
factors are absorbed into the factor on \(e\).  If \(S,T\) differ on
\(e\), a third sample differing from \(S\) on another edge would differ
from \(T\) on two edges.  If all samples are proportional, choose any
edge.  This proves the assertion, including a matching with only one edge,
where \(P\) is a nonzero scalar.

Apply the preceding localization and reduction to each pair of nonzero
samples at a fixed deleted pair.  The closures need preserve only that
pair and may differ between comparisons.  On the continuing branch they
give the hypothesis above for the union of the two sectors, so the same
\(P\) works in both.  Each nonzero pencil has at least two nonzero samples
at distinct phases because \(m\ge3\).  Inverting those two evaluations
puts both endpoint slices in \(\mathbb C P\otimes V_e\). An identically
zero pencil has two zero endpoint slices.  Thus all four slices share
\(P\), which gives the factorization of \(f\) used below.

\begin{lemma}[Sector-ruling rigidity]
\label{lem:app-nd-sector-ruling}
Let \(U+tV\), \(t\in\mu_m\), \(m\ge3\), be a tensor pencil on an
even number of inputs whose samples are zero or current
\(\DihZ{2m}\)-matching signatures.  Its nonzero samples have one fixed
matching and all but at most one binary factor class fixed projectively.
In particular this holds for each of \(E(t)\) and \(O(t)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We use only the sampled matching hypothesis.  A proportional pencil has
one projective value.  Otherwise
its \(m\) samples are nonzero and projectively distinct.  Write
\(N=2^s\) for the support size of an \(s\)-edge matching product.
Two different matching supports \(S_0,S_1\) are affine cosets of the
same dimension, so \(|S_0\cap S_1|\le N/2\).  Every nontrivial linear
combination of the corresponding tensors has at least
\(2N-2|S_0\cap S_1|\ge N\) supported strings outside the intersection.
For a third sample to have exactly \(N\) strings requires equality and
cancellation on the whole intersection.  A single common string fixes that
linear combination projectively.  There is therefore at most one such
third matching point, contradicting \(m\ge4\).

For \(m=3\), every ratio of two nonzero coefficients within a matching
sample lies in \(\mu_3\).  Use a string of \(S_0\cap S_1\) to express
the required cancellation, and choose one string in each of
\(S_0\setminus S_1\) and \(S_1\setminus S_0\).  Their coefficient
ratio in the third sample lies in \(-\mu_3\), disjoint from \(\mu_3\).
This is again impossible.  Thus all sample supports coincide.

The weight-two strings of the common support translation space determine its
matching uniquely, including the parity of each support coset.  In that
fixed tensor factorization every \(2\times2\) Segre flattening
minor is a polynomial in \(t\) of degree at most two.  It vanishes at
all \(m\ge3\) sampled phases and hence identically.  The whole
projective line consists of simple tensors.  The Segre-line criterion
in \hyperref[subsubsec:projective-tensor-geometry]{the projective tensor conventions} therefore permits variation
in only one factor, proving the assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Binary pencils and endpoint compatibility}
\label{proofsubsec:nd-endpoints}

By \cref{lem:app-nd-sector-ruling}, each sector has one
fixed residual matching and at most one varying factor class.  This
subsection classifies those binary factor pencils and proves the
six-input base for the standard case. Further endpoint comparisons
will be used in the weighted reconstruction below.

\subsubsection{Möbius geometry}
\label{subsubsec:mobius-geometry}

These M\"obius conventions control the
ratio of the two nonzero entries in a binary factor.  That ratio is a
fractional-linear function of the contraction phase.  Requiring it to
remain in the same finite phase group forces the two endpoint matrices
onto complementary coordinate axes.

The Riemann sphere is
\(\widehat{\mathbb C}:=\mathbb C\cup\{\infty\}\cong\mathbb P(\mathbb C^2)\),
with affine chart \(z=[z:1]\) and \(\infty=[1:0]\).  A Möbius transformation
is a projective automorphism of this sphere. In the affine chart it is
\[
 z\longmapsto\frac{az+b}{cz+d},
 \qquad ad-bc\ne0,
\]
where \(cz+d=0\) is sent to \(\infty\) and \(\infty\) is interpreted
projectively.  A \emph{generalized circle} is a Euclidean circle
\(\{z:|z-z_0|=r\}\), \(r>0\), or a real affine line together with
\(\infty\). Such a line has the form
\(\{z:\operatorname{Re}(\overline\alpha z)=\beta\}\) for
\(\alpha\in\mathbb C^\times\) and \(\beta\in\mathbb R\).
Möbius transformations carry generalized circles to
generalized circles \cite[Chapter~3]{Beardon1983}.  The unit circle is
\(S^1:=\{z\in\mathbb C:|z|=1\}\). Its counterclockwise cyclic order is the
order of arguments modulo \(2\pi\).


\begin{lemma}[M\"obius stabilizer of a root polygon]
\label{lem:nd-root-mobius}
Let \(m\ge3\), and let the M\"obius map
\[
 \phi:\widehat{\mathbb C}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb C},
 \qquad
 \phi:z\longmapsto\frac{c+dz}{a+bz},
 \qquad ad-bc\ne0,
\]
have no pole on \(\mu_m\) and permute \(\mu_m\).  Then
\[
 \phi(z)=\alpha z
 \qquad\text{or}\qquad
 \phi(z)=\alpha/z,
 \qquad \alpha\in\mu_m.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A M\"obius map sends generalized circles to generalized circles.  Three
distinct roots and their images force \(\phi\) to preserve the unit circle.
Its action on the regular \(m\)-gon preserves or reverses cyclic order, so it
is a rotation or a rotation followed by reflection.  The corresponding
M\"obius maps agree with \(\phi\) on at least three points and hence agree
identically.
\end{proof}

Use the even and odd parity planes and their ordered coordinate axes
defined above.  For a nonsingular
monomial matrix, its \emph{ordered nonzero-entry ratio} is the coefficient
of the second displayed axis divided by that of the first, in the fixed input
orientation.

\begin{lemma}[Classification of binary pencils]
\label{lem:nd-root-pencil}
Let \(A,B\in\Mat_2(\mathbb C)\) be binary signatures, and define the binary
matrix pencil
\[
 K(t):=A+tB,
 \qquad t\in\mu_m,\qquad m\ge3.
\]
Suppose every sampled binary signature \(K(t)\) is zero or a nonsingular
monomial binary whose ordered nonzero-entry ratio lies in \(\mu_m\).  Then
either \(A,B\) are proportional, or
there are a fixed parity plane with coordinate axes \(E_0,E_1\),
\(\lambda\ne0\), and \(\alpha\in\mu_m\) such that
\[
 K(t)=\lambda(E_0+\alpha tE_1)
 \qquad\text{or}\qquad
 K(t)=\lambda(tE_0+\alpha E_1).
\]
Here \(K(1)=A+B\) is a sampled value, whereas \(A=K(0)\) is an analytic
endpoint because \(0\notin\mu_m\).  In the application below, \(K(t)\) is
the one varying binary factor of a higher-arity realizable matching card. Its coefficients are recovered from the sector pencil.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In the nonproportional case no sample is zero and the projective samples are
distinct.  Two of the at least three samples lie in one of the two monomial
parity planes. They span the pencil, so all samples lie in that plane.  In
these axes, write the matrix pencil with coefficient scalars \(a,b,c,d\)
and projective coordinate ratio \(\phi\) as
\[
 K(t)=(a+tb)E_0+(c+td)E_1,
 \qquad \phi(t)=\frac{c+td}{a+tb}.
\]
Nonproportionality gives \(ad-bc\ne0\), and nonsingularity excludes zeros
and poles on \(\mu_m\).  Thus \(\phi\) permutes \(\mu_m\).  By
\cref{lem:nd-root-mobius}, it equals \(\alpha t\) or \(\alpha/t\).
Cross-multiplication gives a degree-two identity valid on at least three
points.  Its coefficients yield respectively
\(b=c=0,d=\alpha a\), or \(a=d=0,c=\alpha b\), which are the two displayed
forms.  A zero sample already makes the endpoints proportional.
\end{proof}

Use the following names for the three binary-pencil outcomes of
\cref{lem:nd-root-pencil}:
\[
 \begin{array}{ccl}
 \mathsf Z&:&\text{identically zero},\\
 \mathsf P&:&\text{nonzero proportional},\\
 \mathsf C&:&\text{nonproportional complementary-axis}.
 \end{array}
\]

For a deleted pair \(p\), factor the even-sector card into the common matching
factor \(P_E\) and endpoint matrix pencil \(K_E(t)\), after ordering the
\(2k=\arity(f)-2\) residual inputs of a nonconstant even ruling:
\begin{equation*}
  E(t)=P_E\otimes K_E(t),
  \qquad
  K_E(t):=K_{E,0}+tK_{E,1},
  \qquad K_E(t),K_{E,0},K_{E,1}\in\Mat_2(\mathbb C),
\end{equation*}
with \(k-1\) fixed factors. Define \(O(t)\) and its binary factor pencil
\(K_O(t)\) analogously.  To obtain one tensor identity with a fixed scalar, take
two distinct nonzero samples, use their linear inversion to reconstruct the
two analytic endpoint tensors, normalize the common factor \(P_E\) by a
fixed linear functional \(\eta_E(P_E)=1\), and define
\[
 K_E(t)=(\eta_E\otimes\operatorname{id})(E(t)).
\]
The normalization fixes the common factor once for the whole pencil.
The whole sector inherits the type of this exact residual binary pencil.
An identically zero whole pencil is type \(\mathsf{Z}\), and a nonzero proportional
whole pencil is type \(\mathsf{P}\) before any varying factor is selected.  In a
\(\mathsf{C}/\mathsf{C}\) branch the variable endpoints are complementary rank-one matrices.

\subsubsection{The six-input base}

\begin{lemma}[Direct six-input base case]
\label{lem:nd-six-prime-base}
Let \(h\ne0\) be a retained arity-six signature in the standard
\(\DihZ{2m}\) sector, where \(m\ge3\).  Suppose every realizable oriented
pair card of \(h\), in both kernel parities and at every phase in
\(\mu_m\), is zero or a product of two nonsingular current-group
monomial binaries.  Then, after a permutation of inputs, \(h\) has an exact
nontrivial factorization of type \(1+5\) or \(2+4\).  In particular,
\(h\) is not irreducible.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{lem:app-nd-sector-ruling,lem:nd-root-pencil} at each
deleted pair \(p\).  A type-\(\mathsf P\)
sector is a scalar pencil times one fixed nonsingular matching product
on the four residual inputs.  A type-\(\mathsf C\) sector is one fixed
nonsingular binary factor tensored with a complementary-axis pencil.
Its two endpoint supports are complementary singletons on the varying
edge.  A type-\(\mathsf Z\) sector vanishes identically.

If a pair has type \(\mathsf{Z}/\mathsf{P}\), then \(h=U_p\otimes P\) for a nonzero
binary \(U_p\) and a nonzero residual matching product \(P\).  If it
has type \(\mathsf{Z}/\mathsf{C}\), the fixed binary factor of the \(\mathsf C\) ruling factors
from \(h\).  The reversed cases are identical, and \(\mathsf{Z}/\mathsf{Z}\) would give
\(h=0\).  Each remaining case containing \(\mathsf Z\) therefore proves the
assertion.

Suppose a pair has type \(\mathsf{P}/\mathsf{P}\).  Write
\[
 h=U_p\otimes P_0+V_p\otimes P_1,
\]
where \(U_p,V_p\ne0\) are supported on the two opposite parities of
\(p\), and \(P_0,P_1\) are nonsingular matching products on the four
residual inputs.  Choose a residual pair \(e\) belonging to neither
matching.  Such a pair exists because the two matchings contain at most
