\qquad t\in\mu_m.
\end{equation*}
Their effective kernels are \(D(t),XD(t)\) for \(\DihY{2m}\), and
\[
 D(\delta)D(t)=D(\delta t),
 \qquad
 D(\delta)XD(t)=\begin{pmatrix}0&t\\ \delta&0\end{pmatrix},
\]
for \(\DihXone{2m}\).  The diagonal kernels are symmetric.  Define the
off-diagonal kernel
\(K^{\rm off}(t):=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&t\\\delta&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\).
It satisfies
\begin{equation}
 K^{\rm off}(t)^{\mathsf T}
 =\frac{t}{\delta}K^{\rm off}(\delta^2/t),
 \qquad \delta^2/t\in\mu_m;
 \label{eq:marked-A-card-reversal}
\end{equation}
so reversal permutes the labels and restores a nonzero scalar. The
\(\DihY{2m}\) formula is \(\delta=1\).

With \(\beta_\tau\) fixed above, three
phases imply that
each two-coordinate pencil is zero, lies on a fixed line with ordered
ratio in \(\mathscr R_\tau\), or has its endpoints on the two
complementary coordinate axes.  Indeed, a zero makes the endpoints
proportional.  Otherwise the quotient \(\phi(t)\) of the two independent
affine coordinates is injective on \(\mu_m\) and takes its values in the
fixed coset \(\beta_\tau\mu_m\).  Hence
\(\psi(t)=\beta_\tau^{-1}\phi(t)\) permutes \(\mu_m\), and
\cref{lem:nd-root-mobius} gives
\(\psi(t)=\lambda t\) or \(\lambda/t\).  Cross-multiplication leaves the
opposite coordinate axes.  Multiplication by \(\beta_\tau^{-1}\) is only an
analytic scaling of one endpoint coordinate.   Formula \cref{eq:marked-A-card-reversal} covers the opposite
orientation with its nonzero scalar.

Apply this three-type pencil classification to both card columns for
each of the six deleted pairs.  After analytic quotient normalization, the
Boolean support constraints are exactly those enumerated in
\cref{thm:cert-interface-equality-q4}\textnormal{(ii)}: every nonempty solution consists of
one total parity together with a nonempty subset of its four complementary
string pairs.  Quotient normalization changes no zero pattern, so the same
exact enumeration applies in marked coordinates.  Choose a nonzero
complementary pair and use realizable sector-\(X\) dressings, justified by
\cref{eq:marked-sector-fusion}, to send it to \(0000,1111\).  The other
pairs of nonzero entries are then among the three complementary weight-two blocks, giving
\cref{eq:marked-q4-complement-form}.  This includes both orientations and
every zero-card pattern. The resulting support description is in sector coordinates.
\end{proof}

In \cref{eq:marked-q4-complement-form}, an included complementary
weight-two block has \(b_\ell d_\ell\ne0\). All other blocks are zero.  Ratios below use the
displayed order of the two nonzero binary entries.

\begin{lemma}[The \(\DihXone{2m}\) quaternary coset]
\label{lem:marked-A-q4}
Let \(m\ge4\) be even, let \(G(\cF)=\DihXone{2m}\), and let
\(0\ne q\) be a retained quaternary occurrence satisfying
\[
 \operatorname{RelStable}(\cF,\DihXone{2m};q).
\]
Suppose every member of the complete oriented dressed deck of \(q\) is
zero or a safe \(\DihXone{2m}\)-binary.  Then the quaternary analysis yields an established
resolved complexity leaf or a successor with a strictly larger complete transfer group, a genuine factorization of
\(q\) into two binary signatures whose complete factor batch is retained,
or a realizable endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature in the current coordinates. In this last alternative the original
sector-coordinate signature also satisfies
\[
 (U_{X,1}^{-1})^{\otimes4}q\in\cP.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{lem:marked-q4-support-atlas} and fix the resulting realizable
sector-\(X\) dressings.  In sector coordinates those dressings are
invertible monomial transformations, so they preserve membership in
\(\cP\), reducibility, all flattening ranks, and exact factorization.  We may therefore work with
\cref{eq:marked-q4-complement-form} and undo the dressings afterward.

Recall the phase scalar \(\delta\) and ratio coset \(\mathscr R_{X,1}\):
\[
 \delta=-\eta^{-1},\qquad
 \mathscr R_{X,1}=\delta^{-1}\mu_m=\eta\mu_m.
\]
Since \(\eta\notin\mu_m\) and \(m\) is even,
\begin{equation*}
 \mathscr R_{X,1}\cap\mu_m=\varnothing,
 \qquad \mathscr R_{X,1}^{-1}=\mathscr R_{X,1}.
\end{equation*}
By \cref{lem:marked-sector-dictionary}, every safe sector binary has ratio
in \(\mathscr R_{X,1}\).  In \cref{eq:marked-q4-complement-form}, denote the endpoint
pair by \((a,h)\) and an active central pair by \((b_i,d_i)\).

For one active block, the corresponding diagonal pencil is
\begin{equation*}
 \diag(a+\delta t d_i,\ b_i+\delta t h),
 \qquad t\in\mu_m.
\end{equation*}
The root-pencil type classification in
\cref{lem:marked-q4-support-atlas} excludes a
sample with exactly one vanishing coordinate and forces
\begin{equation*}
 (b_i,d_i)=(a\rho_i,h/\rho_i),
 \qquad \rho_i\in\mathscr R_{X,1},
 \qquad b_i d_i=ah,
\end{equation*}
whereas an inactive block gives the diagonal card pencil
\[
 \diag(a,\delta t h),\qquad t\in\mu_m.
\]
Its ordered ratio \(\delta t h/a\) belongs to
\(\mathscr R_{X,1}=\delta^{-1}\mu_m\).  Since
\(\delta^2=\eta^{-2}\in\mu_m\), this is equivalent to
\[
 h/a\in\delta^{-2}\mu_m=\mu_m.
\]

If two blocks \(j,k\) are active, the crossing deleted pair and the realizable
reflections
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}0&t\\ \delta&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad t\in\mu_m
\]
give, up to common endpoint factors,
\[
 t a\rho_j+\delta h/\rho_k,
 \qquad
 t a\rho_k+\delta h/\rho_j.
\]
Writing
\[
 L(t)=ta+\frac{\delta h}{\rho_j\rho_k},
\]
these entries are \(\rho_jL(t)\) and \(\rho_kL(t)\).  Every nonzero sample
therefore has ratio
\(\rho_j/\rho_k\in\mu_m\), because both \(\rho_j,\rho_k\) lie in the same
coset \(\mathscr R_{X,1}\).  This ratio is outside the coset
\(\mathscr R_{X,1}\).  The nonzero affine function \(L(t)\) vanishes for at most
one phase, so another allowed phase gives a nonsingular binary whose transfer class lies outside the current group.
Complete binary closure by \cref{lem:actual-binary-completeness} gives a
resolved complexity leaf or a successor with a strictly larger complete transfer group, contradicting a remaining
deck-stable branch.

Thus at most one block is active.  If exactly one block \(i\) is active,
then \(ah-b_id_i=0\), giving a genuine \(2+2\) factorization exposed by
\cref{thm:external-factor,lem:factor-saturation}.  Its two binaries are
then considered individually: a safe binary \(B\) with \([BX]\notin G\) gives a successor with a strictly larger complete transfer group,
and unsafe binary factors reach their established
complexity conclusions.

With no active block, let \(\lambda=h/a\in\mu_m\).  Before any further
rotation, the sector-coordinate tensor is already
\[
 a\delta_{0000}+h\delta_{1111},
\]
a generalized equality and hence a member of \(\cP\).  Undoing the fixed
sector-\(X\) dressings preserves \(\cP\), proving
\[
 (U_{X,1}^{-1})^{\otimes4}q\in\cP.
\]
Moreover \(\eta^2/\lambda\in\mu_m\), so it is the product of four available
phase parameters.  Realizable rotations with this product of input phases give the
sector tensor
\[
 a\bigl(\delta_{0000}+\eta^2\delta_{1111}\bigr).
\]
Since \(s_0^4=\eta^{-2}\),
\begin{align*}
 U_{X,1}^{\otimes4}
 a\bigl(\delta_{0000}+\eta^2\delta_{1111}\bigr)
 &=
 a\bigl((1,1)^{\otimes4}+(1,-1)^{\otimes4}\bigr)\\
 &=2a\,\one[\wt({\mathbf{x}})\text{ is even}].
\end{align*}
This is a current-coordinate endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature.  The rotations are
realizable by \cref{eq:marked-sector-fusion}. \(U_{X,1}\) only verifies
the identity.
\end{proof}

To apply \cref{lem:marked-A-q4} to the selected quaternary occurrence
or a quaternary output of a higher-arity construction, first add it with its input order,
scalar, and reduction, jointly add all its irreducible factors, consider the complete transfer group of the resulting set, and regenerate its complete oriented one-copy deck.  Test all
three \(2+2\) flattenings.  Every rank-one flattening is retained as
an exact factor identity.  Unary factors and unsafe binaries
reach their established reductions, and a safe binary whose transfer class lies outside
\(\DihXone{2m}\) gives a successor with a strictly larger complete transfer group.  If all exposed binary factors
are safe current-group binaries, the child quaternary is a \(\DihXone{2m}\)-matching signature and its originating fixed token is completed. If the quaternary itself is
the selected irreducible occurrence, such a factorization contradicts its
irreducibility.

On the remaining branch the quaternary is irreducible, every member of its complete deck
is zero or a safe binary whose transfer class lies in \(\DihXone{2m}\), and the closure just performed gives
\[
 \operatorname{RelStable}(\cF,\DihXone{2m};q)
\]
in the sense of \cref{def:occurrence-relative-deck-stability}.  For a later
occurrence, processing of its fixed local queue and prior processing of every
lower-arity occurrence give this certificate directly.  Apply \cref{lem:marked-A-q4}, which starts from the support in sector
coordinates given by \cref{lem:marked-q4-support-atlas} and constructs
the required eight-vertex signature in current coordinates.

In the no-active-block branch the output signature is
\(2a\,\one[\wt({\mathbf{x}})\text{ is even}]\), with \(a\ne0\).
It is irreducible: the GE signature used to construct
it has rank two across every nontrivial partition, and the invertible
transformations at individual inputs preserve these ranks.  The output is realizable,
has nonzero endpoints, and is an eight-vertex signature.  Apply the already
proved \cref{thm:p1-quaternary-kholant-dichotomy} to the signature set. This gives a hard or tractable case satisfying \(\ReTract(K\cF)\) for the
entire current signature set, with the reduction supplied by realization. The procedure is the
\emph{marked-coordinate reduction}.
\phantomsection\label{par:marked-coordinate-reduction}

\subsubsection{Gauges and product weights}
\label{subsubsec:gauge-product-weights}

An \emph{input-wise gauge} is an invertible diagonal rescaling on specified
tensor inputs. On a binary flattening it gives invertible diagonal row or
column scaling.  Its use as a gadget requires a separate realization.  A genuine character of
\((\Ftwo^r,+)\) has the form
\({\mathbf z}\mapsto\prod_j\epsilon_j^{z_j}\) with
\(\epsilon_j\in\{1,-1\}\).  More generally,
\[
 {\mathbf z}\longmapsto\prod_j\gamma_j^{z_j},
 \qquad \gamma_j\in\mathbb C^\times,
\]
is a \emph{product weight}. It is a
character exactly when every \(\gamma_j^2=1\).

For the remainder of the \(\DihXone{2m}\)-sector, fix
\[
 \delta:=-\eta^{-1},
\]
with the input-wise gauge defined above. In particular,
\((D(\delta)^{(i)}h)({\mathbf{x}})=\delta^{x_i}h({\mathbf{x}})\).

To compare coefficients across several outputs, we need the kernel
weights to act on the same parent inputs. Call a finite family of
marked-sector contraction outputs a \emph{gauge-coherent comparison batch} if its members use the same ordered contraction
multigraph, the same orientations on all contracted edges, and the same
external-input order, and differ only in the chosen phase or product at
designated kernels.  For a set \(\mathscr S\) of parent inputs write
\[
 D(\delta)^{(\mathscr S)}:=\prod_{i\in\mathscr S}D(\delta)^{(i)},
 \qquad
 (D(\delta)^{(\mathscr S)}h)({\mathbf{x}})
   =\delta^{\sum_{i\in\mathscr S}x_i}h({\mathbf{x}}).
\]
The embedded single-input operators commute.  The set \(\mathscr S\), and
hence the gauged parent \(D(\delta)^{(\mathscr S)}h\), is fixed throughout
one comparison batch.  Different ordered contraction graphs may yield
different sets.

\begin{lemma}[Explicit weighted-gauge transport]
\label{lem:marked-A-gauge-transport}
Let \(m\ge4\) be even and use the fixed
\(\delta=-\eta^{-1}\), where \(\eta\in\mu_{2m}\setminus\mu_m\), and the
diagonal gauge matrix \(D(\delta)\) above.  In the analytic \(\DihXone{2m}\)-sector the
effective diagonal and reflection kernels are
\begin{equation*}
 K^0(t)=D(\delta) D(t)=\diag(1,\delta t),
 \qquad
 K^1(t)=D(\delta) XD(t)=\begin{pmatrix}0&t\\ \delta&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad t\in \mu_m.
\end{equation*}
For every binary kernel \(C\) and every two-input slice \(g\) on ordered
inputs \((i,j)\), pointwise in all remaining variables, every occurrence of
\(D(\delta)\) can be moved from the kernel to the corresponding endpoint of
the parent tensor:
\begin{equation}
 \langle D(\delta) C,g\rangle_{\mathrm{Frob}}
   =\langle C,D(\delta)^{(i)}g\rangle_{\mathrm{Frob}},
 \qquad
 \langle C D(\delta),g\rangle_{\mathrm{Frob}}
   =\langle C,D(\delta)^{(j)}g\rangle_{\mathrm{Frob}},
 \label{eq:marked-A-gauge-push}
\end{equation}
where \(i\) and \(j\) are the first and second ordered inputs and
\(D(\delta)^{(i)}\) means multiplication by \(\delta^{x_i}\).  Consequently
for every gauge-coherent comparison batch there is one fixed set
\(\mathscr S\),
independent of the phase and product choices, such that the entire batch
is exactly the corresponding standard \(\DihZ{2m}\) batch on the one
locally gauged parent \(D(\delta)^{(\mathscr S)}h\), followed by invertible
row/column gauges and the substitutions
\begin{equation}
 \widehat t=c\,t^{\varepsilon},\qquad
 c\in\{1,\delta,\delta^{-1}\},\quad \varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}.
 \label{eq:marked-A-gauge-phase-substitution}
\end{equation}
In particular:
\begin{enumerate}
\item support, zero or incomplete-support status, matching translation spaces, all flattening ranks,
      and exact factorization are unchanged by the gauges;
\item whenever both product coefficients on a common edge are nonzero and their
      trace is not identically zero, its trace has the form
      \(\kappa(1+\theta t^{\varepsilon})\) with
      \(\kappa\theta\ne0\) and \(\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}\). A one-term
      trace has no forbidden phase, while an identically-zero product is a
      case of the zero-output or incomplete-support analysis, according to the other product.  Whenever
      the corresponding selected minor is
      defined, its transported value is a nonzero monomial in \(\delta,t\)
      times the standard selected determinant;
\item reversal sends \(t\) to \(\delta^2/t\in \mu_m\), so both orientations
      use the same finite phase set, and the ordered ratios of all safe sector binaries lie in the
      single coset \(\delta^{-1}\mu_m\).
\item the four samples
      \(E(t_0),E(t_1),O(t_0),O(t_1)\) at one fixed ordered deleted pair,
      the two products of one fixed trace-cover graph, the phase family
      producing a quaternary from an arity-six tensor, and the two-copy outputs on the complete polarization grid
      are gauge-coherent.  Hence every joint multi-card comparison in the
      transported proof is made on one common locally gauged parent.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The two displayed kernel identities are direct multiplication.  For the
first identity in \cref{eq:marked-A-gauge-push}, expand the Frobenius pairing:
\(\sum_{a,b}\delta^aK(a,b)g(a,b)\). The second is identical with the
factor \(\delta^b\).  Transposing or reversing an ordered kernel merely
switches \(i\) and \(j\) and contributes the nonzero scalar in
  \cref{eq:marked-A-card-reversal}.  Induction over the contraction edges
  therefore pushes all \(D(\delta)\)'s to vertex inputs.  For a fixed
  ordered contraction graph, the endpoint at which each \(D(\delta)\) lands is
  determined only by the ordered edge, not by its phase or by the choice of
  diagonal versus reflection product.  Hence every member of a
  gauge-coherent batch has the same parent-input set \(\mathscr S\).  Reversed cards
  are first put in the common orientation using
  \cref{eq:marked-A-card-reversal}, which only changes the scalar
  and replaces the phase by \(\delta^2/t\).  On a matching product the
  remaining external gauges are row and column gauges of its binary
  factors, so they preserve support, nonsingularity, ranks, and exact
  tensor-factor identities.  Unrelated ordered contraction graphs may induce
  different sets \(\mathscr S\), but their raw coefficients are never compared:
  support assertions are gauge-invariant, and coefficient identities pull
  back with their explicit nonzero product-weight factors.

Pushing the entire gauge to the parent leaves the phase unchanged, so
\(c=1\).  Alternatively, keeping the raw parent uses
\(K^0(t)=D(\delta t)\) and \(K^1(t)=\delta XD(t/\delta)\), giving
\(c=\delta\) and \(c=\delta^{-1}\), respectively.  These are the
substitutions in \cref{eq:marked-A-gauge-phase-substitution}. A reversed reflection has
\(\delta^2/t\), and \(\delta^2=\eta^{-2}\in \mu_m\).  A trace of two monomial
factors is a sum of two nonzero monomials.  If the two terms have the same
exponent and cancel, that product is identically zero (the full card may
consequently be zero or partially supported). Otherwise, after
extracting one term it is \(\kappa(1+\theta t)\) with
\(\kappa\theta\ne0\) (or a one-term trace).  A unique-lift
fusion multiplies a selected minor by the nonzero entry and orientation
scalars. A row/column gauge multiplies a determinant by the product of its
diagonal entries.  This proves the second item and also shows that a zero
or partially supported card is zero or partially supported before and after transport.
Finally, \(\delta^{-1}\mu_m=\eta \mu_m\) is disjoint from \(\mu_m\) and is invariant
under inversion because \(\eta^{-2}\in \mu_m\).  This proves the ratio and
orientation assertions.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The six-input argument with weighted kernels.}
\label{par:marked-A-six-base}
The weighted kernels also preserve the support argument of
\cref{lem:nd-six-prime-base}. We record this observation to explain the
support part of the transport below. The proof of
\cref{thm:marked-A-rigidity} uses the full reconstruction in
\cref{lem:marked-A-primitive-transport}.  Work analytically with
\(h^{(X,1)}=(U_{X,1}^{-1})^{\otimes6}h\).
The exact sector-ruling argument uses only monomial supports and
Segre factorization.  In its individual binary pencils, the ratios of safe sector binaries
lie in \(\delta^{-1}\mu_m\). Dividing that one ratio by
\(\delta^{-1}\) gives the same \(\mathsf Z/\mathsf P/\mathsf C\) support classification.
This is a separate analytic calculation for each pencil, not a common
input gauge or an available binary.  In the proof of \cref{lem:nd-six-prime-base}, replace the
parity kernels \(I,X\) by the realizable effective kernels
\[
 D(\delta) D(1)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&\delta\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 D(\delta) XD(1)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\\delta&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
They have the same respective supports and nonzero entries.  Every
cross-pair lift in the \(\mathsf{P}/\mathsf{P}\) and \(\mathsf{P}/\mathsf{C}\) arguments and every
injective lift in the support classification of
\cref{prop:cert-nd-q6-support-lowering} therefore acquires only a nonzero
weight. All support counts and projection obstructions are unchanged.
A matching coset still has an identically zero opposite-parity sector.
The resulting exact factorization of \(h^{(X,1)}\) pulls back by
the tensor powers of \(U_{X,1}\) to an exact
factorization of the signature \(h\) in the original basis, with its nonzero scalar.  

\begin{lemma}[Transport of contractions and reconstruction in the \(\DihXone{2m}\) sector]
\label{lem:marked-A-primitive-transport}
Let
\[
 \Stable(\cF,\DihXone{2m},f_0),\qquad \Even(\cF),
 \qquad m\ge4\text{ even}.
\]
Use the phase group \(\mu_m\) and recall the fixed scalar, gauge matrix,
and ratio coset
\[
 \delta=-\eta^{-1},\qquad
 D(\delta)=\diag(1,\delta),\qquad \mathscr R_{X,1}=\delta^{-1}\mu_m.
\]
Let \(h\in\Pi(\cF)\) be a selected unresolved nonbinary irreducible signature
occurrence of even arity at least six.  Regenerate and close its complete
realizable oriented deck over the current signature set.  Suppose that this
occurrence is relatively deck-stable and has produced neither a resolved
complexity leaf nor a strict stable successor. In particular, all lower-arity
irreducible occurrences exposed by its proper cards or factors have already been
processed, and every surviving binary factor has its transfer class in the current
complete group.  Then every concrete local comparison batch in the
standard \(\DihZ{2m}\) proof has a gauge-coherent counterpart in the original basis.  Its
required invariants are the following:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The realizable kernels are monomial, their traces remain affine in
      \(t\in \mu_m\), reversal permutes \(\mu_m\), and the ordered ratio of every safe sector binary lies
      in the fixed coset \(\mathscr R_{X,1}\).  Analytically dividing that ratio by
      \(\delta^{-1}\) gives the pencil normal form.  Hence all support,
      ruling, trace-cover, coefficient-minor, endpoint-type, and
      bounded-reduction arguments transport with their zero cases.
      Unique-lift monomial fusions remain cancellation-free.
\item For a logical coefficient tensor
      \(c=(c_{\mathbf z})_{{\mathbf z}\in\Ftwo^d}\) on an oriented matching and orientation
      signs \(\epsilon_j\in\{1,-1\}\), the product-weight change
      \(\widetilde c_{\mathbf z}=\delta^{\sum_j\epsilon_jz_j}c_{\mathbf z}\) sends the logical ratios of safe binary factors from \(\mathscr R_{X,1}\) to \(\mu_m\) while preserving
      \cref{eq:app-nd-six-relations} and the cross ratio \(r\).  The final
      quaternary signature is handled by the marked-coordinate reduction.
\item Fixed \(\delta\)-weights do not alter support integration.  They
      multiply the Reed--Muller obstruction by a nonzero scalar.  In the
      polarized determinant, they merely reparameterize the full grid and
      multiply each tested coefficient by a nonzero product-weight value.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently every branch either
\begin{enumerate}
\item reaches a resolved complexity leaf or a strict stable successor;
\item returns an exact proper-factor or child-card factor batch to the
      lower-arity occurrence induction; or
\item certifies
      \[
       (U_{X,1}^{-1})^{\otimes\arity(h)}h\in\cP.
      \]
\end{enumerate}
When \(h=f_0\), the first two alternatives are resolved in the
sense of \cref{def:protocol-discharged}.  For a later occurrence they are
charged to the occurrence-level induction described in
\cref{proofsubsec:nd-completion}. No decrease of the active tuple's
\(\Xi_{\mathrm{full}}\) is asserted.
The bounded outputs are realizable quaternary or arity-six tensors in the
current coordinates.  Factor alternatives are exact tensor identities
pulled back to those coordinates and exposed through
\cref{thm:external-factor,lem:factor-saturation}.  Their Turing reductions
are retained in the worklist.  The conclusion concerns the selected
occurrence \(h\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{lem:marked-sector-dictionary}, a realizable sector binary is a
nonzero multiple of \(TD(\delta)^{-1}\), and its effective kernel is
\(D(\delta) T\).
The gauge identity \cref{lem:marked-A-gauge-transport} moves each kernel
weight to a specified parent input.  It gives the effective kernels
\hypertarget{eq:marked-A-effective-kernels}{}
\begin{equation*}
 D(\delta) D(t)=\diag(1,\delta t),
 \qquad
 D(\delta) XD(t)=\begin{pmatrix}0&t\\ \delta&0\end{pmatrix},
\end{equation*}
so the two sector pencils are \(A+\delta tD_{11}\) and \(tB+\delta C\).  For a
nonproportional pencil, multiply the ratio of a safe sector binary by \(\delta\) and use the
  coset-normalized pencil argument. The resulting ratio is in
\(\mu_m\), so \cref{lem:nd-root-mobius,lem:nd-root-pencil} applies.  This is
an analytic rescaling only.  Reversal sends \(t\) to
\(\delta^2/t\in \mu_m\), and the gauge lemma shows that a common-edge trace
has at most one forbidden phase in each product.  Hence the two-product
trace cover still has an admissible phase because \(m\ge4\), while every
zero or partially supported card is preserved exactly by the invertible gauges.
By item~4 of \cref{lem:marked-A-gauge-transport}, each comparison of
samples, products, or a full phase grid uses one common locally gauged
parent. Its determinant identity gives, with an explicitly nonzero
scalar \(\kappa\),
\[
 \det_{X,1}(t)
   =\kappa\,\delta^e t^u
      \det_{Z}(c\,t^{\varepsilon}),
\qquad e,u\in\mathbb Z,\quad c\ne0,\quad\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\},
\]
where \(\det_{Z}\) denotes the same selected minor for the
locally gauged tensor in the standard sector,
so selected discrepancies, the six-to-four Laurent coefficient, and the
endpoint minor are nonzero exactly in the corresponding standard
calculation.  Therefore
\cref{lem:app-nd-sector-ruling,lem:app-nd-compatible-closure,%
lem:app-nd-coefficient-line-factor,lem:app-nd-localization,%
lem:app-nd-bounded-consumer,lem:app-nd-parallelogram,%
lem:app-nd-exceptional-endpoints,lem:app-nd-endpoint-type,%
lem:app-nd-six-to-four}
transport with the same realizable closures.  Every bounded quaternary output
is handled by the marked-coordinate reduction,
and every exact factor identity is pulled
back by the corresponding tensor power of \(U_{X,1}\) and
then handled with a recorded Turing reduction.  For the active minimum
occurrence these transported concrete batches are resolved.  For a later
occurrence, the role of minimum arity is replaced by the closed proper
batches and the earlier lower-arity occurrences, exactly as in the
hypothesis check of \cref{lem:nd-relative-reentry}. The changes of kernel
are justified separately by \cref{lem:marked-A-gauge-transport}.
Each batch is completely closed inside that occurrence's fixed local
queue and charged to the outer occurrence induction.  On the branch
surviving the corresponding closure, the standard support, ruling, and
coefficient invariants remain available.

Use the product-weight convention of
\cref{subsubsec:gauge-product-weights}. For this logical
coefficient cube put
\[
 \chi({\mathbf z}):=\prod_j\gamma_j^{z_j}\qquad(\gamma_j\in\mathbb C^\times).
\]
Multiplication by \(\chi\) preserves supports and all two-face
parallelogram identities.  It also preserves every complementary-pair
product up to one common nonzero scalar.  For oriented matched pairs, the logical ratio of a safe binary factor is
\(\delta^{-\epsilon_j}h_j\), \(h_j\in \mu_m\),
\(\epsilon_j\in\{1,-1\}\).  The product-weight change
\[
  \widetilde c_{\mathbf z}
  =\delta^{\sum_j\epsilon_jz_j}c_{\mathbf z}
\]
puts all ratios in \(\mu_m\), multiplies both sides of every equality in
\cref{eq:app-nd-six-relations} equally, and leaves
\[
  r=\frac{c_{000}c_{110}}{c_{100}c_{010}}
\]
unchanged.  Here the product-weight change is analytic.  Since
\(\delta^2\in \mu_m\), absorb orientation changes into the phases and write
the ratios of the three initial safe binary factors as \(\delta^{-1}h_j\), \(h_j\in \mu_m\).
The crossing factor cards used in the standard six-input calculation
expose these raw ratios: the extra \(\delta t\) multiplies a column of
the card and does not change the ratio on its unclosed matching edge.
Realizable rotations \(D(h_j^{-1})\) therefore leave the signature in the current coordinates
\[
 c_{\mathbf z}=\delta^{-\wt({\mathbf z})}N_r({\mathbf z}),
 \qquad N_r({\mathbf z})=\begin{cases}1,&\wt({\mathbf z})\le1,\\r,&\wt({\mathbf z})\ge2,\end{cases}
\]
after its nonzero realization scalar is recorded.  To check \(r\in \mu_m\)
without realizing the analytic weight change, use the standard
\(X_{12}X_{34}X_{56}\) support and close inputs \((1,3)\) by \(K^1(t)\).
The resulting quaternary signature, with logical row \(x_2\) and column
\(x_5\), has coefficient matrix
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}1&r/\delta\\t/\delta&tr/\delta^2\end{pmatrix}
 =\binom{1}{t/\delta}(1,r/\delta).
\]
Expose its exact binary factors through the factor theorem.  Unless a
binary exit occurs, \(r/\delta\in\delta^{-1}\mu_m\), proving \(r\in \mu_m\).
Closing the third matching pair of the signature in the current coordinates by \(K^1(t)\) gives
\[
 Q(t)=\diag(1,\delta^{-1})
 \begin{pmatrix}1+t&r+t\\r+t&r(1+t)\end{pmatrix}
 \diag(1,\delta^{-1}),\qquad
 \det Q(t)=\delta^{-2}(r-1)(t^2-r).
\]
If \(r=1\), the displayed arity-six signature already factors.  Otherwise
at most two phases satisfy \(t^2=r\). Choose another phase in \(\mu_m\).
The resulting nonzero quaternary signature enters the marked-coordinate
reduction with the displayed nonzero minor.

Support integration is Boolean and unchanged.  On its sole Reed--Muller
residue the raw face, in one orientation, is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}A&\delta tB\\\delta tC&D\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad A,B,C,D\ne0.
\]
The realizable \(t=1\) matching card gives \(AD=\delta^2BC\), so its
determinant is \(\delta^2BC(1-t^2)\).  The complementary exponent pattern
gives the same obstruction up to sign and a nonzero scalar.  It is nonzero
for some \(t\in \mu_m\setminus\{\pm1\}\).  In polarization,
each grid variable is merely reparameterized as
\(\lambda_j=c_jt_j\), \(c_j\in\mathbb C^\times\), and the remaining weights form
a nonzero product weight \(\chi\).  The grid is a product of cosets
\(c_j\mu_m\), so \cref{lem:nd-grid-interpolation} applies without requiring
\(c_j\in \mu_m\).  Hence the adjacent coefficient becomes
\[
 [\lambda^{{\mathbf z}+{\mathbf z}'}]\Delta_\xi
 =\chi({\mathbf z})\chi({\mathbf z}')(u_{\mathbf z}v_{{\mathbf z}'}-u_{{\mathbf z}'}v_{\mathbf z})^2
 \qquad\text{for adjacent }{\mathbf z},{\mathbf z}'.
\]
The cross-slice coefficient is similarly multiplied by \(\chi({\mathbf z})^2\).
Thus the full grid and all vanishing tests in
\cref{lem:app-nd-support-integration,lem:nd-q6-minor-localization,%
lem:app-nd-polarized-determinant}
remain valid.

The transported support integration therefore gives one global matching
support for the selected occurrence, and the transported polarization
identities integrate its nonzero coefficients into a product of binary
factors on that matching.  This is precisely
\((U_{X,1}^{-1})^{\otimes\arity(h)}h\in\cP\).
If an intermediate identity factors the selected signature \(h\) itself,
replace its occurrence by the irreducible occurrences of its proper
factors.  If instead only a constructed child card factors, process that
child's complete factorization tree inside the selected occurrence's fixed
local queue. The token for \(h\) remains unresolved until its analysis
resumes.  Together with the
product certificate above, this proves the occurrence-level alternatives.
\end{proof}



\subsubsection{Completion of the weighted dihedral case}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:marked-A-rigidity}]
Fix \(U_{X,1}\) from \cref{lem:marked-sector-dictionary} and
let \(n=\arity(f)\) be the active minimum arity.  If \(n=4\), the
\hyperref[par:marked-coordinate-reduction]{marked-coordinate reduction},
using \cref{lem:marked-A-q4} after its prescribed factor and deck batches,
gives a resolved complexity leaf for the whole signature set or an established strict successor.
For \(n=6\), apply \cref{lem:marked-A-primitive-transport} to the active
minimum occurrence.  It is relatively deck-stable by
\cref{def:occurrence-relative-deck-stability}. On a branch without a
resolved leaf or strict successor, its lower-arity batches have already
been processed.  The weighted support and polarization calculation in
that lemma gives a proper factorization, contradicting irreducibility.

Suppose \(n\ge8\), and work analytically with
\(f^{(X,1)}=(U_{X,1}^{-1})^{\otimes n}f\).
The two-card localization proof transports directly to the realizable
weighted kernels: common-edge traces exclude at most two phases. Joining
two parity discrepancies gives a fused weight ratio
\(\kappa t^{\pm2}\), \(\kappa\ne0\). Cycle shortening or vertex
replacement has the same unique-lift matching support.  These are the
explicit kernel and fusion identities in
\cref{lem:marked-A-gauge-transport,lem:app-nd-localization}.
As \(m\ge4\), each required allowed phase exists.  A disagreeing pair
of sampled cards would therefore localize to a nonmatching arity-six tensor.  But the first proper gadget contraction of the distinguished irreducible signature is
zero or a product of safe current-group binaries, and subsequent
contractions preserve that property by the group fusion law.  The
inherited nonzero disagreeing cards give a contradiction.

Thus at a fixed deleted pair all nonzero samples have one matching and
one common varying edge.  The
\hyperref[par:nd-common-factor-consequence]{common-factor consequence}
fixes the other factor classes across both sectors. Its hypothesis concerns
projective factor classes on the common matching and is unchanged by the
coherent invertible gauges.
For each weighted pencil \(A+\delta tD_{11}\) and \(tB+\delta C\),
evaluation at two distinct nonzero phases gives an invertible linear
system for its two slices, since \(\delta\ne0\). The four recovered
slices have one common nonzero binary product factor, proving an exact
proper factorization of \(f^{(X,1)}\).  Pulling it back by the
invertible matrices at individual inputs \(U_{X,1}\) gives a proper
factorization of the signature \(f\) in the current coordinates, again impossible.

No higher-arity deck-stable signature branch survives, and the quaternary route
already resolves the entire retained set.  This proves \(\Close_{\{\cP\}}(\cF,\DihXone{2m},f)\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Completion of the marked-dihedral classification}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:marked-dihedral}]
If \(G=\DihZ{2m}\), then deck stability and \(\Even(\cF)\) give
\(\arity(f)=\nu(\cF)=2k+2\) for some \(k\), so apply
\cref{thm:normalized-dihedral}.  If \(G=\DihY{2m}\), apply
\cref{thm:marked-B-reduction}.  If \(G=\DihXzero{2m}\), apply
\cref{thm:marked-A0-reduction}.  If \(G=\DihXone{2m}\), apply
\cref{thm:marked-A-rigidity}.  These are exactly the four cases in the
statement.
\end{proof}
\section{Cyclic Matching Decks and Reed--Muller Rigidity}
\label{sec:proper-deck}

In this case the transfer span is \(\mathscr K_G=\Span\{I,X\}\).
In Walsh coordinates, the corresponding contractions impose equality
of two bits with a possible phase on their common value. In the remaining
higher-arity case, the matching cards determine the Reed--Muller signature
\(\RMcore\) up to the equivalences in \cref{thm:reed-muller-rigidity}.
If the core remains in this case under every realizable single-input
dressing, \cref{lem:rm-safe-group-collapse} forces the current complete
group to be \(\CycX{2}\). This excludes every larger cyclic group in the
surviving case. After the binary and arity-four cases have been resolved,
the reduction in \cref{rem:rm-binary-enlargement-shortcut} can adjoin
Bell signatures and continue the complexity classification with a strictly
larger group. For the synthesis in \cref{sec:p1-synthesis}, we prove the
stronger conclusion that the entire retained
set and the edge admit one common flat-Lagrangian presentation in the surviving cyclic branch.

To obtain this presentation, we use \(\RMcore\) to test the other retained
signatures. Networks built from the core realize flat-Lagrangian signatures.
They also realize Radon contractions of a signature \(g\). On a chosen
hyperplane, the new signature takes the value \(g(\mathbf x)+g(\mathbf x+\mathbf v)\)
on each fibre \(\{\mathbf x,\mathbf x+\mathbf v\}\). It has two
fewer inputs, allowing a failure of affine structure to be localized at
lower arity. An induction, followed by affine patching and recognition, yields one common
flat-Lagrangian presentation for the whole set.
Appendix~\ref{proofsubsec:rm-pauli-tools} extends the network results
to all four Bell signatures.

Use the lexicographic indexing of the eight inputs by \(\Ftwo^3\) fixed in
\cref{par:reed-muller-code}.
Define the eight-ary Reed--Muller indicator signature
\(\RMcore:\Ftwo^8\to\{0,1\}\) by
\begin{equation}
  \RMcore({\mathbf{x}}):=\one[{\mathbf{x}}\in RM(1,3)].
  \label{eq:RM-core}
\end{equation}

For \(S\subseteq\Ftwo^r\), write \(\one[S]\) for the indicator
signature \({\mathbf{x}}\mapsto\one[{\mathbf{x}}\in S]\).

\begin{theorem}[Reed--Muller deck rigidity]
\label{thm:reed-muller-rigidity}
Assume
\[
 \Stable(\cF,G,f),\qquad
 \arity(f)=\nu(\cF),\qquad
 \mathscr K_G=\Span\{I,X\}.
\]
Then \(\Close_{\{\FLag\}}(\cF,G,f)\).  In the remaining tractable case with a common flat-Lagrangian presentation,
\[
 G=\{[I],[X]\},\qquad
 f\sim\RMcore=\one[RM(1,3)],
\]
where \(\sim\) means equality up to a nonzero scalar, a permutation of
inputs, and realizable single-input \(X\)-dressings.
\end{theorem}

For \(s\in\AlgNums\), the Walsh coordinates satisfy
\begin{equation*}
  P^{-1}\!\left[\frac12
  \begin{pmatrix}1+s&1-s\\1-s&1+s\end{pmatrix}\right]P
  =D(s),
  \qquad
  P^{\mathsf T}XP=2Z
\end{equation*}




\subsection{Cyclic phase pencils and binary attachments}
\label{subsec:proper-cyclic-phase-pencils}

The cyclic group supplies a finite family of phase contractions.  The next
three lemmas describe which binary pencils they permit and which changes
to a quaternary signature their realizable input dressings can realize.

A \emph{sampled phase pencil} evaluates a pencil \(U+tV\) on a finite
root-of-unity group, here \(\mu_N\), retaining zero members and the
projective classes of nonzero members.

For each integer \(N\ge3\), use the diagonal phase matrices
\(D(s)\), \(s\in\mu_N\), defined in \cref{subsec:holant-definitions}.
Realizable networks use \(X\)-edges. \Cref{eq:Walsh-coordinates}
gives their analytic matrix \(P^{\mathsf T}XP=2Z\) in the tables below.

For \(N\ge3\) and \(a,b,c,d\in\AlgNums\), define the condition
\(\mathcal R_N(a,b,c,d)\) by declaring that, for some \(\alpha\in\mu_N\), at least
one of the following systems holds:
\begin{align}
  c&=-\alpha a,&d&=-\alpha b,
  &&\text{(proportional)},
  \label{eq:odd-dih-R-proportional}\\
  b&=c=0,&d&=\alpha a,
  &&\text{(diagonal monomial)},
  \label{eq:odd-dih-R-diagonal}\\
  a&=d=0,&c&=\alpha b,
  &&\text{(anti-diagonal monomial)}.
  \label{eq:odd-dih-R-antidiagonal}
\end{align}
The alternatives may overlap and include the all-zero quadruple.

For a quaternary signature \(q\), write \(\widetilde q\) for its
Walsh coordinates from \cref{eq:Walsh-coordinates}. When it is
supported on even Hamming weight, define its endpoint and central Walsh
coordinates by
\begin{equation}
  p=\widetilde q_{0000},\qquad
  t=\widetilde q_{1111},\qquad
  x_{ab}=\widetilde q_{\one_{\{a,b\}}},\qquad 1\le a<b\le4.
  \label{eq:odd-dih-q4-coordinates}
\end{equation}
Here \(\one_{\{a,b\}}\in\Ftwo^4\) has ones precisely at inputs
\(a,b\), so \(x_{ab}\) indexes one of the six weight-two strings.

\begin{lemma}[Cyclic phase-pencil rigidity, including zeros]
\label{lem:odd-dih-RN}
For every integer \(N\ge3\), the condition
\(\mathcal R_N(a,b,c,d)\) is equivalent to the following
condition: for every \(s\in\mu_N\),
\[
  \diag(a-sb,c-sd)Z
  \quad\text{is zero or is projectively }D(\eta)
  \text{ for some }\eta\in\mu_N.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If \(a=b=c=d=0\), both conditions hold, so assume otherwise.  Set
\[
  u(s)=-c+sd,\qquad v(s)=a-sb.
\]
The phase-card condition says that \(u(s)\) and \(v(s)\) are either both zero
or both nonzero, and in the latter case
\(u(s)/v(s)\in\mu_N\).  If the two affine linear forms are dependent, the
possibility that exactly one is identically zero is excluded by this
condition.  Hence \(u=\alpha v\) for some \(\alpha\in\mu_N\), giving
\cref{eq:odd-dih-R-proportional}.  If they are independent, neither form
vanishes on \(\mu_N\), and
\(\psi(s):=u(s)/v(s)\) is a nonconstant M\"obius
transformation.  The card condition gives
\(\psi(\mu_N)\subseteq\mu_N\). Injectivity and equality of the finite
cardinalities give \(\psi(\mu_N)=\mu_N\).  Thus
\cref{lem:nd-root-mobius} applies and gives
\(\psi(s)=\alpha s\) or \(\alpha/s\), with
\(\alpha\in\mu_N\).  Cross multiplication yields
\cref{eq:odd-dih-R-diagonal,eq:odd-dih-R-antidiagonal}.  Direct substitution
proves the converse, including the all-zero case.
\end{proof}

For the two cyclic lemmas below, fix a finite algebraic retained set \(\cF\) in \(K\)-coordinates, with edge signature \(X\), and
fix an integer \(N\ge3\).  For every \(s\in\mu_N\), fix a realizable ordered binary gadget
\(C_s\) over \(\cF\), with a specified orientation, whose analytic Walsh
matrix is
\[
  \widetilde C_s=\kappa_sD(s)Z,
  \qquad \kappa_s\in\AlgNums^\times.
\]
The nonzero scalar \(\kappa_s\) is recorded with the gadget.  Throughout
these lemmas, ``realizable'' and ``directly realizable'' are relative to this same
\(\cF\), and every displayed phase-card matrix is divided by its nonzero scalar. In the cyclic case,
\(P^{-1}G(\cF)P=C_N^{\rm std}=\{[D(s)]:s\in\mu_N\}\). The Walsh transfer class of \(C_s\)
is \([\widetilde C_sZ]=[D(s)]\), so these binaries represent all classes
in that form.

\begin{lemma}[Cyclic quaternary phase family]
\label{lem:odd-cyc-q4-family}
Let \(q\) be a realizable quaternary signature over \(\cF\), and suppose
\(\widetilde q=(P^{-1})^{\otimes4}q\) has even-parity support.  Define \(p,t,x_{ab}\) by \cref{eq:odd-dih-q4-coordinates}.  Assume
that, for every complementary pair, both orders of its two residual inputs,
and every \(s\in\mu_N\), contraction with the fixed chosen representative
\(C_s\) gives either the zero card or a nonsingular binary card whose Walsh
transfer class belongs to
\(C_N^{\rm std}=\{[D(\eta)]:\eta\in\mu_N\}\).  Equivalently, define
\[
  \mathfrak I=\bigl\{\{1,2\},\{1,3\},\{1,4\}\bigr\},
  \qquad I^c=[4]\setminus I,
\]
and write \(x_I:=x_{ab}\) for \(I=\{a,b\}\).  For every
\(I\in\mathfrak I\), \(s\in\mu_N\), and
\(\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}\), the corresponding normalized realizable Walsh card
has matrix
\begin{align*}
 B_{I,+}(s)&=\diag(p-sx_I,x_{I^c}-st),\\
 B_{I,-}(s)&=\diag(p-sx_{I^c},x_I-st).
\end{align*}
Here \(+\) records deletion of \(I\) and \(-\) deletion of \(I^c\). Both
orders of the remaining two inputs are part of the assumption.  Each displayed
card is zero or satisfies
\[
  [B_{I,\varepsilon}(s)Z]\in C_N^{\rm std}.
\]
The right factor \(Z\) comes from the Walsh transfer convention.  Organize the eight coordinates into the endpoint block
\(\mathbf v_0=(p,t)\) and the three central complement blocks
\(\mathbf v_I=(x_I,x_{I^c})\), \(I\in\mathfrak I\).  Then exactly one of the
following holds:
\begin{enumerate}
\item If \(p,t\ne0\), then \(\lambda=t/p\in\mu_N\), and each central
      block \(\mathbf v_I\) is zero or
      \hypertarget{eq:odd-cyc-q4-nonzero-endpoints}{}
\begin{equation*}
        \mathbf v_I=(-p\lambda\alpha_I^{-1},-p\alpha_I),
        \qquad \alpha_I\in\mu_N.
      \end{equation*}
\item If \(p=t=0\), each central block \(\mathbf v_I\) is zero or
      \[
        \mathbf v_I=\rho_I(1,\alpha_I),
        \qquad \rho_I\in\AlgNums^\times,
        \quad \alpha_I\in\mu_N.
      \]
\end{enumerate}
In particular, exactly one of \(p,t\) cannot be nonzero.
Conversely, every even Walsh coefficient tensor in these alternatives satisfies the stated
phase-card condition under every phase contraction.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For a complementary block \(I,I^c\), the phase card is
\[
  \diag(p-sx_I,x_{I^c}-st),
  \qquad s\in\mu_N.
\]
Apply \cref{lem:odd-dih-RN} to
\((a,b,c,d)=(p,x_I,x_{I^c},t)\), and then interchange \(I,I^c\).
If \(p,t\ne0\), the anti-diagonal system is impossible.  If one of the
two complementary-deletion systems is diagonal, then \(x_I=x_{I^c}=0\) and
\(t/p\in\mu_N\). The other system is then diagonal as well.
In the remaining case both systems are proportional.  Taking their
parameters to be \(\alpha,\beta\in\mu_N\) gives
\[
 x_{I^c}=-\alpha p,\qquad x_I=-\beta p,\qquad
 t=\alpha\beta p.
\]
Thus \(t/p=\alpha\beta\in\mu_N\), and choosing
\(\alpha_I=\alpha\) gives the displayed parametrization.  If exactly one
endpoint is nonzero, neither monomial system is possible and the two
proportional systems force respectively \(x_I=0,x_{I^c}\ne0\) and
\(x_{I^c}=0,x_I\ne0\), a contradiction.  If \(p=t=0\), proportional and
diagonal systems give only the zero block. Every nonzero block is therefore
anti-diagonal, \(\mathbf v_I=\rho_I(1,\alpha_I)\).  Direct substitution checks
both complementary deletions and every card arising from a zero complementary
block, proving the converse.
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}[Realizable quaternary cyclic dressing]
\label{lem:odd-cyc-four-port-dressing}
Let \(q\) be a realizable quaternary signature over \(\cF\).  For
\(r_1,r_2,r_3,r_4\in\mu_N\), attach the fixed representative \(C_{r_i}\), in
its specified orientation, to input \(i\) of \(q\) through one \(X\)-edge, leaving the other input of \(C_{r_i}\) exposed.  Denote the
normalized output signature by \(h\).  After division of the direct-gadget
boundary tensor by the nonzero scalar
\(2^4\prod_i\kappa_{r_i}\), its Walsh coordinates are
\begin{equation*}
  \widetilde h_{\mathbf{y}}
  =\widetilde q_{\mathbf{y}}\prod_{i=1}^4r_i^{y_i}.
\end{equation*}
Define the map \(F_N:\mu_N^4\to\mu_N^4\) by
\begin{equation*}
  F_N(r_1,r_2,r_3,r_4)
  =(R,r_{12},r_{13},r_{14}),
  \qquad
  R=\prod_{i=1}^4r_i,
  \quad r_I=\prod_{i\in I}r_i,
\end{equation*}
and put \((\mu_N)^2:=\{z^2:z\in\mu_N\}\).  A target
\((R,u,v,w)\in\mu_N^4\) lies in the image of \(F_N\) exactly when
\begin{equation}
  \frac{uvw}{R}\in(\mu_N)^2.
  \label{eq:cyclic-dressing-image}
\end{equation}
For odd \(N\), \(F_N\) is bijective.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
At input \(i\), the fixed Walsh matrix
\(\kappa_{r_i}D(r_i)Z\), followed by the analytically transformed disequality edge \(P^{\mathsf T}XP=2Z\), acts as
\(2\kappa_{r_i}D(r_i)\).  The four attachments therefore give the stated
realizable dressing and scalar.  Necessity is
\(uvw/R=r_1^2\). For sufficiency choose
\[
  r_1^2=uvw/R,
  \qquad
  r_2=u/r_1,
  \qquad
  r_3=v/r_1,
  \qquad
  r_4=w/r_1.
\]
These choices satisfy
\(F_N(r_1,r_2,r_3,r_4)=(R,u,v,w)\). For odd \(N\), squaring on
\(\mu_N\) is bijective.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The arity-four case}

The cyclic groups in \cref{rem:xia-binary-taxonomy} have transfer span
\(\mathscr K_G=\Span\{I,X\}\). We treat arity four before reconstructing
the higher-arity Reed--Muller signature.

\begin{lemma}[The cyclic arity-four case]
\label{lem:proper-cyclic-q4}
Let \((\cF,G,q)\) be a deck-stable retained state with
the supplied equality \(I\) available,
\(\arity(q)=\nu(\cF)=4\), transfer span \(\mathscr K_G=\Span\{I,X\}\), and finite cyclic \(G\ni[X]\).  Then either an
unsafe realizable binary is exposed, factor access strictly enlarges the
complete transfer group of the resulting set, or a gadget using at most two copies of
\(q\) realizes an irreducible endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature.  Thus one obtains a resolved complexity leaf, an outer deck successor
under \cref{lem:matching-synthesis-measure}, or the tractable case
\(\Tract(K\cF)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In Walsh coordinates the transfer span is diagonal. Therefore
\begin{equation*}
  P^{-1}GP=C_N^{\rm std}=\{[D(s)]:s\in\mu_N\},
  \qquad 2\mid N.
\end{equation*}
Here \([X]\) becomes \([Z]=[D(-1)]\), and chosen representatives are
proportional to \(D(s)Z\).  To justify the parity reduction, fix an ordered
deleted pair and an odd-weight string \({\mathbf{y}}\) on the two residual inputs.  Up to
a fixed nonzero representative scalar and a possible sign, the corresponding
off-diagonal phase-card entry is \(u+sv\), where \(u\) and \(v\) are the
contributions of the two Walsh coefficients whose deleted-pair assignments
are \(00\) and \(11\), respectively, and whose assignment to the remaining inputs is \({\mathbf{y}}\).  A zero or
cyclic card is diagonal, so this entry vanishes.  At the two allowed phases \(s=1,-1\) we obtain \(u+v=u-v=0\), hence \(u=v=0\).  Running through
all six deleted pairs and both input orders (equivalently, the complete
ordered deck) forces every odd-weight Walsh coefficient to vanish.  Thus
\(\widetilde q\) is supported on even Walsh parity, as required by
\cref{lem:odd-cyc-q4-family}.  Item~1 of
\cref{thm:equality-q4-boundary} handles \(N=2\). Hence
assume \(N\ge4\) and use the endpoint and complement-block coordinates of
\cref{eq:odd-dih-q4-coordinates}, indexed by
\[
  I_1=\{1,2\},\qquad I_2=\{1,3\},\qquad I_3=\{1,4\}.
\]
The cyclic quaternary-family result in \cref{lem:odd-cyc-q4-family} leaves two
endpoint regimes.  We treat the zero-endpoint regime first. In the nonzero
regime, the dressing-image criterion further separates successful dressing
from the residual square obstruction.
Define the block phase parameters \(\alpha_i:=\alpha_{I_i}\) and, for
nonzero endpoints, the endpoint ratio \(\lambda:=t/p\).
Call the complementary block indexed by \(I_i\) \emph{active} when
\((x_{I_i},x_{I_i^c})\ne(0,0)\), and \emph{inactive} otherwise.

For each two-copy output below, let \(\cF^+\) be the signature set after preprocessing, complete factor closure, and group
recomputation.  Zero, nullary, unary, odd, singular, and infinite-order
factors are handled by the corresponding reductions.  The remaining calculations concern
the all-even finite-group branch.  Proper factors become available over
\(\cF^+\) through the factor theorem, with its Turing reduction and
nonzero normalization scalars recorded.

\emph{Zero endpoints.}
Write each complementary block with scale \(\rho_i\) as
\((x_{I_i},x_{I_i^c})=\rho_i(1,\alpha_i)\), taking \(\rho_i=0\)
for an inactive block and then choosing any \(\alpha_i\in\mu_N\), and define the auxiliary scalar
\(X_i:=\alpha_i\rho_i^2\).  For each \(i\), choose the remaining
indices \(j<k\), so \(\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}\), and order
\(I_i=(1,i+1)\).  Connect the first input
of one copy to the first input of the other by the chosen representative
\(D(\alpha_j\alpha_k)Z\), and connect the second inputs by \(I\) (with the
same orientation on both copies).  If \(Q_i\) is the \(I_i\mid I_i^c\)
flattening, write the normalized output in Walsh coordinates as \(\widetilde g_i\), where
\[
 \widetilde g_i=Q_i^{\mathsf T}K_iQ_i,\qquad
 K_i=(D(\alpha_j\alpha_k)Z)\otimes I
     =\diag(1,1,-\alpha_j\alpha_k,-\alpha_j\alpha_k).
\]
Order the residual inputs as the two inputs of \(I_i^c\) in the first copy
followed by those in the second copy, each in inherited order.  The only
potentially nonzero complementary pairs of \(\widetilde g_i\) are
\[
 E=(\widetilde g_i(0000),\widetilde g_i(1111)),
 \qquad
 D=(\widetilde g_i(0101),\widetilde g_i(1010)).
\]
Their entries are
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
  (E_0,E_1)
    &=(-\alpha_j\alpha_k\rho_i^2,\ \alpha_i^2\rho_i^2),\\
  (D_0,D_1)
    &=(\alpha_j(X_j-X_k),\ -\alpha_k(X_j-X_k)),
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
and, whenever both displayed pairs \(E\) and \(D\) are nonzero,
\[
 \frac{D_1/D_0}{E_1/E_0}=\left(\frac{\alpha_k}{\alpha_i}\right)^2.
\]
Set
\[
 d_i:=(X_j-X_k)^2-X_i^2.
\]
We now verify endpoint nondegeneracy, including the case of an inactive
\(i\)-block.  If \(\rho_i\ne0\), the binary-gadget attachment criterion
\cref{lem:odd-cyc-four-port-dressing,eq:cyclic-dressing-image} is applied
in the displayed residual-input order.  Put
\((u,v,w)=(r_{12},r_{13},r_{14})\) for these four output positions.
The entries at \(0101\) and \(1010\) are multiplied by \(R/v\)
and \(v\), respectively.  Take
\[
 R=(E_1/E_0)^{-1},
 \qquad u=1,
 \qquad v=-\alpha_j/\alpha_i,
 \qquad w=R/v.
\]
All four parameters lie in \(\mu_N\), since \(N\) is even, and
\(uvw/R=1\).  Moreover
\[
 E_1R=E_0,\qquad
 D_0R/v=D_1v=\frac{\alpha_j\alpha_k}{\alpha_i}(X_j-X_k).
\]
Thus the two entries in each displayed block are equal.  Since the Walsh
support is even, the two endpoint values in the original basis coincide. Up to one common
nonzero scalar their common value is
\[
 (X_j-X_k)-X_i.
\]
It is nonzero whenever \(d_i\ne0\).  If instead \(\rho_i=0\),
then \(E_0=E_1=0\), while \(d_i\ne0\) implies
\(D_0D_1\ne0\).  Set
\[
 u=v=1,
 \qquad R=D_1/D_0=-\alpha_k/\alpha_j,
 \qquad w=R.
\]
Here \(uvw/R=1\), so the same criterion applies and makes the two dressed
entries of the \(D\)-block equal and nonzero.  Inverse Walsh transformation again gives two
nonzero endpoint values in the original basis.  Thus every index with \(d_i\ne0\),
active or inactive, yields an endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex
  signature.  In the displayed four-string matching-code support, the two
  crossed \(2+2\) flattenings have rank at least two. The remaining logical
  flattening has determinant, up to a nonzero scalar,
\begin{equation*}
  \alpha_j\alpha_kd_i.
\end{equation*}
If some \(d_i\ne0\), the corresponding realizable output has no binary
factor.  The dressing is a product of nonsingular local maps, so it preserves
tensor factorizations.  Even parity and its complementary endpoints exclude a unary factor,
so the dressed endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature is irreducible.

It remains to exclude simultaneous vanishing of the three minors.  If
\(d_1=d_2=d_3=0\), choose signs
\(\epsilon_i\in\{1,-1\}\) such that
\hypertarget{eq:proper-cyclic-simultaneous-zero}{}
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
  X_2-X_3&=\epsilon_1X_1,\\
  X_3-X_1&=\epsilon_2X_2,\\
  X_1-X_2&=\epsilon_3X_3.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
The coefficient matrix of this system is
\[
 M_\epsilon=
 \begin{pmatrix}
  -\epsilon_1&1&-1\\
  -1&-\epsilon_2&1\\
  1&-1&-\epsilon_3
 \end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 \det M_\epsilon
 =-(\epsilon_1\epsilon_2\epsilon_3+
     \epsilon_1+\epsilon_2+\epsilon_3).
\]
For the two constant sign triples this determinant is nonzero.  For each of
the other six, direct substitution gives, up to permutation, precisely the
kernel lines
\(\Span_{\mathbb C}\{(1,1,0)\}\),
\(\Span_{\mathbb C}\{(1,0,1)\}\), and
\(\Span_{\mathbb C}\{(0,1,1)\}\).  Hence every nonzero solution is a permutation of
  \((\xi,\xi,0)\), with \(\xi\ne0\).  Since \(q\ne0\), not all three
  \(X_i\) vanish.

Relabel so that \((X_1,X_2,X_3)=(\xi,\xi,0)\).  Then
\(\rho_1\rho_2\ne0\) and \(\rho_3=0\).  In the
\(I_3\mid I_3^c\) flattening of \(\widetilde q\), order the odd assignments
on either side as \(10,01\).  Its only nonzero block is
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{pmatrix}
  \rho_1&\rho_2\\
  \alpha_2\rho_2&\alpha_1\rho_1
 \end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 \det=\alpha_1\rho_1^2-\alpha_2\rho_2^2=X_1-X_2=0.
\end{equation*}
All four entries are nonzero, so the block has rank one and
\(\widetilde q\) has a genuine binary-by-binary tensor factorization across
\(I_3\mid I_3^c\).  The Walsh change is a product of invertible local maps,
so \(q\) has the same factorization, contradicting its irreducibility.
Therefore some \(d_i\ne0\), as required.

\emph{Nonzero endpoints and successful dressing.}
Define the ratio scalar \(R:=\lambda^{-1}\) and the dressing scalar
\(r_I:=\epsilon_I\alpha_I\lambda^{-1}\) on each active block, where
\(\epsilon_I\in\{\pm1\}\). Abbreviate
\(\epsilon_i:=\epsilon_{I_i}\).  An inactive block removes the square
constraint: its otherwise irrelevant dressing parameter may be chosen so
that the criterion's product equation \(uvw/R=1\) holds.
With all three active,
\cref{lem:odd-cyc-four-port-dressing,eq:cyclic-dressing-image} applies
exactly when
\(\epsilon_1\epsilon_2\epsilon_3
 \alpha_1\alpha_2\alpha_3\) is a square.  If
\(N\equiv2\pmod4\), signs remove the obstruction. When \(4\mid N\), this
works precisely if \(\alpha_1\alpha_2\alpha_3\) is a square.  The dressed
endpoint block is \((p,p)\). Every active complementary block becomes
\((-\epsilon_Ip,-\epsilon_Ip)\), while every inactive block remains
\((0,0)\).  Let \(h\) be
the resulting signature in the original basis.  Equality of complementary Walsh blocks
makes \(h\) even-parity, while its even Walsh support makes it
  complement-invariant, meaning
  \(h({\mathbf{y}})=h({\mathbf{y}}+\one^4)\) for every \({\mathbf{y}}\in\Ftwo^4\). It is nonzero because
  \(p\ne0\).  Choose an even
string \({\mathbf{x}}\) with \(h({\mathbf{x}})\ne0\), and attach realizable \(X\)-dressings on
exactly the coordinates where \({\mathbf{x}}\) is one.  This even translation preserves
parity, and the translated signature \(h'\) satisfies
\[
 h'(0000)=h({\mathbf{x}})=h({\mathbf{x}}+\one^4)=h'(1111)\ne0.
\]
Thus \(h'\) is an endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature.  Every
dressing used here is an invertible single-input map, so it also preserves the
irreducibility of \(q\).

\emph{Square obstruction.}
The only remaining case is
\begin{equation*}
 4\mid N,\qquad
 p,t\ne0,\qquad
 \text{all three blocks active},\qquad
 \alpha_1\alpha_2\alpha_3\notin(\mu_N)^2.
\end{equation*}
  Normalize \(p=1\).  For a permutation \((i,j,k)\) of \((1,2,3)\),
  abbreviate
  \[
   (a,b,c):=(\alpha_i,\alpha_j,\alpha_k),
  \]
  and order
the \(I_i\mid I_i^c\) flattening as \(00,01,10,11\):
\begin{equation*}
 Q_i=
 \begin{pmatrix}
  1&0&0&-a\\
  0&-b&-c&0\\
  0&-\lambda c^{-1}&-\lambda b^{-1}&0\\
  -\lambda a^{-1}&0&0&\lambda
 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation*}
Let \(D:=Z\otimes Z\) be the diagonal matrix on the four two-bit assignments, and let \(S\) be
the permutation matrix that swaps \(01,10\).  The realizable joins are
\begin{align*}
 \widetilde G_i^{\parallel}&=4Q_iDQ_i^{\mathsf T},
 \\
 \widetilde G_i^{\times}&=4Q_iDSQ_i^{\mathsf T}.
\end{align*}
The crossed one is
\begin{equation*}
 \frac14\widetilde G_i^{\times}
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}
  1+a^2&0&0&-\lambda(a+a^{-1})\\
  0&-2bc&-2\lambda&0\\
  0&-2\lambda&-2\lambda^2/(bc)&0\\
  -\lambda(a+a^{-1})&0&0&\lambda^2(1+a^{-2})
 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation*}
By \cref{lem:actual-binary-completeness}, an unsafe phase card is already a
binary and resolves the complexity. Otherwise the cyclic pencil
of the displayed central block forces, when \(1+a^2\ne0\),
\[
 \frac{2\lambda}{1+a^2}\in\mu_N.
\]
Since \(a\) is a root of unity, \(|1+a^2|=2\) gives \(a^2=1\), whereas
\(1+a^2=0\) gives \(a^2=-1\).  Hence
\begin{equation*}
 \alpha_i^2\in\{1,-1\}\qquad(i=1,2,3).
\end{equation*}

If \(8\mid N\), all fourth roots are squares, contradicting the
  obstruction.  Thus \(N\equiv4\pmod8\), and an odd number of the three
  squares equals \(-1\).  Choose the permutation \((i,j,k)\) so that
  \(\alpha_i^2=a^2=-1\), define the ratio scalar
  \(r:=\lambda/(bc)\), and evaluate the straight join.  Its sole nonzero
block has rank one and factors through the nonsingular anti-diagonal matrix
\(M_1(r)=D(r)X\), giving
\begin{equation*}
  \frac14\widetilde G_i^\parallel
  =\pm2 M_1(r)\otimes M_1(r),
  \qquad
  M_1(r)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\ r&0\end{pmatrix},
  \qquad r\ne0,
\end{equation*}
on the two residual copies of \(I_i\).  \Cref{lem:factor-saturation} exposes
the nonsingular anti-diagonal \(M_1(r)\). Its transfer obeys
\[
  (M_1(r)Z)^2=-rI,
  \qquad
  (M_1(r)Z)D(s)(M_1(r)Z)^{-1}=sD(s^{-1})\quad(s\in\mu_N).
\]
The class \([M_1(r)Z]\) is an involution outside \(C_N^{\rm std}\) and acts on it by inversion.
Together they generate a subgroup isomorphic to \(D_{2N}\).
After factor closure, the complete transfer group \(G(\cF^+)\) in the original coordinates contains
\(G(\cF)\) and the additional class \([P M_1(r)Z P^{-1}]\), so \(G(\cF)<G(\cF^+)\). The first two cases
pass a realizable endpoint-nondegenerate eight-vertex signature to
\cref{cor:p1-eight-vertex-reduced-interface}, while the third strictly enlarges the
complete group.  This proves the lemma.
\end{proof}

The arity-four case therefore gives a resolved complexity leaf or an
outer deck successor. We next reconstruct the higher-arity core needed
for \cref{thm:reed-muller-rigidity}.



\subsection{Reconstruction of the minimum Reed--Muller core}

Let \(f\) be a minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature of the retained set,
and let its arity be \(n:=\arity(f)\).  If \(n\) is odd, apply
\cref{thm:external-odd} to the ordinary-coordinate set \(K\cF\).  Its
hardness conclusion pulls back through the holographic \(K\)-coordinate
equivalence, while its tractable conclusion is exactly \(\Tract(K\cF)\).
Thus, on the continuing branch, \(n\) is even.  Every
proper card is zero or a nonsingular \(G\)-matching signature. In Walsh
coordinates the latter is a weighted-equality product.  Define its analytic
Walsh coefficients by
\[
  a_S:=\widetilde f(\one_S),
  \qquad S\subseteq[n].
\]
For distinct \(i,j\), label and normalize the two realizable cards obtained
from the \(I/X\)-closures as \(C_{ij}^{+}\) and \(C_{ij}^{-}\) so that,
for every \(T\subseteq[n]\setminus\{i,j\}\),
\begin{equation}
  \widetilde C_{ij}^{+}(\one_T)=2(a_T+a_{Tij}),
  \qquad
  \widetilde C_{ij}^{-}(\one_T)=2(a_T-a_{Tij}),
  \label{eq:rm-card-pencil}
\end{equation}
where \(Tij=T\cup\{i,j\}\).  A nonzero card has the form
\begin{equation}
  c\prod_{\{p,q\}\in M}
  \one[z_p=z_q]r_{pq}^{z_p},
  \qquad c\prod_{\{p,q\}\in M}r_{pq}\ne0,
  \label{eq:rm-weighted-matching}
\end{equation}
for a perfect matching \(M\). It is nonzero at zero, and its supported
weight-two strings are exactly the edges of \(M\).  Arity four is
\cref{lem:proper-cyclic-q4}, so assume \(n\ge6\).

Define the zero-card graph \(Z_f\) on \([n]\), writing \(ij\) for the
unordered pair \(\{i,j\}\), by
\[
  ij\in E(Z_f)
  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  C_{ij}^{+}=C_{ij}^{-}=0.
\]
By \cref{eq:rm-card-pencil},
\begin{equation*}
  ij\in E(Z_f)
  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  \supp\widetilde f\subseteq\{{\mathbf z}:z_i+z_j=1\}.
\end{equation*}

\begin{lemma}[Elimination of the zero-card graph]
\label{lem:rm-zero-graph}
\label{lem:rm-no-zero-edge}
Let \(f\ne0\) be the fixed minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature of arity \(n\).  Every connected
component of \(Z_f\) is complete bipartite.  If \(n>4\), then
\(E(Z_f)=\varnothing\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Zero-edge equations alternate along paths, so every nontrivial connected
component is complete bipartite.  Suppose that \(uv\in E(Z_f)\).  Since
\(n>4\), the set \([n]\setminus\{u,v\}\) has at least three vertices.  The
graph induced there is a disjoint union of bipartite graphs and therefore
is not complete. Choose distinct \(i,j\notin\{u,v\}\) with
\(ij\notin E(Z_f)\).  At least one of \(C_{ij}^{+},C_{ij}^{-}\) is then a
nonzero realizable card.  Because \(u,v\) remain external and
\(\supp\widetilde f\subseteq\{{\mathbf z}:z_u+z_v=1\}\), that card vanishes at the
all-zero boundary string.  This contradicts \cref{eq:rm-weighted-matching},
under which every nonzero card is nonzero at zero.  Hence
\(E(Z_f)=\varnothing\).
\end{proof}

Thus, for the remaining arities, no coordinate pair is invisible to both
realizable contractions: every pair supports at least one nonzero matching card.
We may therefore compare the local card matchings through the common pencil
\cref{eq:rm-card-pencil}.  The first global datum extracted from that
comparison is the following weight-two graph.

\paragraph{The weight-two graph.}

Assume henceforth that \(n\ge6\) and \(E(Z_f)=\varnothing\).  Define the
weight-two edge set
\begin{equation*}
  W_f:=\{ij:1\le i<j\le n,\ a_{ij}\ne0\}.
\end{equation*}
Let \(\mathcal W_f:=([n],W_f)\) be the corresponding simple graph.  For
\(S\subseteq[n]\), write
\[
 W_f[S]:=\{pq\in W_f:p,q\in S\},
 \qquad
 W_f[S]-k:=W_f[S\setminus\{k\}]
\]
for the restricted edge sets.

\begin{lemma}[Matching consistency]
\label{lem:rm-matching-consistency}
Let \(f\) be the fixed minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature of arity \(n\ge6\), and assume
\(E(Z_f)=\varnothing\).  Then the following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(a_\varnothing\ne0\).
\item \(W_f\) is a matching.
\item If both \(C_{ij}^{+}\) and \(C_{ij}^{-}\) are nonzero, their
      support matchings are equal.
\item Every edge \(pq\in W_f\) is a global Walsh equality:
\[
  \supp\widetilde f\subseteq\{{\mathbf z}:z_p=z_q\}.
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If \(a_\varnothing=0\), then
\(\widetilde C_{ij}^{\pm}(\zero)=\pm2a_{ij}\).  Since
\(E(Z_f)=\varnothing\)
and matching cards are nonzero at zero, every \(a_{ij}\ne0\).  The two
\(ij\)-cards would then cover every edge on the remaining at least four
inputs, impossible for two matchings.  Hence \(a_\varnothing\ne0\).

For a deleted pair \(i,j\), let \(P,Q\) be the support matchings when both
cards survive, and write \(P\mathbin\triangle Q\) for their symmetric
difference.  Their weight-two entries imply
\begin{equation*}
  W_f[[n]\setminus\{i,j\}]\subseteq P\cup Q,
  \qquad
  P\mathbin\triangle Q
  \subseteq W_f[[n]\setminus\{i,j\}].
\end{equation*}
Indeed, for \(pq\in W_f[[n]\setminus\{i,j\}]\) the two pencil values cannot
both vanish. If one whole card vanishes, the other has value
\(\pm4a_{pq}\) at \(pq\).  Thus, with one nonzero card,
\(W_f[[n]\setminus\{i,j\}]\) lies in its perfect
matching.  We use the following elementary graph argument.  If a vertex of
\(\mathcal W_f\) had degree at least three, delete two vertices outside it and its
three neighbours (possible since \(n\ge6\)). The induced graph has degree at
least three, whereas the union of two perfect matchings has maximum degree
two (and one matching has maximum degree one), a contradiction.  Hence every
component of \(\mathcal W_f\) is a path or a cycle.

Suppose a path component has at least two edges.  If two vertices lie outside
that component, delete them. Otherwise (then the component has at least
\(n-1\ge5\) vertices) delete two consecutive vertices at one end, leaving a
path with at least two edges.  For this deleted pair, one nonzero card is
impossible.  If both cards are nonzero, the two edges at the middle of the
retained path must lie one in
\(P\) and one in \(Q\), hence in \(P\triangle Q\).  Alternating along the
path forces an edge of \(P\triangle Q\) at an endpoint, but
\(P\triangle Q\) is a disjoint union of cycles and
\(P\triangle Q\subseteq W_f[[n]\setminus\{i,j\}]\), whose endpoint has no
second edge.  This is impossible.  A cycle is handled similarly: a triangle
is retained by deleting vertices outside it and cannot alternate. For a
4-cycle delete one cycle vertex and one outside vertex, and for a cycle of
length at least five delete two adjacent cycle vertices, leaving a path with
at least two edges.  Thus every component has at most one edge, so \(W_f\)
is a matching.  Finally, for any pair with both cards nonzero,
\(P\triangle Q\) is a union of cycles contained in the matching \(W_f\).
Therefore \(P\triangle Q=\varnothing\) and \(P=Q\).

For \(pq\in W_f\), suppose \({\mathbf{z}}\in\supp\widetilde f\) and
\(z_p\ne z_q\).  Choose distinct \(i,j\in[n]\setminus\{p,q\}\) with
\(z_i=z_j\).  Both \(ij\)-cards vanish on this restriction because every
nonzero one contains \(pq\). \Cref{eq:rm-card-pencil} then kills both
lifts, contradicting \({\mathbf{z}}\in\supp\widetilde f\).  Hence \(z_p=z_q\) on the
support.
\end{proof}

When \(W_f\ne\varnothing\), \cref{lem:rm-matching-consistency} supplies
global equality constraints.  The remaining issue is whether the
coefficients separate across these pairs.  The following lemma recovers
that product structure from diagonal restrictions.

\begin{lemma}[Diagonal-slice integration]
\label{lem:diagonal-slice-integration}
Let \(r\ge3\), and let \(h:\Ftwo^r\to\mathbb C^\times\) satisfy
\(h(\zero)=1\).  Suppose that, for every \(i\ne j\), the restriction
\(h|_{t_i=t_j}\), viewed as a signature on the common bit and the other
\(r-2\) bits, is a product of unary signatures (equivalently, is a simple
tensor).  If \(r\ge4\), then
\[
  h({\mathbf t})=\prod_{i=1}^r\rho_i^{t_i}
  \qquad(\rho_i\in\mathbb C^\times).
\]
If \(r=3\), there are
\(\rho_1,\rho_2,\rho_3,\kappa\in\mathbb C^\times\) such that, for distinct
\(i,j\),
\begin{equation}
  h({\mathbf{e}}_i)=\rho_i,\qquad
  h({\mathbf{e}}_i+{\mathbf{e}}_j)=\kappa\rho_i\rho_j,\qquad
  h(\one^3)=\kappa\rho_1\rho_2\rho_3.
  \label{eq:diagonal-slice-q3-table}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Use the multiplicative subset expansion
\begin{equation}
  h({\mathbf t})=\prod_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq[r]}
       \theta_S^{\prod_{i\in S}t_i},
   \qquad \theta_S\in\mathbb C^\times.
  \label{eq:multiplicative-moebius-expansion}
\end{equation}
Multiplicative M\"obius inversion determines its parameters:
\[
  \theta_S
  =\prod_{T\subseteq S}h(\one_T)^{(-1)^{|S|-|T|}}.
\]
The parameter \(\theta_S\) measures the interaction among the coordinates
in \(S\). Unary factors account exactly for the parameters with
\(|S|=1\).  Thus the coefficient tensor is simple if and only if
\(\theta_S=1\) for every \(|S|\ge2\).  On setting \(t_i=t_j\), a parameter indexed by a
nonempty \(U\subseteq[r]\setminus\{i,j\}\) and the common bit is
\begin{equation}
  \theta_{U\cup\{i\}}
  \theta_{U\cup\{j\}}
  \theta_{U\cup\{i,j\}}=1.
  \label{eq:diagonal-slice-moebius-relation}
\end{equation}
Parameters \(\theta_S\) with \(S\cap\{i,j\}=\varnothing\) and
\(|S|\ge2\) must also equal one.

Assume \(r\ge4\).  Choosing \(i,j\) outside \(S\) first gives
\(\theta_S=1\) for \(2\le|S|\le r-2\).  For \(|S|=r-1\), choose distinct
\(i,j\in S\) and define the index set \(U:=S\setminus\{i,j\}\) in
\cref{eq:diagonal-slice-moebius-relation}. The other two factors have
index-set cardinality \(r-2\), so \(\theta_S=1\).  Taking
\(U=[r]\setminus\{i,j\}\) then gives \(\theta_{[r]}=1\).  Only the unary
parameters remain in \cref{eq:multiplicative-moebius-expansion}, proving
the product formula.

Let \(r=3\).  The three instances of
\cref{eq:diagonal-slice-moebius-relation} imply
\[
  \theta_{12}=\theta_{13}=\theta_{23}=:\kappa,
  \qquad \theta_{123}=\kappa^{-2}.
\]
Substitution in \cref{eq:multiplicative-moebius-expansion}, with
\(\rho_i=\theta_{\{i\}}\), gives
\cref{eq:diagonal-slice-q3-table}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Integration of a nonempty weight-two graph]
\label{lem:rm-weight-two-integration}
Let \(f\) be the fixed minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature of arity \(n\ge6\), with
\(E(Z_f)=\varnothing\).  If \(W_f\ne\varnothing\), then either \(n=6\) and a
constant-size two-copy gadget realizes the even-parity signature of arity
four, or \(f\) admits a nontrivial tensor factorization.  In particular, a
minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature of arity
at least eight has \(W_f=\varnothing\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix \(E_1=\{u,v\}\in W_f\) and define the residual input set
\(R:=[n]\setminus E_1\).  For \(k\in R\), let
\(A_k:=C_{uk}^{+}\).  Since
\(\widetilde A_k(\zero)=2a_\varnothing\ne0\), this is a nonzero matching card.
The global equality on \(E_1\) gives the unique lift
\begin{align*}
  \widetilde A_k(z_v,{\mathbf z}_{R\setminus\{k\}})
  &=2\widetilde f(z_u=z_v,z_k=z_v,{\mathbf z}_{R\setminus\{k\}}),\\
  \widetilde A_k(\zero)&=2a_\varnothing\ne0,
  \\
  \widetilde A_k({\mathbf{e}}_p+{\mathbf{e}}_q)&=2a_{pq}
  \quad(p,q\in R\setminus\{k\}),\\
  \widetilde A_k({\mathbf{e}}_v+{\mathbf{e}}_q)&=2a_{\{u,v,k,q\}}
  \quad(q\in R\setminus\{k\}).
\end{align*}
Its matching therefore consists of \(W_f[R]-k\) and at most one edge from
\(v\).  To justify the last assertion, let \(U\) be the set of vertices not
covered by \(W_f\). It excludes \(u,v\).  For every \(k\), all vertices of
\(U\setminus\{k\}\) remain on the card boundary and have no available
weight-two edge, except that at most one can be paired through \(v\).
If \(W_f=\{E_1\}\), then \(|U|=n-2\ge4\), and the preceding restriction
already gives a contradiction.  Otherwise a covered vertex of \(W_f\) lies
in \(R\), so choose \(k\in R\setminus U\). Hence \(|U|\le1\).
Since \(|U|=n-2|W_f|\) is even, \(U=\varnothing\).  Thus \(W_f\) is
perfect. Write
its edges \(E_1,\ldots,E_s\), where \(s=n/2\).

By the global equality conclusion of
\cref{lem:rm-matching-consistency}, for every string \({\mathbf z}\) constant on each
\(E_r\), let \(t_r({\mathbf z})\) denote that common edge bit.  There is a logical
coefficient tensor \(h:\Ftwo^s\to\mathbb C\) such that
\begin{equation*}
  \widetilde f({\mathbf z})=
  \begin{cases}
    h(t_1({\mathbf z}),\ldots,t_s({\mathbf z})),
      &{\mathbf z}|_{E_r}\text{ is constant for every }r,\\
    0,&\text{otherwise}.
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
For two distinct \(W_f\)-edges \(E_i,E_j\), close one endpoint of \(E_i\)
to one endpoint of \(E_j\), forming separately the two cards with the
realizable \(+\) and \(-\) kernels.  Since
cross coefficients between different \(W_f\)-edges vanish and
\(a_\varnothing\ne0\), both cards are nonzero.  Both matching supports
impose \(t_i=t_j\). The \(+/-\) choice changes only the sign character in
that common logical bit.  Since \(s=n/2\ge3\),
every \({\mathbf t}\in\Ftwo^s\) has a pair \(i\ne j\) with \(t_i=t_j\). Use the
plus card for that pair.  The boundary string obtained from \({\mathbf t}\)
by retaining the mates of \(E_i,E_j\) and the two inputs of every other
\(E_r\) lies in this card's matching support.  The closure equation has a
unique compatible lift in the global equality code, namely the string with
edge bits \({\mathbf t}\), so the plus-card value is a fixed nonzero scalar times \(h({\mathbf t})\)
(there is no second lift with which it could cancel).  A nonzero matching
product is nonzero at every supported boundary string. Hence \(h({\mathbf t})\ne0\) for
every \({\mathbf t}\), and \(h\) has full support.
Rescale it by \(a_\varnothing^{-1}\) (absorbing
this nonzero scalar into \(f\)), so that \(h(\zero)=1\).  Every cross-pair
diagonal slice \(t_i=t_j\) is nonzero and rank one.  By
\cref{lem:diagonal-slice-integration},
\(s\ge4\) would make \(f\) a product of \(s\) nonsingular binaries, which
is the factorization alternative of the lemma.  Hence, on the remaining
branch, \(s=3\). Use the parameters
\(\rho_1,\rho_2,\rho_3,\kappa\ne0\) of
\cref{eq:diagonal-slice-q3-table}.  Let \(H_i^\pm\) be the \(2\times2\)
logical coefficient matrix obtained by closing \(E_i\) with the \(\pm\)
kernel.  For \(\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}\), one has
\begin{equation*}
  \det H_i^{\pm}
  =\rho_j \rho_k(\kappa-1)(1-\kappa \rho_i^2).
\end{equation*}
If \(\kappa=1\), \(h\), and hence \(f\), has the asserted product
factorization.  On the remaining branch \(\kappa\ne1\), and rank one of the
nonzero cards forces
\begin{equation*}
  \kappa \rho_1^2=\kappa \rho_2^2=\kappa \rho_3^2=1.
\end{equation*}

In Walsh coordinates, write \(E_i=\{i^0,i^1\}\) for the two
inputs of each matching edge. The realizable binary closing chains for
the \(I/X\)-contractions have Walsh kernels proportional to
\(\epsilon^u\one[u=v]\), with \(\epsilon=1\) for \(I\) and
\(\epsilon=-1\) for \(X\). Two such chains joining matching edges
contribute \((\epsilon_0\epsilon_1)^u\). Choosing their signs gives
either character on the common bit, using the original \(X\)-edges.

Let \(E'_1,E'_2,E'_3\) denote the corresponding matching edges in the second
copy.  Use two such binary closing chains for each displayed edge-to-edge join:
join the two inputs of \(E_3\) in the first copy to the two inputs of \(E'_1\)
in the second copy, and join the two inputs of \(E'_2\) to those of \(E'_3\)
inside the second copy. Leave both inputs of \(E_1\) and \(E_2\) in the first
copy external.  Evaluating these contractions by
\cref{eq:rm-card-pencil} gives the logical characters below, and every
internal matching assignment has a unique lift.  Choose the realizable
\(I/X\)-signs
\[
  \theta=\kappa \rho_2 \rho_3,
  \qquad
  \eta=-\kappa \rho_1 \rho_3,
\]
which lie in \(\{\pm1\}\).  Writing \(x=t_1\) and \(y=t_2\) for the two
external logical bits, the logical coefficient matrix is
\begin{equation*}
  Q(x,y)=
  \sum_{a,c\in\Ftwo}
  \eta^a\theta^c h(x,y,a)h(a,c,c).
\end{equation*}
Using \(\sum_c\theta^c h(a,c,c)=2\rho_1^a\), this becomes
\begin{equation*}
  Q\doteq
  \frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}
  \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&\kappa \rho_1 \rho_2\end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation*}
Thus the realized quaternary signature has Walsh coordinates
\[
 \widetilde F
 =c\bigl(\delta_{0000}+\sigma\delta_{1111}\bigr),
 \qquad
 c\ne0,
 \qquad \sigma=\kappa \rho_1\rho_2\in\{\pm1\},
\]
where the two nonzero coefficients follow from \(\kappa\ne1\).  Applying
the inverse analytic change of coordinates, namely
\(F=P^{\otimes4}\widetilde F\), gives
\[
 F({\mathbf{x}})=c\bigl(1+\sigma(-1)^{x_1+x_2+x_3+x_4}\bigr).
\]
Define the even-parity signature by
\[
 \mathrm{Even}_4({\mathbf{x}}):=\one[x_1+x_2+x_3+x_4=0].
\]
Thus the realizable tensor is a nonzero scalar multiple of
\(\mathrm{Even}_4\) when \(\sigma=1\), and of its odd-parity coset when
\(\sigma=-1\). A realizable \(X\)-dressing on one exposed input toggles the
coset. For the equality-anchor condition, use the
matrix \(U:=D(i)P^{-1}\). It satisfies
\[
 U^{\otimes4}\mathrm{Even}_4\doteq(\Eq_4),\qquad
 (U^{-1})^{\mathsf T}XU^{-1}
 =D(i)^{-1}P^{\mathsf T}XP D(i)^{-1}=2I=2\,(\Eq_2).
\]
Consequently a realizable \(\mathrm{Even}_4\) equality anchor is obtained, and
\cref{lem:equality-anchor} applies.
\end{proof}

A nonempty \(W_f\) has therefore already produced an equality anchor or a
proper factorization.  After those alternatives have been resolved, the
remaining case has
\[
  W_f=\varnothing.
\]
Scale \(a_\varnothing=1\).  With all weight-two coefficients absent, the
common card matchings determine the entire Walsh support.

\begin{lemma}[Reed--Muller support reconstruction]
\label{lem:rm-local-slices}
\label{lem:rm-support-classification}
Let \(f\) be the fixed minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature of arity \(n\ge6\), normalized by
\(a_\varnothing=1\), and assume \(E(Z_f)=\varnothing\) and
\(W_f=\varnothing\).  For \(i\ne j\), let \(M_{ij}\) be the common support
matching of the two \(ij\)-cards.  Fix an arbitrary ordering of its
\(n/2-1\) edges and, for \({\mathbf t}\in\Ftwo^{n/2-1}\), write \(t_e\) for the
coordinate indexed by \(e\in M_{ij}\).  Let
\(D:=\supp\widetilde f\) be the support set.  Its equal-\(ij\) slice is
the \((n/2-1)\)-dimensional code
\begin{equation}
  D_{ij}=
  \left\{
    \one_{T({\mathbf t})}+(\wt({\mathbf t})\bmod2)({\mathbf{e}}_i+{\mathbf{e}}_j):
    {\mathbf t}\in\Ftwo^{n/2-1}
  \right\},
  \label{eq:rm-local-code}
\end{equation}
where
\(
  T({\mathbf t})=\bigcup_{e\in M_{ij}:\,t_e=1}e.
\)
Every codeword of \(D_{ij}\) has weight divisible by four.  Moreover,
\[
  n=8,
  \qquad
  D=RM(1,3),
\]
after a bijective relabelling of the inputs by \(\Ftwo^3\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{lem:rm-matching-consistency}, the two \(ij\)-cards survive with a
common support matching \(M_{ij}\).  Normalize them at the zero string
\({\mathbf t}=\zero^{n/2-1}\) in matching coordinates, and write \(r_{ij,e}^{+}\) and
\(r_{ij,e}^{-}\) for the corresponding normalized edge ratios (the
\(t_e=1\) factor entry divided by its \(t_e=0\) entry).  For each
\(e\in M_{ij}\), set \(\rho_{ij,e}:=r_{ij,e}^{-}/r_{ij,e}^{+}\).  Their
quotient on the matching code---the repetition code consisting of assignments
constant on every edge of \(M_{ij}\), parametrized by \({\mathbf t}\)---is
\[
  \frac{\widetilde C_{ij}^{-}({\mathbf t})}
       {\widetilde C_{ij}^{+}({\mathbf t})}
  =\prod_{e\in M_{ij}}\rho_{ij,e}^{t_e}.
\]
Each single-edge quotient is \(-1\) by \(a_{pq}=0\) and
\cref{eq:rm-card-pencil}. Hence the quotient is \((-1)^{\wt({\mathbf t})}\).  Inverting
the pencil selects the \(ij=00\) lift for even \(\wt({\mathbf t})\) and \(ij=11\) for
odd \(\wt({\mathbf t})\), proving \cref{eq:rm-local-code}. The selected string has
weight \(2\wt({\mathbf t})\) in the even case and \(2\wt({\mathbf t})+2\) in the odd case, so
its weight is divisible by four in either case.

For \({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in D\), some two coordinates have the same pair
\((x_i,y_i)\). The linear slice \(D_{ij}\) then contains \({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{y}}\).
Thus \(D\) is linear.  For the linear functional
\(\ell_{ij}:\Ftwo^n\to\Ftwo\) defined by
\(\ell_{ij}({\mathbf z}):=z_i+z_j\), one has
\[
  D\cap\ker\ell_{ij}=D_{ij}.
\]
This subspace has dimension \(n/2-1\).  If
\(\dim D=n/2-1\), then \(D=D_{ij}\subseteq\ker\ell_{ij}\) for every
\(i\ne j\).  Hence every codeword of \(D\) has all coordinates equal, so
\(\dim D\le1\), contradicting \(n/2-1\ge2\).  Therefore
\(\dim D=n/2\).
Every codeword of \(D\) lies in one of the displayed slices and hence has weight
divisible by four.  For \({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\in D\),
\[
 0\equiv \wt({\mathbf{x}})+\wt({\mathbf{y}})-\wt({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{y}})=2{\mathbf{x}}\cdot {\mathbf{y}}\pmod 4.
\]
Thus \(D\subseteq D^\perp\), and equal dimensions give
\begin{equation*}
  D=D^\perp.
\end{equation*}

For distinct \(i,j,p\), the mate \(q\) of \(p\) in \(M_{ij}\) gives the
unique weight-four codeword \(\{i,j,p,q\}\).  Now
\cref{lem:self-dual-steiner-reconstruction} yields
\(n=8\) and \(D=RM(1,3)\).
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Coefficient rigidity and the order of the cyclic group.}
